Infinity-harmonic functions and inverse mean curvature flow clusters
Kai Xu
Abstract
An \infty-harmonic function is a viscosity solution of \nabla^{2}u(\nabla u,\nabla u)=0, or equivalently, an absolute minimizer of \|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}}. We prove a variety of new structural and regularity results in two dimensions, including:
•
\infty-harmonic functions in domains of \mathbb{R}^{2} are C^{1,1/3}.
•
Critical points are isolated, and at each critical point, the solution has a unique quasiradial blow-up.
•
Entire solutions with polynomial growth have unique quasiradial blow-downs, and are determined by their Fourier modes at infinity.
These results are consequences of a new theory relating \infty-harmonic functions to inverse mean curvature flow (IMCF) clusters – which are piecewise weak solutions of IMCF with common obstacle-type boundary conditions on the interfaces (a simple example is an embedded family of cuspidal curves evolving by inverse curvature). This connection arises as the p\to\infty limit of the classical duality between p-harmonic and q-harmonic functions in \mathbb{R}^{2}, where \frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1.
Contents
1 Introduction
For p\in(1,\infty), we say that u is a p-harmonic function if it is a distributional solution of the equation
\Delta_{p}u:=\div\big(|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\big)=0,
(1.1)
equivalently, if u minimizes the L^{p} Dirichlet energy \|\nabla u\|_{L^{p}} among compactly supported perturbations. Let \Omega be a simply-connected domain in \mathbb{R}^{2}. Given a p-harmonic function u in \Omega, one may construct a q-harmonic function v, where \frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1, such that
\nabla v=|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla^{\perp}u,\qquad\text{or}\qquad\nabla u=-|\nabla v|^{q-2}\nabla^{\perp}v.
(1.2)
In this paper, the symbol “\perp” means rotation of vectors by 90^{\circ} counterclockwise.
As p\to\infty, p-harmonic functions are known to converge to \infty-harmonic functions. The study of this class of objects was pioneered by Aronsson [10, 11] in the 1960s. It can be understood from the following perspectives. Expanding (1.1), we find
\Delta_{p}u=|\nabla u|^{p-2}\Delta u+(p-2)|\nabla u|^{p-4}\nabla^{2}u(\nabla u,\nabla u),
and the dominant term is \nabla^{2}u(\nabla u,\nabla u) as p\to\infty. The operator \nabla^{2}u(\nabla u,\nabla u) is called the \infty-Laplacian and is denoted by \Delta_{\infty}. Next, formally taking p\to\infty of the L^{p} Dirichlet energy, we obtain the L^{\infty} Dirichlet energy \|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}}. In summary, we find the following candidate definitions of \infty-harmonicity:
(i)
A viscosity solution of \nabla^{2}u(\nabla u,\nabla u)=0.
(ii)
A minimizer of \|\nabla u\|_{L^{\infty}} among compactly supported perturbations.
(iii)
The C^{0}_{\loc} limit of a sequence of p-harmonic functions, as p\to\infty.
The following comparison with cones property, based on maximum principle, is also useful:
(iv)
A function u so that, for any cone function c(x)=a+b|x-x_{0}| with u\geqslant c or u\leqslant c on \partial(V\setminus\{x_{0}\}) for a precompact V, it holds u\geqslant c or u\leqslant c in V.
The four formulations are all equivalent, as was shown in a series of works by Bhattacharya-DiBenedetto-Manfredi [22], Jensen [47], and Crandall-Evans-Gariepy [29]. Initially, Aronsson [10, 11] worked with definitions (i)(ii) and only considered C^{2} solutions. The use of viscosity solutions in (i) was first introduced in [22]. As we shall see, \infty-harmonic functions are typically non-C^{2}. Comparison with cones played a central role in the more recent developments [28, 37, 69]. Later, Peres-Schramm-Sheffield-Wilson [67] studied the \infty-Laplacian on metric spaces and discovered the connection to the random tug-of-war game, and Daskalopoulos-Uhlenbeck [31, 32, 33] studied \infty-harmonic maps in relation with Thurston’s asymmetric metric on Teichmüller spaces.
The regularity of \infty-harmonic functions remains a central question. In \mathbb{R}^{2}, the best known result is C^{1,\alpha} for some \alpha>0, by Evans-Savin [37]; see also Savin [69], Zhang-Zhou [76]. Koch-Zhang-Zhou [48] also showed that |\nabla u|\in W^{1,2}_{\loc}. Much more is known for \infty-harmonic potentials in convex rings [53, 54, 66]. In \mathbb{R}^{n} (n\geqslant 3), the best known regularity is everywhere differentiability, by Evans-Smart [38, 39]. Many standard techniques fail since \Delta_{\infty} is degenerate elliptic. The fine structure of \infty-harmonic functions remains largely unknown; it is one of the main objectives of this paper.
In \mathbb{R}^{2}, the solution
u_{0}=|x|^{4/3}-|y|^{4/3}
(1.3)
was discovered by Aronsson [12] in 1984, and was the first known nonsmooth \infty-harmonic function. For each d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 2}, there exists a solution in \mathbb{R}^{2} of the form
u=r^{\frac{d^{2}}{2d-1}}\varphi(\theta),
(1.4)
which we call a degree d quasiradial solution. This includes (1.3) as the d=2 instance; see Example 4.3. These examples are of C^{1,1/3} regularity and no better.
Since Aronsson’s work, it remained open whether one should expect the sharp C^{1,1/3} regularity in general. We answer this question affirmatively.
Theorem 1.1.
Suppose u is \infty-harmonic in a domain \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}. Then u\in C^{1,1/3}_{\loc}(\Omega).
The main component of the proof is the following \varepsilon-regularity theorem: when u is almost linear, we prove a sharp C^{1,1/3} estimate of the type
\|u-x\|_{C^{0}}\leqslant\delta^{2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\big\|\log|\nabla u|\big\|_{C^{0,2/3}}\leqslant C\delta^{4/3},\quad\Big\|\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}-\partial_{x}\Big\|_{C^{0,1/3}}\leqslant C\delta^{2/3}.
(1.5)
See Theorems 8.1 and 8.2 for the precise statements. It is noteworthy that |\nabla u| gains more regularity than the vector part of \nabla u.
The consequences of the main theory include a structural result near critical points:
Theorem 1.2.
Suppose u is nonconstant and \infty-harmonic in a domain \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}. Then \Crit(u) is discrete in \Omega. For each z_{0}\in\Crit(u), there exists d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 2} so that the family of functions
\lambda^{-\frac{d^{2}}{2d-1}}\big[u(\lambda z+z_{0})-u(z_{0})\big]
converges in C^{1}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}) to a degree d quasiradial solution, as \lambda\to 0.
We also study entire \infty-harmonic functions with polynomial growth:
Theorem 1.3.
Suppose u is a nonlinear entire \infty-harmonic function in \mathbb{R}^{2}, with
|u|\leqslant C\big(1+|z|^{n}\big)\qquad\text{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}^{2},
for some C,n>0. Then there exists d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 2} so that the family of functions
\lambda^{-\frac{d^{2}}{2d-1}}u(\lambda z)
converges in C^{1}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}) to a degree d quasiradial solution, as \lambda\to\infty.
Let us call d the degree of a polynomial growth solution. We also investigate low-degree solutions: in Theorem 12.2, we show that u_{0}=|x|^{4/3}-|y|^{4/3} is the unique degree 2 solution, up to rigid motion and scaling. The family of degree 3 solutions is constructed in Subsection 4.1. Further structural properties of the solutions in Theorem 1.2, 1.3 are discussed later in this introduction. The d=1 case was classified by Savin [69] by showing that entire solutions with linear growth must be linear. See also [34] for an integral version of linear Liouville theorem.
Let us turn to the q\to 1 side of the duality (1.2). It is known that q-harmonic functions converge to 1-harmonic functions, or functions of least gradient [42], once suitably normalized (for instance, one may multiply v by a constant so that \int|\nabla v|=1). A renormalization is necessary since \nabla v=|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla^{\perp}u does not converge in general. Taking p\to\infty and q\to 1 simultaneously, this yields a duality between \infty-harmonic and 1-harmonic functions. This duality was studied by Daskalopoulos-Uhlenbeck [31], see also [16, 32, 43, 64]: roughly stated, the result is that the maximal gradient set
S=\Big\{x\in\Omega:|\nabla u(x)|=\sup_{\Omega}|\nabla u|\Big\}
(1.6)
of the \infty-harmonic function u is a geodesic lamination, and the dual 1-harmonic function v\in\BV has Dv supported on S. From an analytic point of view, this duality only reveals information about u on the maximal gradient set. In the study of regularity, a finer duality is perhaps needed.
A key idea of R. Moser [63], built on the earlier work [61], is to consider the transformation
w=(1-q)\log v,
(1.7)
and the q\to 1 limit of w. Using \Delta_{q}v=0, one may verify
\Delta_{q}w=|\nabla w|^{q}.
(1.8)
Hence, the limit should formally solve the equation
\div\Big(\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}\Big)=\Delta_{1}w=|\nabla w|.
(1.9)
The equation (1.9) is known as the level set form of the inverse mean curvature flow (IMCF). If w is a smooth solution of (1.9), then the level sets \gamma_{t}=\{w=t\} evolve with normal speed equal to 1/(\text{curvature of $\gamma_{t}$}) everywhere.
The study of IMCF arose from its connections to scalar curvature via the monotonicity of Hawking mass. A milestone in the development was the introduction of the weak IMCF by Huisken-Ilmanen [44]. The weak IMCF is a variational form of (1.9). The function w is no longer smooth. The evolving curves, now \gamma_{t}=\partial\{w<t\}, may jump forward instantaneously. A jump corresponds to a set \Int\big(\{w=t\}\big), see the grey regions in Figure 1. The portion of the curve after each jump, namely the set \partial\{w\leqslant t\}\setminus\partial\{w<t\}, is always a line segment (blue segments in Figure 1; see also Remark 2.9(iv)). We refer to Subsection 2.3 or [44, 74] for more discussions.
Figure 1: A right-evolving weak IMCF.
The renormalization (1.7) was first introduced by R. Moser in [61], where he showed the following: whenever a sequence of q-harmonic functions v_{q} satisfies
\lim_{q\searrow 1}(1-q)\log v_{q}=w\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{0}_{\loc}(\Omega),
(1.10)
then w is a weak IMCF in \Omega. This major connection was further developed by subsequent works including [2, 3, 17, 18, 19, 49, 59]. As a model example, the function v_{q}=|x|^{-\frac{n-q}{q-1}} is q-harmonic in \mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}, and its renormalized limit (1.10) in C^{0}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) is the spherically symmetric IMCF w=(n-1)\log|x|.
To summarize: given an \infty-harmonic function u, we have considered an approximating sequence of p-harmonic functions u_{p}\to u as p\to\infty, and then the procedure
\displaystyle\Delta_{p}u_{p}=0\quad\to\quad\nabla v_{p}=|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\nabla^{\perp}u_{p}
(1.11)
\displaystyle\to\quad w_{p}=(1-q)\log v_{p}\quad\to\quad w=\lim_{q\searrow 1}w_{p}.
In [63], R. Moser applied (1.11) to a subclass of \infty-harmonic functions called oriented solutions. An oriented solution u has no critical points, and |\nabla u| is monotone in a uniform direction along all level sets of u. Applying (1.11) locally to an oriented solution, it is shown that the limit function is w=-\log|\nabla u|. Thus, [63] shows that:
if u is an oriented \infty-harmonic function, then -\log|\nabla u| is a weak IMCF.
The arguments of [63] inspired us in particular in the proof of Theorem 6.1. The reason that w=-\log|\nabla u| for oriented solutions lies at the core of the proof; we refer to the detailed discussion in Section 6.
In fact, more is known from [63]: denoting \nu=\pm\nabla^{\perp}u/|\nabla u| (where the sign depends on the orientation), it is shown that w=-\log|\nabla u| satisfies
|\nu|\leqslant 1,\qquad\langle\nu\,,\,\nabla w\rangle=|\nabla w|\ \ \text{a.e.},\qquad\div(\nu)=|\nabla w|\ \ \text{distributionally.}
(1.12)
A function w satisfying (1.12) for some measurable vector field \nu is called a calibrated IMCF, and \nu is called a calibration of w. A calibrated IMCF is always a weak IMCF. The condition |\nu|\leqslant 1 is trivial in the context of this paragraph, but is substantial in general.
Condition (1.12) is another weak form of the IMCF equation (1.9): indeed, the calibration \nu is a weak replacement of \nabla w/|\nabla w| in (1.9). In calibrated IMCFs, \nu is shown to be the outer unit normal of the level sets \gamma_{t}=\partial\{w<t\}. Thus, each \gamma_{t} is “calibrated” by \nu similarly to the traditional sense. It is a general principle that calibrated submanifolds are area-minimizing. This explains many of the area-minimizing properties in IMCF: for example, in a calibrated IMCF, each sub-level set \{w<t\} minimizes the perimeter-like energy
J_{u}(E)=|\partial E|-\int_{E}|\nabla u|
among compact perturbations (this is in fact a defining property of weak IMCFs). See Subsection 2.3 for more discussions. In this paper, we mostly work with calibrated IMCFs.
We turn our attention back to oriented solutions. The orientedness condition is non-generic. The solution u_{0}=|x|^{4/3}-|y|^{4/3} is not oriented: it is oriented when restricted to each quadrant, but not across. Therefore, -\log|\nabla u_{0}| is a weak IMCF (in fact, a smooth IMCF in this case) in each quadrant. It is natural to investigate the behavior on the x- and y-axes. Figure 2 shows the shape of the level sets
\gamma_{t}=\big\{\!-\log|\nabla u_{0}|=t\big\}=\big\{|\nabla u_{0}|=e^{-t}\big\},
where we see cusps forming on the interfaces. More precisely: \{\gamma_{t}\} is a self-shrinking solution of the IMCF consisting of 4 identical copies of hypocycloids.
Figure 2: Level sets of |\nabla u_{0}|, where u_{0}=|x|^{4/3}-|y|^{4/3}.
As a useful note for future discussions: the edges of \gamma_{t} are streamlines of u_{0} (meaning integral lines of the gradient flow) in addition to being level sets of |\nabla u_{0}|. Indeed, as u_{0} is smooth in each quadrant, we may write
0=\Delta_{\infty}u_{0}=\nabla^{2}u_{0}(\nabla u_{0},\nabla u_{0})=\frac{1}{2}{\big\langle{\nabla u_{0}}\,,\,{\nabla|\nabla u_{0}|^{2}}\big\rangle},
hence the level sets of |\nabla u_{0}| are streamlines of u_{0}.
The structure of cusps and streamlines are general: for an \infty-harmonic function u, each set \partial\{|\nabla u|<e^{-t}\} is a union of concave curves with cusps at their endpoints, and all edges are streamlines of u (Theorem 9.1).
The boundary condition on the interfaces can be understood in two natural ways: either as an IMCF of curves with cusps, or as a continuously glued piecewise IMCF where the level sets are tangent to the interfaces. The latter tangential condition is known as the outer obstacle condition, initially introduced in [75]. For calibrated IMCFs, i.e. solutions of (1.12), the outer obstacle condition for a region D naturally states
\nu=\nu_{D}\qquad\text{on}\ \ \partial D
(1.13)
(in a suitable sense), where \nu_{D} denotes the outer unit normal of D. We refer the reader to [75] for more discussions on the weak outer obstacle condition. This paper mainly involves calibrated solutions, and the main existence result in [75] is not used.
The notion of simple IMCF cluster refers to the structures of calibrated piecewise IMCF with outer obstacles. A simple IMCF cluster consists of a continuous function w and vector field \nu, and a partition of \Omega into C^{1} regions D_{i}, so that each w|_{D_{i}} is a weak IMCF with outer obstacle \partial D_{i} and is calibrated by \pm\nu (Definition 3.1). The sign of \nu in the calibration corresponds to the orientations of D_{i}. The interfaces, i.e. the common boundaries of D_{i}, are called ridges.
Figure 3: Quasiradial solutions with degree 2,3,4.
For u_{0}=|x|^{4/3}-|y|^{4/3}, the function w=-\log|\nabla u_{0}| is a simple IMCF cluster in \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}, with the four quadrants being the natural partition and the x, y-axes being the ridges. For a degree d quasiradial solution, w=-\log|\nabla u| is a simple IMCF cluster in \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\} under the division into 2d sectors with angles \pi/d (Figure 3).
It remains to be seen how the structure of ridges appears in the procedure (1.11). In Moser’s transformation (1.7), the logarithm requires the positivity of v. However, as v comes from integrating |\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla^{\perp}u, it need not have a definite sign. The ridges in fact arise from the sign changes. We observe the general philosophy
\displaystyle\text{The $q\to 1$ limit of the Dirichlet condition for $q$-harmonic functions}
(1.14)
\displaystyle\text{is the outer obstacle condition for the weak IMCF.}
This idea first appeared in [75] and was formally addressed in [18, Theorem 1.10]. It is shown in [18] that for smooth domains E_{0}\Subset\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n}, the q-capacitary potentials v with
\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{q}v=0\qquad\text{in}\ \ \Omega\setminus\overline{E_{0}}\\
&v|_{\partial E_{0}}=1\\
&v|_{\partial\Omega}=0\end{aligned}\right.
have (1-q)\log v converging to a weak IMCF with outer obstacle \partial\Omega, as q\to 1.
In our present context, we have q-harmonic functions v_{p} obtained from integrating |\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\nabla^{\perp}u_{p}. Then naturally, |v_{p}| is a positive q-harmonic function in both \{v_{p}>0\} and \{v_{p}<0\}, with common Dirichlet conditions on \{v_{p}=0\} (which moves as p changes). We consider the limiting procedures:
(1)
The set \{v_{p}=0\} subsequentially converges to a collection of C^{1} curves \{\gamma_{j}\}, as p\to\infty and q\to 1.
(2)
Take \{D_{i}\} to be the connected components of \Omega\setminus\cup\gamma_{j}.
(3)
Take a subsequential limit w_{i}=\lim_{q\to 1}(1-q)\log|v_{p}| in C^{0}_{\loc}(D_{i}), for each i.
Then, we show that w_{i} is a weak IMCF in each D_{i}, and satisfies the outer obstacle condition, in accordance with (1.14). This finally yields a simple IMCF cluster. The limit curves \{\gamma_{j}\} become the ridges in the resulting cluster.
To summarize, we now consider the process
\displaystyle\Delta_{\infty}u=0\quad\xleftarrow[\,p\to\infty]{C^{1}_{\loc}}\quad\Delta_{p}u_{p}=0
\displaystyle\to\quad\nabla v_{p}=|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\nabla^{\perp}u_{p}
(1.15)
\displaystyle\to\quad w_{p}=(1-q)\log|v_{p}|
\displaystyle\to\quad w=\lim_{q\searrow 1}w_{p}\text{ is a simple IMCF cluster}.
We refer to Section 6 for the full statement, proofs, and further discussion about this process. Notice that the convergence u_{p}\to u holds in C^{1}_{\loc}: this follows from Theorem 5.1 and is new as well. The convergence of gradients is important to us.
The C^{1,1/3} regularity in Theorem 1.1 relies on the optimal regularity of simple IMCF clusters, in particular, the regularity near ridges. A central tool therein is the use of support functions. The support function of a C^{1} curve \gamma is
h(\gamma(s))=\langle\gamma(s)\,,\,\nu(s)\rangle,
(1.16)
where \nu(s)=-\gamma^{\prime}(s)^{\perp} is the orientation-compatible unit normal vector along \gamma.
For a smooth solution of IMCF by strictly convex curves \gamma_{t}, we may parametrize all curves by the angle of the tangent vectors. Then, the support function satisfies the evolution equation
\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}=\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial\theta^{2}}+h.
(1.17)
Indeed, \partial_{t}h is the normal speed, and \partial_{\theta\theta}h+h is the inverse of the curvature (which is a crucial observation). This equation was observed by Chow-Tsai [27] and Urbas [72].
We prove that (1.17) holds in a suitable sense for weak IMCFs and for simple IMCF clusters as well. This is carried out in Section 7. Note that, on a union of concave curves with cusps at endpoints, there is a continuous angle parameter through all cusps.
Under this parametrization, the edges alternate between being concave and convex. In terms of support functions, the inverse curvature \partial_{\theta\theta}h+h changes sign, and the zeros of \partial_{\theta\theta}h+h are the angles at the cusps. A main component of Theorem 7.1 is that (1.17) holds across the cusps.
For instance, in a degree d quasiradial solution, the level sets \gamma_{t}=\{|\nabla u|=e^{-t}\} have support functions given by
h(t,\theta)=\exp\Big(\frac{1-2d}{(d-1)^{2}}t\Big)\sin\frac{-d\theta}{d-1},\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\big(2(d-1)\pi\big),
(1.18)
where \mathbb{S}^{1}(l) denotes the circle with length l. Observe that \partial_{\theta\theta}h+h changes sign 2d times, corresponding to the 2d cusps in \gamma_{t}. See Example 4.3 for details. It is notable that (1.18) is a parabolic scaling of the degree d Fourier mode.
If h solves (1.17), then the signed inverse curvature \kappa^{-1}=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h also solves the same equation \partial_{t}\kappa^{-1}=\partial_{\theta\theta}\kappa^{-1}+\kappa^{-1}. At a point (t_{0},\theta_{0}) where \kappa^{-1} changes sign (corresponding to a cusp), one has the asymptotics
\kappa^{-1}\approx c(\theta-\theta_{0})
(1.19)
due to the nondegeneracy of roots. Then, switching to a length parameter s, we have
\frac{d\theta}{ds}=\kappa\approx\frac{1}{c(\theta-\theta_{0})}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\frac{d\big[(\theta-\theta_{0})^{2}\big]}{ds}\approx\frac{2}{c}.
(1.20)
This shows that the tangential angle changes in a C^{0,1/2} manner in length parameter, and hence, the edges of \gamma_{t} have C^{1,1/2} regularity up to the cusps. This regularity is sharp, since in quasiradial solutions, the edges of \gamma_{t} are hypocycloids, hence are all C^{1,1/2}.
The following principle applies to the edges of \gamma_{t}, after a suitable formulation:
\displaystyle\text{If a disjoint family of curves has uniform $C^{1,\alpha}$ regularity,}
(1.21)
\displaystyle\text{then the collection of all their normal vectors is $C^{0,\alpha/(1+\alpha)}$.}
See Lemma 2.10, 2.14. The Hölder exponent \frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha} is sharp as well. Finally, since
\frac{1/2}{1+1/2}=\frac{1}{3},
the C^{1,1/2} regularity of \gamma_{t} turns into the C^{0,1/3} regularity of the joint normal vector field. As \gamma_{t} are streamlines of u, this explains the source of C^{1,1/3} regularity in Theorem 1.1.
These ideas are used in Section 8 to prove the \varepsilon-regularity statement (1.5). We mention that, hidden in the technicality, the Sturmian principle is our main tool from parabolic theory. The Sturmian principle states that the number of roots of a solution of the heat equation is non-increasing in time; see for example [5, 7]. This is useful in showing a uniform version of (1.19). See Section 8 for further discussion on the techniques.
Conversely, the equation (1.17) can be used to construct simple IMCF clusters. Given an abstract function h(t,\theta) solving \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h, one may reconstruct a family of curves \gamma_{t} (with cusps) that solve the IMCF, so that each \gamma_{t} is parametrized by the angle \theta and has support function h(t,\cdot). The exact formula is
\gamma_{t}(\theta)=h(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}h(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta},
(1.22)
where in this paper we denote
\nu_{\theta}=(\sin\theta,-\cos\theta)=e^{i(\theta-\pi/2)},\qquad\tau_{\theta}=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)=e^{i\theta}.
(1.23)
Once \{\gamma_{t}\} is shown to be an embedded family, this yields a simple IMCF cluster, with the ridges being the trajectories of cusps. From such a simple cluster, using Theorem 4.1, one may then construct an \infty-harmonic function u so that
\nabla u(\gamma_{t}(\theta))=e^{-t}\tau_{\theta}.
(1.24)
The formula (1.24) is chosen so that \gamma_{t}=\{-\log|\nabla u|=t\} and \gamma_{t} are streamlines of u (since \tau_{\theta} is tangent to \gamma_{t} at \gamma_{t}(\theta)). This is consistent with the previous discussions: recall that for an \infty-harmonic function u, all edges of \partial\{|\nabla u|<e^{-t}\} are streamlines.
In Subsection 4.1, we construct \infty-harmonic functions that are asymptotic to degree 3 quasiradial solutions at infinity. To our knowledge, this is the first construction of non-quasiradial degree 3 solutions. The idea is to perturb the quasiradial support function (1.18) (with d=3) by adding lower order terms, namely, to consider
h(t,\theta)=\sum_{j=0}^{3}\exp\Big(t-\frac{j^{2}}{4}t\Big)\Big(a_{j}\sin\frac{j\theta}{2}+b_{j}\cos\frac{j\theta}{2}\Big),\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}(4\pi).
(1.25)
Then, we apply the above procedure (1.22) – (1.24) to this h.
By an alternating length identity (see Subsection 4.1 for details), the constant term must vanish for the construction to succeed. By translation symmetry, we may drop the j=2 term. Eventually, it suffices to consider
h(t,\theta)=e^{-5t/4}\Big(a\sin\frac{3}{2}\theta+b\cos\frac{3}{2}\theta\Big)+e^{3t/4}\Big(c\sin\frac{\theta}{2}+d\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\Big),\quad\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}(4\pi).
(1.26)
Modulo rigid motions and scalings (thus reducing to the case a=1,b=0), we obtain a 2-dimensional family of solutions parametrized by c,d.
The major obstacle in the construction is to ensure the embeddedness of \{\gamma_{t}\}. In fact, the \gamma_{t} arising from (1.22) (1.26) does not stay embedded for all t\in\mathbb{R} (Figure 5, left), except for the exact quasiradial case c=d=0. A new ingredient – polygon splitting – is needed for the construction.
In reality, we first build \gamma_{t} from (1.22) (1.26) for all sufficiently negative t. Denote by Z_{t} the interior of \gamma_{t} (it is combinatorially a 6-gon). We find a suitable time T and use a suitable segment \sigma to cut Z_{T} into two 4-gons. Then, we evolve the two 4-gons individually by IMCF with cusps (Figure 5, middle). Finally, we construct the \infty-harmonic function u by asking that all \gamma_{t} are level sets of |\nabla u| as well as streamlines of u, and additionally -\log|\nabla u|\equiv T on \sigma.
Figure 5: Splitting of polygon and mixed IMCF cluster.
We call the segment \sigma a valley. The solution locally forms two IMCFs evolving away from \sigma (as opposed to moving toward a ridge). An IMCF cluster with both ridges and valleys is an instance of a mixed IMCF cluster (Definition 3.4). The mixed cluster associated to our construction is shown in Figure 5 (right); see also Figures 15, 16, 17 for other degree 3 solutions.
Remark 1.4.
In all IMCF cluster diagrams in this paper, orange curves are ridges and blue segments are valleys. Curves without colors are \gamma_{t}. All curves are streamlines of u, and all valleys and \gamma_{t} are level sets of |\nabla u| (ridges need not be level sets).
The role of valleys is perhaps more apparent from an analytic point of view. Recalling |\nabla u|=e^{-t} on \gamma_{t}, and comparing the direction of evolution, we find that |\nabla u| becomes smaller when moving away from a valley. Thus, a valley is the maximal gradient set in its neighborhood. Also, it is mentioned below (1.6) that the maximal gradient set of an \infty-harmonic function is a disjoint union of line segments. This explains that valleys must be line segments.
A mixed IMCF cluster also allows “poles” where the solution is quasiradial-like (e.g., there are two poles in Figure 5, right). The poles correspond to critical points in the resulting \infty-harmonic functions. See Sections 3 and 4 for details.
In Section 10, we prove a converse of the above construction. Suppose u is \infty-harmonic in a domain \Omega, and F is a connected component of \{|\nabla u|<e^{-T}\} with F\Subset\Omega. Then the curves \gamma_{t}=\{|\nabla u|=e^{-t}\}\cap F follow the IMCF-and-splitting pattern for t>T:
(1)
For all but finitely many t, the curves \gamma_{t} evolve smoothly by IMCF with cusps.
(2)
At each exceptional time, some connected components of \gamma_{t} are split into polygonal curves with fewer edges. The cutting segments form the maximal gradient set in the interior of \gamma_{t}.
Each isolated critical point is contained in a precompact connected component of \big\{|\nabla u|<e^{-T}\big\}, for some T\gg 1. Thus, the above pattern applies. By the finiteness of exceptional times, there is no splitting beyond time T^{\prime} for some T^{\prime}>T. Then, by investigating the t\to\infty limit of the support function (which solves \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h for t\in(T^{\prime},\infty)), we eventually prove the asymptotics in Theorem 1.2.
In Section 11, we prove the discreteness of critical set in Theorem 1.2. The \infty-Laplacian does not have the unique continuation property: there are distinct (nonconstant) \infty-harmonic functions that coincide in an open subset [55, p.21]. However, by Theorem 11.1, a nonconstant \infty-harmonic function cannot be constant in an open subset. This settles a question asked by Lindqvist [55, p.22].
In Section 12, we prove Theorem 1.3. The key is to show that for an entire solution u with polynomial growth, the curves \gamma_{t}=\{|\nabla u|=e^{-t}\} follow the IMCF-and-splitting pattern for all t\in\mathbb{R}, with finitely many splitting times in total. Thus, there is {\underline{T}}\in\mathbb{R} so that \gamma_{t} do not split, thus evolve as smooth IMCF with cusps, for t\in(-\infty,{\underline{T}}). Correspondingly, the support functions of \gamma_{t} solve \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h for t\in(-\infty,{\underline{T}}). An analytic lemma (Theorem 12.3) is invoked to show that h has finitely many Fourier frequencies. More specifically, it must take the form
h(t,\theta)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\exp\Big(t-\frac{j^{2}t}{(d-1)^{2}}\Big)\Big(a_{j}\cos\frac{j\theta}{d-1}+b_{j}\sin\frac{j\theta}{d-1}\Big),
(1.27)
for some d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 2}, where \theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\big(2(d-1)\pi\big). The constant term and terms with frequency above d must all vanish. The dominant term has j=d and again corresponds to degree d quasiradial solutions (see (1.18)). Then, Theorem 1.3 follows by taking the rescaled limit as t\to-\infty, with d being the degree of u.
By Jensen’s maximum principle, two \infty-harmonic functions in \mathbb{R}^{2} must coincide if they coincide in \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B(R) for some R. Hence, each choice of parameters \{a_{j},b_{j}\} in (1.27) gives rise to a unique solution (if it exists). The moduli space of degree d entire solutions is thus a subspace of \mathbb{R}^{2d-2} (where a_{j},b_{j} are the free parameters; by translation symmetry, the j=d-1 term can always be removed).
It is natural to ask whether any parameter yields a solution. The main difficulty is the determination of the splitting times and angles. The degree 3 case involves only one splitting with a unique combinatorial type (one 6-gon into two 4-gons), and existing necessary conditions already imply a unique choice (see Subsection 4.1). When d\geqslant 4, the existence of combinatorially different splitting types poses a complication. How to determine the canonical splitting among the many different ones, given that the resulting \infty-harmonic function is unique?
1.1 Structure of this paper; remarks and discussions
The main content of each section has been outlined above and is also suggested by its title. Each section is organized around a main theorem, with the relevant main ideas and discussions included at the front. The proofs in different sections are mostly independent. The dependencies among sections are summarized below. In the diagram, “[1.x]” means Theorem 1.x.
23A, 564798101112[1.1][1.2][1.3]
A quick route towards understanding the main theorems might be to first read the introductions (while temporarily skip the proofs) of Sections 3, 6, 8, then to have a glance of the examples in Section 4, then to proceed with Sections 10, 11, 12. Sections 5, 7, 9 may be treated as tools for the first read.
We end this introduction with a list of remarks of possible interest.
Remark 1.5.
(i)
It is natural to ask whether \infty-harmonic functions satisfy a C^{1,1/3} estimate
\|u\|_{C^{1,1/3}(B(1))}\leqslant C\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B(2))}.
(1.28)
This seems to require new ingredients, for instance, a quantitative version of Theorem 11.1 which relates the distribution of critical points and the growth of u.
(ii)
Several integral regularity conjectures were also posed for planar \infty-harmonic functions (for example, see [48]): one may ask if it always hold
u\in W^{2,3/2-\varepsilon}_{\loc},\qquad|\nabla u|\in W^{1,3-\varepsilon}_{\loc},\qquad\log|\nabla u|\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\loc}.
(1.29)
These are sharply modelled on u_{0}=|x|^{4/3}-|y|^{4/3} as well. We remark that the integral regularity for the vector part of simple IMCF clusters has not been established.
(iii)
According to Iwaniec-Manfredi [46], p-harmonic functions (1<p<\infty) in the plane are C^{k,\alpha}, where
k+\alpha=\frac{1}{6}\Big(7+\frac{1}{p-1}+\sqrt{1+\frac{14}{p-1}+\frac{1}{(p-1)^{2}}}\Big).
Observe that
\lim_{p\to\infty}(k+\alpha)=\frac{4}{3}.
Is it true that p-harmonic functions (2\leqslant p\leqslant\infty) are uniformly C^{1,1/3}? The proof in [46] combines the C^{k,\alpha} regularity at critical points and the smoothness away from critical points (due to strict ellipticity). The former may pass well to p=\infty, but the latter does not. The ridges in an \infty-harmonic function, where the optimality of C^{1,1/3} also appears, are smoothed out in the approximating p-harmonic functions.
(iv)
The ridges are in natural correspondence with the “attracting streamlines” in the setting of Lindgren-Lindqvist [54]. See precisely [54, p.4].
(v)
A very recent work of Brustad [24] showed the following: if u has no critical points and the map z\mapsto\nabla u(z) has a Lipschitz inverse, then u\in C^{1,1/3}(\Omega). We include a discussion in Remark 6.8.
(vi)
Quasiradial solutions are the only known examples in \mathbb{R}^{2} with only one critical point, to the author’s knowledge. Example 4.4 is the only known solution in \mathbb{R}^{2} without critical points. Do there exist other examples?
Tool and computational resource disclosure. The author thanks ChatGPT 5.5 Pro for suggesting improvements on the exposition and for finding mathematical and linguistic typos.
Acknowledgements. The author would like to thank Richard Bamler for many useful discussions, and Erik Lindgren for clarifications on the reference [54]. The author also thanks Peking University and Yuguang Shi for their hospitality, as this work was partially completed during his visit.
2 Preliminaries and notations
The following symbols usually have a fixed meaning:
•
z,x,y: a point in \mathbb{R}^{2} and its coordinates.
•
u: an \infty-harmonic function.
•
\Omega: a domain (i.e. connected open set) in \mathbb{R}^{2}.
•
w: an IMCF or IMCF cluster.
The following are our standard notations:
•
[z_{1},z_{2}]: the closed line segment connecting z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.
•
\mathbb{S}^{1}(r): the circle with total length r; and \mathbb{S}^{1}=\mathbb{S}^{1}(2\pi).
•
B(z,r), Q(z,r): the open disk and square with center z and radius r.
•
B(r)=B(0,r), and Q(r)=Q(0,r)=(-r,r)\times(-r,r).
•
Q_{\delta}(r)=(-r,r)\times(-\delta r,\delta r).
•
N(A,r): the r-neighborhood of A, namely, N(A,r)=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:d(z,A)<r\}.
•
\nu_{D}: the outer unit normal of a region D, whenever D has sufficient regularity.
•
Streamline: a streamline of a C^{1} function u is a gradient flow line: namely, it is a C^{1} curve \gamma satisfying \gamma^{\prime}(t)=\nabla u(\gamma(t)).
•
\nu^{\perp} means J\nu, namely, the counterclockwise 90^{\circ} rotation of \nu.
•
A degenerate root of a C^{1} function f(t,\theta) is a point where f(t,\theta)=\partial_{\theta}f(t,\theta)=0.
We assume the following conventions:
•
The curl of a vector field X=(X_{1},X_{2}) is defined as
\curl(X)=\partial_{x}X_{2}-\partial_{y}X_{1}.
•
A “line segment” means either a line segment or point or ray or line (this is particularly assumed in Section 7, 8). A “finite line segment” means a line segment with finite length. A “nontrivial line segment” means a line segment that is not a point.
•
A function f is convex (or concave) if f^{\prime\prime}\geqslant 0 (or f^{\prime\prime}\leqslant 0) in the barrier sense.
•
The closures and boundaries of all sets are relative to \mathbb{R}^{2}. The relative boundary of a set A in a domain \Omega is written as \partial A\cap\Omega.
•
We say that f_{i}\to f in C^{0}_{\loc}(\Omega), if for all K\Subset\Omega it holds
\dom(f_{i})\supset K\ \ \forall\,i\gg 1,\qquad f_{i}\to f\ \ \text{in}\ \ C^{0}(K).
2.1 \infty-Harmonic functions
We define \infty-harmonic functions as viscosity solutions of \nabla^{2}u(\nabla u,\nabla u)=0:
Definition 2.1.
Assume \Omega is a domain in \mathbb{R}^{n}, and u\in C^{0}(\Omega), and z\in\Omega. We say that:
•
\varphi\in C^{2} touches u from above (resp. from below) at z, if \varphi(z)=u(z) and \varphi\geqslant u (resp. \varphi\leqslant u) in a small neighborhood of z;
•
u is \infty-subharmonic at z, if for all \varphi\in C^{2} that touches u from above at z, we have \nabla^{2}\varphi(\nabla\varphi,\nabla\varphi)(z)\geqslant 0;
•
u is \infty-superharmonic at z, if for all \varphi\in C^{2} that touches u from below at z, we have \nabla^{2}\varphi(\nabla\varphi,\nabla\varphi)(z)\leqslant 0;
•
u is \infty-harmonic at z, if it is both \infty-subharmonic and \infty-superharmonic at z;
•
u is \infty-harmonic (\Delta_{\infty}u=0) in \Omega, if it is \infty-harmonic at every point in \Omega.
A main property is that it is the C^{0}_{\loc} limit of p-harmonic functions as p\to\infty:
Lemma 2.2 ([22, 47, 56]).
Suppose \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n} and u\in C^{0}(\Omega). Then u is \infty-harmonic in \Omega if and only if u is the C^{0}_{\loc}(\Omega) limit of a sequence of p_{i}-harmonic functions, with p_{i}\to\infty.
Infinity-harmonicity is also equivalent to the AMLE (absolutely minimizing Lipschitz extension) condition and the comparison with cones condition. To state the former, denote
\Lip(u,K)=\sup_{x,y\in K}\frac{|u(x)-u(y)|}{|x-y|},\qquad F_{\infty}(u,K)=\esssup_{K}|\nabla u|.
The AMLE condition states that for all V\Subset\Omega and all functions v\in C^{0}(\overline{V}) with u|_{\partial V}=v|_{\partial V}, it holds F_{\infty}(u,V)\leqslant F_{\infty}(v,V). This is (nontrivially) equivalent to \Lip(u,\overline{V})\leqslant\Lip(v,\overline{V}) for all V\Subset\Omega and all v\in C^{0} with u|_{\partial V}=v|_{\partial V}, [28, Theorem 6.1]. The AMLE property is not directly used in this paper. The comparison with cone property states that for all V\Subset\Omega and cone functions of the form c(x)=a+b|x-x_{0}|, we have
u\leqslant c\text{ (resp. $u\geqslant c$) on $\partial(V\setminus\{x_{0}\})$}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad u\leqslant c\text{ (resp. $u\geqslant c$) in $V$.}
The equivalence of these properties and \infty-harmonicity were shown in [22, 28, 29, 47]. For a detailed introduction of the fundamental theory, we refer to [8, 14, 28, 55, 73].
In addition to Lemma 2.2, we will also need the following facts (most of which follow from comparison with cones). The gradient estimate is stated as follows:
Lemma 2.3 ([29, Corollary 2.6]).
If \Delta_{\infty}u=0 in \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}, then u\in\Lip_{\loc}(\Omega), and
|\nabla u(x)|\leqslant\frac{2\sup_{\Omega}|u|}{d(x,\partial\Omega)},\qquad\forall\,x\in\Omega.
In fact, it was shown by Evans-Smart [38, 39] that u is everywhere differentiable (in all dimensions). The maximal gradient set of an \infty-harmonic function has a special structure: the following is a consequence of [70, Theorem 2.2] or [28, Proposition 6.2].
Lemma 2.4.
If \Delta_{\infty}u=0 and |\nabla u|\leqslant L in \Omega, then the set \{|\nabla u|=L\} is a disjoint union of nontrivial line segments which are streamlines of u and have no endpoints in \Omega.
This lemma plays a crucial role in Section 10: these are the line segments that cut the concave polygons. The lemma implies that \{x\in\Omega:|\nabla u(x)|=\sup_{\Omega}|\nabla u|\} cannot be precompact in \Omega if nonempty. Hence, the maximum principle holds for |\nabla u|:
Lemma 2.5.
If \Delta_{\infty}u=0 in \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}, and K\Subset\Omega, then \sup_{K}|\nabla u|=\sup_{\partial K}|\nabla u|.
We recall Jensen’s weak maximum principle:
Lemma 2.6 ([9, 47]).
If \Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n} and u,v\in C^{0}(\overline{\Omega}) are respectively \infty-subharmonic and \infty-superharmonic in \Omega, then
\max_{\overline{\Omega}}(u-v)=\max_{\partial\Omega}(u-v).
In Section 4, we need a removable singularity theorem in Savin-Wang-Yu [70]:
Lemma 2.7.
If u\in C^{1}(\Omega) is \infty-harmonic in \Omega\setminus\{z_{0}\}, then u is \infty-harmonic in \Omega.
In dimension 2, knowing the C^{1,\alpha} regularity [37, 69] (in fact, knowing the C^{1} equicontinuity is sufficient) is important to us throughout the paper:
Lemma 2.8 ([69, Theorem 3]).
The family of \infty-harmonic functions in B(2)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} that are bounded by 1 is C^{1}-equicontinuous in B(1).
2.2 Planar curves, concave polygons, support functions
For a parametrized C^{1} planar curve \gamma, we denote by \tau=\gamma^{\prime} its velocity vector. Whenever having fixed a unit speed parametrization of \gamma, it induces a choice of normal vector field \nu=-\tau^{\perp}. Conversely, whenever having chosen a normal vector field \nu, we arrange the length parametrization so that \tau=\nu^{\perp}.
Convex curves, angle parameter and support function.
Let \gamma be an embedded open C^{1} curve in \mathbb{R}^{2}, and \nu be a continuous unit normal vector on \gamma. We say that \gamma is convex (or strictly convex) with respect to \nu, if for each z\in\gamma there is r>0 and a rigid motion that sends \gamma\cap B(z,r) to a convex (or strictly convex) graph and sends \nu to -\partial_{y}.
For an angle \theta\in\mathbb{R} or \theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}(2k\pi), and k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}, we fix the notation
\nu_{\theta}=(\sin\theta,-\cos\theta),\qquad\tau_{\theta}=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta).
(2.1)
Thus \tau_{\theta}=\nu_{\theta}^{\perp}.
Let \gamma be an open convex C^{1} curve with respect to a choice of normal vector \nu. Let s be a compatible arclength parametrization (so that \gamma^{\prime}=\nu^{\perp}). An angle parameter of \gamma, usually denoted by \vartheta, is a continuous lift of \nu^{\perp} to \mathbb{R}. If \gamma is the graph of a convex function f, then
\vartheta(x,f(x))=\arctan f^{\prime}(x)\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
is a choice of angle parameter. If \gamma is the graph of a concave function f (it is convex with respect to the upward pointing normal and left-moving length parameter), then
\vartheta(x,f(x))=\pi+\arctan f^{\prime}(x)\in(\pi/2,3\pi/2)
is a choice of angle parameter.
For the induced length parameter s, the function s\mapsto\vartheta(\gamma(s)) is non-decreasing. If \vartheta(\gamma(s_{1}))=\vartheta(\gamma(s_{2})) for some s_{1}<s_{2}, then \gamma|_{[s_{1},s_{2}]} is a line segment.
Consider the following function on \gamma:
\overline{h}(\gamma(s))=\langle\gamma(s)\,,\,\nu(\gamma(s))\rangle.
As each preimage \vartheta^{-1}(\theta) is a line segment orthogonal to \nu, the function \overline{h} is constant on each \vartheta^{-1}(\theta) and hence descends to a function h on the image J=\vartheta(\gamma). We call h the support function of \gamma. Clearly,
h(\theta)=\langle z\,,\,\nu(z)\rangle=\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta}\rangle,\qquad\forall\,\theta\in J,\ z\in\vartheta^{-1}(\theta).
(2.2)
When we translate \gamma by a vector a\partial_{x}+b\partial_{y}, the angle parameter stays unchanged, while the support function becomes h(\theta)+a\sin\theta-b\cos\theta. If we rotate \gamma counterclockwise by an angle \omega and denote the resulting curve by \gamma_{\omega}, then the angle parameter translates by \omega, and the support functions are related by
h_{\gamma_{\omega}}(\theta)=h_{\gamma}(\theta-\omega).
If \gamma is smooth and strictly convex, then \vartheta is a diffeomorphism onto its image, with \frac{d\vartheta}{ds}=\kappa, where \kappa is the curvature of \gamma. Then we may also parametrize \gamma by the angle. Differentiating (2.2) with respect to \theta, we have
\frac{dh}{d\theta}={\Big\langle{\frac{d\gamma}{d\theta}}\,,\,{\nu_{\theta}}\Big\rangle}+\langle\gamma(\theta)\,,\,\tau_{\theta}\rangle=\langle\gamma(\theta)\,,\,\tau_{\theta}\rangle.
Combining h(\theta)=\langle\gamma(\theta)\,,\,\nu_{\theta}\rangle, we have the recovery formula
\gamma(\theta)=h\nu_{\theta}+\frac{dh}{d\theta}\tau_{\theta}.
(2.3)
We may further differentiate
\frac{d^{2}h}{d\theta^{2}}={\Big\langle{\frac{d\gamma}{d\theta}}\,,\,{\tau_{\theta}}\Big\rangle}-\langle\gamma\,,\,\nu_{\theta}\rangle=\kappa^{-1}-h(\theta).
This gives the curvature formula
\frac{1}{\kappa}=\frac{d^{2}h}{d\theta^{2}}+h.
(2.4)
Geometric properties of support function.
Let \gamma be a convex C^{1,1} curve with curvature bounded by K. Denote J=\vartheta(\gamma). Then
\frac{d^{2}h}{d\theta^{2}}+h\geqslant\frac{1}{K}\qquad\text{in the barrier sense in $\Int(\vartheta(\gamma))$.}
(2.5)
To verify (2.5), we may perform a rotation and assume that the reference point is \theta=0\in\Int(\vartheta(\gamma)). Set \sigma=\vartheta^{-1}(0). As 0\in\Int(J), notice that \sigma has finite length and lies in the interior of \gamma. Since (2.5) is invariant under h\mapsto h+a\sin\theta-b\cos\theta, we may translate in \mathbb{R}^{2} and assume \sigma=[-l,l]\times\{0\}. Thus \gamma is a convex graph in a neighborhood of \sigma. Denote P=[-l,l]\times[1/K,\infty). By our convexity and curvature assumption, there is \overline{\theta}\ll 1 so that
\vartheta^{-1}\big((-\overline{\theta},\overline{\theta})\big)\subset\{y\geqslant 0\}\setminus N(P,1/K).
Hence, the support function of \gamma is no smaller than the support function of \partial N(P,1/K) in (-\overline{\theta},\overline{\theta}). But the latter function is calculated to be
\widetilde{h}(\theta)=K^{-1}+\sqrt{K^{-2}+l^{2}}\,\sin\Big(|\theta|-\arctan(1/Kl)\Big),
hence
\widetilde{h}+\frac{d^{2}\widetilde{h}}{d\theta^{2}}=\frac{1}{K}+2l\delta_{0},
where \delta_{0} is the Dirac delta centered at \theta=0. This implies (2.5) as desired.
Suppose \gamma is a C^{1} convex curve, and 0\in\gamma, \vartheta(0)=0, 0\in\Int(\vartheta(\gamma)). Denote \vartheta^{-1}(0)=[a,b]\times\{0\}. Then in a small neighborhood of \vartheta^{-1}(0), we may write \gamma as the graph of a convex function f:(a-r,b+r)\to\mathbb{R} with f|_{[a,b]}=0 and f|_{(a-r,a)\cup(b,b+r)}>0. For each x\in(b,b+r), the corresponding point z=(x,f(x)) satisfies
\vartheta(z)=\arctan f^{\prime}(x),\qquad\nu(z)=\frac{(f^{\prime}(x),-1)}{\sqrt{1+f^{\prime}(x)^{2}}},\qquad h(z)=\frac{xf^{\prime}(x)-f(x)}{\sqrt{1+f^{\prime}(x)^{2}}}.
(2.6)
Thus
h^{\prime}_{+}(0)=\lim_{\theta\searrow 0}\frac{h(\theta)}{\theta}=\lim_{x\searrow b}\frac{h(z)}{\vartheta(z)}=b.
(2.7)
Likewise, it can be shown that h^{\prime}_{-}(0)=a. It follows that
\vartheta^{-1}(0)=[a,b]\times\{0\}=\big[h^{\prime}_{-}(0),h^{\prime}_{+}(0)\big]\times\{0\}.
Taking symmetry into account, we have in general
\vartheta^{-1}(\theta)=\Big\{h(\theta)\nu_{\theta}+c\tau_{\theta}:c\in\big[h^{\prime}_{-}(\theta),h^{\prime}_{+}(\theta)\big]\Big\},\qquad\forall\,\theta\in\Int(J).
(2.8)
This is understood as the weak version of (2.3). As a consequence, h is differentiable at a point \theta\in\Int(J) if and only if \vartheta^{-1}(\theta) is a point.
Suppose \gamma is a complete C^{1} convex curve, and \gamma is not a line, and \gamma is a ray at both ends. Then J=\vartheta(\gamma)=[\theta_{1},\theta_{2}] for some \theta_{1}\leqslant\theta_{2}. If \theta_{1}=0 and \vartheta^{-1}(0) is contained in the x axis, then arguing similarly as in (2.6) (2.7), we have
\vartheta^{-1}(0)=\big(\!-\infty,h^{\prime}_{+}(0)\big]\times\{0\}.
By symmetry, this implies the general recovery formula for the ray ends
\displaystyle\vartheta^{-1}(\theta_{1})
\displaystyle=\Big\{h(\theta_{1})\nu_{\theta_{1}}+c\tau_{\theta_{1}}:c\leqslant h^{\prime}_{+}(\theta_{1})\Big\},
(2.9)
\displaystyle\vartheta^{-1}(\theta_{2})
\displaystyle=\Big\{h(\theta_{2})\nu_{\theta_{2}}+c\tau_{\theta_{2}}:c\geqslant h^{\prime}_{-}(\theta_{2})\Big\}.
Conversely, given a smooth function h, we may construct its profile curve
\gamma(\theta)=h(\theta)\nu_{\theta}+h^{\prime}(\theta)\tau_{\theta}.
(2.10)
The resulting curve \gamma is parametrized by angle \theta and has support function h. We have
\displaystyle\frac{d\gamma}{d\theta}
\displaystyle=\big(h^{\prime}\nu_{\theta}+h\tau_{\theta}\big)+\big(h^{\prime\prime}\tau_{\theta}-h^{\prime}\nu_{\theta}\big)=(h^{\prime\prime}+h)\tau_{\theta}.
Thus \gamma(\theta) is a smoothly immersed curve as long as h+h^{\prime\prime}\neq 0. It is convex with respect to \nu_{\theta} if h+h^{\prime\prime}>0, and is concave with respect to \nu_{\theta} if h+h^{\prime\prime}<0.
Concave polygons
Let E be a domain in an ambient domain \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}. We say that E is a concave polygon in \Omega, if for each z\in\partial E\cap\Omega, there is a rigid motion sending z to 0, after which we have:
•
either
E\cap Q(r)=\Big\{x\in(-r,r),\ y<g(x)\Big\}
for a C^{1} convex function g:(-r,r)\to(-r/2,r/2) with g(0)=g^{\prime}(0)=0;
•
or
E\cap Q(r)=\Big\{x\in(0,r),\ g_{1}(x)<y<g_{2}(x)\Big\}
for a C^{1} concave function g_{1}:[0,r)\to(-r/2,0] and C^{1} convex function g_{2}:[0,r)\to[0,r/2) with g_{1}(0)=g_{2}(0)=g^{\prime}_{1}(0)=g^{\prime}_{2}(0)=0 and g_{1}<g_{2} on (0,r),
for some small r>0. Hence, \partial E is a union of concave curves (with respect to the outer unit normal), and E is cusplike at the common endpoints of edges.
Let \gamma be a connected component of \partial E, with the counterclockwise orientation. Define the normal line map \hat{\nu}:\gamma\to\mathbb{S}^{1}(\pi) by \hat{\nu}(z)=\text{(the unit normal vector of $E$ at $z$, mod $\pi$)}. Looking at the local models, we find that this map is well-defined, continuous, and locally non-increasing on \gamma (due to the concavity). We are primarily concerned with two cases:
(i)
E is simply-connected, E\Subset\Omega, and \gamma=\partial E contains N edges where N is even;
(ii)
E is simply-connected, E\not\Subset\Omega, and \gamma is a connected component of \partial E.
For case (i), \gamma is a simple loop and E lies inside \gamma. By Gauss-Bonnet, the total curvature of \gamma is -(N-2)\pi, thus the map \hat{\nu} has degree -(N-2). We may lift \hat{\nu} to a map
\nu:\gamma\to\mathbb{S}^{1}.
Then \nu is a continuous normal vector field on \gamma, and \deg(\nu)=-\frac{N-2}{2}. Denote \tau=\nu^{\perp}, which is a continuous tangential vector field on \gamma. In many cases, the edges of \partial E will be streamlines of a function u. In this case, we usually choose our lift so that \tau=\nabla u/|\nabla u|. The edges of \gamma alternate between being concave and convex with respect to \nu, and \nu=\pm\nu_{E} alternatively (\nu_{E} always denotes the outer unit normal of E). Next, we may lift \nu^{\perp} to a map
\vartheta:\gamma\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N-2)\pi\big).
Thus \vartheta is a degree -1 non-increasing map. We call \vartheta an angle parameter of \gamma. For case (ii), we likewise define the lifted maps
\nu:\gamma\to\mathbb{S}^{1},\qquad\vartheta:\gamma\to\mathbb{R},
with \vartheta being non-increasing as well. In either case, we denote J=\vartheta(\gamma).
In both cases, each preimage \vartheta^{-1}(\theta) is a line segment orthogonal to \nu_{\theta}. Hence, the map
\overline{h}(z)=\langle z\,,\,\nu(z)\rangle,\qquad z\in\gamma,
descends to a map
h(\theta)=\langle z\,,\,\nu(z)\rangle\big|_{z\in\vartheta^{-1}(\theta)},\qquad\forall\,\theta\in J,\qquad\text{where}\ \ J=\vartheta(\gamma),
which we call the support function of \gamma. As \nu(z)=\nu_{\theta} for any z\in\vartheta^{-1}(\theta), we may also write
h(\theta)=\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta}\rangle\big|_{z\in\vartheta^{-1}(\theta)},\qquad\forall\,\theta\in J.
It is easy to see that h does not depend on the choice of \vartheta once \nu is fixed, and switching between the two choices of \nu changes the sign of h. (Reminder: if \partial E has an odd number of edges, then the lift \nu does not exist, hence h is not defined.)
Let \{\gamma_{i}\} be the edges of \gamma, and suppose that the interior of all \gamma_{i} are C^{1,1}. We may arrange the indices so that \gamma_{i} contacts \gamma_{i-1} and \gamma_{i+1}. Then \vartheta(\gamma_{i}) is an interval for each i. Denote it by J_{i}; then \overline{J}=\bigcup\overline{J_{i}}. Then note that
\pm\Big(\frac{d^{2}h}{d\theta^{2}}+h\Big)>0\qquad\text{in}\ \ \Int(J_{i}),
due to (2.5). Here, the sign changes alternatively in i. (Note: some J_{i} may be a point.)
Suppose \theta\in\Int(J), and denote \sigma=\vartheta^{-1}(\theta). Then we have \sigma\Subset\Omega, by the monotonicity of \vartheta and the connectedness of \gamma. With a rigid motion and a replacement \vartheta\to\vartheta+2k\pi, we may assume
\sigma=[x_{1},x_{2}]\times\{0\}\quad(x_{1}\leqslant 0\leqslant x_{2}),\qquad\nu(0)=-\partial_{y},\qquad\vartheta(0)=0.
Then, the local shape of E near \sigma falls into one of the cases in Figure 6 below: shaded region represents E, and horizontal segment represents \vartheta^{-1}(0). The second row is the “orientation reversal” of the first row. If \sigma is a point, then cases 2,6 and cases 3,7 are of the same type, and cases 4,8 do not exist. In cases 1,5 we have 0\in\Int(J_{i}) for some i, and in cases 2,3,6,7 we have 0\in\partial J_{i}\cap\partial J_{i+1}, and in cases 4,8 we have J_{i}=\{0\}.

In particular, note that h is left and right differentiable at \theta=0, and
\sigma=\big[h^{\prime}_{-}(0),h^{\prime}_{+}(0)\big]\times\{0\},
where we denote [a,b]=[b,a] if a>b. In view of symmetry, this shows that h is everywhere left and right differentiable in \Int(J), with
\vartheta^{-1}(\theta)=\Big\{h(\theta)\nu_{\theta}+c\tau_{\theta}:c\in\big[h^{\prime}_{-}(\theta),h^{\prime}_{+}(\theta)\big]\Big\},\qquad\theta\in\Int(J).
(2.11)
As a consequence, h is differentiable at \theta\in\Int(J) if and only if \vartheta^{-1}(\theta) is a point, and in this case we have
\vartheta^{-1}(\theta)=h(\theta)\nu_{\theta}+h^{\prime}(\theta)\tau_{\theta}.
(2.12)
Again, notice that (2.11) (2.12) are the polygonal versions of (2.3).
Profile polygons.
Suppose h\in C^{\infty}(J) for J=\mathbb{S}^{1}(2k\pi) or J\subset\mathbb{R}, so that h^{\prime\prime}+h only has nondegenerate roots. Then
\gamma(\theta)=h(\theta)\nu_{\theta}+h^{\prime}(\theta)\tau_{\theta}
(2.13)
is an immersed concave polygonal curve (namely, it is a union of concave / convex curves with cusps at common endpoints), with angle parameter \theta and support function h. Each cusp corresponds to a root of h^{\prime\prime}+h. Indeed, suppose \theta=0 is a nondegenerate root of h^{\prime\prime}+h. We may add a suitable multiple of \sin\theta and \cos\theta to h (which is equivalent to a translation), and assume that h(0)=h^{\prime}(0)=0. Hence, we obtain h^{\prime\prime}(\theta)=a\theta+O(\theta^{2}) near 0. By a scaling, we may assume a=6. Under these reductions, the Taylor expansion of h becomes
h(\theta)=\theta^{3}+O(\theta^{4}),\qquad h^{\prime}(\theta)=3\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3}).
Then (2.13) gives
\displaystyle\gamma(\theta)
\displaystyle=\big(\theta^{3}+O(\theta^{4})\big)(\sin\theta,-\cos\theta)+\big(3\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3})\big)(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)
\displaystyle=\big(3\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3}),2\theta^{3}+O(\theta^{4})\big).
Hence, the curve \gamma is asymptotic to a canonical cubic cusp y^{2}=4x^{3}/27 near the origin.
Suppose \gamma is a loop, namely, J=\mathbb{S}^{1}(2k\pi) for some k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}. Also, suppose that \gamma has an even number of cusps. Let \{\theta_{i}\} be the roots of h^{\prime\prime}+h, and J_{i}=[\theta_{i},\theta_{i+1}], and \gamma_{i}=\gamma(J_{i}). Let us assume that h^{\prime\prime}+h>0 in J_{0}, thus (-1)^{i}(h^{\prime\prime}+h)>0 in each J_{i}. Then we have the alternating length formula
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}(2k\pi)}h(\theta)\,d\theta=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}|\gamma_{i}|.
(2.14)
Indeed, if s is a length parametrization, then \big|\frac{d\theta}{ds}\big|=|\kappa|=\frac{1}{|h^{\prime\prime}+h|} in each \Int(\gamma_{i}), then
\displaystyle\sum(-1)^{i}|\gamma_{i}|
\displaystyle=\sum\int_{\gamma_{i}}(-1)^{i}\,ds=\sum\int_{\theta_{i}}^{\theta_{i+1}}(-1)^{i}|h^{\prime\prime}+h|\,d\theta=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}(2k\pi)}(h^{\prime\prime}+h)\,d\theta
\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}(2k\pi)}h\,d\theta.
2.3 Inverse mean curvature flow in \mathbb{R}^{2}
Smooth IMCF in \mathbb{R}^{2} (or inverse curve shortening flow).
A smooth family of strictly convex curves \{\gamma_{t}\subset\Omega\} is a solution of the inverse mean curvature flow (IMCF) if the normal speed is everywhere equal to 1/\kappa. A function w\in C^{\infty}(\Omega) with nonvanishing gradient is called a smooth IMCF if it satisfies \div\big(\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}\big)=|\nabla w|. The latter is known as the level set form of IMCF. The transformation between these two formulations is given by \gamma_{t}=\{w=t\}: indeed, for a smooth function w with nonvanishing gradient, the quantity \div\big(\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}\big) is equal to the mean curvature of level sets, while |\nabla w| is equal to 1/\text{(normal speed of movement)}.
Suppose that \{\gamma_{t}\} is smoothly parametrized by angles. Let h(t,\theta) be the (collection of) support functions of \gamma_{t}. Then recall from (2.3) that
\gamma_{t}(\theta)=h(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}h(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}.
Calculating its speed:
\frac{\partial\gamma_{t}}{\partial t}=\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial t\,\partial\theta}(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}.
The normal velocity is thus \partial_{t}h. As the curvature of \gamma_{t} is 1/(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h), the IMCF equation is therefore
\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}=\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial\theta^{2}}+h.
(2.15)
This calculation can also be found in [27, 72].
Conversely, suppose h(t,\theta) is a smooth function in a domain R\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}, with \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h>0. Consider the map
F(t,\theta)=h(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}h(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}.
(2.16)
We may compute
\partial_{t}F=(\partial_{t}h)\nu_{\theta}+(\partial_{t\theta}h)\tau_{\theta},\qquad\partial_{\theta}F=(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)\tau_{\theta},
(2.17)
thus \det(DF)=(\partial_{t}h)(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)>0. Therefore, F is a local diffeomorphism, and the family of (possibly non-embedded) curves \gamma_{t}=F\big(\{t\}\times(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\big) form a smooth solution of IMCF. Whenever F is known to be injective, it is then a diffeomorphism onto its image, and thus \{\gamma_{t}\} form an embedded smooth IMCF in F(R).
A particular case is R=(t_{1},t_{2})\times(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}) with \theta_{2}-\theta_{1}<\pi. Indeed, after a rotation, we may assume 0<\theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<\pi, then DF has (2,2)-entry (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)\sin\theta>0 and determinant (\partial_{t}h)(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)>0. Then the Gale-Nikaidô theorem [40, Theorem 4] implies that F is injective, hence a diffeomorphism from R to F(R).
The weak IMCF.
We call w\in\Lip_{\loc}(\Omega) a weak IMCF in \Omega, if every sub-level set \{w<t\} is a local minimizer of the energy
J_{w}^{K}(E):={P(E;K)}-\int_{E\cap K}|\nabla w|,
(2.18)
meaning that J_{w}^{K}(\{w<t\})\leqslant J_{w}^{K}(E) for all t\in\mathbb{R}, all sets E with locally finite perimeter, and all K\Subset\Omega, so that E\Delta\{w<t\}\Subset K. Here, {P(E;K)} denotes the perimeter of a set E in K, see [57]. In most places, the objects have sufficient regularity, thus {P(E;K)} is the area of \partial E\cap K.
Given a weak IMCF w, the sets \partial\{w<t\} are viewed as the evolving hypersurfaces. A hypersurface could instantly move forward at a time t if \{w=t\} has nonempty interior. A connected component of \Int\big(\{w=t\}\big) is often called a jump. The weak IMCF can be (heuristically) understood as a combination of the 1/H-evolution and jumps.
We refer the reader to [44, 50, 74] for general introductions of the weak IMCF and how the energy (2.18) was motivated. The introduction in this subsection is specialized to providing preliminary materials for this paper. For the same reason, we specialize the ambient space to domains in \mathbb{R}^{2}. As a note to experts, the perspective of strictly outward minimizing hulls (see [44, Property 1.4] or [74, Subsection 1.2]) is less important for us, since the weak IMCF encountered in this paper are mostly non-proper. Nevertheless, the following geometric observation is important: the sets \partial\{w\leqslant t\}\setminus\partial\{w<t\}, namely the hypersurface reached after each jump, are minimal surfaces (i.e. line segments).
The perspective of calibration is more central. We call w\in\Lip_{\loc}(\Omega) a calibrated weak IMCF, if there is a measurable vector field \nu (called a calibration of w) that satisfies
|\nu|\leqslant 1\quad\text{and}\quad\nu\cdot\nabla w=|\nabla w|\ \ \text{a.e.},\qquad\div(\nu)=|\nabla w|\ \ \text{distributionally.}
(2.19)
Note that this implies
\div(e^{-w}\nu)=0\qquad\text{distributionally.}
(2.20)
A smooth IMCF is always a calibrated weak IMCF: indeed, if w\in C^{\infty} satisfies \div\big(\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}\big)=|\nabla w|, then \nu=\nabla w/|\nabla w| serves as a calibration. A calibrated weak IMCF is always a weak IMCF; see the end of this subsection for the proof. Modulo analytic subtlety, this can be understood as follows: denoting E_{t}=\{w<t\}, we have \nu=\nu_{E_{t}} on \partial E_{t}, so for any competitor set E the divergence theorem gives
\displaystyle J_{w}^{K}(E)-J_{w}^{K}(E_{t})
\displaystyle={P(E;K)}-{P(E_{t};K)}-\int(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E_{t}})|\nabla w|
\displaystyle\geqslant\int_{\partial E\cap K}\langle\nu_{E}\,,\,\nu\rangle-\int_{\partial E_{t}\cap K}\langle\nu_{E_{t}}\,,\,\nu\rangle-\int(\chi_{E}-\chi_{E_{t}})|\nabla w|
\displaystyle=0.
The notion of calibrated weak IMCF first appeared in [44, p. 391], and also played a central role in various L^{1} variational problems, see for instance [4, 42, 60] and the references therein. We further say that w is
•
continuously calibrated, if there is a continuous vector field \nu satisfying (2.19);
•
unit-calibrated, if there is a continuous \nu satisfying (2.19) with |\nu|=1 everywhere.
We include a list of useful properties of the weak IMCF. For the reader’s convenience, we include the nontrivial proofs in the end of this subsection.
Remark 2.9.
Suppose w is a weak IMCF in a domain \Omega.
(i)
The regions \{w<t\}, \{w\leqslant t\} are locally outward perimeter-minimizing [74, Fact 1.2.9, 1.2.11], hence have mean convex boundaries.
(ii)
The boundaries \gamma_{t}=\partial\{w<t\}\cap\Omega, \gamma_{t}^{+}=\partial\{w\leqslant t\}\cap\Omega are C^{1,1} with curvature almost everywhere |\nabla w| for almost every t. See [44, (1.12)]. As w\in\Lip_{\loc}(\Omega), the curves \gamma_{t},\gamma_{t}^{+} are locally uniformly C^{1,1}. Observe that
\bigcup_{s<t}\{w<s\}=\bigcup_{s<t}\{w\leqslant s\}=\{w<t\},\qquad\bigcap_{s>t}\{w<s\}=\bigcap_{s>t}\{w\leqslant s\}=\{w\leqslant t\}.
Hence
\lim_{s\nearrow t}\gamma_{s}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}\gamma_{s}^{+}=\gamma_{t},\qquad\lim_{s\searrow t}\gamma_{s}=\lim_{s\searrow t}\gamma_{s}^{+}=\gamma_{t}^{+},
(2.21)
all in C^{1}_{\loc} graphical sense.
(iii)
If w is continuously calibrated, then \nu is the outer unit normal of \gamma_{t},\gamma_{t}^{+}, for all t.
(iv)
\gamma_{t}^{+}\setminus\gamma_{t} is a union of line segments, for all t.
(v)
The regions \Int\big(\{w=t\}\big) are called jumps. If w is unit-calibrated, then each jump is foliated by line segments which are orthogonal to \nu.
(vi)
The following fact is useful in Section 7, 8: suppose f:[-r,r]\to(-l,l) is a C^{1} convex function, and w is a weak IMCF in the region
D=\Big\{|x|<r,\ f(x)<y<l\Big\},
so that w\in\Lip(\overline{D}) and w\leqslant T and w|_{\graphh(f)}\equiv T. Then the new function
w^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &w\qquad\text{in}\ \ D\\
&T\qquad\text{in}\ \ \big((-r,r)\times(-l,l)\big)\setminus D\end{aligned}\right.
is a weak IMCF in (-r,r)\times(-l,l).
The following lemma is useful. Its proof is postponed to the end of this subsection.
Lemma 2.10.
Suppose \alpha\in(0,1], and \{E_{i}\}_{i\in I} is a family of open subsets of \Omega, so that:
(i)
the C^{1,\alpha} norms of \partial E_{i} are locally uniformly bounded in \Omega;
(ii)
for any i,j\in I we have either E_{i}\subset E_{j} or E_{j}\subset E_{i}.
Then the outer unit normals of E_{i} glue together to give a well-defined C^{0,\alpha/(1+\alpha)}_{\loc} map
\nu:\bigcup_{i}\partial E_{i}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}.
(2.22)
The Hölder exponent \alpha/(1+\alpha) is sharp: for example, taking \alpha=1 and
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2},\qquad E_{1}=\{y<0\},\qquad E_{2}=\{y<x^{2}\},
the upward pointing unit normal of \partial E_{1}\cup\partial E_{2} is exactly C^{0,1/2} in \mathbb{R}^{2}.
For a weak IMCF w, we consider
\Gamma_{0}=\Big(\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\gamma_{t}\Big)\cup\Big(\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\gamma_{t}^{+}\Big).
(2.23)
Then Lemma 2.10 yields a well-defined C^{0,1/2}_{\loc} normal vector field
\nu_{0}:\Gamma_{0}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}.
(2.24)
When w is continuously calibrated, by Remark 2.9(iii) we have \nu_{0}=\nu|_{\Gamma_{0}}, and hence, the calibration \nu is always C^{0,1/2} on \Gamma_{0}. It is generally impossible to obtain regularity of \nu outside \Gamma_{0}. If w is known to be unit-calibrated, then Remark 2.9(v) and a finer use of Lemma 2.10 is possible to yield \nu\in C^{0,1/2}_{\loc}(\Omega). This observation is not used anywhere in this paper, but it implies the following: if u is an oriented \infty-harmonic function in \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} (in the sense of Moser [63]), then u\in C^{1,1/2}_{\loc}(\Omega).
IMCF with outer obstacle.
Let D be a C^{1} open set in an ambient domain \Omega. We call w\in C^{0}(\overline{D}\cap\Omega)\cap\Lip_{\loc}(D) a continuously calibrated weak IMCF in D with outer obstacle \partial D\cap\Omega, if there is a continuous vector field \nu on \overline{D}\cap\Omega, such that
|\nu|\leqslant 1,\qquad\langle\nu\,,\,\nabla w\rangle=|\nabla w|,\qquad\div(\nu)=|\nabla w|\qquad\text{in}\ \ D,
(2.25)
and
\nu\cdot\nu_{D}\equiv 1\qquad\text{on}\ \ \partial D\cap\Omega.
(2.26)
We often call (2.26) the boundary tangency or outer obstacle condition. In the smooth case, this asks each hypersurface to stay tangent to \partial D.
A solution w of (2.25) (2.26) is a weak IMCF with outer obstacle \partial D\cap\Omega in the sense of [75, Definition 3.1]. This is due to [75, Lemma 3.19]. However, it is not known whether all solutions in [75, Definition 3.1] can be calibrated. As another difference, we assume w here to be continuous up to \partial D, while this is not assumed in [75]. We choose to narrow the definition since all solutions in this paper are continuously calibrated and continuous up to \partial D. We refer interested readers to [74, 75] for the general theory.
The solitons of IMCF in \mathbb{R}^{2} turn out to satisfy the boundary tangency condition in suitable domains. This is in particular the case for the shrinking soliton in (i) below which models quasiradial \infty-harmonic functions.
Example 2.11 (Solitons).
The translating, shrinking and expanding solitons of IMCF include cycloids, hypocycloids and epicycloids respectively, and other unbounded spiraling curves, see [72] and [26, 36]. We are primarily interested in the classical curves.
Figure 7: Solitons of IMCF and their angle parameters.
(i)
For each \alpha\in(0,\pi), consider the sector D=\{\pi-\alpha<\Arg(z)<\pi\}. There is a hypocycloid \gamma_{0} contained in this sector and contacts the two boundary rays tangentially: \gamma_{0} is the trajectory of the point (-1,0) as we roll the disk B(-1+\alpha/2\pi,\alpha/2\pi) clockwise against the unit circle. With an explicit calculation (see the end of this subsection), \gamma_{0} has angle parameter ranging in (0,\pi-\alpha) and has support function
h(\theta)=(1-\alpha/\pi)\sin\Big(\frac{-\theta}{1-\alpha/\pi}\Big),\qquad\theta\in(0,\pi-\alpha).
(2.27)
Then, (after a scaling that removes the (1-\alpha/\pi) factor,) consider
h(t,\theta)=\exp\Big(t-\frac{t}{(1-\alpha/\pi)^{2}}\Big)\sin\Big(\frac{-\theta}{1-\alpha/\pi}\Big),\qquad t\in\mathbb{R},\ \theta\in(0,\pi-\alpha).
(2.28)
It is easy to see that \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h>0 in \mathbb{R}\times(0,\pi-\alpha). Denoting
c=\frac{1}{(1-\alpha/\pi)^{2}}-1>0,
the curves \gamma_{t}=e^{-ct}\gamma_{0} solve the IMCF and have support functions h(t,\cdot), up to a scaling. Another calculation (which leads to the same result) can be found in [74, Section 3.1]. This soliton (more precisely, the function w so that \{w=t\}=\gamma_{t}) is then a smooth IMCF in D that satisfies the boundary tangency condition on \partial D\setminus\{0\}. In our language introduced above, setting the ambient domain as \Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}, then w is a continuously calibrated IMCF in D with outer obstacle \partial D\cap\Omega. Note: the origin needs to be removed since w\to+\infty as z\to 0.
(ii)
For each \alpha\in(0,2\pi), consider the sector \{0<\Arg(z)<\alpha\} and the epicycloid \gamma_{0} supported in this sector. Now \gamma_{0} is obtained as the trajectory of (1,0) as we roll the exterior disk B(1+\alpha/2\pi,\alpha/2\pi) counterclockwise against the unit disk. Through a similar calculation, the family of support functions
h(t,\theta)=\exp\Big(t-\frac{t}{(1+\alpha/\pi)^{2}}\Big)\sin\Big(\frac{\theta}{1+\alpha/\pi}\Big),\qquad\,t\in\mathbb{R},\ \theta\in(0,\pi+\alpha)
(2.29)
generate a self-expanding solution \gamma_{t}=e^{ct}\gamma_{0}, where c=1-\frac{1}{(\alpha/\pi+1)^{2}}.
(iii)
For each \lambda>0, consider the cycloid \gamma_{0} obtained as the trajectory of 0 by rolling B(\lambda/2\pi,\lambda/2\pi) upward against the y-axis. Then \gamma_{0} is calculated to have support function
h(\theta)=\frac{\lambda}{\pi}(\sin\theta-\theta\cos\theta),\qquad\theta\in(0,\pi).
(2.30)
This induces a family of support functions
h(t,\theta)=\frac{\lambda}{\pi}(1+2t)\sin\theta-\frac{\lambda}{\pi}\theta\cos\theta,\qquad t\in\mathbb{R},\ \theta\in(0,\pi).
(2.31)
One may verify that \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h>0. Then the self-translating solution
\gamma_{t}=\gamma_{0}+\Big(\frac{2\lambda}{\pi}t,0\Big)
has support function given by h(t,\cdot).
We will need the following properties of solutions of (2.25) (2.26):
Remark 2.12.
Let w,\nu be as in (2.25) (2.26), with the notations introduced there.
(i)
Each sub-level set \{w<t\}, \{w\leqslant t\} has C^{1} boundary in \Omega. Their relative boundaries in \Omega are orthogonal to \nu everywhere, and contact \partial D\cap\Omega tangentially. Note: these sub-level sets are only mean convex in the interior D.
(ii)
The following is useful in Section 11. Denote E_{t}=\{w<t\}, then for any C^{1} domain F\Subset\Omega with \mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\partial E_{t}\cap\partial F)=0, we have
|\partial E_{t}\cap F|\leqslant e^{t-\inf_{D}(w)}|\partial F\cap E_{t}|.
(2.32)
Here |\cdot| denotes the area. Indeed, using \div(e^{-w}\nu)=0 in E_{t}\cap F we have
\displaystyle 0
\displaystyle=\int_{\partial E_{t}\cap F}\langle e^{-w}\nu\,,\,\nu_{E_{t}}\rangle+\int_{\partial F\cap E_{t}}\langle e^{-w}\nu\,,\,\nu_{F}\rangle,
Using \nu=\nu_{E_{t}}, w\leqslant t on \partial E_{t}\cap F, and |\langle\nu\,,\,\nu_{F}\rangle|\leqslant 1, w\geqslant\inf_{D}(w) on \partial F\cap E_{t}:
e^{-t}|\partial E_{t}\cap F|\leqslant\int_{\partial E_{t}\cap F}\langle e^{-w}\nu\,,\,\nu_{E_{t}}\rangle=-\int_{\partial F\cap E_{t}}\langle e^{-w}\nu\,,\,\nu_{F}\rangle\leqslant e^{-\inf_{D}(w)}|\partial F\cap E_{t}|,
as desired. In fact, (2.32) holds without calibration, see [75, Lemma 4.2].
Maximum principles.
The following results are useful in Section 7. A function w\in C^{\infty}(\Omega) with nonvanishing gradient is called a smooth subsolution (resp. supersolution) of IMCF if
\div\Big(\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}\Big)\geqslant|\nabla w|,\qquad\text{resp.}\qquad\div\Big(\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}\Big)\leqslant|\nabla w|.
This is equivalent to its level sets \gamma_{t}=\{w=t\} evolving with normal speed at least 1/\kappa (resp. at most 1/\kappa) everywhere.
A function w\in\Lip_{\loc}(\Omega) is called a weak subsolution (resp. supersolution) of IMCF in a domain \Omega, if the energy (2.18) is locally outward minimized (resp. inward minimized), namely, if J_{w}^{K}(\{w<t\})\leqslant J_{w}^{K}(F) for all competitors F\supset\{w<t\} (resp. F\subset\{w<t\}) with \{w<t\}\Delta F\Subset K\Subset\Omega.
Remark 2.13.
(i)
A smooth sub / supersolution of IMCF is also a weak sub / supersolution.
(ii)
Suppose h(t,\theta) is smooth in a box R=(t_{1},t_{2})\times(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}), with \partial_{t}h\geqslant\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h>0 (resp. \partial_{\theta\theta}h+h\geqslant\partial_{t}h>0) and \theta_{2}-\theta_{1}<\pi. Set F(t,\theta)=h(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}h(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}. Arguing as in (2.16) (2.17), the curves \gamma_{t}=F\big(\{t\}\times(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\big) form a smooth subsolution (resp. supersolution) of IMCF. Setting w=t\circ F^{-1}, namely, setting w=t on each \gamma_{t}, then w is a smooth subsolution (or supersolution) of IMCF.
(iii)
If w is a weak subsolution (resp. supersolution) of IMCF in a domain, then so is \min\{w,T\} for any T\in\mathbb{R}. See [75, Remark 2.1.4(iv)].
(iv)
If w_{1},w_{2} are a supersolution and subsolution of IMCF in \Omega, respectively, and \{w_{1}<w_{2}\}\Subset\Omega, then w_{1}\geqslant w_{2} in \Omega. See [44, Theorem 2.2]
(v)
If w_{1} is a continuously calibrated IMCF in D with outer obstacle \partial D\cap\Omega, and w_{2} is a weak subsolution of IMCF in D (with no boundary condition assumed), and
\{w_{1}<w_{2}\}\Subset\Omega,
then w_{1}\geqslant w_{2} in D. Notice: we a priori allow w_{1}<w_{2} near \partial D\cap\Omega. This follows by combining Theorem 3.8 and Theorem 3.21 in [75].
q-Harmonic functions as q\to 1.
Recall that the q-Laplacian is
\Delta_{q}v=\div\big(|\nabla v|^{q-2}\nabla v\big),
and that v is q-harmonic if \Delta_{q}v=0 distributionally. Suppose q\leqslant 2, and v_{q} is a positive q-harmonic function in a domain \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}. Take w_{q}=(1-q)\log v_{q}. It may be verified that w_{q} satisfies
\Delta_{q}w_{q}=|\nabla w_{q}|^{q},
(2.33)
see [61]. We have the interior gradient estimate [49], see also [61, 59]
\sup_{\Omega^{\prime}}|\nabla w_{q}|\leqslant\frac{C(n)}{d(\Omega^{\prime},\partial\Omega)},\qquad\forall\,\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega.
(2.34)
The following general observation was due to Moser [61, 62]: if v_{q} is a sequence of positive q-harmonic functions, and w_{q}:=(1-q)\log v_{q} converges in C^{0}_{\loc}(\Omega) to a function w\in\Lip_{\loc}(\Omega) as q\to 1, then w is a weak IMCF in \Omega.
Along with the convergence w_{q}\to w, it is also possible to show that the vector fields \nu_{q}=|\nabla w_{q}|^{q-2}\nabla w_{q} converge weakly in L^{1}_{\loc}(\Omega) to a calibration of w. This requires an adaptation of the arguments in [4, 60].
In the recent work [18] the following is shown. Suppose E_{0}\Subset\Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{n} are smooth domains. Consider the q-capacitary potentials v_{q} satisfying
\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{q}v_{q}=0\qquad\text{in}\ \ \Omega\setminus\overline{E_{0}},\\
&v_{q}|_{\partial E_{0}}=1,\qquad v_{q}|_{\partial\Omega}=0.\end{aligned}\right.
Then w_{q}:=(1-q)\log v_{q} converges in C^{0}_{\loc}(\Omega\setminus E_{0}) to the weak IMCF in \Omega with initial value E_{0} and outer obstacle \partial\Omega (see [75, Definition 3.6] for its meaning).
These observations partially inspire the main theorem of Section 6 and its proof. In this paper, we have that v_{q} are the conjugate of a p-harmonic function u_{p}, and u_{p} that approximates a given \infty-harmonic function. The C^{1} equicontinuity theorem in Section 5 will imply that |\nabla v_{q}|^{q-2}\nabla v_{q} converges in C^{0}_{\loc} to a continuous calibration of w. This significantly simplifies the analysis when q\to 1. Eventually, we do not need adaptations of the convergence arguments in [4, 18, 62, 60].
Proofs of facts and lemmas.
We end this subsection with the proof of the facts mentioned above. In the proofs we employ the notations E_{t}=\{w<t\}, E_{t}^{+}=\{w\leqslant t\} and \gamma_{t}=\partial E_{t}\cap\Omega, \gamma_{t}^{+}=\partial E_{t}^{+}\cap\Omega. It is well-known that each E_{t}^{+} also locally minimizes the energy (2.18) [74, Fact 1.2.10].
A calibrated IMCF is a weak IMCF.
Let w\in\Lip_{\loc}(\Omega) be a calibrated weak IMCF. Then for any v\in\Lip_{\loc}(\Omega) and set K so that \{v\neq w\}\Subset K\Subset\Omega, we test \div(\nu)=|\nabla w| with the function v-w, and obtain
\int(v-w)|\nabla w|=-\int\langle\nu\,,\,\nabla v-\nabla w\rangle\geqslant\int\big(|\nabla w|-|\nabla v|\big).
Hence
\int_{K}|\nabla w|+w|\nabla w|\leqslant\int_{K}|\nabla v|+v|\nabla w|.
The well-known equivalence [44, Lemma 1.1] then implies that w is a weak IMCF.
∎
Proof of Remark 2.9(iii).
For any K\Subset\Omega, the coarea formula and \langle\nu\,,\,\nabla w\rangle=|\nabla w| gives
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{P(E_{t};K)}\,dt=\int_{K}|\nabla w|=\int_{K}\langle\nu\,,\,\nabla w\rangle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{\partial E_{t}\cap K}{\Big\langle{\nu}\,,\,{\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}}\Big\rangle}\,dt.
Hence, \nu=\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|} almost everywhere on \partial E_{t} for a.e. t. On the other hand, since w\in\Lip_{\loc}, it is well-known that \nu_{E_{t}}=\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|} almost everywhere on \partial E_{t} for a.e. t. Hence
\nu=\nu_{E_{t}}\text{ a.e. on $\partial E_{t}$, for a.e. $t$.}
Using the continuity of \nu and regularity of \partial E_{t} and (2.21), the full result follows.
∎
Proof of Remark 2.9(iv).
Assume 0\in\gamma_{t}^{+}\setminus\gamma_{t}. Then there is r\ll 1 so that w\geqslant t in B(r) and w\equiv t in E_{t}^{+}\cap B(r). For any E with E\Delta E_{t}^{+}\Subset K\Subset B(r), we have
{P(E;K)}=J_{w}^{K}(E)+\int_{E\cap K}|\nabla w|\geqslant J_{w}^{K}(E)\geqslant J_{w}^{K}(E_{t}^{+})={P\big(E_{t}^{+};K\big)}.
Hence, E_{t}^{+} is perimeter-minimizing in B(r).
∎
Proof of Remark 2.9(v).
In each open disk B\subset\Int\big(\{w=t\}\big), we have 0=\div(\nu)=\curl(\nu^{\perp}) distributionally, hence there is a function f\in C^{1}(B) with \nabla f=\nu^{\perp}. Since |\nabla f|\equiv 1, the streamlines of f (which are integral curves of \nu^{\perp}) must all be line segments. The global statement follows from a continuation argument.
∎
Proof of Remark 2.9(vi).
Denote R=(-r,r)\times(-l,l) and E_{t}=\{w<t\}\subset D\subset R. Notice that \{w^{\prime}<t\}=E_{t} when t\leqslant T, and \{w^{\prime}<t\}=R when t>T. Thus, it suffices to show that
J_{w^{\prime}}^{K}(E_{t})\leqslant J_{w^{\prime}}^{K}(F),\qquad\forall\,t\leqslant T,\ \forall\,F,\text{ so that }E_{t}\Delta F\Subset K\Subset R.
We first assume t<T. Denote D_{\varepsilon}=\{|x|<r,\,f(x)+\varepsilon<y<l\}. By continuity, we have E_{t}\subset D_{\varepsilon} for some \varepsilon>0. Since the latter set is outward perimeter-minimizing in R, we obtain
\displaystyle J_{w^{\prime}}^{K}(F)
\displaystyle={P(F;K)}-\int_{F\cap K}|\nabla w^{\prime}|\geqslant{P\big(F\cap D_{\varepsilon};K\big)}-\int_{F\cap K}|\nabla w^{\prime}|
\displaystyle=J_{w}^{K\cap D_{\varepsilon/2}}(F\cap D_{\varepsilon})-\int_{(F\setminus D_{\varepsilon})\cap K}|\nabla w^{\prime}|.
Using the energy minimizing of E_{t} in D, we have
J_{w^{\prime}}^{K}(F)\geqslant J_{w}^{K\cap D_{\varepsilon/2}}(E_{t})-\int_{(F\setminus D_{\varepsilon})\cap K}|\nabla w^{\prime}|.
Taking \varepsilon\to 0, it follows that
J_{w^{\prime}}^{K}(F)\geqslant J_{w}^{K\cap D}(E_{t})=J_{w^{\prime}}^{K}(E_{t}),
as desired. Then, taking t\nearrow T shows that E_{T} minimizes J_{w^{\prime}} as well.
∎
Derivation of (2.27).
Set r=\alpha/2\pi. The given hypocycloid has the well-known parametrization
\gamma(s)=-(1-r)e^{-is}-re^{\frac{1-r}{r}is},
with parameter s=\pi-\Arg(z) where z is the point where rolling circle with the unit circle contact. The velocity is then computed as
\gamma^{\prime}(s)=(1-r)ie^{-is}\big[1-e^{is/r}\big].
Therefore, the angle parameter is given by
\vartheta(s)=\Arg\gamma^{\prime}(s)=\frac{\pi}{2}-s-\frac{\pi-s/r}{2}=\frac{1-2r}{2r}s.
Finally, switching to the angle parametrization, we obtain (notice \nu_{\theta}=e^{i(\theta-\pi/2)})
\displaystyle h(\theta)
\displaystyle=\langle\gamma(s(\theta))\,,\,e^{i(\theta-\pi/2)}\rangle=\Ree\Big[\Big(\!-(1-r)e^{-\frac{2r}{1-2r}i\theta}-re^{\frac{1-r}{1-2r}2i\theta}\Big)\cdot e^{i(\pi/2-\theta)}\Big]
