\displaystyle=\Ree\Big[\!-(1-r)ie^{-\frac{i\theta}{1-2r}}-ire^{\frac{i\theta}{1-2r}}\Big]=(1-2r)\sin\Big(\frac{-\theta}{1-2r}\Big).
The result follows by inserting r=\alpha/2\pi. Note that
s\in(0,\alpha)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\theta\in\Big(0,\frac{1-2r}{2r}\alpha\Big)=(0,\pi-\alpha).\qed
Derivation of (2.30).
Set r=\lambda/2\pi. Then the cycloid can be parametrized as
\gamma(s)=r+is-re^{is/r}.
Its velocity and angle parameter is
\gamma^{\prime}(s)=i(1-e^{is/r}),\qquad\vartheta(s)=\Arg\gamma^{\prime}(s)=s/2r.
Hence, the support function is
h(\theta)=\Ree\Big[\Big(r+2ri\theta-re^{2i\theta}\Big)\cdot e^{i(\pi/2-\theta)}\Big]=2r(\sin\theta-\theta\cos\theta).\qed
Proof of Remark 2.13(i).
Suppose w is a smooth subsolution, namely, \div\big(\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}\big)\geqslant|\nabla w|. Denote E_{t}=\{w<t\}. For any competitor F\supset E_{t} and K as stated, the divergence theorem gives
\displaystyle J_{w}^{K}(E_{t})
\displaystyle={P(E_{t};K)}-\int_{E_{t}\cap K}|\nabla w|=\int_{\partial E_{t}\cap K}{\Big\langle{\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}}\,,\,{\nu_{E_{t}}}\Big\rangle}-\int_{E_{t}\cap K}|\nabla w|
\displaystyle=\int_{\partial^{*}F\cap K}{\Big\langle{\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}}\,,\,{\nu_{F}}\Big\rangle}-\int_{F\setminus E_{t}}\div\Big(\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}\Big)-\int_{E_{t}\cap K}|\nabla w|
\displaystyle\leqslant{P(F;K)}-\int_{F\cap K}|\nabla w|=J_{w}^{K}(F),
as desired. The case of supersolution is similar.
∎
Proof of Lemma 2.10.
Clearly, the contact of any \partial E_{i} with \partial E_{j} is tangential. Hence, the outer unit normals of \partial E_{i} agree on overlaps, hence the map (2.22) is defined. We first state a local version of the regularity, which will also be useful later.
Lemma 2.14.
Suppose f,g are functions on (-1,1), with f^{\prime}=g^{\prime} whenever f=g, and
\sup_{(-1,1)}|f^{\prime}|\leqslant 1,\qquad\sup_{(-1,1)}|g^{\prime}|\leqslant 1,
and
\|f^{\prime}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(-1,1)}\leqslant\mu,\qquad\|g^{\prime}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(-1,1)}\leqslant\mu,
for some \alpha\in(0,1] and \mu>0. Let \nu be the (well-defined) upper normal vector field of \graphh(f)\cup\graphh(g). For s\leqslant 1, denote S=\big(\graphh(f)\cup\graphh(g)\big)\cap\{|x|\leqslant 1-s\}. Then
\|\nu\|_{C^{0,\alpha/(1+\alpha)}(S)}\leqslant 16\mu^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}s^{-\alpha}.
Back to the main lemma. It suffices to show that for any z\in\Omega, there is r>0 so that \nu\in C^{\alpha/(1+\alpha)}\big(B(z,r)\cap(\cup\,\partial E_{i})\big). We may assume z=0. Choosing r sufficiently small and magnifying the picture by r^{-1}, we may assume that the C^{1,\alpha} norm of all \partial E_{i} are bounded by e^{-100} in B(100), and aim ourselves at showing \nu\in C^{0,\alpha/(1+\alpha)}\big(B(1)\cap(\cup\,\partial E_{i})\big).
Fix an i with \partial E_{i}\cap B(1)\neq\emptyset. After a rotation, we may assume that \partial E_{i}\cap B(100) is a e^{-90}-Lipschitz subgraph. Hence
\{y<-2\}\cap B(100)\subset E_{i}\cap B(100)\subset\{y<2\}\cap B(100).
For any other j with \partial E_{j}\cap B(1)\neq\emptyset, there is \theta_{j}\in\mathbb{S}^{1} so that
\big\{\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta_{j}}\rangle<-2\big\}\cap B(100)\subset E_{j}\cap B(100)\subset\big\{\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta_{j}}\rangle<2\big\}\cap B(100).
Since E_{i}\subset E_{j} or E_{j}\subset E_{i}, these imply |\theta_{j}-\pi|<1/10. So \partial E_{j}\cap B(100) is a 1/5-Lipschitz graph (without rotation). Now for any j,k with \partial E_{j}\cap B(1)\neq\emptyset and \partial E_{k}\cap B(1)\neq\emptyset, we apply Lemma 2.14 to obtain
\|\nu\|_{C^{0,\alpha/(1+\alpha)}}\leqslant e^{10}\qquad\text{on}\ \ (\partial E_{j}\cup\partial E_{k})\cap B(1).
This implies the lemma since j,k are arbitrary.
∎
Proof of Lemma 2.14.
Note that
\nu=\frac{(-f^{\prime},1)}{\sqrt{1+(f^{\prime})^{2}}}\text{\ \ \ on\ \ }\graphh(f),\qquad\nu=\frac{(-g^{\prime},1)}{\sqrt{1+(g^{\prime})^{2}}}\text{\ \ \ on\ \ }\graphh(g),
(2.35)
and the map a\to(-a,1)/(1+a^{2})^{1/2} is 1-Lipschitz. It suffices to show that
|f^{\prime}(x)-g^{\prime}(x)|\leqslant 4\mu^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}s^{-\alpha}|f(x)-g(x)|^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}},\qquad x\in[-1+s,1-s].
(2.36)
Indeed, once (2.36) is shown, for all x,y\in[-1+s,1-s] we have as desired
\displaystyle\big|\nu(x,f(x))-\nu(y,g(y))\big|
\displaystyle\leqslant|f^{\prime}(x)-g^{\prime}(y)|\leqslant|f^{\prime}(x)-g^{\prime}(x)|+|g^{\prime}(x)-g^{\prime}(y)|
\displaystyle\leqslant 4\mu^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}s^{-\alpha}|f(x)-g(x)|^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}}+2\big(\mu|x-y|^{\alpha}\big)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}
\displaystyle\leqslant 4\mu^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}s^{-\alpha}\Big[|f(x)-g(y)|^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}}+|g(y)-g(x)|^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\Big]
\displaystyle+2\mu^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}s^{-\alpha}|x-y|^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}}
\displaystyle\leqslant 8\mu^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}s^{-\alpha}\Big[|f(x)-g(y)|^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}}+|x-y|^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}}\Big]
\displaystyle\leqslant 16\mu^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}s^{-\alpha}\big|(x,f(x))-(y,g(y))\big|^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}}.
In the second line, we used |g^{\prime}(x)-g^{\prime}(y)|\leqslant\min\big\{\mu|x-y|^{\alpha},2\big\}.
As (2.36) is linear, we may replace f\to f-g and g\to 0 (thus f^{\prime}=0 whenever f=0). It suffices to show
\|f^{\prime}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(-1,1)}\leqslant 2\mu\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad|f^{\prime}|\leqslant 4\mu^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}|f|^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}}s^{-\alpha}\ \ \text{in}\ \ [-1+s,1-s].
Fix x\in[-1+s,1-s]. By changing x\to-x and f\mapsto-f, we may assume f(x)>0 and f^{\prime}(x)>0. If f(x)\geqslant\mu s^{1+\alpha}, then using |f^{\prime}|\leqslant 2\mu we have
f^{\prime}(x)\leqslant 2\mu\leqslant 2\mu\big[f(x)\mu^{-1}s^{-(1+\alpha)}\big]^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}}=2\mu^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}f(x)^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}}s^{-\alpha}.
Now assume f(x)<\mu s^{1+\alpha}. Set \Delta x=[f(x)/\mu]^{1/(1+\alpha)}, thus x-\Delta x\in(-1,1). If f(x-\Delta x)<0, then there is y\in(x-\Delta x,x) with f^{\prime}(y)=f(y)=0, hence
f^{\prime}(x)\leqslant 2\mu(x-y)^{\alpha}\leqslant 2\mu(\Delta x)^{\alpha}=2\mu^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}f(x)^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}}.
If f(x-\Delta x)\geqslant 0, then
\displaystyle 0
\displaystyle\leqslant f(x-\Delta x)=f(x)-\int_{x-\Delta x}^{x}f^{\prime}(t)\,dt\leqslant f(x)-f^{\prime}(x)\Delta x+\int_{x-\Delta x}^{x}2\mu(x-t)^{\alpha}\,dt
\displaystyle=f(x)-f^{\prime}(x)f(x)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}\mu^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}+\frac{2}{1+\alpha}f(x),
hence
f^{\prime}(x)\leqslant 4\mu^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}f(x)^{\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}}.
In either case, the result follows.
∎
2.4 Sturmian theory for 1D heat equation
Let f(t,\theta) be a function that solves the heat equation \partial_{t}f=\partial_{\theta\theta}f. Suppose (t_{0},\theta_{0}) is a degenerate root of f, meaning that f(t_{0},\theta_{0})=\partial_{\theta}f(t_{0},\theta_{0})=0. Let k be the order of this root, meaning that f(t_{0},\theta_{0})=\partial_{\theta}f(t_{0},\theta_{0})=\cdots\partial_{\theta}^{k-1}f(t_{0},\theta_{0})=0 but \partial_{\theta}^{k}f(t_{0},\theta_{0})\neq 0. The following is shown by Angenent [5] and also Angenent-Fiedler [7]: for any \varepsilon>0, there are \mu,\delta<\varepsilon so that
(i)
f has no roots on the sides [t_{0}-\mu,t_{0}+\mu]\times\{\theta_{0}\pm\delta\}, and \theta_{0} is the only root of f in \{t_{0}\}\times[\theta_{0}-\delta,\theta_{0}+\delta];
(ii)
f(t_{0}+\tau,\cdot) has n\in\{0,1\} nondegenerate roots in (\theta_{0}-\delta,\theta_{0}+\delta) for all 0<\tau\leqslant\mu, where n=0 if k is even and n=1 if k is odd;
(iii)
f(t_{0}-\tau,\cdot) has k nondegenerate roots in (\theta_{0}-\delta,\theta_{0}+\delta) for all 0<\tau\leqslant\mu.
In particular, (ii) (iii) implies that the number of roots of f(t,\cdot) locally drops by at least 2 whenever we encounter a degenerate root.
Item (i) follows from [5, Theorem A, B], and from the same source it is known that f(t_{0}+\tau,\cdot) has at most one root and f(t_{0}-\tau,\cdot) has at least two roots. Also, from this one knows that the number of roots must locally drop at a degenerate root. The full item (iii) follows from the asymptotic analysis in [7, p.566]. The full item (ii) follows from the maximum principle: if k is even, then f has the same sign in the set
\partial_{0}P:=\Big(\{t_{0}\}\times[\theta_{0}-\delta,\theta_{0}+\delta]\Big)\cup\Big([t_{0},t_{0}+\mu]\times\{\theta_{0}\pm\delta\}\Big),
which implies f>0 in (t_{0},t_{0}+\mu]\times(\theta_{0}-\delta,\theta_{0}+\delta). If k is odd, then f does not change sign in the following regions respectively:
\displaystyle\partial_{-}P:=\{t_{0}\}\times[\theta_{0}-\delta,\theta_{0})\cup[t_{0},t_{0}+\mu]\times\{\theta_{0}-\delta\},
\displaystyle\partial_{+}P:=\{t_{0}\}\times(\theta_{0},\theta_{0}+\delta]\cup[t_{0},t_{0}+\mu]\times\{\theta_{0}+\delta\}.
If f(t+\tau,\cdot) has more than one root in (\theta_{0}-\delta,\theta_{0}+\delta) for some \tau\in(0,\mu], then either \{f>0\} or \{f<0\} would have a connected component not intersecting \partial_{0}P, contradicting the strong maximum principle.
Globally, suppose f solves the heat equation on (t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}. Then the number of roots of f(t,\cdot) is nonincreasing in t, and decreases by at least 2 whenever a degenerate root appears at time t. If the number of roots stays constant in (t_{3},t_{4})\subset(t_{1},t_{2}), then all the roots in (t_{3},t_{4})\times\mathbb{S}^{1} are nondegenerate.
If a function f solves \partial_{t}f=\partial_{\theta\theta}f+f, then e^{-t}f solves the heat equation. Therefore, the above theory holds for solutions of \partial_{t}f=\partial_{\theta\theta}f+f as well.
For a function 0\not\equiv f\in C^{0}(\mathbb{S}^{1}), recall its Fourier coefficients
a_{0}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}f,\quad b_{0}=0,\quad a_{k}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}f\cos(k\theta),\quad b_{k}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}f\sin(k\theta).
The Sturm-Hurwitz theorem states that if f satisfies a_{k}=b_{k}=0 for all k\leqslant n, then f has at least 2n+2 roots in \mathbb{S}^{1}. See also Section 12 for relevant discussions. In particular, if f is smooth with \int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}f=0, then integrating by parts gives
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(\partial_{\theta\theta}f+f)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(\partial_{\theta\theta}f+f)\cos\theta=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(\partial_{\theta\theta}f+f)\sin\theta=0,
hence \partial_{\theta\theta}f+f has at least 4 roots in \mathbb{S}^{1}.
3 IMCF clusters
We start with defining simple IMCF clusters:
Definition 3.1.
We call \big(w,\nu,\{D_{i}\},\{\chi_{i}\}\big) a simple IMCF cluster in a domain \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}, where w is a function and \nu is a vector field in \Omega, if the following hold:
(i)
\{D_{i}\} are disjoint C^{1} domains in \Omega, and \Omega\setminus\bigcup D_{i} is the disjoint union of countably many C^{1} curves \gamma_{j} without endpoints in \Omega (we call \gamma_{j} ridges).
(ii)
\chi_{i}\in\{\pm 1\} for each i (it is understood that \chi_{i} is a choice of orientation);
(iii)
w,\nu are continuous with |\nu|\leqslant 1 in \Omega;
(iv)
w|_{D_{i}}\in\Lip_{\loc}(D_{i}), and
\chi_{i}\nu\cdot\nabla w=|\nabla w|\quad\text{a.e.,}\qquad\div(\chi_{i}\nu)=|\nabla w|\quad\text{weakly\ \ in $D_{i}$,}
(3.1)
and
\chi_{i}\nu=\nu_{D_{i}}\qquad\text{on}\ \ \partial D_{i}.
(3.2)
We say that the simple IMCF cluster is unit-calibrated, if |\nu|=1 everywhere.
Remark 3.2.
The following facts are trivial but may help clarify the definition:
(i)
Each \partial D_{i} is a union of ridges.
(ii)
A continuously calibrated weak IMCF is trivially a simple cluster, by setting D_{1}=\Omega, \chi_{1}=1 and \{\gamma_{j}\}=\emptyset. On the other hand, we remind that not all weak IMCFs are known to be continuously calibrated.
(iii)
w|_{D_{i}} is a continuously calibrated weak IMCF with outer obstacle \partial D_{i}\cap\Omega. The calibration is given by \chi_{i}\nu.
(iv)
Adjacent regions have the opposite orientations, due to (3.2).
(v)
If we change \nu\to-\nu and switch all orientations \chi_{i} to -\chi_{i}, then the resulting data remains a simple IMCF cluster.
Remark 3.3.
Since e^{-w}\nu is divergence-free in each D_{i} and is continuous across all \gamma_{j}, it follows that
\div(e^{-w}\nu)=0\qquad\text{distributionally in }\Omega.
(3.3)
Then, we define mixed clusters which allow valleys in addition to ridges:
Definition 3.4.
We say that (w,\nu) is a mixed IMCF cluster in a domain \Omega, if there are points \{z_{i}\} and disjoint relatively closed line segments \sigma_{j} in \Omega so that:
(i)
w:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\} is continuous, and e^{-w}\nu is a continuous vector field in \Omega;
(ii)
\{w=+\infty\}\subset\{z_{i}\}, and w,\nu are constant with \nu\equiv\pm(\sigma^{\prime}_{j})^{\perp} on each \sigma_{j};
(iii)
Each compact subset of \Omega meets finitely many z_{i} and \sigma_{j};
(iv)
Denote A=\{z_{i}\}\cup(\cup\sigma_{j}). Then (w,\nu) is a simple IMCF cluster in \Omega\setminus A.
We say that w is unit-calibrated if |\nu|=1 everywhere in \{w<\infty\}.
In (iv), we mean that there exists a division of \Omega\setminus A into regions D_{i}, and a choice of orientations \chi_{i}, so that \big(w,\nu,\{D_{i}\},\{\chi_{i}\}) is a simple IMCF cluster in \Omega\setminus A. Such a division need not be unique.
See Section 4 for examples of mixed IMCF clusters and their associated \infty-harmonic functions. An exceptional segment \sigma is called a valley if w forms two IMCFs evolving away from \sigma in its neighborhood. More precisely, assuming that \sigma lies in the x-axis and \nu|_{\sigma}=-\partial_{y} after a rigid motion, we say that \sigma is a valley if for all points (x,0)\in\Int(\sigma), there is r>0 so that (x-r,x+r)\times(-r,0) and (x-r,x+r)\times(0,r) lie in two regions D_{1},D_{2} in the simple cluster in (iv), so that
\chi_{1}=1,\qquad\chi_{2}=-1.
In the examples in Section 4, all exceptional segments are valleys. In general, an exceptional segment could be a valley or other trivial objects (e.g. a straight portion in the level set of a weak IMCF that is intentionally removed). A valley and a ridge (in \Omega\setminus A) may contact: Figure 8 shows a possible local model.
Figure 8: A local picture when a valley and a ridge contact.
A nontrivial exceptional point z\in\{z_{i}\} contains two cases: near z, the solution w either is asymptotic to the quasiradial solution in Example 4.3, or consists of two IMCFs colliding together as in Figure 9. The second type of exceptional point may also be viewed as a degenerate valley. Trivial cases (e.g. a point removed from a simple cluster) may occur as well. For simplicity, we choose not to formally classify the types of exceptional segments and points in Definition 3.4.
Figure 9: Exceptional point in mixed cluster.
We prove several auxiliary lemmas on simple IMCF clusters. In the lemmas, we denote
Q_{\delta}(r)=(-r,r)\times(-\delta r,\delta r)
for \delta\leqslant 1. Note that Q_{\delta}(r)=Q(r) if \delta=1.
Lemma 3.5.
Let \big(w,\nu,\{D_{i}\},\{\chi_{i}\}\big) be a simple cluster in \Omega\supset Q_{\delta}(r) with \delta\leqslant 1, so that
|\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}\delta\qquad\text{in}\ \ Q_{\delta}(r).
(3.4)
If 0\in\cup\gamma_{j}, then (\cup\gamma_{j})\cap Q_{\delta}(r) is a connected e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitz graph.
Proof.
(3.2) (3.4) imply that (\cup\gamma_{j})\cap Q_{\delta}(r) is a disjoint union of e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitz graphs whose boundaries are on \partial Q_{\delta}(r), and one of these graphs passes through 0. If the lemma is false, then there is a connected component of Q_{\delta}(r)\setminus\cup\gamma_{j}, called D, with the following property: \partial D\cap Q_{\delta}(r) contains two C^{1} curves \gamma_{1},\gamma_{2} so that \nu_{D} points almost upwards on \gamma_{1} and points almost downwards on \gamma_{2}. If D is positively oriented, then this violates (3.2) (3.4) on \gamma_{1}. If D is negatively oriented, then this violates (3.2) (3.4) on \gamma_{2}.
∎
Corollary 3.6.
In a simple cluster, each compact set intersects finitely many ridges.
Proof.
This follows from Lemma 3.5, the continuity of \nu, and a covering argument.
∎
Throughout this paper, we reserve the following notations:
Y_{t}=\{w>t\},\qquad\widetilde{Z}_{t}=\{w\geqslant t\},\qquad Z_{t}=\Int(\widetilde{Z}_{t}).
If w is a weak IMCF, then it is known that \widetilde{Z}_{t}=\overline{Z_{t}}, and Y_{t},Z_{t} are mean concave.
The following lemma concerns the shape of these sets in a simple cluster. Roughly saying, each Y_{t},Z_{t} is a “concave polygon” except that some vertices could stick together (Figure 10(v)), and each \widetilde{Z}_{t} is a “closed concave polygon” but possibly with line segments attached to the vertices (Figure 11). In any case where -r<a=b<r in (i)(ia) could be ruled out, it follows that Y_{t} or Z_{t} is a disjoint union of concave polygons. One notable outcome is that \partial Y_{t} and \partial Z_{t} must have a cusp when they travel across regions.
Using the strategies developed in Section 7, it can be shown that Figure 11(i)(ii)(iii) cannot happen. More precisely, for any segment \sigma\subset\partial\widetilde{Z}_{t} it can be shown that \sigma\not\Subset\Omega. As it is not used in this paper, we omit the formal statement.
Lemma 3.7.
Suppose \big(w,\nu,\{D_{i}\},\{\chi_{i}\}\big) is a simple IMCF cluster in a domain \Omega, with \{\gamma_{j}\} the associated collection of ridges. Then the following hold for each t\in\mathbb{R}.
(i)
Suppose 0\in\cup\gamma_{j} and \Omega\supset Q_{\delta}(r) for a \delta\leqslant 1, and
|\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}\delta\qquad\text{in}\ \ Q_{\delta}(r).
(3.5)
Then (\cup\gamma_{j})\cap Q_{\delta}(r) is the graph of a e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitz function f on (-r,r) with f(0)=0. In Q_{\delta}(r) we have:
(ia)
Let F denote either Y_{t} or Z_{t}. Then there is a set I of the form
I=(-r,r)\qquad\text{or}\qquad I=(-r,a)\cup(b,r)\quad(\text{where }-r\leqslant a\leqslant b\leqslant r),
and functions g_{1},g_{2}:\overline{I}\cap(-r,r)\to[-\delta r,\delta r], with g_{1}<g_{2} and g_{1}\leqslant f\leqslant g_{2} in I, so that
F\cap Q_{\delta}(r)=\Big\{x\in I,\ g_{1}(x)<y<g_{2}(x)\Big\}.
(3.6)
If F=Y_{t}, then g_{1}<f<g_{2} in I. More properties of g_{1},g_{2} are found in (i)(ic).
(ib)
There is a set
I=(-r,r)\qquad\text{or}\qquad I=(-r,a]\cup[b,r)\quad(\text{where}-r\leqslant a<b\leqslant r),
and two functions g_{1},g_{2}:I\to[-\delta r,\delta r], with g_{1}\leqslant f\leqslant g_{2}, so that
\widetilde{Z}_{t}\cap Q_{\delta}(r)=\Big\{x\in I,\ g_{1}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant g_{2}(x)\Big\}.
(3.7)
(ic)
The functions g_{1},g_{2} in (i)(ia) (i)(ib) satisfy the following common properties:
•
If I=(-r,r), then g_{1} is concave (resp. g_{2} is convex) in each connected component of \{g_{1}>-\delta r\} (resp. \{g_{2}<\delta r\});
•
If I\neq(-r,r), then g_{1} is concave and g_{2} is convex in each connected component of \overline{I}\cap(-r,r);
•
g_{1}=g_{2}=f on \partial I\cap(-r,r), and g^{\prime}_{1}=g^{\prime}_{2}=f^{\prime} whenever g_{1}=g_{2};
•
\graphh(g_{1}) and \graphh(g_{2}) have \nu as unit normal vector.
(ii)
\widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z}_{t} is contained in \cup\gamma_{j} and is a disjoint union of nontrivial line segments.
(iii)
Let \sigma be a connected component of \widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z}_{t}. If \sigma\Subset\Omega, then \overline{\sigma}\cap\partial Z_{t}\neq\emptyset.
(iv)
For any z\in\gamma_{j}, either
•
w is monotone along \gamma_{j} in a small neighborhood of z, or
•
z is a strict local minimum of w|_{\gamma_{j}} (i.e. w>w(z) in U\cap\gamma_{j}\setminus\{z\} for some neighborhood U\ni z).
The second type of points are discrete in \Omega.
Figure 10: Possible local shapes of Y_{t} or Z_{t}. The fifth picture prevents Y_{t},Z_{t} from being a concave polygon.
Figure 11: Possible local shapes \widetilde{Z}_{t}. Blue segments represent \widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}}.
Proof.
Fix j so that 0\in\gamma_{j}. By Lemma 3.5, the set (\cup\gamma_{j})\cap Q_{\delta}(r) is connected and hence equals \gamma_{j}\cap Q_{\delta}(r). We may write \gamma_{j}\cap Q_{\delta}(r) as the graph of a e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitz function f:(-r,r)\to(-e^{-9}\delta r,e^{-9}\delta r) with f(0)=0. Denote
D_{1}=Q_{\delta}(r)\cap\{y<f(x)\},\qquad D_{2}=Q_{\delta}(r)\cap\{y>f(x)\}.
By (3.5) and that \gamma_{j} is a ridge, we have \chi_{1}=-1 and \chi_{2}=1, and the cluster consists of an upward evolving IMCF in D_{1} and a downward evolving IMCF in D_{2}. Thus
w(x,y)\text{ is }\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{nondecreasing in $y$ for $y\leqslant f(x)$,}\\
&\text{nonincreasing in $y$ for $y\geqslant f(x)$,}\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\forall\,x\in(-r,r),
(3.8)
and that y=f(x) attains a maximum of y\mapsto w(x,y) in (-\delta r,\delta r), for all x\in(-r,r).
Recall Y_{t}=\{w>t\}. By (3.8), there is a function \overline{g}_{1}:(-r,r)\to[-\delta r,\delta r) with \overline{g}_{1}\leqslant f, so that
Y_{t}\cap D_{1}=\Big\{\!-r<x<r,\ \overline{g}_{1}(x)<y<f(x)\Big\}.
Since w is a weak IMCF with outer obstacle in D_{1} and is calibrated by -\nu, it follows that \overline{g}_{1} is concave in each connected component of \{-\delta r<\overline{g}_{1}<f\}, and \graphh(\overline{g}_{1})\cap Q_{\delta}(r) have \nu as unit normal vector. For brevity, throughout this proof, we say that a function g is \nu-orthogonal if \nu is the unit normal vector of \graphh(g). The \nu-orthogonality and (3.5) imply that \overline{g}_{1} is e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitz. Similarly, there is a function \overline{g}_{2}:(-r,r)\to(-\delta r,\delta r] with \overline{g}_{2}\geqslant f, so that
Y_{t}\cap D_{2}=\Big\{\!-r<x<r,\ f(x)<y<\overline{g}_{2}(x)\Big\},
and \overline{g}_{2} has similar convexity properties as \overline{g}_{1}. Furthermore, notice that \overline{g}_{1}(x)<f(x)<\overline{g}_{2}(x) whenever \overline{g}_{1}(x)<\overline{g}_{2}(x): this follows from (3.8) and Y_{t}=\{w>t\}. And obviously, we have \overline{g}_{1}(x)=f(x)=\overline{g}_{2}(x) whenever \overline{g}_{1}(x)=\overline{g}_{2}(x).
Set I=\big\{x:\overline{g}_{1}(x)<\overline{g}_{2}(x)\big\}\subset(-r,r). Then
Y_{t}\cap Q_{\delta}(r)=\Big\{x\in I,\ \overline{g}_{1}(x)<y<\overline{g}_{2}(x)\Big\}.
We claim that either I=(-r,r), or I=(-r,a)\cup(b,r) for some -r\leqslant a\leqslant b\leqslant r. If this is not true, then some connected component (c,d) of I would satisfy -r<c<d<r. Recall that we have obtained:
(1)
\overline{g}_{1}(c)=f(c)=\overline{g}_{2}(c), \overline{g}_{1}(d)=f(d)=\overline{g}_{2}(d);
(2)
\overline{g}_{1}<f<\overline{g}_{2} in (c,d);
(3)
\overline{g}_{1},\overline{g}_{2} are e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitz and f:(-r,r)\to(-e^{-9}\delta r,e^{-9}\delta r);
(4)
\overline{g}_{1} (resp. \overline{g}_{2}) is concave (resp. convex) in each connected component of \{-\delta r<\overline{g}_{1}<f\} (resp. \{f<\overline{g}_{2}<\delta r\}).
Combining (1)(3) we know that \overline{g}_{1}>-\delta r and \overline{g}_{2}<\delta r in [c,d], then (4) implies that \overline{g}_{1} is concave and \overline{g}_{2} is convex in (c,d) hence in [c,d] as well. Combining this and (1)(2), we obtain a contradiction with the following elementary lemma, as desired.
Lemma 3.8.
There does not exist a concave function f_{1} and convex function f_{2} on an interval J, so that f_{1}<f_{2} in \Int(J) and f_{1}=f_{2} on \partial J.
Set g_{1}=\overline{g}_{1}|_{\overline{I}\cap(-r,r)} and g_{2}=\overline{g}_{2}|_{\overline{I}\cap(-r,r)}. If I=(-r,r), then note that g_{1}<f<g_{2} in (-r,r). The convexity property (i)(ic) follows from (4). If I\neq(-r,r), then clearly g_{1}=g_{2}=f on \partial I\cap(-r,r). Since \overline{g}_{1},\overline{g}_{2} are e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitz, they never reach \pm\delta r. The convexity follows from (4) as well. Finally, the fact g^{\prime}_{1}=g^{\prime}_{2}=f^{\prime} whenever g_{1}=g_{2} follows from the \nu-orthogonality of their graphs. This proves items (i)(ia)(i)(ic) for F=Y_{t}.
Then we analyze \widetilde{Z}_{t} using \widetilde{Z}_{t}=\bigcap_{s<t}Y_{s}. If the ridge \gamma_{j}\cap Q_{\delta}(r) is contained in \widetilde{Z}_{t}, then for all s<t there are functions g_{1,s}:(-r,r)\to[-\delta r,\delta r) and g_{2,s}:(-r,r)\to(-\delta r,\delta r] with g_{1,s}<f<g_{2,s}, so that
Y_{s}\cap Q_{\delta}(r)=\Big\{x\in(-r,r),\ g_{1,s}(x)<y<g_{2,s}(x)\Big\}.
Moreover, g_{1,s} is concave and \nu-orthogonal in each connected component of \{g_{1,s}>-\delta r\}, and similarly for g_{2,s}. Taking an ascending / descending limit
g_{1}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{1,s},\qquad g_{2}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{2,s},
we find
\widetilde{Z}_{t}\cap Q_{\delta}(r)=\Big\{x\in(-r,r),\ g_{1}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant g_{2}(x)\Big\}.
The convexity and \nu-orthogonality of g_{1,s},g_{2,s} pass to g_{1},g_{2}, thus proving (i)(ib) (i)(ic) for this case (the fact that g^{\prime}_{1}=f^{\prime}=g^{\prime}_{2} whenever g_{1}=g_{2} follows from \nu-orthogonality). Note that g_{1,s}<f<g_{2,s} passes to g_{1}\leqslant f\leqslant g_{2}, and equality may hold somewhere.
Assume \gamma_{j}\cap Q_{\delta}(r)\not\subset\widetilde{Z}_{t}. Pick x_{0}\in(-r,r) so that w(x_{0},f(x_{0}))<t. Then for all s<t close enough to t, there are intervals I_{s}=(-r,a_{s})\cup(b_{s},r) with -r\leqslant a_{s}<x_{0}<b_{s}\leqslant r, and functions g_{1,s}:\overline{I_{s}}\cap(-r,r)\to[-\delta r,\delta r] and g_{2,s}:\overline{I_{s}}\cap(-r,r)\to[-\delta r,\delta r] as in items (i)(ia) (i)(ic), so that
Y_{s}\cap Q_{\delta}(r)=\Big\{x\in I_{s},\ g_{1,s}(x)<y<g_{2,s}(x)\Big\}.
Recall that g_{1,s},g_{2,s} are concave / convex in each interval in their domains.
Notice the strict monotonicity (which comes from (3.8) and the continuity of w):
•
a_{s}<a_{s^{\prime}} whenever t>s>s^{\prime} and a_{s^{\prime}}>-r,
•
g_{1,s}(x)>g_{1,s^{\prime}}(x) whenever t>s>s^{\prime} and x\in(-r,a_{s}].
Similar facts hold for b_{s}, g_{2,s} as well. So the following monotone limits are defined:
a=\lim_{s\nearrow t}a_{s},\qquad b=\lim_{s\nearrow t}b_{s},
and
g_{1}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{1,s},\qquad g_{2}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{2,s}\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{1}_{\loc}((-r,a])\ \ \text{and}\ \ C^{1}_{\loc}([b,r)).
The C^{1} convergence is due to \nu-orthogonality. It follows that
\widetilde{Z}_{t}\cap Q_{\delta}(r)=\bigcap_{s<t}\big(Y_{s}\cap Q_{\delta}(r)\big)=\Big\{x\in I,\ g_{1}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant g_{2}(x)\Big\},
where I:=(-r,a]\cup[b,r). Note that a<x_{0}<b since (x_{0},f(x_{0}))\notin\widetilde{Z}_{t}. The convexity and \nu-orthogonality of g_{1},g_{2} follows from taking the limit. This proves (i)(ib) (i)(ic) for \widetilde{Z}_{t}.
Next, consider Z_{t}=\Int(\widetilde{Z}_{t}). By (i)(ib), there is a set \widetilde{I}=(-r,r) or \widetilde{I}=(-r,\widetilde{a}]\cup[\widetilde{b},r), and functions \widetilde{g}_{1},\widetilde{g}_{2}:\widetilde{I}\to[-\delta r,\delta r] with properties stated in (i)(ib) (i)(ic), so that
\widetilde{Z}_{t}\cap Q_{\delta}(r)=\Big\{x\in\widetilde{I},\ \widetilde{g}_{1}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant\widetilde{g}_{2}(x)\Big\}.
(3.9)
Letting
I=\Big\{x\in\widetilde{I}:\widetilde{g}_{1}(x)<\widetilde{g}_{2}(x)\Big\},
it follows that
Z_{t}\cap Q_{\delta}(r)=\Big\{x\in I,\ \widetilde{g}_{1}(x)<y<\widetilde{g}_{2}(x)\Big\}.
(3.10)
If I=(-r,r), then setting g_{i}=\widetilde{g}_{i} already proves (i)(ia)(i)(ic) with F=Z_{t}. Now suppose I\neq(-r,r). This implies \widetilde{g}_{1}=\widetilde{g}_{2} hence \widetilde{g}_{1}=\widetilde{g}_{2}=f somewhere. Thus \widetilde{g}_{1},\widetilde{g}_{2} do not reach \pm\delta r due to e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitzness, and hence \widetilde{g}_{1} is concave and \widetilde{g}_{2} is convex in each connected component of \widetilde{I}. The following fact is elementary:
Lemma 3.9.
Suppose f_{1} is concave and f_{2} is convex in an interval, with f_{1}\leqslant f_{2}. Then \{f_{1}=f_{2}\} is a subinterval, on which f_{1},f_{2} are both linear.
This implies that
•
if \widetilde{I}=(-r,r), then \widetilde{I}\setminus I is a subinterval;
•
if \widetilde{I}\neq(-r,r), then I has the form I=(-r,a)\cup(b,r) with a\leqslant\widetilde{a} and b\geqslant\widetilde{b}.
In either case, I has the form as in (i)(ia). Then set g_{1}=\widetilde{g}_{1}|_{\overline{I}\cap(-r,r)} and g_{2}=\widetilde{g}_{2}|_{\overline{I}\cap(-r,r)}. The convexity, \nu-orthogonality and endpoint tangency for \widetilde{g}_{1},\widetilde{g}_{2} pass to their restrictions. This shows (i)(ia) (i)(ic) for F=Z_{t}.
Furthermore, from (3.9) (3.10) and Lemma 3.9 we find
(\widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}})\cap Q_{\delta}(r)=\Big\{x\in\widetilde{I}\setminus\overline{I},\ y=\widetilde{g}_{1}(x)=\widetilde{g}_{2}(x)=f(x)\Big\}.
Hence, (\widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}})\cap Q_{\delta}(r) is either empty or is a union of at most two nontrivial (open or half-open) line segments contained in \graphh(f). This also implies that each segment is orthogonal to \nu. Since \nu is continuous, this proves item (ii) by a covering argument.
Next, we prove (iii). Suppose there exists a \sigma as stated, so that \overline{\sigma}\cap\partial Z_{t}=\emptyset. This implies \overline{\sigma}\cap\overline{Z_{t}}=\emptyset. By (ii) we know that \sigma is nontrivial, and \sigma\subset\gamma_{j} for some ridge \gamma_{j}. We may assume \overline{\sigma}=[-l,l]\times\{0\} for some l>0, and \nu=-\partial_{y} on \sigma. Choose a small r>0 so that
|\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}\qquad\text{in}\ \ P:=(-l-r,l+r)\times(-r,r).
So there is a e^{-9}-Lipschitz function f:(-l-r,l+r)\to(-e^{-9}r,e^{-9}r) with \gamma_{j}\cap P=\graphh(f). Recall that \overline{\sigma}\cap\overline{Z_{t}}=\emptyset, and from the proof of (ii) above, that \widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}} is a discrete union of line segments in \cup\gamma_{j} (i.e. any compact set intersects finitely many segments). Hence, for some sufficiently small r, we have \widetilde{Z}_{t}\cap\partial P=\emptyset. Then it follows that, for some s<t close enough to t, it holds Y_{s}\cap P\Subset P.
Arguing similarly as in (i)(ia), there are functions \overline{g}_{1}:(-l-r,l+r)\to(-r,r) and \overline{g}_{2}:(-l-r,l+r), with \overline{g}_{1}\leqslant f\leqslant\overline{g}_{2}, so that
Y_{s}\cap P=\Big\{x\in(-l-r,l+r),\ \overline{g}_{1}(x)<y<\overline{g}_{2}(x)\Big\}.
Note that Y_{s}\cap P\neq\emptyset since \sigma\subset\widetilde{Z}_{t}\subset Y_{s}. Then, a connected component of \{\overline{g}_{1}<\overline{g}_{2}\} would take the form (c,d) with -l-r<c<d<l+r, contradicting Lemma 3.8.
Finally, we prove (iv). After a rotation, we may assume z=0 and \exists\,r\ll 1 so that |\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10} in Q(r). Let f:(-r,r)\to(-e^{-9}r,e^{-9}r) be such that \gamma_{j}\cap Q(r)=\graphh(f). Call w^{\prime}(x)=w(x,f(x)). By Item (i)(ia), for all z\in\gamma_{j}\cap Q(r) and all squares Q(z,s)\subset Q(r), we have Y_{t}\cap Q(z,s)\not\Subset Q(z,s) if the former is nonempty. Hence
\max_{I}(w^{\prime})=\max_{\partial I}(w^{\prime}),\qquad\forall\text{ interval }I\subset(-r,r),
or
w^{\prime}(x_{2})\leqslant\max\big\{w^{\prime}(x_{1}),w^{\prime}(x_{3})\big\},\qquad\forall\,-r<x_{1}<x_{2}<x_{3}<r.
(3.11)
It is then elementary to verify that: there can be at most one strict local minimum of w^{\prime} in (-r,r), and w is monotone in a neighborhood of each non strict local minimum.
∎
4 From IMCF clusters to \infty-harmonic functions
Mixed IMCF clusters can be used to produce \infty-harmonic functions:
Theorem 4.1.
Suppose \Omega is simply-connected, and (w,\nu) is a unit-calibrated mixed IMCF cluster in \Omega. Then there is an \infty-harmonic function u\in C^{1}(\Omega) so that \nabla u=e^{-w}\nu^{\perp}.
We start with collecting several known examples and describing their IMCF cluster counterparts. Then in Subsection 4.1, we construct all degree 3 entire solutions in \mathbb{R}^{2} (meaning that they are asymptotic to degree 3 quasiradial solutions at \infty). In all figures, orange curves are ridges and blue segments are valleys.
Example 4.2 (elementary examples).
(1)
The linear function u(z)=x is \infty-harmonic in \mathbb{R}^{2}. It corresponds to a constant weak IMCF with constant calibration.
(2)
The cone function u(z)=|z| is \infty-harmonic in \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}. It corresponds to the constant weak IMCF with calibration \nu=-z^{\perp}/|z|.
(3)
The angle function u(re^{i\theta})=\theta is \infty-harmonic in \mathbb{R}^{2} with any ray removed (or, it is an \infty-harmonic map from \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\} to \mathbb{S}^{1}). It corresponds to the smooth IMCF w=-\log|\nabla u|=\log|z| whose level sets are \gamma_{t}=\{|z|=e^{t}\}.
Example 4.3 (quasiradial solutions).
For each k=\frac{d^{2}}{2d-1}, d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 2}, there is a (unique up to scaling and rotation) \infty-harmonic function of the form
u(z)=r^{k}\phi(\theta),
(4.1)
where \phi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1}) satisfies \phi(\theta+\pi/d)=-\phi(\theta). We call it a degree d quasiradial solution. It was constructed by Aronsson in [12] (see also [13]) by converting the \infty-Laplacian into polar coordinates, and solving the corresponding second-order ODE [13, (6)]
(\phi^{\prime})^{2}\phi^{\prime\prime}+(2k-1)k\phi(\phi^{\prime})^{2}+(k-1)k^{3}\phi^{3}=0.
(4.2)
The generic solutions (see [12, Section 5]) are given by the implicit representation formula
\phi=\frac{C}{k}\Big(1-\frac{1}{k}\cos^{2}\omega\Big)^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\cos\omega,\qquad\theta=\theta_{0}+\int_{0}^{\omega}\frac{\sin^{2}x}{k-\cos^{2}x}\,dx,
(4.3)
and \phi(\theta) is smooth in (\theta_{0},\theta_{0}+\pi/d) and C^{1} in [\theta_{0},\theta_{0}+\pi/d], with
\phi(\theta_{0}+\pi/d)=-\phi(\theta_{0}),\qquad\phi^{\prime}(\theta_{0})=\phi^{\prime}(\theta_{0}+\pi/d)=0.
Here, C>0, \theta_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1} are generic parameters. Then, the solution is extended periodically to \mathbb{S}^{1}. For the case d=2, this coincides (up to scaling) with the well-known explicit solution
u=|x|^{4/3}-|y|^{4/3}.
(4.4)
Let us consider the IMCF counterparts (see also Figure 3). Let \{\overline{\gamma}_{t}\} be the shrinking hypocycloid supported in the sector D_{0}=\big\{\pi-\pi/d<\operatorname{Arg}(z)<\pi\big\}. See Example 2.11(i), where it is set \alpha=\pi/d. It has support function
h_{\overline{\gamma}_{t}}(\theta)=\exp\Big(\frac{1-2d}{(d-1)^{2}}t\Big)\sin\frac{-d\theta}{d-1},\qquad t\in\mathbb{R},\ \theta\in\Big(0,\frac{d-1}{d}\pi\Big).
(4.5)
Then, consider 2d identical copies with each rotated by angle \{-k\pi/d\}_{0\leqslant k<2d}. Set
\gamma_{t}=\bigcup_{0\leqslant k<2d}e^{-k\pi i/d}\overline{\gamma}_{t},\qquad D_{k}=e^{-k\pi i/d}D_{0},
and set the function
w|_{\gamma_{t}}=t,\qquad w(0)=+\infty,
and set the orientation and vector
\chi_{k}=(-1)^{k},\qquad\nu=\chi_{k}\,\frac{\nabla w}{|\nabla w|}\ \ \text{in}\ \ D_{k},\qquad\nu=(-1)^{k}\frac{z^{\perp}}{|z|}\ \ \text{in}\ \ -e^{-k\pi i/d}\,\mathbb{R}_{+},
for each k\in\{0,1,\cdots,2d-1\}. Then, these data form a mixed IMCF cluster in \mathbb{R}^{2}, with z=0 being an exceptional point, and \{D_{k}\} be the natural partition of \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}.
Let u be the resulting \infty-harmonic function by applying Theorem 4.1. Note that \nabla u=e^{-t}\nu^{\perp} on \gamma_{t}. The homogeneity of \{\gamma_{t}\} implies
\nabla u(\lambda z)=\lambda^{\frac{(d-1)^{2}}{2d-1}}\nabla u(z),\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in(0,\infty),
hence u is \frac{(d-1)^{2}}{2d-1}+1=\frac{d^{2}}{2d-1}-homogeneous, hence a degree d quasiradial solution.
Denote
h(t,\theta)=\exp\Big(\frac{1-2d}{(d-1)^{2}}t\Big)\sin\frac{-d\theta}{d-1},\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\big(2(d-1)\pi\big).
(4.6)
Let us show that the support function of \gamma_{t} is h(t,\cdot). By Example 2.11(i), this is true for the first copy of \overline{\gamma}_{t}, corresponding to \theta\in(0,\frac{d-1}{d}\pi). In the k-th copy of \gamma_{t}, considering the rotation and change of sign of the normal vector, the support function is given by
(-1)^{k}h\Big(t,\theta+\frac{k\pi}{d}\Big),\qquad\theta\in\Big(\!-\frac{k\pi}{d},\frac{d-1}{d}\pi-\frac{k\pi}{d}\Big).
Changing variable \theta^{\prime}=\theta+k\pi, this is the same as
(-1)^{k}h\Big(t,\theta^{\prime}-\frac{d-1}{d}k\pi\Big),\qquad\theta^{\prime}\in\Big(\frac{d-1}{d}k\pi,\frac{d-1}{d}(k+1)\pi\Big).
Noting that
h\Big(t,\theta^{\prime}-\frac{d-1}{d}k\pi\Big)=(-1)^{k}h(t,\theta^{\prime}),
this exactly fits as the k-th portion of (4.6), as desired.
Thus, by rotating and scaling, for each (a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}, there is a quasiradial solution u so that Y_{t}=\{|\nabla u|<e^{-t}\} has support function
h(t,\theta)=\exp\Big(\frac{1-2d}{(d-1)^{2}}t\Big)\Big[a\sin\frac{d\theta}{d-1}+b\cos\frac{d\theta}{d-1}\Big],
for t\in\mathbb{R} and \theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\big(2(d-1)\pi\big). By the recovery formula (2.11) and the fact that \partial Y_{t} are streamlines of u, we have the general gradient formula
\nabla u\Big(h(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}h(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}\Big)=e^{-t}\tau_{\theta}.
Note: for a non-integer d>1, the formula (4.3) still gives a \frac{d^{2}}{2d-1}-homogeneous \infty-harmonic function in an open sector with angle \pi/d, but this solution cannot be extended periodically to \mathbb{R}^{2}. For d\in\mathbb{Z}_{<0}, one may also construct a \frac{d^{2}}{2d-1}-homogeneous \infty-harmonic function in \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}, with z=0 being a nonremovable singularity. The corresponding IMCF cluster is made of 2|d| copies of the expanding soliton supported in a sector with angle \pi/|d|. We refer to [20, 21, 70] for general studies on isolated singularities.
Example 4.4.
There is an entire solution in \mathbb{R}^{2} of the form
u=e^{-x}\phi(y),
(4.7)
with \phi(y) a C^{1} periodic function that solves
(\phi^{\prime})^{2}\phi^{\prime\prime}+2\phi(\phi^{\prime})^{2}+\phi^{3}=0.
This solution corresponds to a simple IMCF cluster obtained by tessellating the translating cycloids. See Figure 12. Indeed, up to a scaling, the level sets \gamma_{t} satisfy \gamma_{t}=\gamma_{s}+(t-s,0). Hence
\nabla u(x+\lambda,y)=e^{-\lambda}\nabla u(x,y),
hence u is of the form (4.7).
Figure 12: Translating soliton and solution u=e^{-x}\phi(y).
Example 4.5 (\infty-harmonic potential in the square).
Let u be the unique continuous function in \overline{Q(1)}, so that u is \infty-harmonic in Q(1)\setminus\{0\}, and u(0)=1, u|_{\partial Q(1)}=0. See [23, 25, 53, 54] for related works. It is known that (see [54]) u is 1-Lipschitz in Q(1), and is linear with slope 1 on the x- and y-axes. Thus, u can be constructed from odd reflection by solving the following boundary value problem in Q=(0,2)\times(0,2):
\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{\infty}u=0\qquad\text{in}\ \ Q,\\
&\begin{aligned} &u(s,0)=1-s,\qquad u(s,2)=s-1,\\
&u(0,s)=1-s,\qquad u(2,s)=s-1,\end{aligned}\qquad\forall\,s\in[0,2].\end{aligned}\right.
(4.8)
To solve this boundary value problem, we view \partial Q as a degenerate concave 4-gon in the sense that adjacent edges do not meet tangentially. Consider the initial support function
h_{0}(\theta)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &0\qquad(0\leqslant\theta\leqslant\pi/2),\\
&\!-2\cos\theta\qquad(\pi/2\leqslant\theta\leqslant\pi),\\
&2\sin\theta-2\cos\theta\qquad(\pi\leqslant\theta\leqslant 3\pi/2),\\
&2\sin\theta\qquad(3\pi/2\leqslant\theta\leqslant 2\pi).\end{aligned}\right.
Then let h(t,\theta) solve \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h on [0,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}, with initial value h_{0}. Using similar argument as in Subsection 4.1 below, it may be shown that the profile curves of h generate a mixed IMCF cluster in Q, so that (1,1) is an exceptional point and \{y=x\}, \{y=2-x\} are ridges. See Figure 13: the x- and y-axes are valleys, and \{x=\pm y\} are ridges.
Figure 13: \infty-harmonic potential in Q(1), and its odd reflection.
We now prove Theorem 4.1. The following is a useful criterion for showing \infty-harmonicity. Recall that a streamline is an ascending gradient flow line.
Lemma 4.6.
Suppose u\in C^{1}(\Omega), and z\in\Omega, and \nabla u(z)\neq 0. Suppose \gamma:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\Omega is a streamline of u parametrized by arclength, with \gamma(0)=z.
(i)
If t\mapsto u(\gamma(t)) is convex for t\in[0,\varepsilon), then u is \infty-subharmonic at z.
(ii)
If t\mapsto u(\gamma(t)) is concave for t\in(-\varepsilon,0], then u is \infty-superharmonic at z.
Proof.
We may assume z=0, u(0)=0 and \nabla u(0)=\partial_{x}. Let \varphi\in C^{2} be a test function that touches u at 0; hence \varphi(0)=0 and \nabla\varphi(0)=\partial_{x}. We may Taylor expand \varphi:
\varphi(x,y)=x+ax^{2}+bxy+cy^{2}+o\big(x^{2}+y^{2}\big).
Note that \nabla^{2}\varphi(\nabla\varphi,\nabla\varphi)(0)=2a. We need to show that if \varphi touches u from above as in (i), then a\geqslant 0, and if \varphi touches u from below as in (ii), then a\leqslant 0.
Let \gamma be the given streamline. Then our conditions imply u(\gamma(t))\geqslant t for t\geqslant 0 in (i), or u(\gamma(t))\leqslant t for t\leqslant 0 in (ii). Since \gamma is a C^{1} curve, there is a continuous function \theta:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\mathbb{R}, with \theta(0)=0, so that \gamma^{\prime}(t)=e^{i\theta(t)}. Then
\gamma(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\big(\!\cos\theta(s),\sin\theta(s)\big)\,ds=\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\cos\theta(s)\,ds,\,o(t)\Big).
The Taylor expansion of \varphi gives
\displaystyle\varphi(\gamma(t))
\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\cos\theta(s)\,ds+at^{2}+o(t^{2}),\quad\text{which is}\quad\left\{\begin{aligned} &\leqslant t+at^{2}+o(t^{2})\quad(t\geqslant 0),\\
&\geqslant t+at^{2}+o(t^{2})\quad(t\leqslant 0).\end{aligned}\right.
If \varphi touches from above, then t+at^{2}+o(t^{2})\geqslant\varphi(\gamma(t))\geqslant u(\gamma(t))\geqslant t for all t\geqslant 0, hence a\geqslant 0. If \varphi touches from below, then t+at^{2}+o(t^{2})\leqslant\varphi(\gamma(t))\leqslant u(\gamma(t))\leqslant t for all t\leqslant 0, hence a\leqslant 0, as desired.
∎
Proof of Theorem 4.1.
Let \{z_{i}\}, \{\sigma_{j}\} be as in Definition 3.4. Let \sigma^{\prime}_{j} be the interior of the segment \sigma_{j}. Denote
A=\{z_{i}\}\cup(\cup\sigma_{j}),\qquad\Omega^{\prime}=\Omega\setminus A,\qquad\Omega^{\prime\prime}=\Omega^{\prime}\cup(\cup\sigma^{\prime}_{j}).
Note that \Omega\setminus\Omega^{\prime\prime} is a discrete set, by Definition 3.4(iii). Recall that (w,\nu) fits into a simple cluster in \Omega^{\prime}. Let \{D_{i}\} be any compatible choice of partition of \Omega^{\prime}, with \{\gamma_{j}\subset\Omega^{\prime}\} be the corresponding set of ridges and \{\chi_{i}\} the set of orientations.
By Remark 3.3, we have \curl(e^{-w}\nu^{\perp})=\div(e^{-w}\nu)=0 distributionally in \Omega^{\prime}. Then since e^{-w}\nu^{\perp} is continuous near each \sigma_{j}, we have \curl(e^{-w}\nu^{\perp})=0 distributionally in \Omega^{\prime\prime}. Finally, since \Omega\setminus\Omega^{\prime\prime} is discrete, we have \curl(e^{-w}\nu^{\perp})=0 distributionally in the entire \Omega. By the simply-connectedness of \Omega, there is u\in C^{1}(\Omega) so that \nabla u=e^{-w}\nu^{\perp}.
It remains to verify that u is \infty-harmonic at every point z\in\Omega.
Case 1: z\in D_{i} for some i. Recall that w is a weak IMCF in D_{i} and is calibrated by the unit vector field \chi_{i}\nu. If z\in\partial\{w<t\} or z\in\partial\{w\leqslant t\} for some t, then \partial\{w<t\} or \partial\{w\leqslant t\} is a streamline of u on which u is linear (under the length parametrization). Then we may apply Lemma 4.6 as desired. If z\in\Int\big(\{w=t\}\big) for some t, then \nu^{\perp} gives a line foliation near z (see Remark 2.9(v)), and each line is a streamline of u on which u is linear. The result follows as well.
Case 2: z\in\sigma^{\prime}_{j} for an exceptional curve \sigma_{j}. Then \sigma^{\prime}_{j} is a streamline of u on which u is linear, by Definition 3.4(ii), and the result follows easily from Lemma 4.6.
Case 3: z lies on a ridge \gamma, and w|_{\gamma} is monotone in a neighborhood of z. Then after a rigid motion and scaling and orientation reversing, we may assume
•
\Omega^{\prime}\supset Q(1), z=0, u(0)=0, w(0)=0, \nu(0)=-\partial_{y} (so \nabla u(0)=\partial_{x}),
•
|\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10} and |w|<e^{-10} in Q(1),
•
\gamma\cap Q(1)=\graphh(f) for a e^{-9}-Lipschitz function f:(-1,1)\to(-e^{-9},e^{-9}),
•
w(x,f(x)) is nondecreasing in x for x\in(-1,1),
•
Q(1)=D_{1}\cup\gamma\cup D_{2}, where D_{1} lies below \gamma, and \chi_{1}=-1, \chi_{2}=1.
Parametrize \gamma by arclength, so that \gamma(0)=0 and \gamma^{\prime}(t)=\nu^{\perp}(\gamma(t)). Notice that \frac{d}{dt}u(\gamma(t))=\langle\nabla u(\gamma(t))\,,\,\gamma^{\prime}(t)\rangle=e^{-w(\gamma(t))} is non-increasing in t. Hence u(\gamma(t)) is concave in t for -\frac{1}{2}\leqslant t\leqslant\frac{1}{2}, and by Lemma 4.6(ii), it follows that u is \infty-superharmonic at 0.
Next, we show that u is \infty-subharmonic at 0. Suppose there exists \varphi\in C^{2}(Q(1)) that touches u from above at 0. Then \varphi(0)=0, \nabla\varphi(0)=\partial_{x}. Since u\leqslant\varphi\in C^{2}, there is a constant C>1 so that
u(0,y)\leqslant Cy^{2}\qquad\forall\,y\in(-1,1).
(4.9)
We claim that w(x,0)=0 for all x\in[-\frac{1}{8C},0]. Once this is shown, then |\partial_{x}u(x,0)|\leqslant e^{-w(x,0)}=1, hence \varphi(x,0)\geqslant u(x,0)\geqslant x for all x\in[-\frac{1}{8C},0], hence \partial_{x}^{2}\varphi(0)\geqslant 0, as desired.
It remains to prove our claim. For each x\in[-1,1], note that w(x,y) is nondecreasing in y when y\leqslant f(x), and is nonincreasing in y when y\geqslant f(x). In addition, we have assumed that w|_{\gamma} is nondecreasing in x. Hence, w(x,0)\leqslant 0 for all x\leqslant 0. If our claim is not true, then there exists t<0 and x_{0}\in(-1/8C,0) so that [x_{0},0]\times\{0\}\subset\overline{Y_{t}} while (x_{0},0)\in\partial Y_{t}. By Lemma 3.7(i)(ia) and the monotonicity of w|_{\gamma\cap Q(1)}, there is a subset I=(b,1) with b\leqslant x_{0} and functions g_{1},g_{2}:[b,1)\to\mathbb{R}, so that
Y_{t}\cap Q(1)=\Big\{x\in I,\ g_{1}(x)<y<g_{2}(x)\Big\}.
So either g_{1}(x_{0})=0 or g_{2}(x_{0})=0. We may assume g_{1}(x_{0})=0 (same argument if g_{2}(x_{0})=0). So g_{1} is a concave e^{-9}-Lipschitz function on [b,1). Denote \sigma=\graphh(g_{1}|_{[x_{0},0]}) and y_{0}=g_{1}(0). Notice that |y_{0}|\leqslant|x_{0}|. Recall \div(e^{-w}\nu)=0, and observe that
\langle\partial_{x}\,,\,e^{-w}\nu\rangle=\langle\partial_{y}\,,\,e^{-w}\nu^{\perp}\rangle=\partial_{y}u.
The divergence theorem and (4.9) and w\geqslant t on [x_{0},0]\times\{0\} gives
\displaystyle 0
\displaystyle=\int_{\sigma}\langle-(\sigma^{\prime})^{\perp}\,,\,e^{-w}\nu\rangle+\int_{[x_{0},0]\times\{0\}}\langle\partial_{y}\,,\,e^{-w}\nu\rangle+\int_{\{0\}\times[0,y_{0}]}\langle\partial_{x}\,,\,e^{-w}\nu\rangle
\displaystyle=e^{-t}|\sigma|+\int_{[x_{0},0]\times\{0\}}\langle\partial_{y}\,,\,e^{-w}\nu\rangle+\big[u(0,0)-u(0,y_{0})\big]
\displaystyle\geqslant e^{-t}\sqrt{x_{0}^{2}+y_{0}^{2}}-e^{-t}|x_{0}|-Cy_{0}^{2}\geqslant\frac{e^{-t}y_{0}^{2}}{2\sqrt{x_{0}^{2}+y_{0}^{2}}}-Cy_{0}^{2}\geqslant\Big(\frac{1}{4|x_{0}|}-C\Big)y_{0}^{2}.
Hence y_{0}=0, and then 0\in\partial Y_{t}, then t=w(0)=0, contradicting our hypothesis t<0.
Case 4: z lies on a ridge \gamma, and w|_{\gamma} is not monotone near z. By Lemma 3.7(iv), these points are discrete in \Omega^{\prime}. Then Lemma 2.7 implies that u is \infty-harmonic at z as well.
In summary, we have shown that u is \infty-harmonic at all points in \Omega^{\prime} and in all \sigma^{\prime}_{j}. The remaining set of points is discrete in \Omega, hence Lemma 2.7 applies to show that u is \infty-harmonic in \Omega.
∎
4.1 Degree 3 entire solutions
For each a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{R} with (a,b)\neq(0,0) and (c,d)\neq(0,0), we construct a mixed IMCF cluster in \mathbb{R}^{2} whose level set \gamma_{t} for all t\ll-1 has support function
h(t,\theta)=e^{-5t/4}\Big(a\sin\frac{3}{2}\theta+b\cos\frac{3}{2}\theta\Big)+e^{3t/4}\Big(c\sin\frac{\theta}{2}+d\cos\frac{\theta}{2}\Big),\quad\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}(4\pi).
(4.10)
The e^{-5t/4} terms dominate as t\to-\infty, thus the resulting \infty-harmonic function is asymptotic to a degree 3 quasiradial solution at \infty. It also has two critical points at which it is asymptotic to degree 2 quasiradial solutions (see the proof of Theorem 10.1(ii) and 12.1).
As was mentioned in the introduction, the support function (4.10) is intended as a perturbation of the quasiradial model by adding lower order terms. A generic perturbation should take the form
h(t,\theta)=\sum_{j=0}^{3}\exp\Big(t-\frac{j^{2}}{4}t\Big)\Big(a_{j}\sin\frac{j\theta}{2}+b_{j}\cos\frac{j\theta}{2}\Big),\qquad\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}(4\pi).
To see how this is reduced to (4.10): first, noting that h\mapsto h+a\sin\theta-b\cos\theta corresponds to translations, we may assume that the j=2 term vanishes. We claim that the constant term must also vanish. By (2.14), the quantity \int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}(4\pi)}h(t,\theta)\,d\theta is the alternating length of \gamma_{t}: namely, denoting by \alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{6} the edges of \gamma_{t}, we have
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}(4\pi)}h(t,\theta)\,d\theta=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}|\alpha_{i}|.
However, each \alpha_{i} is a streamline of the resulting \infty-harmonic function u, with |\nabla u|=e^{-t} there. Noting that \int_{\gamma_{t}}du=0 since du is exact, and that du has alternating orientations on \alpha_{i}, we obtain \sum(-1)^{i}|\alpha_{i}|=0, hence \int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}(4\pi)}h=0, hence a_{0}=b_{0}=0.
Now, let h be as in (4.10). Then (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\cdot) has 6 roots for t\ll-1, and \gamma_{t} has 6 vertices. The solution cannot be constructed by running (4.10) for all times, since otherwise \gamma_{t} would stop being embedded at a certain t. To produce an IMCF cluster, we split the 6-gon into two 4-gons at an appropriate time by cutting along a line segment (see Figure 14).
Figure 14: Splitting of 6-gon into two 4-gons.
Then we evolve each resulting 4-gon by IMCF with cusps. Let T be the splitting time, and \theta_{0} be the angle of direction of the dividing segment. This segment clearly must be tangent to \gamma_{T} at both endpoints. Translating to the language of h, this means
h(T,\theta_{0})=h(T,\theta_{0}+2\pi).
Also, each edge of \gamma_{t} is a streamline of u on which |\nabla u|=e^{-t}. By the same alternating length reasoning as above, for each t>T we must have
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}h(t,\theta)\,d\theta=0.
Letting t\searrow T, we obtain the second constraint
\int_{\theta_{0}}^{\theta_{0}+2\pi}h(T,\theta)\,d\theta=0.
(4.11)
In the proof, we will see that there exists a unique pair (T,\theta_{0}) with these properties. It is possibly surprising that the alternating length argument only serves to inspire (4.10) and (4.11) in our initial discussion. It does not appear in the actual construction below.
Formal construction.
Let h be as in (4.10), with (a,b)\neq(0,0) and (c,d)\neq(0,0). Observe \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h. It is helpful to see that
h(t,\theta+2\pi)=-h(t,\theta).
(4.12)
Let T\in\mathbb{R} and \theta_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}(4\pi) be determined by
h(T,\theta_{0})=h(T,\theta_{0}+2\pi)=0,\qquad\int_{\theta_{0}}^{\theta_{0}+2\pi}h(T,y)\,dy=0.
(4.13)
Such (T,\theta_{0}) exists uniquely up to \theta_{0}\leftrightarrow\theta_{0}+2\pi: indeed, consider
H(t,\theta)=\int_{\theta}^{\theta+2\pi}h(t,y)\,dy.
So (4.13) is equivalent to H(T,\theta_{0})=\partial_{\theta}H(T,\theta_{0})=0, namely, (T,\theta_{0}) is a degenerate root of H. Note that H(t,\cdot) has 2 roots for t\gg 1 and has 6 roots for t\ll-1, due to the specific form of h given by (4.10). By the Sturmian theory, the number of roots decrease by at least 2 whenever a degenerate root appears. By (4.12), degenerate roots always come in pair (t,\theta)\leftrightarrow(t,\theta+2\pi). So H has a unique degenerate root (modulo \theta_{0}\leftrightarrow\theta_{0}+2\pi) in \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}(4\pi), as desired.
Let \mathbb{S}^{1}_{1} and \mathbb{S}^{1}_{2} denote two identical copies of \mathbb{S}^{1}. Identify \mathbb{S}^{1}_{1} as [\theta_{0},\theta_{0}+2\pi] with the endpoints glued, then identify \mathbb{S}^{1}_{2} as [\theta_{0}+2\pi,\theta_{0}+4\pi] with endpoints glued. Denote
M_{0}=(-\infty,T]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}(4\pi),\qquad M_{1}=[T,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}_{1},\qquad M_{2}=[T,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}_{2}.
Let M be the topological manifold obtained by gluing M_{0} with M_{1}, M_{2} in the natural way. The inclusion M_{0}\to M is not injective since (T,\theta_{0}) and (T,\theta_{0}+2\pi) are identified. However, h(T,\theta_{0})=h(T,\theta_{0}+2\pi)=0 ensures that h is a continuous function on M\cap\{t\leqslant T\}. Then, we evolve h by \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h individually in M_{1},M_{2} from the initial data h|_{t=T}. For notational clarity, we denote
h_{0}=h|_{M_{0}},\qquad h_{1}=h|_{M_{1}},\qquad h_{2}=h|_{M_{2}}.
The second condition of (4.13) is preserved in \mathbb{S}^{1}_{1} and \mathbb{S}^{1}_{2} respectively, hence
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}_{1}}h_{1}(t,\cdot)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}_{2}}h_{2}(t,\cdot)=0,\qquad\forall\,t\geqslant T.
(4.14)
As t\to\infty, the function h has Fourier expansions in M_{1},M_{2} of the form
\displaystyle h_{1}=x_{1}\sin\theta-y_{1}\cos\theta+O\big(e^{-3t}\big)\qquad\text{in}\ \ M_{1},
(4.15)
\displaystyle h_{2}=x_{2}\sin\theta-y_{2}\cos\theta+O\big(e^{-3t}\big)\qquad\text{in}\ \ M_{2},
for some x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}. Denote z_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1}), z_{2}=(x_{2},y_{2}). Due to the odd symmetry of h, we have z_{1}=-z_{2}. It is shown later that z_{1},z_{2}\neq 0. Then denote
\{z_{3},z_{4}\}=\big\{\!\pm\partial_{\theta}h_{0}(T,\theta_{0})\tau_{\theta_{0}}\big\},
and let \sigma be the closed line segment connecting z_{3},z_{4}. We set z_{3}=z_{4}=0 and \sigma=\{0\} if \partial_{\theta}h_{0}(T,\theta_{0})=0. Finally, define the function
F(t,\theta)=h(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}h(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}
(4.16)
on \mathring{M}:=M\setminus\{(T,\theta_{0})\}, where we recall
\nu_{\theta}=(\sin\theta,-\cos\theta),\qquad\tau_{\theta}=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta).
(4.17)
Note: h,\nu_{\theta},\tau_{\theta} are continuous in M, while \partial_{\theta}h is only continuous in \mathring{M}.
Lemma 4.7.
Let M,\mathring{M},h,F be defined as above. Then z_{1},z_{2}\neq 0 and z_{1},z_{2}\notin\sigma, and F is a homeomorphism from \mathring{M} to \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\big(\{z_{1},z_{2}\}\cup\sigma\big). Define the function and vector
w(F(t,\theta))=t,\qquad\nu(F(t,\theta))=\nu_{\theta},
and define w(z_{1})=w(z_{2})=+\infty, w|_{\sigma}=T, \nu|_{\sigma}=\nu_{\theta_{0}}. Then (w,\nu) is a unit-calibrated mixed IMCF cluster in \mathbb{R}^{2}, with exceptional points and segments given by \{z_{1},z_{2},\sigma\}. Consequently, the function u given by \nabla u=e^{-w}\nu^{\perp} is \infty-harmonic in \mathbb{R}^{2}.
Figure 15, 16 and 17 respectively describe the solutions with data
(a,b,c,d)=(1,0,1,0),\qquad(a,b,c,d)=(1,0,-1,0)
and
a=1,\quad b=0,\quad c=\frac{1}{3}\Big(\!\sin\frac{3\pi}{14}-2\cos\frac{\pi}{7}\Big),\quad d=-\frac{4}{3}\cos^{2}\frac{\pi}{14}\sin\frac{\pi}{7}.
In Figure 15, the segment \sigma degenerates to a point. The parameters for Figure 17 are chosen so that T=0 and \theta_{0}=\pi/7. Blue segments in the figures are valleys, while red curves are ridges.
Figure 15: The solution with data (a,b,c,d)=(1,0,1,0).
Figure 16: The solution with data (a,b,c,d)=(1,0,-1,0).
Figure 17: The solution so that T=0 and \theta_{0}=\pi/7.
Proof of Lemma 4.7.
For a point z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}, we know from (4.16) (4.17) that F(t,\theta)=z is equivalent to
h(t,\theta)=x\sin\theta-y\cos\theta,\qquad\partial_{\theta}h(t,\theta)=x\cos\theta+y\sin\theta,
hence equivalent to
(t,\theta)\text{\ \ is a degenerate root of\ \ }g_{z}:=h-x\sin\theta+y\cos\theta.
Step 1. Injectivity and image of F. Let z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2} be given. We first collect some information about the function g_{z}=h-x\sin\theta+y\cos\theta. Observe that g_{z}(t,\cdot) has 6 nondegenerate roots for all t\ll-1, and for large positive time we have:
•
g_{z}(t,\cdot) has 2 roots in \mathbb{S}^{1}_{1} for all t\gg 1 if z\neq z_{1}, by the asymptotics (4.15);
•
g_{z_{1}}(t,\cdot) has at least 4 roots in \mathbb{S}^{1}_{1} for all t>T, by the Sturm-Hurwitz theorem and the fact \int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}g_{z_{1}}(t,\theta)\,d\theta=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}g_{z_{1}}(t,\theta)\sin\theta\,d\theta=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}g_{z_{1}}(t,\theta)\cos\theta\,d\theta=0.
Similar fact holds for \mathbb{S}^{1}_{2}. Hence, g_{z} has 4 roots for all t\gg 1 if z\notin\{z_{1},z_{2}\}, and has \geqslant 6 roots for all t\gg 1 if z=z_{1}\neq z_{2} or z=z_{2}\neq z_{1}, and has \geqslant 8 roots if z=z_{1}=z_{2}.
The following lemma keeps track of the change of roots at the saddle point:
Lemma 4.8.
Suppose (T,\theta_{0}) is a root of g_{z}. For \mu,\delta>0, denote
\displaystyle P_{0}
\displaystyle=[T-\mu,T]\times\Big([\theta_{0}-\delta,\theta_{0}+\delta]\cup[\theta_{0}+2\pi-\delta,\theta_{0}+2\pi+\delta]\Big)\subset M_{0},
\displaystyle P_{1}
\displaystyle=[T,T+\mu]\times[\theta_{0}-\delta,\theta_{0}+\delta]\subset M_{1},
\displaystyle P_{2}
\displaystyle=[T,T+\mu]\times[\theta_{0}+2\pi-\delta,\theta_{0}+2\pi+\delta]\subset M_{2}.
Call P=P_{0}\cup P_{1}\cup P_{2}. Let P_{0,t} be the t-slice of P_{0}, similarly for P_{1,t}, P_{2,t}. Denote
\displaystyle\partial_{\text{side}}P
\displaystyle=\Big([T-\mu,T]\times\{\theta_{0}\pm\delta,\theta_{0}+2\pi\pm\delta\}\Big)
(4.18)
\displaystyle\cup\Big([T,T+\mu]\times\{\theta_{0}\pm\delta\}\Big)\cup\Big([T,T+\mu]\times\{\theta_{0}+2\pi\pm\delta\}\Big)
For any \varepsilon>0, there are \mu,\delta<\varepsilon so that g_{z} has no root in \partial_{\text{side}}P, and the following hold:
(i)
If |z|<|\partial_{\theta}h(T,\theta_{0})|, then g_{z} has two nondegenerate roots in P_{0,t} for each t\in[T-\mu,T], and g_{z} has no root in P_{1,t},P_{2,t} for all t\in(T,T+\mu].
(ii)
If |z|>|\partial_{\theta}h(T,\theta_{0})|, then g_{z} has two nondegenerate roots in P_{0,t} for all t\in[T-\mu,T], and has one nondegenerate root in P_{1,t},P_{2,t} respectively for each t\in(T,T+\mu].
(iii)
If |z|=|\partial_{\theta}h(T,\theta_{0})|, then g_{z} has n\geqslant 3 nondegenerate roots in P_{0,t} for all t\in[T-\mu,T), and has n^{\prime}\leqslant n-2 nondegenerate roots in P_{1,t}\cup P_{2,t} for all t\in(T,T+\mu].
The proof is postponed later. Combined with the standard Sturmian theory, it follows that the number of roots of g_{z}(t,\cdot) is non-increasing in t, and whenever a degenerate root in \mathring{M} or type (i)(iii) root at (T,\theta_{0}) appears, the number of roots drop by at least 2.
Using this, it quickly follows that z_{1}\neq z_{2}, hence z_{1},z_{2}\neq 0 (since z_{2}=-z_{1}). If z\in\{z_{1},z_{2}\}, then g_{z}(t,\cdot) has 6 roots for all t\ll-1 and in total \geqslant 6 roots for all t\gg 1. Therefore, g_{z} has 6 roots for all t\in\mathbb{R}, and hence no degenerate roots can appear in \mathring{M}. This shows z_{1},z_{2}\notin F(\mathring{M}). If z\in\sigma, then z=c\partial_{\theta}h(T,\theta_{0})\tau_{\theta_{0}} for some c\in[-1,1], hence
g_{z}(T,\theta_{0})=h(T,\theta_{0})-\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta_{0}}\rangle=0,
hence (T,\theta_{0}) is a root of g_{z}. Note that |z|\leqslant|\partial_{\theta}h_{0}(T,\theta_{0})|, thus (T,\theta_{0}) is a type (i)(iii) root as in Lemma 4.8, and the number of roots drop by at least 2 at (T,\theta_{0}). This forces g_{z} to have 4 roots for t\gg 1, and the saddle point (T,\theta_{0}) occupies the unique decrease of number of roots. Hence g_{z} has no degenerate root in \mathring{M}. We conclude that \sigma\cap F(\mathring{M})=\emptyset and z_{1},z_{2}\notin\sigma. Denoting
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\big(\{z_{1},z_{2}\}\cup\sigma\big),
we have shown that F(\mathring{M})\subset\Omega.
If z\notin\{z_{1},z_{2}\}\cup\sigma, then g_{z} has 6 roots for all t\ll-1 and 4 roots for all t\gg 1, and (T,\theta_{0}) either is not a root or is a type (ii) root of g_{z}. Therefore, there must be exactly one degenerate root of g_{z} in \mathring{M}. This shows that F maps \mathring{M} bijectively to \Omega.
Step 2. Continuity of F^{-1},w,\nu. By the invariance of domain, note that F:\mathring{M}\to\Omega is a homeomorphism. Then, (w,\nu) is continuous in \Omega since (w,\nu)=(\id,\theta\mapsto\nu_{\theta})\circ F^{-1}.
Assume z\in\sigma. Then (T,\theta_{0}) is a type (i)(iii) root of g_{z} as in Lemma 4.8, by Step 1 above. For any given \varepsilon, we use Lemma 4.8 to obtain a “box” P=P_{0}\cup P_{1}\cup P_{2}, so that g_{z} has no roots in \partial_{\text{side}}P, and has n\geqslant 2 nondegenerate roots in \partial P\cap\{t=T-\mu\}, and has n^{\prime}\leqslant n-2 nondegenerate roots in \partial P\cap\{t=T+\mu\}. By continuity, g_{z^{\prime}} have the same pattern of roots in \partial P for all |z^{\prime}-z|<\rho, for some \rho=\rho(\varepsilon). So the number of roots of g_{z^{\prime}} goes through a decrease in P, which shows that there is either a degenerate root in P\cap\mathring{M} or a type (i)(iii) root at (T,\theta_{0}). In the former case, if the degenerate root is (t^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}), then w(z^{\prime})=t^{\prime} and \nu(z^{\prime})=\nu_{\theta^{\prime}} and hence
|w(z^{\prime})-w(z)|=|t^{\prime}-T|<\varepsilon,\qquad|\nu(z^{\prime})-\nu(z)|\leqslant|\theta^{\prime}-\theta_{0}|<\varepsilon.
In the latter case, we have z^{\prime}\in\sigma hence w(z^{\prime})=w(z), \nu(z^{\prime})=\nu(z) by construction. In either case, this shows the continuity of w,\nu at z.
Assume z=z_{1} (same argument for z=z_{2}). Let \varepsilon>0 be given. Recall that g_{z_{1}}(1/\varepsilon,\cdot) has 4 nondegenerate roots in \mathbb{S}^{1}_{1} (Sturm-Hurwitz implies that it has \geqslant 4, but it can only have 4 roots due to the global behavior of roots). By continuity, there is \rho>0 so that g_{z^{\prime}}(1/\varepsilon,\cdot) also has 4 nondegenerate roots in \mathbb{S}^{1}_{1}, for all z^{\prime}\in B(z_{1},\rho)\setminus\{z_{1}\}. It follows that the unique degenerate root of g_{z^{\prime}} must occur in \mathbb{S}^{1}_{1}\cap\{t>1/\varepsilon\} for all z^{\prime}\in B(z_{1},\rho)\setminus\{z_{1}\}. This shows w(z^{\prime})>1/\varepsilon for all z^{\prime}\in B(z,\rho). Hence w is continuous at z_{1}.
To summarize, we have shown that w is a continuous function from \mathbb{R}^{2} to \mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}, and \nu is continuous in \{w<\infty\}.
Step 3. Finally, we show that (w,\nu) produces a simple IMCF cluster in \Omega. Once this is done, Definition 3.4 implies that (w,\nu) is a mixed cluster in \mathbb{R}^{2} (unit calibration is obvious by our construction). Denote
\kappa^{-1}=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h=\partial_{t}h\qquad\text{in}\ \ \mathring{M}.
Here we view \kappa^{-1} only as a notation. We first show that all roots of \kappa^{-1} in \mathring{M} are simple. Note that \kappa^{-1} has 6 roots for t\ll-1, and by the Sturm-Hurwitz theorem and the fact
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(\partial_{\theta\theta}f+f)\sin\theta=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(\partial_{\theta\theta}f+f)\cos\theta=0,\qquad\forall\,f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1}),
\kappa^{-1} has \geqslant 8 roots for t\gg 1. The change of roots at the saddle point is as follows:
Lemma 4.9.
Adopt the notations in Lemma 4.8. Then there exists \mu,\delta>0 so that \kappa^{-1} has no roots in \partial_{\text{side}}P, and has n\geqslant 0 roots in P_{0,t} for each t\in[T-\mu,T), and has n^{\prime}\leqslant n+2 roots in P_{1,t}\cup P_{2,t} for each t\in(T,T+\mu].
We postpone the proof later. Since \partial_{t}\kappa^{-1}=\partial_{\theta\theta}\kappa^{-1}+\kappa^{-1}, the Sturmian principle and this lemma imply that \kappa^{-1} cannot have degenerate roots in \mathring{M}, as desired.
Let \Gamma=\big\{(t,\theta)\in\mathring{M}:\kappa^{-1}(t,\theta)=0\big\}. By the nondegeneracy of roots, \Gamma is a disjoint union of smooth curves \{\Gamma_{j}\} in \mathring{M}, with each \Gamma_{j} of the form \{\theta=g(t)\}. The implicit function theorem gives
g^{\prime}(t)=-\frac{\partial_{t}\kappa^{-1}(t,g(t))}{\partial_{\theta}\kappa^{-1}(t,g(t))}\qquad\text{on}\ \ \Gamma_{j}.
The image of \Gamma_{j} under F is given by
\gamma_{j}(t)=h(t,g(t))\nu_{g(t)}+\partial_{\theta}h(t,g(t))\tau_{g(t)}.
Its velocity vector is
\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}_{j}(t)
\displaystyle=\partial_{t}h(t,g(t))\nu_{g(t)}+\partial_{\theta}h(t,g(t))g^{\prime}(t)\nu_{g(t)}+h(t,g(t))g^{\prime}(t)\tau_{g(t)}
\displaystyle+\partial_{t\theta}h(t,g(t))\tau_{g(t)}+\partial_{\theta\theta}h(t,g(t))g^{\prime}(t)\tau_{g(t)}-\partial_{\theta}h(t,g(t))g^{\prime}(t)\nu_{g(t)}
\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}\kappa^{-1}(t,g(t))\cdot\tau_{g(t)}\neq 0.
Hence, each \gamma_{j} is an embedded C^{1} curve in \Omega, with \gamma^{\prime}_{j}\perp\nu (recall \nu(F(t,\theta))=\nu_{\theta}\perp\tau_{\theta}).
Let \{V_{i}\} be the connected components of \mathring{M}\setminus\bigcup\Gamma_{j}, and let D_{i}=F(V_{i}). Set \chi_{i}=1 if \kappa^{-1}>0 in V_{i}, and \chi_{i}=-1 otherwise. Definition 3.1(i)(ii)(iii) and (3.1) are readily verified by our construction, and it remains to verify the ridge condition (3.2), namely \chi_{i}\nu=\nu_{D_{i}} on each \partial D_{i}.
Consider any point z\in\partial D_{i}\cap\partial D_{j}. Let (t_{1},\theta_{1})\in\mathring{M} be the preimage of z. By symmetry, it suffices to consider the case t_{1}=\theta_{1}=0. So (0,0)\in\Gamma_{k} for some k. Let \varepsilon be small enough so that \{0\}\times(-\varepsilon,0)\subset V_{i} and \{0\}\times(0,\varepsilon)\subset V_{j}. Consider the Taylor expansion of h at \theta=0, in which we used (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(0,0)=0:
h(0,\theta)=a+b\theta-\frac{1}{2}a\theta^{2}+c\theta^{3}+O(\theta^{4}),\qquad\partial_{\theta}h(0,\theta)=b-a\theta+3c\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3}).
Then
\displaystyle F(0,\theta)
\displaystyle=h(0,\theta)(\sin\theta,-\cos\theta)+\partial_{\theta}h(0,\theta)(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)
(4.19)
\displaystyle=\Big(b+\frac{1}{2}\big(b+6c\big)\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3}),\ -a+\frac{1}{3}\big(b+6c\big)\theta^{3}+O(\theta^{4})\Big).
Observe that
b+6c=\partial_{\theta}\kappa^{-1}(0,0)\left\{\begin{aligned} &>0\qquad\text{if $\chi_{i}=-1$, $\chi_{j}=1$,}\\
&<0\qquad\text{if $\chi_{i}=1$, $\chi_{j}=-1$.}\end{aligned}\right.
(4.20)
Denote \gamma_{\pm}=F\big(\{0\}\times\pm(0,\varepsilon)\big). If \chi_{i}=-1 and \chi_{j}=1, then (4.19) (4.20) imply that \gamma_{-} is the graph of y=-a-C(x-b)^{3/2}+o(|x-b|^{3/2}) for 0\leqslant x-b\ll 1 and \gamma_{+} is the graph of y=-a+C(x-b)^{3/2}+o(|x-b|^{3/2}) for 0\leqslant x-b\ll 1, where
C=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3\sqrt{b+6c}}>0.
As \gamma_{-}\in D_{i} and \gamma_{+}\in D_{j}, this shows \nu_{D_{i}}(z)=\partial_{y} and \nu_{D_{j}}(z)=-\partial_{y}. Then recalling \nu(z)=\nu(F(0,0))=\nu_{0}=-\partial_{y}, it follows that \nu_{D_{i}}=\chi_{i}\nu and \nu_{D_{j}}=\chi_{j}\nu at z. For \chi_{i}=1 and \chi_{j}=-1 one may argue in the same manner. This completes the construction.
∎
Proof of Lemma 4.8.
By a rotation in \mathbb{R}^{2}, we may assume \theta_{0}=0. Recall
z=(x,y),\qquad g_{z}=h-x\sin\theta+y\cos\theta.
Note h(T,0)=h(T,2\pi)=0 by construction. As (T,0) is a root of g_{z}, we have (x\sin\theta-y\cos\theta)|_{\theta=0}=0, thus y=0, |z|=|x|. Notice that g_{z}(T,2\pi)=0 as well. (Note: h is odd and x\sin\theta-y\cos\theta is even under the symmetry \theta\to\theta+2\pi.) Let us summarize
h(T,0)=h(T,2\pi)=g_{z}(T,0)=g_{z}(T,2\pi)=0,\qquad y=0,\qquad|z|=|x|.
(4.21)
We further denote
P_{0}^{-}=[T-\mu,T]\times[-\delta,\delta],\qquad P_{0}^{+}=[T-\mu,T]\times[2\pi-\delta,2\pi+\delta].
So P=P_{0}^{-}\cup P_{0}^{+}\cup P_{1}\cup P_{2}. Then denote g_{z,0}=g_{z}|_{P_{0}}, g_{z,1}=g_{z}|_{P_{1}}, g_{z,2}=g_{z}|_{P_{2}}. The local gluing pattern for P is shown in Figure 18 (edges with the same color are glued).
Figure 18: Coordinates in P.
Since solutions of heat equation have isolated roots, we may choose \delta<\varepsilon so that
g_{z,0}\neq 0\text{\ \ everywhere in\ \ }\Big\{t=T,\,\theta\in\big([-\delta,\delta]\cup[2\pi-\delta,2\pi+\delta]\big)\setminus\{0,2\pi\}\Big\}.
(4.22)
By continuity, there is \mu<\varepsilon so that g_{z} has no roots in \partial_{\text{side}}P. Here, \varepsilon is the small constant given in Lemma 4.8. The standard maximum principle (see Subsection 2.4) implies that g_{z,1} has n_{1}\in\{0,1\} nondegenerate roots in P_{1,t} for all t\in(T,T+\mu], with n_{1}=1 if g_{z,1}(T,\pm\delta) has the opposite sign, and n_{1}=0 otherwise. Similar fact holds for g_{z,2}.
Expand g_{z,0} as Taylor polynomial near \theta=0,2\pi using (4.21) and the symmetry of h:
\displaystyle g_{z,0}(T,\theta)
\displaystyle=(a-x)\theta+b\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3}),
(4.23)
\displaystyle g_{z,0}(T,\theta+2\pi)
\displaystyle=(-a-x)\theta-b\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3}),
(4.24)
where a=\partial_{\theta}h(T,0)=-\partial_{\theta}h(T,2\pi) and b=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta\theta}h(T,0)=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\theta\theta}h(T,2\pi). Then g_{z,1}, g_{z,2} have the following expansions at (T,0) respectively:
g_{z,1}(T,\theta)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &(a-x)\theta+b\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3})\qquad(\theta\geqslant 0),\\
&(-a-x)\theta-b\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3})\qquad(\theta\leqslant 0).\end{aligned}\right.,
(4.25)
and
g_{z,2}(T,\theta)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &(-a-x)\theta-b\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3})\qquad(\theta\geqslant 0),\\
&(a-x)\theta+b\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3})\qquad(\theta\leqslant 0).\end{aligned}\right..
(4.26)
The no-sign-change condition (4.22) implies that the signs of g_{z,1}(T,\pm\delta) and g_{z,2}(T,\pm\delta) coincide with the signs of the leading terms in (4.25) (4.26).
If |a|>|x|, then (4.25) (4.26) implies that g_{z,1}(T,\pm\delta) have the same sign, and g_{z,2}(T,\pm\delta) have the same sign. So g_{z} has no root in P_{1},P_{2} when t>T. If |a|<|x|, then (4.25) (4.26) implies that g_{z,1}(T,\pm\delta) have the opposite signs, and g_{z,2}(T,\pm\delta) also have opposite signs, so g_{z,1},g_{z,2} have respectively one root in P_{1},P_{2} when t>T. Also, \theta=0,2\pi are nondegenerate roots of g_{z,0} if |a|\neq|x|. These imply Lemma 4.8(i)(ii).
If |a|=|x|\neq 0, then g_{z,0} has a degenerate root at \theta=0 (if a=x) or at \theta=2\pi (if a=-x), due to (4.23) (4.24). Therefore, g_{z} has at least 3 roots in P_{0,t} for t\in[T-\mu,T). If b>0, then:
•
If a=x>0, then (4.25) gives (for 0<\theta\ll 1)
g_{z,1}(T,\theta)=b\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3})>0,\qquad g_{z,1}(T,-\theta)=2a\theta+O(\theta^{2})>0.
•
If a=-x<0, then (4.25) gives
g_{z,1}(T,\theta)=2a\theta+O(\theta^{2})<0,\qquad g_{z,1}(T,-\theta)=-b\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3})<0.
•
If a=x<0, then (4.26) gives
g_{z,2}(T,\theta)=-2a\theta+O(\theta^{2})>0,\qquad g_{z,2}(T,-\theta)=b\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3})>0.
•
If a=-x>0, then (4.26) gives
g_{z,2}(T,\theta)=-b\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{3})<0,\qquad g_{z,2}(T,-\theta)=-2a\theta+O(\theta^{2})<0.
In either case, g_{z,1}(T,\pm\delta) or g_{z,2}(T,\pm\delta) have the same sign. Hence g_{z} has at most one root in P_{1,t}\cup P_{2,t} for all t\in(T,T+\mu]. The case b<0 is analogous. If b=0, then one of the roots \theta=0,2\pi has degree \geqslant 3, so g_{z,0} has at least 4 roots in P_{0} for t<T. Finally, if a=x=0, then both roots of g_{z,0} are degenerate, so g_{z} has at least 4 roots in P_{0} when t<T. So in either case, the number of roots for t>T decreases by at least 2 compared to t<T. This proves Lemma 4.8(iii).
∎
Proof of Lemma 4.9.
We may assume \theta_{0}=0. Similar to the proof of Lemma 4.8, we may choose \mu,\delta so that \partial_{\text{side}}P contains no roots of \kappa^{-1}, and
\kappa^{-1}\neq 0\text{\ \ everywhere in\ \ }\Big\{t=T,\,\theta\in\big([-\delta,\delta]\cup[2\pi-\delta,2\pi+\delta]\big)\setminus\{0,2\pi\}\Big\}.
Denote \kappa^{-1}_{0}=\kappa^{-1}|_{P_{0}}, \kappa^{-1}_{1}=\kappa^{-1}|_{P_{1}}, \kappa^{-1}_{2}=\kappa^{-1}|_{P_{2}}. Notice that \kappa^{-1}_{0}(t,\theta+2\pi)=-\kappa^{-1}_{0}(t,\theta). Recall h(T,0)=0. Denote a=\partial_{\theta}h(T,0) and b=\kappa_{0}^{-1}(T,0). Then the evolution of \kappa^{-1}_{1}, \kappa^{-1}_{2} in P_{1},P_{2} have the initial data (see Figure 18 for the coordinate change in gluing)
\kappa^{-1}_{1}(T,\theta)=2a\delta_{0}+\left\{\begin{aligned} b+O(\theta)\qquad\theta>0,\\
-b+O(\theta)\qquad\theta<0,\end{aligned}\right.
(4.27)
and
\kappa^{-1}_{2}(T,\theta)=-2a\delta_{0}+\left\{\begin{aligned} -b+O(\theta)\qquad\theta>0,\\
b+O(\theta)\qquad\theta<0,\end{aligned}\right.
(4.28)
where \delta_{0} is the Dirac delta at \theta=0.
If b\neq 0, then \kappa^{-1}_{1}, \kappa^{-1}_{2} has 1 root in P_{1,t}, P_{2,t} respectively for all t\in(T,T+\mu]. If b=0, then \theta=0,2\pi are roots of \kappa_{0}^{-1}(T,\cdot), thus \kappa^{-1}_{0} has at least 2 roots in P_{0,t} for all t\in[T-\mu,T); moreover, by (4.27) (4.28) we know that \kappa_{1}^{-1},\kappa_{2}^{-1} has at most 2 roots in P_{1,t},P_{2,t} respectively for all t\in(T,T+\mu]. In either case, the number of roots at most increases by 2, as desired.
∎
5 C^{1} equicontinuity of p-harmonic functions
In this section we prove the following:
Theorem 5.1.
The family
\Big\{v\in\Lip_{\loc}(B(4)):\Delta_{p}v=0,\ p\in[3,\infty],\ \|v\|_{L^{\infty}}\leqslant 1\Big\}
is C^{1} equibounded and equicontinuous in B(1).
A major consequence is that the p-harmonic approximation, initially set up in C^{0}_{\loc} in [22, 47, 56], can automatically be upgraded to C^{1}_{\loc}:
Theorem 5.2.
Suppose u is \infty-harmonic in a domain \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}, and u_{i} is a sequence of p_{i}-harmonic functions with p_{i}\to\infty and u_{i}\to u in C^{0}_{\loc}(\Omega). Then in fact
u_{i}\to u\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{1}_{\loc}(\Omega).
The following consequence is also useful:
Corollary 5.3.
Suppose u is \infty-harmonic in \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}, and K\Subset\Omega, and u has no critical points in K. Then \inf_{K}|\nabla u|=\inf_{\partial K}|\nabla u|.
The convergence |\nabla u_{i}|\to|\nabla u| was previously shown by Lindgren-Lindqvist [54, Section 3] in the context of \infty-harmonic potentials in planar convex rings. Theorem 5.1 answers a question raised in [54]. More recently, Peng-Zhang-Zhou [66] proved the full convergence \nabla u_{p}\to\nabla u for \infty-harmonic potentials in convex rings in all dimensions.
The proof of Theorem 5.1 combines the following ingredients:
•
integral estimates by Koch-Zhang-Zhou [48];
•
pointwise flatness estimates by Evans-Smart [39] (with a technical improvement);
•
the convergence arguments in [48, Theorem 1.4] and [54, Theorem 7];
•
a new observation on the presence of critical points (see Case 2 in the proof below).
We need to adapt the estimates in [39, 48] to the context of p-harmonic functions (since they were originally carried out for exponentially harmonic functions). We provide details in Appendix A for the reader’s convenience; see also the appendix of [54].
Proof of Theorem 5.1.
Let us prove that the family of solutions of the equation
\div\Big(\big(\varepsilon^{2}+|\nabla v|^{2}\big)^{p/2-1}\nabla v\Big)=0,\qquad\text{where}\ p\in[3,\infty),\ \varepsilon\in(0,1],
(5.1)
with |v|\leqslant 1 in B(4), is C^{1}-equicontinuous in B(1). Notice that the equation (5.1) is invariant under v(z)\mapsto\lambda v(\lambda^{-1}z), but not invariant under v(z)\mapsto\lambda v(z) if \varepsilon\neq 0.
The following preliminary estimates are useful; see Appendix A for the proofs.
Lemma 5.4.
There is a universal constant C_{1} so that the following holds.
(i)
If v either solves (5.1) or is p-harmonic (3\leqslant p\leqslant\infty) in a ball B(z,4r), then
\sup_{B(z,3.5r)}|\nabla v|\leqslant C_{1}r^{-1}\sup_{B(z,4r)}|v|.
(5.2)
(ii)
If v solves (5.1) in B(z,4r), then
\int_{B(z,3r)}\big|\nabla|\nabla v|^{2}\big|^{2}\leqslant C_{1}r^{-2}\int_{B(z,3.5r)}\big(\varepsilon^{2}+|\nabla v|^{2}\big)^{2}.
(5.3)
(iii)
If v solves (5.1) in B(z,4r), and P is an affine function, then
\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B(z,r)}\big(|\nabla v|^{2}-\langle\nabla P\,,\,\nabla v\rangle\big)^{2}
\displaystyle\leqslant C_{1}\Big(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B(z,2r)}\big(\varepsilon^{2}+|\nabla v|^{2}\big)^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
(5.4)
\displaystyle\quad\times\Big(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B(z,2r)}\frac{(v-P)^{4}}{r^{4}}+\frac{(v-P)^{2}}{r^{2}}\big(|\nabla P|+|\nabla v|\big)^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.
(iv)
If 0<\lambda\leqslant a, and v solves (5.1) and satisfies |v-ay|\leqslant\lambda r in B(4r), then
|\nabla v|^{2}\leqslant a\partial_{y}v+C_{1}\big(\varepsilon p^{-1/2}+a\big)^{3/2}\lambda^{1/2}\qquad\text{in}\ \ B(r).
(5.5)
Item (i) implies that the C^{0}-bounded family of solutions of (5.1), along with the family of p-harmonic functions (3\leqslant p\leqslant\infty), is equicontinuous and C^{1}-equibounded in B(1).
Let us show that the C^{1} equicontinuity for (5.1) implies the main theorem: if Theorem 5.1 is false, then there exists a \delta>0, a sequence of p_{i}-harmonic functions v_{i}, and points z_{i},z^{\prime}_{i}\in B(1), so that |z_{i}-z^{\prime}_{i}|\to 0 but |\nabla v_{i}(z_{i})-\nabla v_{i}(z^{\prime}_{i})|\geqslant\delta. Since \infty-harmonic functions are uniformly C^{1} [69, Theorem 3], we may remove finitely many terms and assume p_{i}<\infty for all i. It is well-known that p-harmonic functions can be C^{1,\alpha(p)} approximated by solutions of (5.1) (see e.g. [51]). Hence, for each i there exists an \varepsilon_{i}\in(0,1] and a solution \widetilde{v}_{i} of (5.1), so that \|\widetilde{v}_{i}-v_{i}\|_{C^{1}(B(1))}\leqslant\delta/4. Then we have |\nabla\widetilde{v}_{i}(z_{i})-\nabla\widetilde{v}_{i}(z^{\prime}_{i})|\geqslant\delta/2, contradicting the hypothesized C^{1} equicontinuity for (5.1).
The rest of the proof is thus devoted to the C^{1}-equicontinuity for solutions of (5.1). Since solutions of (5.1) are smooth, all the computations will be in the classical sense.
We prove by contradiction: suppose that there is a sequence of solutions v_{i} of (5.1), with p=p_{i}\in[3,\infty) and \varepsilon=\varepsilon_{i}\in(0,1], with \|v_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(B(4))}\leqslant 1, and there are points z_{i},z^{\prime}_{i}\in B(1), with |z_{i}-z^{\prime}_{i}|\to 0, but |\nabla v_{i}(z_{i})-\nabla v_{i}(z^{\prime}_{i})|\geqslant\delta for some \delta>0. By the interior C^{1,\alpha(p)} estimate for (5.1), see for example [51, 52], we must have p_{i}\to\infty.
Passing to a subsequence, we may assume z_{i},z^{\prime}_{i}\to z_{\infty}\in\overline{B(1)}, and v_{i}\to u in C^{0}_{\loc}(B(4)) for some u. We replace each v_{i} by the new function z\mapsto 2v_{i}(\frac{1}{2}(z+z_{\infty}))-2u(z_{\infty}). Then, to summarize, we have a sequence v_{i} of solutions to (5.1) in B(4), with
\|v_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(B(4))}\leqslant 4,\qquad z_{i},z^{\prime}_{i}\to 0,\qquad|\nabla v_{i}(z_{i})-\nabla v_{i}(z^{\prime}_{i})|\geqslant\delta,
(5.6)
and
v_{i}\xrightarrow{C^{0}_{\loc}(B(4))}u,\qquad\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(B(4))}\leqslant 4,\qquad u(0)=0.
(5.7)
Following the argument in [47, Theorem 1.22], we find that u is \infty-harmonic in B(4) (this is true even if \varepsilon_{i} does not converge to 0). Recall that planar \infty-harmonic functions are C^{1}: so there is a function \mu:[0,1/2]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}, with \lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\mu(r)=0, such that
\sup_{z\in B(r)}\big|u(z)-\langle z\,,\,\nabla u(0)\rangle\big|\leqslant r\mu(r),\qquad\sup_{z\in B(r)}\big|\nabla u(z)-\nabla u(0)\big|\leqslant\mu(r),
(5.8)
for all r\leqslant 1/2.
Using (5.2) (5.3) and passing to a subsequence, we may assume that |\nabla v_{i}|^{2} converges in L^{2}(B(3)) to some function f. It is shown in [48] that f=|\nabla u|^{2} a.e. in B(3). We recall the proof for the reader’s convenience. Let z_{0}\in B(3) be a Lebesgue point of f. Since u\in C^{1}, for all \lambda<1/100 there is a small r\in(0,1/8), such that
\sup_{z\in B(z_{0},4r)}\big|u(z)-u(z_{0})-\langle\nabla u(z_{0})\,,\,z-z_{0}\rangle\big|\leqslant\lambda r.
Hence, for all sufficiently large i (depending on \lambda,r), it holds
