\sup_{z\in B(z_{0},4r)}\big|v_{i}(z)-v_{i}(z_{0})-\langle\nabla u(z_{0})\,,\,z-z_{0}\rangle\big|\leqslant 2\lambda r.
Using the integral flatness estimate (5.4), for sufficiently large i it follows that
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B(z_{0},r)}\big(|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}-\langle\nabla u(z_{0})\,,\,\nabla v_{i}\rangle\big)^{2}\leqslant C\lambda,
where C is a universal constant. Take i\to\infty of this inequality. Since |\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\to f in L^{2} and \nabla v_{i}\rightharpoonup\nabla u weakly in L^{2}, we obtain by lower semi-continuity
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B(z_{0},r)}\big(f-\langle\nabla u(z_{0})\,,\,\nabla u\rangle\big)^{2}\leqslant C\lambda.
Finally, taking \lambda\to 0 and r\to 0, we obtain f(z_{0})=|\nabla u(z_{0})|^{2} as desired. To summarize, we have obtained
|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\xrightarrow{L^{2}(B(3))}|\nabla u|^{2}.
(5.9)
We are ready to derive a contradiction.
Case 1: \nabla u(0)=0. Choose r\ll 1 so that C_{1}\mu(r)<\delta/40. Notice that |u|\leqslant r\mu(r) in B(r), hence |v_{i}|\leqslant 2r\mu(r) in B(r) for all large enough i. Then the gradient estimate (5.2) implies
|\nabla v_{i}|\leqslant 8C_{1}\mu(r)<\delta/5\qquad\text{in}\ \ B(r/4),
for all large i, which contradicts our hypothesis (5.6).
Case 2: \nabla u(0)\neq 0. With a rotation in \mathbb{R}^{2}, we may assume \nabla u(0)=a\partial_{y}, a>0. Choose a small r so that
\left\{\begin{aligned} &8\mu(4r)\leqslant\frac{a}{64C_{1}^{2}},\\
&a^{2}-\big(a-2\mu(r)\big)^{2}+4C_{1}a^{3/2}\big(8\mu(4r)\big)^{1/2}<(\delta/5)^{2}.\end{aligned}\right.\quad
(5.10)
Since u\in C^{1}, we may further decrease r so that
|\nabla u|^{2}\geqslant\frac{7}{8}a^{2}\qquad\text{in}\ \ B(4r).
(5.11)
We fix our choice of r. By (5.8) and v_{i}\to u in C^{0}_{\loc}(B(4)), for all large enough i we have
\operatorname{sup}_{B(4r)}|v_{i}-ay|\leqslant 8\mu(4r)\cdot r.
(5.12)
By (5.9), there is a sequence \eta_{i}\searrow 0 so that \int_{B(3)}\big||\nabla v_{i}|^{2}-|\nabla u|^{2}\big|^{2}\leqslant\eta_{i}^{2} for all i. Denote
F_{i}=\big|\nabla|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\big|^{2}+\eta_{i}^{-1}\big||\nabla v_{i}|^{2}-|\nabla u|^{2}\big|^{2}.
Disintegrating (5.3) (5.9) on circles and using Fatou’s lemma, we have
\displaystyle\int_{r/3}^{2r/3}\Big(\liminf_{i\to\infty}\int_{\partial B(s)}F_{i}\Big)\,ds\leqslant\liminf_{i\to\infty}\Big(\int_{r/3}^{2r/3}\int_{\partial B(s)}F_{i}\Big)\leqslant\liminf_{i\to\infty}\int_{B(2)}F_{i}<\infty.
Hence, there exists s\in[r/3,2r/3], a constant C, and a subsequence, such that
\int_{\partial B(s)}\big|\nabla|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\big|^{2}\leqslant C\ \ \forall\,i,\qquad\int_{\partial B(s)}\big||\nabla v_{i}|^{2}-|\nabla u|^{2}\big|^{2}\to 0\ \ \text{as}\ i\to\infty.
This implies
|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\xrightarrow{C^{0}(\partial B(s))}|\nabla u|^{2}.
Then by (5.11), it holds |\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\geqslant\frac{3}{4}a^{2} on \partial B(s) for all large i. Using the flatness estimate (5.5) in B(r), together with the conditions (5.12) (5.10), we obtain for all large i
\partial_{y}v_{i}\geqslant a^{-1}\Big(\frac{3}{4}a^{2}-C_{1}\cdot 2a^{3/2}\cdot\big(8\mu(4r)\big)^{1/2}\Big)\geqslant\frac{a}{2}\qquad\text{on}\ \ \partial B(s).
It is well-known that the map z\mapsto\big(\partial_{x}v_{i}(z),-\partial_{y}v_{i}(z)\big) is quasiregular [58, Proof of Theorem 1]. The maximum principle for quasiregular maps then implies \partial_{y}v_{i}\geqslant a/2 in B(s), so v_{i} have no critical points in B(s) (hence in B(r/3) as well), for all large i.
By the maximum principle for quasiregular maps, this implies that
\max_{L}|\nabla v_{i}|=\max_{\partial L}|\nabla v_{i}|,\qquad\min_{L}|\nabla v_{i}|=\min_{\partial L}|\nabla v_{i}|,\qquad\forall\,\text{compact }L\subset B(r/3).
By Lebesgue’s lemma, see [54, p.10], for all balls B(z,\rho)\subset B(z,r/6)\subset B(r/3) we have
\Big(\!\osc_{B(z,\rho)}|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\Big)^{2}\cdot\log\frac{r/6}{\rho}\leqslant\pi\int_{B(z,r/6)}\big|\nabla|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\big|^{2}\leqslant C,
where C is a universal constant. Hence, the functions |\nabla v_{i}|^{2} are equicontinuous in B(r/6). Together with (5.9), we have |\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\to|\nabla u|^{2} in C^{0}(B(r/12)).
Recall by our condition (5.8) that |\nabla u-a\partial_{y}|\leqslant\mu(r) in B(r/12). Hence, for all large enough i it holds
\big(a-2\mu(r)\big)^{2}\leqslant|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}\leqslant\big(a+2\mu(r)\big)^{2}\qquad\text{in}\ \ B(r/12).
Moreover, the flatness estimate (5.5) and conditions (5.12) (5.10) imply
a\partial_{y}v_{i}\geqslant|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}-2C_{1}a^{3/2}\big(8\mu(4r)\big)^{1/2}\qquad\text{in}\ \ B(r/12),
for all large i. Hence, we obtain
\displaystyle|\nabla v_{i}-a\partial_{y}|^{2}
\displaystyle=|\nabla v_{i}|^{2}+a^{2}-2a\partial_{y}v_{i}
\displaystyle\leqslant a^{2}-\big(a-2\mu(r)\big)^{2}+4C_{1}a^{3/2}\big(8\mu(4r)\big)^{1/2}
in B(r/12), for all large i. By our assumption (5.10), this implies
|\nabla v_{i}-a\partial_{y}|\leqslant\delta/5\qquad\text{in}\ \ B(r/12),
for all large i, which contradicts (5.6).
∎
Proof of Theorem 5.2.
It is generally known that if u_{i}\to u in C^{0}_{\loc}(\Omega) and u_{i} is locally C^{1}-equibounded and equicontinuous in \Omega, then u_{i}\to u in C^{1}_{\loc}(\Omega).
∎
Proof of Corollary 5.3.
We may assume that u has no critical points in \overline{K}. Hence there is U\Supset K and c>0 so that
|\nabla u|\geqslant c\qquad\text{in}\ \ \overline{U}.
By Lemma 2.2 and Theorem 5.2, we may consider a sequence of p-harmonic functions u_{p} that C^{1}-converges to u (as p\to\infty) in a neighborhood of \overline{U}. Hence |\nabla u_{p}|\geqslant c/2 in \overline{U} for all large enough p. Recall that z\mapsto\frac{\partial u_{p}}{\partial z} is a quasiregular map. Hence, the map
z\mapsto\frac{1}{\partial u_{p}/\partial z}
is also quasiregular in U. Then the maximum principle implies
\min_{\overline{K}}|\nabla u_{p}|=\min_{\partial K}|\nabla u_{p}|.
Taking p\to\infty, the result follows.
∎
6 Limit of p-q duality and simple clusters
Let u be an \infty-harmonic function in a simply-connected domain \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}. Denote
\Omega_{0}=\Omega\setminus\Crit(u).
Let z_{0}\in\Omega be a basepoint, and u_{i} be any sequence of p_{i}-harmonic functions, so that p_{i}\to\infty and u_{i}\to u in C^{0}_{\loc}(\Omega). For the ease of notation, let us suppress the index i and write “u_{p} are p-harmonic functions so that p\to\infty and u_{p}\to u”. By Theorem 5.2, we in fact have u_{p}\to u in C^{1}_{\loc}(\Omega). Let v_{p} be the conjugate q-harmonic function (where q=\frac{p}{p-1}), defined by
\nabla v_{p}=|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\nabla^{\perp}u_{p},\qquad v_{p}(z_{0})=0.
(6.1)
It is easy to verify that |\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\nabla^{\perp}u_{p} is indeed a gradient, and v_{p} is indeed q-harmonic (see also [15]). Following the idea of Moser [63], we wish to take the renormalization
w_{p}=(1-q)\log v_{p}=-\frac{\log v_{p}}{p-1},
and take a subsequential limit as p\to\infty. The limit is a weak IMCF whenever it is defined, as was seen in [61, 62] (see also (2.33) – (2.34)).
A major “issue” is that v_{p} need not have a definite sign in \Omega (in fact, v_{p} must change sign since we have intentionally set v_{p}(z_{0})=0). The actual limiting process goes roughly as follows. We first take a C^{1}_{\loc} subsequential limit of \{v_{p}=0\}. These curves correspond to \{\gamma_{i}\} in the main theorem below. The curves divide \Omega into regions. In each region D, we individually take the renormalized limit
w_{p}=-\frac{\log|v_{p}|}{p-1},\qquad w=\lim_{p\to\infty}w_{p}\ \ \text{in}\ \ C^{0}_{\loc}(D).
(6.2)
Finally, the limit solutions glue continuously across the interfaces, thus giving a piecewise IMCF. It will be shown that the interfaces are ridges. Namely, we obtain a simple IMCF cluster (Definition 3.1). Note that the limit (6.2) is taken away from \partial D: the Dirichlet condition v_{p}=0 \leftrightarrow w_{p}=+\infty on \{v_{p}=0\} does not pass to the p\to\infty limit. The function w is bounded (in fact, is equal to -\log|\nabla u|) on all ridges \gamma_{i}.
Another layer of complexity arises from the presence of critical points: the set \{v_{p}=0\} may not behave nicely in \Crit(u). We will instead take a subsequential limit of \{v_{p}=0\}\cap\Omega_{0} in C^{1}_{\loc}(\Omega_{0}), and use (the closure of) the union of limit curves to divide \Omega into regions. There are two types of regions: the regular ones, in which (6.2) works well, and the degenerate ones, in which w_{p}\to+\infty. The degenerate regions must all lie inside \Crit(u), and the simple cluster will be objects in the regular regions.
Let us call w the resulting simple cluster. The natural question is the relation between w and the original \infty-harmonic function u. The key ingredient to connecting them is the following observation (inspired by similar estimates in [63], see Lemma 6.13). Suppose \sigma is a C^{1} path joining z_{0} and some z\in\Omega. Then
v_{p}(z)=v_{p}(z)-v_{p}(z_{0})=\int_{\sigma}\langle\nabla v_{p}\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle=\int_{\sigma}|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\langle\nabla^{\perp}u_{p}\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle.
If \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle is uniformly positive or negative on \sigma, then the 1/(p-1)-th power of this integral is roughly the L^{p} norm of |\nabla u_{p}| on \sigma, hence converges to the L^{\infty} norm as p\to\infty. So we have
\lim_{p\to\infty}|v_{p}(z)|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}=\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|,
which in view of (6.2) gives
w(z)=-\log\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|.
Hence w(z)=-\log|\nabla u|(z) whenever there exists a \sigma for which |\nabla u|(z)=\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u| and \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle has a definite sign. This condition is highly restrictive (so w\neq-\log|\nabla u| in general). However, see items (iii), (vi) \sim (ix) and (xii) below, there are several important cases where w=-\log|\nabla u|.
The resulting cluster w is generally nonunique. It depends on the choice of basepoint z_{0} and also on the selected subsequence. By Theorem 6.1(viii), the set \{w\neq-\log|\nabla u|\} is made of jumps in the weak IMCFs. Therefore, w may be viewed as a reduction of u by showing partial information and erasing the remaining and converting into jumps. The full data on u may be recovered by considering the approximations at all basepoints z_{0}. To prove the integral regularity conjectures in (1.29), one perhaps needs to investigate how the different IMCF clusters are patched together.
Theorem 6.1.
Let u be \infty-harmonic in a simply-connected domain \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}. Let z_{0}\in\Omega, and u_{p} be a sequence of p-harmonic functions, with p\to\infty and u_{p}\to u in C^{0}_{\loc}(\Omega). Define v_{p} by
\nabla v_{p}=|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\nabla^{\perp}u_{p},\qquad v_{p}(z_{0})=0.
(6.3)
Set \Omega_{0}=\Omega\setminus\Crit(u). Then there is a continuous function w:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\} and a countable disjoint collection of embedded C^{1} curves \{\gamma_{i}\subset\Omega_{0}\}, so that the following hold.
(i)
Each \gamma_{i} is a streamline of u, and has no endpoint in \Omega_{0}. Each compact set in \Omega_{0} intersects finitely many \gamma_{i}.
(ii)
Let \mathcal{D} be the set of connected components of \Omega\setminus\overline{\cup\gamma_{i}}. Denote
\mathcal{D}_{\Crit}=\Big\{D\in\mathcal{D}:D\subset\Crit(u)\Big\},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{\reg}=\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{\Crit}.
Then by passing to a subsequence of p, it holds:
(a)
If D\in\mathcal{D}_{\Crit}, then w\equiv+\infty in D and |v_{p}|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\to 0 in C^{0}_{\loc}(D).
(b)
If D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}, then w<+\infty everywhere in D, and
-\frac{\log|v_{p}|}{p-1}\xrightarrow{p\to\infty}w\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{0}_{\loc}(D).
(6.4)
(iii)
w\leqslant-\log|\nabla u| everywhere in \Omega;
(iv)
\{w<+\infty\} coincides with the set
\Omega_{\reg}:=\Big(\bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}}D\Big)\cup\big(\!\cup\gamma_{i}\big).
(6.5)
We have \Omega_{\reg}\supset\Omega_{0}, i.e. \Omega\setminus\Omega_{\reg}\subset\Crit(u). Each D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg} is a C^{1} domain in \Omega_{\reg}.
(v)
Set \nu=-e^{w}\nabla^{\perp}u. For each D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}, set \chi_{D} to be the (well-defined) sign of v_{p}(z_{D}) for all large enough p, for any fixed z_{D}\in D. Then the data
\big(w,\nu,\mathcal{D}_{\reg},\{\chi_{D}\}_{D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}}\big)
is a simple IMCF cluster in \Omega_{\reg}.
Furthermore, w has the following properties:
(vi)
w=-\log|\nabla u| at the basepoint z_{0};
(vii)
w=-\log|\nabla u| on all \gamma_{i};
(viii)
w=-\log|\nabla u| on the set
\{w\neq\text{const}\}:=\Big\{z\in\Omega:\text{ $w$ is non-constant in all neighborhoods of $z$}\Big\}.
Consequently, w=-\log|\nabla u| on \partial\{w<t\}\cap\Omega and \partial\{w\leqslant t\}\cap\Omega, \forall\,t\in\mathbb{R}.
(ix)
Suppose z_{1}\in\cup\gamma_{i} or z_{1}=z_{0}, and \sigma:[0,l]\to\Omega is a C^{1} curve joining z_{1} and another point z_{2}\in\Omega. Then
w(z_{2})\geqslant-\log\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|.
(6.6)
Denote
M_{\sigma}=\Big\{t\in[0,l]:|\nabla u|(\sigma(t))=\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|\Big\}.
If there is \chi\in\{\pm 1\} so that \chi\langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle>0 on M_{\sigma}, then z_{2} lies in some D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}, and
w(z_{2})=-\log\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|,\qquad\chi=\chi_{D}.
(6.7)
(x)
If z_{0}\notin\Crit(u), then z_{0}\in\cup\gamma_{i}.
(xi)
If z_{0}\in\Int(\Crit(u)), then z_{0}\in D for some D\in\mathcal{D}_{\Crit}.
Regarding the sublevel sets of |\nabla u|, we have:
(xii)
Denote
\displaystyle Y_{t}=\big\{|\nabla u|<e^{-t}\big\},\qquad\widetilde{Z}_{t}=\{|\nabla u|\leqslant e^{-t}\},\qquad Z_{t}=\Int(\widetilde{Z}_{t}),
(6.8)
\displaystyle Y^{\prime}_{t}=\{w>t\},\qquad\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}=\{w\geqslant t\},\qquad Z^{\prime}_{t}=\Int(\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}).
Let F and F^{\prime} be the connected component of Y_{t} and Y^{\prime}_{t} (or Z_{t} and Z^{\prime}_{t}, or \widetilde{Z}_{t} and \widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}) that contains z_{0}, respectively. Then F=F^{\prime}.
Remark 6.2.
The following facts are obvious but may help clarify the statements.
(i)
The basepoint z_{0} may lie inside or outside \Crit(u).
(ii)
When \Crit(u)=\emptyset, we have \Omega=\Omega_{\reg} hence obtain a simple IMCF cluster in \Omega. In this case, the proof of Theorem 6.1 can be significantly shortened.
(iii)
A regular region may contain critical points of u, and if this occurs, then the critical points must lie in \Int\big(\{w=t\}\big) for some t\in\mathbb{R}. This follows from item (viii).
(iv)
Regarding (xii): since w\leqslant-\log|\nabla u|, we clearly have Y_{t}\supset Y^{\prime}_{t}, \widetilde{Z}_{t}\supset\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t} and Z_{t}\supset Z^{\prime}_{t}. Note that F,F^{\prime} might be empty.
(v)
Eventually, Theorem 11.1 implies that \Int(\Crit(u))=\emptyset, hence \mathcal{D}_{\Crit}=\emptyset. However, Theorem 6.1 is a step towards proving Theorem 11.1, and the proof seems not substantially simplifiable even assuming a priori \Int(\Crit(u))=\emptyset.
It is useful to investigate Theorem 6.1 in the following “\varepsilon-regularity” case which appears in Section 8. Denote Q_{\delta}(r)=(-r,r)\times(-\delta r,\delta r). Suppose \delta\leqslant 1, and u is \infty-harmonic in Q_{\delta}(4), in which
\Big|\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}-\partial_{x}\Big|<e^{-10}\delta.
(6.9)
In particular, \Crit(u)=\emptyset. Consider z_{0}\in Q_{\delta}(3). Notice that
\Big|\frac{\nabla v_{p}}{|\nabla v_{p}|}-\partial_{y}\Big|<e^{-10}\delta\qquad\text{in}\ \ Q_{\delta}(4-\varepsilon),\qquad\forall\,p\geqslant p(\varepsilon).
Hence \{v_{p}=0\}\cap Q_{\delta}(4-\varepsilon) consists of one almost horizontal curve passing through z_{0}, for all large p. As \{\gamma_{i}\} arises from taking subsequential limits of \{v_{p}=0\}\setminus\Crit(u), in the present scenario there is only one almost horizontal ridge, which we name by \gamma (recall that ridges are streamlines of u, hence almost horizontal, by (6.9)). Let f:(-4,4)\to(-4\delta,4\delta) be a e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitz function so that \gamma=\graphh(f). So \gamma divides Q_{\delta}(4) into two regions
D_{1}=\{y<f(x)\},\qquad D_{2}=\{y>f(x)\}.
As \nabla v_{p}=|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\nabla^{\perp}u_{p} points upward, the choice of sign in Theorem 6.1(v) gives \chi_{1}=-1, \chi_{2}=1, and \nu=-e^{w}\nabla^{\perp}u points downward. Hence, the resulting simple cluster in (v) contains an upward moving IMCF in D_{1} and a downward moving IMCF in D_{2}.
Item (ix) gives a precise formula for w as follows. For each z=(x,y)\in Q_{\delta}(4), consider the vertical line segment \sigma connecting z to (x,f(x))\in\gamma. The sign condition in (ix) clearly holds, since \sigma is vertical and \nabla^{\perp}u is almost vertical. Thus, we obtain the formula
w(x,y)=-\log\max\Big\{|\nabla u|(x,y^{\prime}):y^{\prime}\in[y,f(x)]\Big\}.
(6.10)
Example 6.3.
Let \delta=1, and suppose u is \infty-harmonic in Q(4) so that
|\nabla u-\partial_{x}|<e^{-20},\qquad|\nabla u|(x,y)\text{ is nondecreasing in $y$ for each $x$.}
(6.11)
Take the basepoint z_{0}=(0,-2). Then we have \gamma\cap Q(1)=\emptyset and Q(1)\subset D_{2}, and hence w is a weak IMCF in Q(1). Combining (6.10) (6.11), we obtain
w=-\log|\nabla u|,\quad|\nu|=1\qquad\text{in}\ \ Q(1).
In summary, -\log|\nabla u| is a weak IMCF in Q(1) calibrated by \nu=-\frac{\nabla^{\perp}u}{|\nabla u|}, assuming (6.11).
The same conclusion holds under a slightly different assumption
|\nabla u|\text{ is nondecreasing in all upward integral lines of $\nabla^{\perp}u$}.
(6.12)
Indeed, it suffices to apply (6.7) on upward integral lines of \nabla^{\perp}u connecting \gamma to each z\in Q(1). The condition (6.12) is the local version of R. Moser’s orientedness assumption in [63], and our conclusion recovers [63, Proposition 5].
Remark 6.4.
We note a formal calculation: suppose u\in C^{\infty} is \infty-harmonic in a domain \Omega, so that |\nabla u|,|\nabla|\nabla u|| are both nonvanishing. Then there is \chi\in\{\pm 1\} with
\frac{\nabla|\nabla u|}{|\nabla|\nabla u||}=\chi\frac{\nabla^{\perp}u}{|\nabla u|}.
Hence
\displaystyle\div\Big(\frac{-\nabla\log|\nabla u|}{|\nabla\log|\nabla u||}\Big)
\displaystyle=-\div\Big(\frac{\nabla|\nabla u|}{|\nabla|\nabla u||}\Big)=-\chi\div\Big(\frac{\nabla^{\perp}u}{|\nabla u|}\Big)
\displaystyle=-\chi\frac{\div(\nabla^{\perp}u)}{|\nabla u|}+\chi\frac{\langle\nabla|\nabla u|\,,\,\nabla^{\perp}u\rangle}{|\nabla u|^{2}}=\frac{|\nabla|\nabla u||}{|\nabla u|}=|\nabla\log|\nabla u||.
Hence -\log|\nabla u| is a smooth IMCF. This calculation also appeared in Drucker-Williams [35, Theorem 2.1] and Koch-Zhang-Zhou [48, Proposition 4.2] in slightly different forms.
Suppose we have a unit-calibrated mixed IMCF cluster (w_{0},\nu_{0}) in Q(4), so that the calibration \nu_{0} satisfies |\nu_{0}+\partial_{y}|<e^{-20}. Consider first using Theorem 4.1 to obtain an \infty-harmonic function U, then using Theorem 6.1 to obtain a simple IMCF cluster w. In general, we do not have w=w_{0} even if w_{0} is simple. The change can be described precisely by (6.10): recall that \nabla u=e^{-w_{0}}\nu_{0}^{\perp}, and hence |\nabla u|=e^{-w_{0}}, then (6.10) implies
w(x,y)=\min\Big\{w_{0}(x,y^{\prime}):y^{\prime}\in[y,f(x)]\Big\}.
(6.13)
We include several schematic examples.
Example 6.5.
See Figure 19 (left): consider a unit-calibrated weak IMCF in Q(4) with a jump at t=0 represented by the grey region. The line foliation in \{w_{0}=0\} are displayed as the blue segments. Then the above process (with basepoint z_{0}) produces the simple cluster in Figure 19 (right): the ridge \gamma is the integral curve of \nu^{\perp} that passes through z_{0} (in this picture it is the unique allowable curve, but in general \gamma is not unique), and the function w satisfies w|_{D_{1}}\equiv 0 and w|_{D_{2}}\equiv w_{0}.
Figure 19: A weak IMCF and its reduction to a simple cluster.
Example 6.6.
See Figure 20: consider a mixed IMCF cluster in Q(4). The blue segment represents a valley. The above process with basepoint z_{0} produces many possible simple clusters including the three examples shown on the right of the figure. In each possible reduction, a part of the original solution is erased and becomes a jump region.
Figure 20: A nonsimple IMCF cluster and its reductions to simple clusters.
Example 6.7.
Figure 21 shows a mixed cluster. The valleys divide the cluster into pieces of simple clusters. The result of p-harmonic approximation in this picture is to select the “simple component” of the cluster that contains the basepoint (the components not containing the basepoint become jumps, shown as the grey regions).
Figure 21: A mixed cluster with many edges, and its reductions at different basepoints.
Remark 6.8.
If the mixed cluster contains valleys or jumps in the weak IMCF, Then \nabla u (where u is the resulting \infty-harmonic function) is non-injective. Hence, the map z\mapsto\nabla u(z) is generically non-injective. See also the recent work [24], as well as Section 8 for discussion on jumps.
Example 6.9.
Figure 22 shows the mixed cluster in Figure 17 and possible reductions to simple clusters. Light grey regions represent jumps in the weak IMCF with outer obstacle. In the last cluster, notice that the region \Omega_{\reg} does not contain the degree 2 vertex.
Figure 22: Possible clusters arising from Figure 17.
Finally, we notice a possibly interesting phenomenon. Let us call a curve \sigma transversal if \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle has a definite sign on \sigma. By item (ix), a transversal curve cannot leave a region D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg} once it enters D through some curve \gamma_{i}. In particular, a regular level set that enters some D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg} remains in D for all time.
As a note to readers, we stated the strongest version of Theorem 6.1(ix) though it is never used in full generality in this paper. For all places where it is used, \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle in fact has a sign on the entire \sigma (not only on the maximal gradient set).
The proof of Theorem 6.1 is outlined as follows: we first construct the curves \gamma_{i}, then prove some useful preliminary bounds for v_{p}, then study the sets of regions \mathcal{D}_{\Crit} and \mathcal{D}_{\reg}, then perform the limiting argument -\log|v_{p}|/(p-1)\to w in each regular region, then prove item (ix), then extend w continuously to all \Omega, and verify the remaining statements.
Proof of Theorem 6.1.
We may assume p\geqslant 3 in the sequence. By Theorem 5.1, 5.2, we have
\{u_{p}\},\{\nabla u_{p}\}\text{ are equicontinuous in $L$, $\forall\,L\Subset\Omega$},
(6.14)
and
u_{p}\xrightarrow{C^{1}_{\loc}(\Omega)}u.
(6.15)
Recall \Omega_{0}=\Omega\setminus\Crit(u). Hence, for each K\Subset\Omega_{0} there is a constant c(K)>0, so that
|\nabla u_{p}|\geqslant c(K)\qquad\text{in $K$, $\forall\,p\in[p(K),\infty]$.}
(6.16)
Here and for the remaining proof, the phrase “\,\forall\,p\geqslant p(\cdot) it holds \cdots” means that “there exists a constant p_{0} depending on (\cdot), such that for all p\geqslant p_{0} it holds \cdots”.
It is helpful to keep in mind that
Regular level sets of v_{p} are streamlines of u_{p}.
(6.17)
In the proof, all the closures and interiors are taken in \mathbb{R}^{2}.
Selection of \gamma_{i}.
Fix a sequence of smooth domains K_{n} with K_{1}\Subset K_{2}\Subset\cdots\Subset\Omega_{0} and \bigcup K_{n}=\Omega_{0}. If z_{0}\notin\Crit(u), then we may arrange so that z_{0}\in K_{1}. For all n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 2} and p\geqslant p(n), let \Gamma_{p,n}=\{\gamma_{i,p,n}\}_{i\geqslant 1} be the set of curves in \{v_{p}=0\}\cap K_{n} that intersect \overline{K_{n-1}}; thus tiny fragments near \partial K_{n} are removed. If z_{0}\notin\Crit(u), then we may relabel the curves so that z_{0}\in\gamma_{1,p,n} (recall that we set v_{p}(z_{0})=0). By (6.16), \Gamma_{p,n} is a disjoint collection of streamlines of u_{p}, and the endpoints of each \gamma_{i,p,n} lie on \partial K_{n}. By (6.14) (6.16), it follows that \bigcup_{p\geqslant p(n)}\Gamma_{p,n} is a C^{1}-equicontinuous family of curves.
By (6.14) (6.16) again, the following quantitative separation property holds:
Lemma 6.10.
There is a radius \delta_{n}>0 so that the following holds: for all z\in\overline{K_{n}} and p\geqslant p(n), at most one connected component of B(z,5\delta_{n})\cap\{v_{p}=0\} can intersect B(z,\delta_{n}), and any such component is the graph of a 1/100-Lipschitz function after a rotation.
Proof.
Take \delta_{n}<\frac{1}{40}d(K_{n},\partial K_{n+1}) so that |\nabla u_{p}(z_{1})-\nabla u_{p}(z_{2})|<e^{-10}c(K_{n+1}) whenever p\geqslant 3, z_{1},z_{2}\in K_{n+1}, and |z_{1}-z_{2}|<20\delta_{n}. Here c(K_{n+1}) is the constant in (6.16). Given any z\in\overline{K_{n}}, for all p large enough so that |\nabla u-\nabla u_{p}|<e^{-10}c(K_{n+1}) in K_{n+1}, we have
\displaystyle|\nabla u_{p}-\nabla u(z)|
\displaystyle\leqslant|\nabla u_{p}-\nabla u_{p}(z)|+|\nabla u_{p}(z)-\nabla u(z)|
\displaystyle<2\cdot e^{-10}c(K_{n+1})
\displaystyle<e^{-9}|\nabla u(z)|\qquad\text{in}\ \ B(z,10\delta_{n}).
This implies
\Big|\frac{\nabla u_{p}}{|\nabla u_{p}|}-\frac{\nabla u(z)}{|\nabla u(z)|}\Big|<e^{-8}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\Big|\frac{\nabla v_{p}}{|\nabla v_{p}|}-\frac{\nabla^{\perp}u(z)}{|\nabla u(z)|}\Big|<e^{-8}\qquad\text{in}\ \ B(z,10\delta_{n}).
This forbids two connected components of \{v_{p}=0\}\cap B(z,5\delta_{n}) intersecting B(z,\delta_{n}), and shows that any such connected component is almost parallel to \frac{\nabla u(z)}{|\nabla u(z)|}.
∎
As a consequence, for all n\geqslant 2 and p\geqslant p(n) we have:
•
There are at most C(n) curves in \Gamma_{p,n}, each curve has length at most C(n);
•
These curves are at least C(n)^{-1}-separated in K_{n-1}.
By a general compactness theorem [71, Theorem 6.3.11], we may select a diagonal subsequence p_{n} so that \Gamma_{p_{n},n} converge as currents in \Omega_{0}. Denote by \Gamma the limit. By the equicontinuity and separation properties above, \Gamma is a locally finite union of multiplicity one C^{1} curves in \Omega_{0}. Moreover, we have \partial\Gamma\subset\partial\Omega_{0}, and the convergence \Gamma_{p_{n},n}\to\Gamma is C^{1}_{\loc}-graphical with multiplicity one in \Omega_{0}.
Let \{\gamma_{i}\} be the connected components of \Gamma. Thus, each \gamma_{i} is a streamline of u (since \Gamma_{p_{n},n} are made of streamlines of u_{p}), and \gamma_{i} is an open curve without endpoint in \Omega_{0}. Also, we have z_{0}\in\cup\gamma_{i} if z_{0}\notin\Crit(u), since z_{0}\in\{v_{p}=0\} for all p.
For our ease of notation, we suppress the index n and write p_{n}=p, and keep in mind that we have chosen a specific subsequence of p. The exhaustion \{K_{n}\} is only used in constructing \{\gamma_{i}\}. The following useful facts follow directly from the construction:
Lemma 6.11.
\Omega_{0}\setminus\cup\gamma_{i} is open. For each z\in\gamma_{i} where \gamma_{i} is a curve in \Gamma, there are curves \gamma_{p}\subset\{v_{p}=0\} that converge to \gamma_{i} in the C^{1} graphical sense in a neighborhood of z.
Lemma 6.12.
If K\Subset\Omega_{0}\setminus\cup\gamma_{i}, then \{v_{p}=0\}\cap K=\emptyset for all p\geqslant p(K).
Effective bounds on v_{p}.
The following lemma is of central importance.
Lemma 6.13.
Suppose \sigma:[0,l]\to\Omega is a C^{1} curve with z_{1}=\sigma(0) and z_{2}=\sigma(l). Then
\limsup_{p\to\infty}\Big|v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(z_{1})\Big|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\leqslant\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|.
(6.18)
Denote
M_{\sigma}=\Big\{t\in[0,l]:|\nabla u|(\sigma(t))=\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|\Big\}.
If \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle has a definite sign \chi\in\{\pm 1\} on M_{\sigma}, then
\lim_{p\to\infty}\Big[\chi\cdot\big(v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(z_{1})\big)\Big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}=\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|.
(6.19)
Proof.
Note that
v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(z_{1})=\int_{0}^{l}\langle\nabla v_{p}\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle\,dt=\int_{0}^{l}|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\langle\nabla^{\perp}u_{p}\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle\,dt,
(6.20)
and by the C^{1} convergence we have
\nabla u_{p}\to\nabla u\qquad\text{in $C^{0}(\sigma)$.}
Denote A=\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|. For (6.18), we use |\nabla u_{p}|\leqslant|\nabla u|+o_{p}(1)\leqslant A+o_{p}(1) and (6.20) to obtain
\displaystyle\big|v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(z_{1})\big|
\displaystyle\leqslant\big(A+o_{p}(1)\big)^{p-1}\cdot|\sigma|,
and the result follows since |\sigma|^{1/(p-1)}=1+o_{p}(1). Here, o_{p}(1) denotes certain quantities that converge to 0 uniformly on \sigma as p\to\infty.
Then we derive (6.19). We may assume A>0, otherwise \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle would be identically zero. Our sign condition implies \chi\langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle\geqslant b(1+|\nabla u|) on M_{\sigma} for some constant b>0. For each \delta>0, denote
M_{\sigma}^{\delta}:=\big(\text{the $\delta$-neighborhood of $M_{\sigma}$ in $[0,l]$}\big).
By continuity, we may find \delta_{1}>0 so that \chi\langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}b(1+|\nabla u|) in M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{1}}. Next, notice that
\sup\Big\{|\nabla u|(\sigma(t)):t\in[0,l]\setminus M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{1}/2}\Big\}<(1-2\mu)A
(6.21)
for some \mu>0. Suppose that we are given any \varepsilon\in(0,\mu). Choose \delta_{2}\in(0,\delta_{1}/2) so that
|\nabla u|\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)A\qquad\text{in}\ \ M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{2}}.
Notice that M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{2}} has measure at least \delta_{2}. We now estimate
\displaystyle\chi\big(v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(z_{1})\big)
\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{l}|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\cdot\chi\langle\nabla^{\perp}u_{p}\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle\,dt
\displaystyle=\int_{M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{2}}}|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\cdot\chi\langle\nabla^{\perp}u_{p}\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle+\int_{M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{1}}\setminus M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{2}}}|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\cdot\chi\langle\nabla^{\perp}u_{p}\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle
\displaystyle+\int_{[0,l]\setminus M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{1}}}|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\cdot\chi\langle\nabla^{\perp}u_{p}\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle
\displaystyle=\operatorname{I}+\operatorname{II}+\operatorname{III}.
To bound I, we use \chi\langle\nabla^{\perp}u_{p}\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}b|\nabla u_{p}| in M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{1}} (hence in M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{2}}) for all large p:
\displaystyle\operatorname{I}\geqslant\int_{M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{2}}}|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}b|\nabla u_{p}|
\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{2}b|M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{2}}|\cdot\Big(\inf_{M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{2}}}|\nabla u_{p}|\Big)^{p-1}
\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{b\delta_{2}}{2}\Big((1-\varepsilon)\big(1-o_{p}(1)\big)A\Big)^{p-1},\qquad\forall\,p\gg 1,
where the 1-o_{p}(1) term originates from the error between |\nabla u| and |\nabla u_{p}|. Next, we simply relax \operatorname{II}\geqslant 0 for all large p. To estimate \operatorname{III}, we use (6.21) to obtain
\displaystyle\operatorname{III}\geqslant-\Big(\max_{[0,l]\setminus M_{\sigma}^{\delta_{1}}}|\nabla u_{p}|\Big)^{p-1}\cdot|\sigma|\geqslant-\Big((1-2\mu)\big(1+o_{p}(1)\big)A\Big)^{p-1}\cdot|\sigma|.
Hence, in total we have
\displaystyle\liminf_{p\to\infty}\Big[\chi\big(v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(z_{1})\big)\Big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}=\liminf_{p\to\infty}\Big(\!\operatorname{I}+\operatorname{II}+\operatorname{III}\!\Big)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}
\displaystyle\geqslant\liminf_{p\to\infty}\Big[\frac{b\delta_{2}}{2}\Big((1-\varepsilon)\big(1-o_{p}(1)\big)A\Big)^{p-1}-|\sigma|\Big((1-2\mu)\big(1+o_{p}(1)\big)A\Big)^{p-1}\Big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}
\displaystyle=(1-\varepsilon)A,
since 1-\varepsilon>1-2\mu. By the arbitrariness of \varepsilon, it follows that
\liminf_{p\to\infty}\Big[\chi\big(v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(z_{1})\big)\Big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\geqslant A.
Combining this with (6.18), we obtain (6.19) as desired.
∎
An upper bound of w_{p}.
Denote
w_{p}=-\frac{\log|v_{p}|}{p-1}
(6.22)
whenever v_{p}\neq 0. So e^{-w_{p}}=|v_{p}|^{1/(p-1)}. In this step we show that
Lemma 6.14.
For all z\in\Omega_{0}\setminus\cup\gamma_{i} we have
\limsup_{p\to\infty}w_{p}(z)\leqslant-\log|\nabla u|(z),
(6.23)
or equivalently,
\liminf_{p\to\infty}|v_{p}(z)|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\geqslant|\nabla u|(z).
(6.24)
In fact, this bound holds uniformly: for all K\Subset\Omega_{0}\setminus\cup\gamma_{i}, we have
\liminf_{p\to\infty}\Big[\inf_{K}\Big(|v_{p}|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}|\nabla u|^{-1}\Big)\Big]\geqslant 1.
(6.25)
Proof.
For a fixed z\in\Omega_{0}\setminus\cup\gamma_{i}, consider the curve
\sigma(t)=z+t\nabla^{\perp}u(z)\ \ (-\delta\leqslant t\leqslant\delta),\qquad\sigma_{+}=\sigma|_{[0,\delta]},\qquad\sigma_{-}=\sigma|_{[-\delta,0]}.
Choose a small \delta so that \sigma\Subset\Omega_{0}\setminus\cup\gamma_{i} and \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle>0 on \sigma. So v_{p}|_{\sigma} has a definite sign for all large p (Lemma 6.12). Take a subsequence that realizes the liminf in (6.24). If the sign of v_{p}|_{\sigma} is positive for infinitely many p in this subsequence, then we may pass to a further subsequence \{u_{p_{k}}\} and use (6.19) with \chi=1 to estimate
\liminf_{p\to\infty}\Big(v_{p}(z)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\Big)\geqslant\lim_{k\to\infty}\Big(v_{p_{k}}(z)-\overbrace{v_{p_{k}}(\sigma(-\delta))}^{\geqslant 0}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}=\max_{\sigma_{-}}|\nabla u|.
If this sign is negative for infinitely many p in the subsequence, then we may pass to an analogous subsequence and use (6.19) with \chi=1 to estimate
\liminf_{p\to\infty}\Big(\big(\!-v_{p}(z)\big)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\Big)\geqslant\liminf_{k\to\infty}\Big(\overbrace{v_{p_{k}}(\sigma(\delta))}^{\leqslant 0}-v_{p_{k}}(z)\Big)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}=\max_{\sigma_{+}}|\nabla u|.
In combination, we obtain
\liminf_{p\to\infty}|v_{p}(z)|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\geqslant\min\Big\{\max_{\sigma_{-}}|\nabla u|,\max_{\sigma_{+}}|\nabla u|\Big\}.
Taking \delta\to 0, the inequalities (6.23) (6.24) follow.
Finally, we prove (6.25). Note that \{v_{p}=0\}\cap N(K,\varepsilon)=\emptyset for some \varepsilon=\varepsilon(K)>0 and for all p\geqslant p(K), by Lemma 6.12. Then Kotschwar-Ni’s interior gradient estimate [49, Theorem 1.1] implies
\sup_{K}|\nabla w_{p}|\leqslant C(K)\qquad\forall\,K\Subset\Omega_{0}\setminus\cup\gamma_{i},\ \forall\,p\geqslant p(K).
This shows that the lower bound (6.24) is uniform in all compact sets in \Omega_{0}\setminus\cup\gamma_{i}.
∎
Critical and regular regions.
Recall that we have chosen a countable collection of streamlines \{\gamma_{i}\subset\Omega_{0}\}. Define \mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\Crit},\mathcal{D}_{\reg} as in item (ii) of the main theorem:
\mathcal{D}=\Big\{\text{connected components of $\Omega\setminus\overline{\cup\gamma_{i}}$}\Big\},
(6.26)
and
\mathcal{D}_{\Crit}=\Big\{D\in\mathcal{D}:D\subset\Crit(u)\Big\},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{\reg}=\mathcal{D}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{\Crit}.
(6.27)
It is useful to keep in mind that (\overline{\cup\gamma_{i}}\setminus\gamma_{i})\cap\Omega\subset\partial\Crit(u). Finally, set as in (iv)
\Omega_{\reg}=\Big(\bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}}D\Big)\cup\big(\!\cup\!\gamma_{i}\big).
(6.28)
Note that \Omega_{\reg} is open (since any z\in\cup\gamma_{i} is adjacent to two regions in \mathcal{D}_{\reg}), and \Omega_{\reg}\supset\Omega_{0}, which implies \Omega\setminus\Omega_{\reg}\subset\Crit(u). In this step, we prove that:
Lemma 6.15.
(i)
For any L\Subset\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\reg} and any \varepsilon>0, there exists a \delta>0 such that
|v_{p}(z)|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\leqslant\varepsilon\qquad\forall\,z\in N(L,\delta),\ p\geqslant p(\varepsilon,L).
(6.29)
(ii)
If D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}, then for each L\Subset D, it holds \{v_{p}=0\}\cap L=\emptyset for all p\geqslant p(L).
Item (ii) is a consequence of Lemma 6.12 if L\Subset D\cap\Omega_{0}, thus we need to extend this property when D contains critical points. To prove (ii), we need the following Harnack inequality (applied with q=\frac{p}{p-1} \Leftrightarrow q-1=\frac{1}{p-1}), which follows from Kotschwar-Ni’s gradient estimate [49, Theorem 1.1] or R. Moser’s Harnack inequality [62, Section 5]:
Theorem 6.16.
Let K\Subset L\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} be domains (i.e. connected open sets). Then for all q\in(1,3/2] and positive q-harmonic function v in L, it holds
\Big(\frac{\sup_{K}(v)}{\inf_{K}(v)}\Big)^{q-1}\leqslant C(K,L).
Proof of Lemma 6.15.
(i) Let L,\varepsilon be as stated. Choose a small \rho=\rho(L) such that any point in L can be connected to z_{0} by a curve in N(\Omega,-2\rho)\cap B(1/(2\rho)) with length \leqslant C(L). Here we denote
N(\Omega,-\rho)=\big\{z\in\Omega:B(z,\rho)\Subset\Omega\big\}.
Denote
L^{\prime}=\big(N(\Omega,-\rho)\cap B(1/\rho)\big)\setminus\Omega_{\reg}.
We may further decrease \rho and assume L\Subset L^{\prime}. Since L^{\prime}\Subset\Crit(u), there is \delta<\rho/4 so that
\sup_{N(L^{\prime},4\delta)}|\nabla u_{p}|\leqslant\varepsilon/2\qquad\forall\,p\geqslant p(\varepsilon,\rho,L^{\prime}).
(6.30)
Let z\in N(L,\delta) be given. We find a point z_{1}\in L with |z-z_{1}|<\delta, and then find a C^{1} curve
\sigma:[0,l]\to N(\Omega,-2\rho)\cap B(1/(2\rho)),\qquad|\sigma|\leqslant C(L),
that joins z_{1} to z_{0}, as was guaranteed above. Let t be the largest time so that \sigma([0,t])\subset\overline{N(L^{\prime},\delta)}. If t=l, then by (6.30) and direct estimates, we have
\displaystyle|v_{p}(z)|
\displaystyle=|v_{p}(z)-v_{p}(z_{1})+v_{p}(z_{1})-v_{p}(z_{0})|
\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{[z,z_{1}]+\sigma}|\nabla v_{p}|\leqslant\big(\delta+|\sigma|\big)\cdot(\varepsilon/2)^{p-1}\leqslant\varepsilon^{p-1},\qquad\forall p\geqslant p(\varepsilon,\rho,L^{\prime},\delta,L).
Suppose t<l. So \sigma(t)\notin L^{\prime}, and the construction implies \sigma(t)\in\Omega_{\reg}. Then we observe that \sigma([0,t])\cap(\overline{\cup\gamma_{i}})\neq\emptyset: otherwise, \sigma([0,t]) would be contained in some D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}, contradicting \sigma(0)=z_{1}\in L and L\cap\Omega_{\reg}=\emptyset. Recall that \cup\gamma_{i} is obtained by taking the limit of \{v_{p}=0\} (see also Lemma 6.11), so for all p\geqslant p(\delta,\sigma) we have
S\cap\{v_{p}=0\}\neq\emptyset,\quad\text{where}\quad S:=N\big(\sigma([0,t]),\delta\big).
Take any point z_{2} in this set. Then by (6.30) and S\Subset N(L^{\prime},4\delta) we have
|v_{p}(z)|=|v_{p}(z)-v_{p}(z_{2})|\leqslant\big(2\delta+|\sigma|\big)\cdot\sup_{S}|\nabla v_{p}|<\varepsilon^{p-1},\qquad\forall\,p\geqslant p(\varepsilon,\rho,L^{\prime},\delta,L).
In either case, the lemma follows.
Figure 23: Objects in the proof of Lemma 6.15(i).
(ii) Fix D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}. By a covering argument, it suffices to show that for any z\in D, there exists a radius \rho=\rho(z)>0 so that
\{v_{p}=0\}\cap B(z,\rho)=\emptyset\qquad\forall\,p\geqslant p(z).
Let U be the set of points in D that has this property. Clearly, U is open. Recall Lemma 6.12: for any K\Subset D\setminus\Crit(u), we have
\{v_{p}=0\}\cap K=\emptyset\qquad\forall\,p\geqslant p(K).
(6.31)
This implies D\setminus\Crit(u)\subset U, or D\setminus U\subset\Crit(u).
Suppose U\neq D. Then there is a smooth path \sigma:[0,l]\to D and radius r>0, so that
N(\sigma,4r)\subset D,\quad B(\sigma(0),2r)\subset D\setminus\Crit(u),\quad N(\sigma,2r)\subset U,\quad\overline{B(\sigma(l),2r)}\not\subset U.
Denote z_{1}=\sigma(0) and z_{2}=\sigma(l). See Figure 24 for a depiction of these objects. Hence
B(z_{1},2r)\subset D\setminus\Crit(u),\qquad\partial B(z_{2},2r)\setminus U\neq\emptyset.
Figure 24: Objects in the proof of Lemma 6.15(ii).
By Lemma 6.14 and B(z_{1},r)\Subset D\setminus\Crit(u)\subset\Omega_{0}\setminus\cup\gamma_{i}, there is a constant c^{\prime}>0 so that
\inf_{B(z_{1},r)}|v_{p}|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\geqslant c^{\prime},\qquad\forall\,p\geqslant p(z_{1},r).
(6.32)
Then, by our assumption N(\sigma,2r)\subset U, the function v_{p} has a definite sign in N(\sigma,3r/2) for all p\geqslant p(\sigma,r). Applying Theorem 6.16 with \text{``$K$''}=N(\sigma,r) and \text{``$L$''}=N(\sigma,3r/2), we have
\inf_{B(z_{2},r)}|v_{p}|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\geqslant c,\qquad\forall\,p\geqslant p(\sigma,r),
(6.33)
for some other constant c>0.
Next, fix z_{3}\in\partial B(z_{2},2r)\setminus U, so z_{3}\in\Crit(u). According to the definition of U, we may find a subsequence of p and points \zeta_{p}\to z_{3} so that v_{p}(\zeta_{p})=0.
For each s\in[r,2r), we denote a_{s,p}=\min_{\overline{B(z_{2},s)}}|v_{p}|^{1/(p-1)}. We claim that
\lim_{s\to 2r}\Big(\limsup_{p\to\infty}a_{s,p}\Big)=0,
(6.34)
where the limsup is taken along our selected subsequence. To show this, consider z_{s}=z_{2}+\frac{s}{2r}(z_{3}-z_{2}). Note that z_{s}\in\partial B(z_{2},s) and |z_{s}-z_{3}|=2r-s. We estimate
a_{s,p}^{p-1}\leqslant|v_{p}(z_{s})|\leqslant|v_{p}(z_{s})-v_{p}(z_{3})|+|v_{p}(z_{3})-v_{p}(\zeta_{p})|.
Then for all p large enough so that |\zeta_{p}-z_{3}|\leqslant 2r-s, we obtain
a_{s,p}\leqslant\Big(2(2r-s)\cdot\sup_{B(z_{3},2r-s)}|\nabla v_{p}|\Big)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}=\big(2(2r-s)\big)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\cdot\sup_{B(z_{3},2r-s)}|\nabla u_{p}|.
Then (6.34) follows by z_{3}\in\Crit(u) and the uniform convergence \nabla u_{p}\to\nabla u.
For each p in our selected subsequence, consider the q-harmonic function
\phi(\zeta)=\frac{a_{s,p}^{p-1}s^{p-2}-c^{p-1}r^{p-2}}{s^{p-2}-r^{p-2}}+\frac{c^{p-1}-a_{s,p}^{p-1}}{s^{p-2}-r^{p-2}}\Big(\frac{rs}{|\zeta-z_{2}|}\Big)^{p-2},
where c is the constant in (6.33). It can be verified that
\phi|_{\partial B(z_{2},r)}=c^{p-1},\qquad\phi|_{\partial B(z_{2},s)}=a_{s,p}^{p-1}.
Recall that v_{p} is also q-harmonic and has no sign change in B(z_{2},s) for all p\geqslant p(z_{2},s). The maximum principle and (6.33) then show that either v_{p}\geqslant\phi or v_{p}\leqslant-\phi in B(z_{2},s)\setminus B(z_{2},r), for all p\geqslant p(z_{2},\sigma,s). For each s,p we choose an extremal point \zeta_{s,p}\in\partial B(z_{2},s) so that a_{s,p}=|v_{p}(\zeta_{s,p})|^{1/(p-1)}. The boundary gradient comparison then implies
|\nabla v_{p}(\zeta_{s,p})|\geqslant|\nabla\phi(\zeta_{s,p})|=(p-2)\frac{c^{p-1}-a_{s,p}^{p-1}}{s^{p-2}-r^{p-2}}\frac{(rs)^{p-2}}{s^{p-1}},\qquad\forall\,p\geqslant p(z_{2},s).
(6.35)
Taking a sequence s_{n}\nearrow 2r and passing to a further diagonal subsequence p_{n}, we have \zeta_{s_{n},p_{n}}\to z_{\infty} for some z_{\infty}\in\partial B(z_{2},2r), and the limit in (6.34) is attained (namely, a_{s_{n},p_{n}}\to 0), and (6.35) holds along this subsequence. (Note: z_{\infty} need not be z_{3}.) Hence
|\nabla u(z_{\infty})|=\lim_{n\to\infty}|\nabla u_{p_{n}}(\zeta_{s_{n},p_{n}})|=\lim_{n\to\infty}\Big(|\nabla v_{p_{n}}(\zeta_{s_{n},p_{n}})|^{\frac{1}{p_{n}-1}}\Big)\geqslant\frac{c}{2}.
Thus z_{\infty}\notin\Crit(u). On the other hand, by Lemma 6.14, there exists \rho>0 so that
|v_{p}|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u(z_{\infty})|\quad\text{in}\ \ B(z_{\infty},\rho),\ \ \forall\,p\geqslant p(z_{\infty}).
This contradicts
0=\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{s_{n},p_{n}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}|v_{p_{n}}(\zeta_{s_{n},p_{n}})|^{\frac{1}{p_{n}-1}}.
The lemma follows as desired.
∎
Convergence of v_{p} in regular regions.
Fix a basepoint z_{D}\in D\setminus\Crit(u) for each D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}. Passing to a diagonal subsequence of p, we may assume that v_{p}(z_{D}) have the same sign for all sufficiently large p, for each D. Set \chi_{D}=1 if this sign is positive, and \chi_{D}=-1 if it is negative. In Lemma 6.15(ii), we have shown that for each connected L\Subset D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}, the function v_{p}|_{L} has a definite sign for all large p. Clearly, this sign agrees with \chi_{D}.
Each \partial D\cap\Omega is the union of a subset of \cup\gamma_{i} and a subset of \partial\Crit(u), since \overline{\cup\gamma_{i}}\setminus\cup\gamma_{i}\subset\partial\Crit(u). Note that if D_{1},D_{2} are adjacent (meaning that their boundaries contain the same portion of a curve \gamma_{i}), then \chi_{D_{1}}=-\chi_{D_{2}}. This is because the convergence \Gamma_{p,n}\to\{\gamma_{i}\} is one-fold, and v_{p} changes sign when crossing any curve in \Gamma_{p,n}.
Each D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg} is a C^{1} domain in \Omega_{\reg}. Indeed, any z\in\partial D\cap\Omega_{\reg} must lie on some \gamma_{i}. Then, for a small r>0, the set B(z,r)\setminus\cup\gamma_{i} is the union of two C^{1} sub-graphs, and D contains exactly one of them due to sign distinction.
Fix D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}, and consider any connected P_{1},P_{2} such that z_{D}\in P_{1}\Subset P_{2}\Subset D. For all large enough p\geqslant p(P_{2}), we may set
w_{p}=(1-q)\log|v_{p}|=-\frac{\log|v_{p}|}{p-1}=-\frac{\log(\chi_{D}v_{p})}{p-1}\qquad\text{in}\ \ P_{2}.
By Kotschwar-Ni’s interior gradient estimate [49, Theorem 1.1], we have
|\nabla w_{p}|\leqslant\frac{C}{d(P_{1},\partial P_{2})}\qquad\text{in}\ \ \overline{P_{1}}.
(6.36)
To apply Arzelà-Ascoli, we need a pointwise bound for w_{p}. The upper bound follows from (6.23):
\limsup_{p\to\infty}w_{p}(z_{D})\leqslant-\log|\nabla u|(z_{D})<\infty.
(6.37)
To derive a lower bound, choose a C^{1} path \sigma\subset\Omega that joins z_{0} and z_{D}. By (6.18) and v_{p}(z_{0})=0, we have
\liminf_{p\to\infty}w_{p}(z_{D})=-\limsup_{p\to\infty}\log\Big(|v_{p}(z_{D})|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\Big)\geqslant-\log\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|>-\infty.
(6.38)
Combining (6.36) (6.37) (6.38) and the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem, and passing to a subsequence, we have w_{p}\to w in C^{0}(P_{1}) for some w. Letting P_{1},P_{2}\nearrow D, and letting D range in \mathcal{D}_{\reg}, then finally passing to a diagonal subsequence, it follows that we can find functions w_{D}\in C^{0}_{\loc}(D) for each D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}, so that
w_{p}\to w_{D}\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{0}_{\loc}(D).
Define the function w on \bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}}D by setting w|_{D}=w_{D}. We have
w\leqslant-\log|\nabla u|\qquad\text{in}\ \ \bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}}D.
(6.39)
Indeed, if z\in\Omega_{0} then this follows from (6.23), and if z\in\Crit(u) then this is obvious. For the remaining proof, we work with the chosen subsequence of p.
Almost proof of item (ix).
The goal of this step is to prove the following two lemmas:
Lemma 6.17.
Suppose z_{1}\in\cup\gamma_{i} or z_{1}=z_{0}, and \sigma:[0,l]\to\Omega is a C^{1} curve joining z_{1} and a point z_{2}\in D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}. Denote
A=\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|,\qquad M_{\sigma}=\Big\{t\in[0,l]:|\nabla u|(\sigma(t))=A\Big\}.
(6.40)
Then w(z_{2})\geqslant-\log A. If in addition, \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle has a definite sign \chi\in\{\pm 1\} on M_{\sigma}, then this sign agrees with \chi_{D}, and we have w(z_{2})=-\log A.
Lemma 6.18.
Suppose z_{1}\in\cup\gamma_{i} or z_{1}=z_{0}, and \sigma:[0,l]\to\Omega is a C^{1} curve joining z_{1} and a point z_{2}\in\Omega\setminus\bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}}D. Define A,M_{\sigma} as in (6.40). Then \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle cannot have a definite sign on M_{\sigma}.
These almost imply item (ix) of the main theorem; the only (minor) difference is that w has not been defined outside \mathcal{D}_{\reg} yet.
Proof of Lemma 6.17.
In the case z_{1}=z_{0}, Lemma 6.13 and v_{p}(z_{0})=0 together implies
\left\{\begin{aligned} &\limsup_{p\to\infty}|v_{p}(z_{2})|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\leqslant A,\\
&\lim_{p\to\infty}\big[\chi v_{p}(z_{2})\big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}=A\qquad\text{if the sign condition holds.}\end{aligned}\right.
Also, recall that
e^{-w}=\lim_{p\to\infty}|v_{p}|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}=\lim_{p\to\infty}\big(\chi_{D}v_{p}\big)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}.
(6.41)
The result follows directly. We may now assume z_{1}\in\gamma_{i} for some fixed i. Recall that \gamma_{i}\subset\Omega_{0}, so we always have A>0.
We first prove w(z_{2})\geqslant-\log A. Recall that \gamma_{i} is a limit of subsets of \{v_{p}=0\} (see Lemma 6.11). For any r>0 and p\geqslant p(z_{1},r), we may find a point z_{r,p}\in B(z_{1},r)\cap\{v_{p}=0\}. From this we may estimate
|v_{p}(z_{2})|\leqslant|v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(z_{1})|+|v_{p}(z_{1})-v_{p}(z_{r,p})|\leqslant\big(|\sigma|+r\big)\cdot\sup_{\sigma\cup B(z_{1},r)}|\nabla u_{p}|^{p-1}.
Taking \frac{1}{p-1}-th power of both sides and sending p\to\infty, we obtain
e^{-w(z_{2})}\leqslant\max\Big\{A,\sup_{B(z_{1},r)}|\nabla u|\Big\}.
Finally, taking r\to 0, this implies e^{-w(z_{2})}\leqslant A as desired.
For the rest of the proof, we assume that \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle has a sign \chi\in\{\pm 1\} in M_{\sigma}, and we are aimed at showing w(z_{2})=-\log A. We divide the proof into two cases.
Case 1: Suppose 0\notin M_{\sigma}. Hence |\nabla u|(z_{1})<A. Recall that there are points \zeta_{p}\in\{v_{p}=0\} with \zeta_{p}\to z_{1}. And by the uniform convergence \nabla u_{p}\to\nabla u, there exists a \delta>0 so that
\sup_{p>1/\delta}\sup_{B(z_{1},\delta)}|\nabla u_{p}|\leqslant(1-\delta)A.
(6.42)
Then we calculate
\displaystyle e^{-w(z_{2})}
\displaystyle=\lim_{p\to\infty}\big[\chi_{D}v_{p}(z_{2})\big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}
(6.43)
\displaystyle=\lim_{p\to\infty}\Big[\chi_{D}\cdot\big(v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(z_{1})+v_{p}(z_{1})-v_{p}(\zeta_{p})\big)\Big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}},
and by (6.19) we have
\lim_{p\to\infty}\Big[\chi\cdot\big(v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(z_{1})\big)\Big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}=A,
(6.44)
and by direct estimate using (6.42) we have
\big|v_{p}(z_{1})-v_{p}(\zeta_{p})\big|^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\leqslant\Big(\delta\sup_{B(z_{1},\delta)}|\nabla v_{p}|\Big)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\leqslant\delta^{\frac{1}{p-1}}(1-\delta)A,\qquad\forall\,p\geqslant p(z_{1},\delta).
(6.45)
Combining (6.43) \sim (6.45), we obtain \chi=\chi_{D} and e^{-w(z_{2})}=A, as desired.
Case 2: Suppose 0\in M_{\sigma}. So |\nabla u|(z_{1})=A, and \sigma intersects \gamma_{i} transversally at z_{1} (since \gamma^{\prime}_{i} is parallel to \nabla u). Assume \chi\langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle\geqslant b>0 on M_{\sigma} for some constant b>0. Extend \sigma to a C^{1} curve on [-\varepsilon,l]. We may choose a small enough \varepsilon and arrange the extension so that
\chi\langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle(\sigma(t))\geqslant b/2\qquad\forall\,t\in[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon].
(6.46)
Recall by Lemma 6.11 that \gamma_{i} is the C^{1}_{\loc} graphical limit of curves \gamma_{p}\subset\{v_{p}=0\} near z_{1}. So for large enough p, the intersection \gamma_{p}\cap\sigma remains transversal near z_{1}. Let \zeta_{p}=\sigma(t_{p}) be this unique intersection point. Clearly t_{p}\to 0.
For a constant \delta with |\delta| small, we need to bound v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(\sigma(\delta)). For this purpose, we consider the curve \sigma_{\delta}=\sigma|_{[\delta,l]}, and the associated objects
A_{\delta}=\max_{\sigma_{\delta}}|\nabla u|,\qquad M_{\sigma,\delta}=\Big\{t\in[\delta,l]:|\nabla u|(\sigma(t))=A_{\delta}\Big\}.
The following lemma is useful:
Lemma 6.19.
We have \lim_{\delta\to 0}A_{\delta}=A. Furthermore, \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle also has a definite sign on M_{\sigma,\delta} as long as |\delta| is small enough.
Proof.
The first statement is elementary. If the second statement fails, then there are \delta_{i}\to 0 and t_{i}\in M_{\sigma,\delta_{i}}, so that \chi\langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle(t_{i})\leqslant 0. Passing to a subsequence, we may assume t_{i}\to t\in[0,l]. Then |\nabla u|(\sigma(t))=\lim_{i\to\infty}|\nabla u|(\sigma(t_{i}))=\lim_{i\to\infty}A_{\delta_{i}}=A, hence t\in M_{\sigma}, hence \chi\langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle(t)>0, contradiction.
∎
For each 0<\delta<\varepsilon so that Lemma 6.19 holds for \pm\delta, we use Lemma 6.13 to obtain
\displaystyle\lim_{p\to\infty}\Big[\chi\big(v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(\sigma(\delta))\big)\Big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}
\displaystyle=A_{\delta},
(6.47)
\displaystyle\lim_{p\to\infty}\Big[\chi\big(v_{p}(z_{2})-v_{p}(\sigma(-\delta))\big)\Big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}
\displaystyle=A_{-\delta}.
Combining v_{p}(\sigma(t_{p}))=0 and (6.46), we have
\chi v_{p}(\sigma(\delta))>0,\qquad\chi v_{p}(\sigma(-\delta))<0,\qquad\forall\,p\geqslant p(\sigma,\delta).
Then (6.47) implies that
A_{\delta}\leqslant\liminf_{p\to\infty}\big[\chi v_{p}(z_{2})\big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\leqslant\limsup_{p\to\infty}\big[\chi v_{p}(z_{2})\big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\leqslant A_{-\delta}.
Taking \delta\to 0 of this inequality, it follows that
\lim_{p\to\infty}\big[\chi v_{p}(z_{2})\big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}=A,
which implies e^{-w(z_{2})}=A and \chi=\chi_{D} (where D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg} is the region containing z_{2}).
∎
Proof of Lemma 6.18.
Suppose otherwise that \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle has a sign \chi\in\{\pm 1\} on M_{\sigma}.
If 0\in M_{\sigma}, then we must have z_{1}\in\gamma_{i} for some i (since in this case, z_{1}=z_{0} implies z_{0}\notin\Crit(u), hence z_{0}\in\cup\gamma_{i}), and \sigma intersects \gamma_{i} transversally (as \gamma^{\prime}_{i} is parallel to \nabla u). By Lemma 6.11 and the same extension argument in the proof of Lemma 6.17 above, there are t_{p}\to 0 so that v_{p}(\sigma(t_{p}))=0. Integrating from \sigma(t_{p}) to \sigma(\delta) gives
\chi v_{p}(\sigma(\delta))>0\qquad\forall\,\delta<\delta(\sigma),\,p>p(\sigma,\delta).
(6.48)
If 0\notin M_{\sigma}, then |\nabla u(z_{1})|<A, so by the equicontinuity of \nabla u_{p}, there is \varepsilon>0 so that
\sup_{p>1/\varepsilon}\sup_{B(z_{1},\varepsilon)}|\nabla u_{p}|\leqslant(1-\varepsilon)A.
Then, note that B(z_{1},\varepsilon)\cap\{v_{p}=0\}\neq\emptyset for all p\geqslant p(z_{1},\varepsilon), since z_{1}\in\{z_{0}\}\cup(\cup\gamma_{i}). Hence
|v_{p}(\sigma(\delta))|\leqslant 2\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)^{p-1}A^{p-1},\qquad\forall\,\delta<\varepsilon,\,p>p(z_{1},\varepsilon).
(6.49)
Combining both cases, we find that there is always an \varepsilon>0 so that
\chi v_{p}(\sigma(\delta))\geqslant-(1-\varepsilon)^{p-1}A^{p-1},\qquad\forall\,\delta<\delta(\sigma,\varepsilon),\,p>p(\sigma,\delta,\varepsilon).
(6.50)
Consider the other end of \sigma. Set
T=\min\Big\{t\in[0,l]:\sigma|_{[t,l]}\subset\Omega\,\big\backslash\!\bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}}D\Big\}.
Then \sigma([T,l])\subset(\cup\gamma_{i})\cup\Crit(u), and hence \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle=0 on (T,l], and hence M_{\sigma}\subset[0,T]. Also, \sigma(T) lies on the boundary of \bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}}D, hence lies on \overline{\cup\gamma_{i}}. Arguing analogously as above, we conclude that
\chi v_{p}(\sigma(T-\delta))\leqslant(1-\varepsilon)^{p-1}A^{p-1},\qquad\forall\,\delta<\delta(\sigma,\varepsilon),\,p>p(\sigma,\delta,\varepsilon),
(6.51)
after possibly decreasing \varepsilon. Denote
A_{\delta}=\max_{\sigma([\delta,T-\delta])}|\nabla u|,\qquad M_{\sigma,\delta}=\Big\{t\in[\delta,T-\delta]:|\nabla u|(\sigma(t))=A_{\delta}\Big\}.
An analogue of Lemma 6.19 holds in this context as well: for all small enough \delta, we have A_{\delta}>(1-\varepsilon)A, and \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle has a sign in the set where |\nabla u| attains maximum on [\delta,T-\delta]. Then, Lemma 6.13 implies
\lim_{p\to\infty}\Big[\chi\cdot\big(v_{p}(\sigma(T-\delta))-v_{p}(\sigma(\delta))\big)\Big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}=A_{\delta}.
(6.52)
Combining (6.50) (6.51) (6.52) and A_{\delta}>(1-\varepsilon)A, we obtain
0=\lim_{p\to\infty}\Big[\chi v_{p}(\sigma(\delta))+\chi\Big(v_{p}(\sigma(T-\delta))-v_{p}(\sigma(\delta))\Big)-\chi v_{p}(\sigma(T-\delta))\Big]^{\frac{1}{p-1}}=A_{\delta},
which is a contradiction.
∎
Full extension of w.
We first extend w continuously to \cup\gamma_{i}. Fix z\in\gamma_{i}. Up to a translation and rotation, we may assume z=0 and \nabla u(0)=\partial_{x}. Let \rho be small enough so that Q(2\rho)\Subset\Omega_{0}, and
|\nabla u-\partial_{x}|<e^{-10},\quad|\nabla^{\perp}u-\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}\qquad\text{in}\ \ Q(2\rho),
and that \gamma_{i}\cap Q(\rho) is the graph y=f(x) of a function f:(-\rho,\rho)\to(-\rho/10,\rho/10).
For each r<\rho and z_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1})\in Q(r)\setminus\gamma_{i}, we apply Lemma 6.17 to the vertical segment \sigma=\big\{(x_{1},y):y\in[f(x_{1}),y_{1}]\} oriented so that \sigma(0)=(x_{1},f(x_{1})). Note that \langle\nabla^{\perp}u\,,\,\sigma^{\prime}\rangle has a definite sign everywhere on \sigma (which is positive iff y_{1}>f(x_{1})). Lemma 6.17 gives
w(z_{1})=-\log\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|.
Hence
-\log\sup_{Q(r)}|\nabla u|\leqslant\inf_{Q(r)\setminus\gamma_{i}}(w)\leqslant\sup_{Q(r)\setminus\gamma_{i}}(w)\leqslant-\log\inf_{Q(r)}|\nabla u|.
It follows that w is continuous at z=0 if we set w=-\log|\nabla u| on \cup\gamma_{i}. This argument applies to all z\in\cup\gamma_{i}, hence w can be extended continuously to the set
\Big(\bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}}D\Big)\cup\big(\cup\gamma_{i}\big)
by setting w=-\log|\nabla u| on \cup\gamma_{i}.
This set is exactly \Omega_{\reg} as appeared in item (iv) and (6.28). Finally, we define w\equiv+\infty on \Omega\setminus\Omega_{\reg}. By the p\to\infty limit of Lemma 6.15(i), it follows that w is a continuous function from \Omega to \mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}. The upper bound (6.39) clearly extends and yields
w\leqslant-\log|\nabla u|\qquad\text{on}\ \ \Omega.
(6.53)
Production of simple cluster.
Set \nu=-e^{w}\nabla^{\perp}u, which is a continuous vector field on \Omega_{\reg}=\{w<+\infty\}. By (6.53) it follows that |\nu|\leqslant 1. Recall that in each D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg} the function w arises as the limit
w=\lim_{p\to\infty}w_{p}=\lim_{p\to\infty}\Big(\!-\frac{\log(\chi_{D}v_{p})}{p-1}\Big)\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{0}_{\loc}(D).
Recall from [61] that w_{p} is a distributional solution of
\div\big(|\nabla w_{p}|^{q-2}\nabla w_{p}\big)=|\nabla w_{p}|^{q},\qquad q=\frac{p}{p-1}.
(6.54)
Next, we may calculate
|\nabla w_{p}|^{q-2}\nabla w_{p}=-(p-1)^{1-q}|v_{p}|^{1-q}|\nabla v_{p}|^{q-2}\chi_{D}\nabla v_{p}=-\chi_{D}(p-1)^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}e^{w_{p}}\nabla^{\perp}u_{p},
(6.55)
which taking p\to\infty yields
|\nabla w_{p}|^{q-2}\nabla w_{p}\to-\chi_{D}e^{w}\nabla^{\perp}u=\chi_{D}\nu\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{0}_{\loc}(D).
(6.56)
In particular,
|\nabla w_{p}|^{q-1}\to|\nu|\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{0}_{\loc}(D).
Testing (6.54) with the function \varphi(w-w_{p}), where \varphi\in\Lip_{\loc}(D) is nonnegative, we have
\displaystyle-\int\varphi(w-w_{p})|\nabla w_{p}|^{q}
\displaystyle=\int|\nabla w_{p}|^{q-2}{\big\langle{\nabla w_{p}}\,,\,{(w-w_{p})\nabla\varphi+\varphi\nabla w-\varphi\nabla w_{p}}\big\rangle}
\displaystyle\leqslant\int|\nabla w_{p}|^{q-1}|w-w_{p}|\,|\nabla\varphi|+\varphi|\nabla w_{p}|^{q-1}|\nabla w|-\varphi|\nabla w_{p}|^{q}.
Recall that |\nabla w_{p}| is locally uniformly bounded [49], and w_{p}\to w in C^{0}_{\loc}(D). Hence
\limsup_{p\to\infty}\int\varphi|\nabla w_{p}|^{q}\leqslant\limsup_{p\to\infty}\int\varphi|\nabla w_{p}|^{q-1}|\nabla w|\leqslant\int\varphi|\nabla w|.
But the standard lower semi-continuity gives
\int\varphi|\nabla w|\leqslant\liminf_{p\to\infty}\int\varphi|\nabla w_{p}|^{q}.
Hence,
\int\varphi|\nabla w|=\lim_{p\to\infty}\int\varphi|\nabla w_{p}|^{q},\qquad\forall\,\varphi\in\Lip_{0}(D).
(6.57)
Combining (6.54) (6.56) (6.57), we have
\int\langle\chi_{D}\nu\,,\,\nabla\varphi\rangle=-\int\varphi|\nabla w|\qquad\forall\,\varphi\in\Lip_{0}(D).
Testing this condition with \varphi w for any \varphi\in\Lip_{0}(D) gives
\displaystyle-\int\varphi w|\nabla w|
\displaystyle=\int\langle\chi_{D}\nu\,,\,w\nabla\varphi+\varphi\nabla w\rangle
\displaystyle=\int\varphi\langle\chi_{D}\nu\,,\,\nabla w\rangle+\lim_{p\to\infty}\int{\big\langle{|\nabla w_{p}|^{q-2}\nabla w_{p}}\,,\,{w_{p}\nabla\varphi}\big\rangle}
\displaystyle=\int\varphi\langle\chi_{D}\nu\,,\,\nabla w\rangle+\lim_{p\to\infty}\int\Big[\!-\varphi w_{p}|\nabla w_{p}|^{q}-\varphi|\nabla w_{p}|^{q}\Big].
Then by (6.57) (applied to both \varphi and \varphi w) and the interior gradient estimate for w_{p}, we have
\int\varphi\langle\chi_{D}\nu\,,\,\nabla w\rangle=\int\varphi|\nabla w|.
This implies \langle\chi_{D}\nu\,,\,\nabla w\rangle=|\nabla w| a.e.. In summary, we have shown that
|\nu|\leqslant 1,\qquad\langle\chi_{D}\nu\,,\,\nabla w\rangle=|\nabla w|,\qquad\div(\chi_{D}\nu)=|\nabla w|.
(6.58)
Hence w|_{D} is a weak IMCF calibrated by \chi_{D}\nu, for each D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}.
We form the data \big(w,\nu,\mathcal{D}_{\reg},\{\chi_{D}\}\big) with the ambient domain \Omega_{\reg}. Recall that w<\infty in \Omega_{\reg}, and each D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg} is a C^{1} domain in \Omega_{\reg}. It remains to verify that each \gamma_{i} is a ridge (see Definition 3.1(iv)). Fix a point z\in\gamma_{i}. Let \rho be small enough so that up to a rigid motion, we have z=0 and |\nabla u-\partial_{x}|<e^{-10} in Q(2\rho), and (\cup\gamma_{i})\cap Q(2\rho)=\graphh(f) for a function f. Let D_{1},D_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg} be so that D_{1}\cap Q(\rho)=\{y<f(x)\} and D_{2}\cap Q(\rho)=\{y>f(x)\}. Note that |\nabla^{\perp}u-\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}, so \nabla v_{p} points upwards, so \chi_{D_{1}}=-1 and \chi_{D_{2}}=1. Therefore, on \gamma_{i}\cap Q(\rho) we have
\displaystyle\nu\cdot\nu_{D_{1}}
\displaystyle=(-e^{w}\nabla^{\perp}u)\cdot\Big(\frac{\nabla^{\perp}u}{|\nabla u|}\Big)=-1=\chi_{D_{1}},
\displaystyle\nu\cdot\nu_{D_{2}}
\displaystyle=(-e^{w}\nabla^{\perp}u)\cdot\Big(\!-\frac{\nabla^{\perp}u}{|\nabla u|}\Big)=1=\chi_{D_{2}}.
All the conditions in Definition 3.1 are met, thus we obtain a simple cluster in \Omega_{\reg}.
Summary.
Recall that we constructed a collection of curves \gamma_{i}\subset\Omega_{0}, and set \mathcal{D} to be the set of connected components of \Omega\setminus\overline{\cup\gamma_{i}}. We set \mathcal{D}_{\Crit},\mathcal{D}_{\reg},\Omega_{\reg} as in (6.27) (6.28). Then, we constructed a continuous function w:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\} with \Omega_{\reg}=\{w<+\infty\}, by first defining it in \mathcal{D}_{\reg} then extending it to the entire \Omega. Moreover, we showed that w_{p}\to w in C^{0}_{\loc}(D), for each D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}, along our chosen subsequence. Items (i) (ii) (iv) (v) (vii) (x) all follow from the construction. In particular, (iia) comes from Lemma 6.15(i).
To finish item (ix), observe that the case z_{2}\in\bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}}D follows from Lemma 6.17. The case z_{2}\notin\bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}}D follows from
w(z_{2})=-\log|\nabla u(z_{2})|\geqslant-\log\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|
and Lemma 6.18.
Item (vi) comes easily from the construction: if z_{0}\in\Omega_{\reg}, then the result follows from items (vii) (x). If z_{0}\notin\Omega_{\reg}, then we have z_{0}\in\Crit(u), hence w(z_{0})=+\infty=-\log|\nabla u|(z_{0}), recalling that we defined w\equiv+\infty outside \Omega_{\reg}.
To verify item (viii), it suffices to show that w=-\log|\nabla u| on D\cap\{w\neq\text{const}\} for each D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg}. Recall that w is a weak IMCF in D and is calibrated by the continuous vector field \nu=-e^{w}\nabla^{\perp}u. Hence, if z\in\{w\neq\text{const}\}, then |\nu(z)|=1. This implies e^{-w(z)}=|\nabla u(z)|, namely, w(z)=-\log|\nabla u(z)|.
To prove (xi), note that z_{0} cannot lie on \overline{\cup\gamma_{i}} (since \overline{\cup\gamma_{i}}\subset\overline{\Omega_{0}}), and z_{0} cannot lie in any D\in\mathcal{D}_{\reg} (otherwise, Lemma 6.15(ii) would violate v_{p}(z_{0})=0). So z_{0} must lie in some D\in\mathcal{D}_{\Crit}.
Finally, let Y_{t},Z_{t},\widetilde{Z}_{t},F,F^{\prime} be as in item (xii). Since w\leqslant-\log|\nabla u|, we always have Y_{t}\supset Y^{\prime}_{t}, \widetilde{Z}_{t}\supset\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}, Z_{t}\supset Z^{\prime}_{t}, hence F\supset F^{\prime}. It remains to prove the reverse inclusion. To this end, we may assume F\neq\emptyset. We first consider F\subset Y_{t} or F\subset Z_{t}. For each z\in F, we may find a C^{1} path \sigma\Subset F joining z_{0} to z (since F is open and connected). Then (6.6) implies
w(z)\geqslant-\log\max_{\sigma}|\nabla u|\left\{\begin{aligned} &>t\qquad\text{(if $F\subset Y_{t}$)},\\
&\geqslant t\qquad\text{(if $F\subset Z_{t}$)}.\\
\end{aligned}\right.
For F\subset Y_{t}, this immediately implies z\in F^{\prime}. For F\subset Z_{t}, this implies z\in\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t} for all z\in F, hence F\subset\Int(\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t})=Z^{\prime}_{t}. Hence F\subset F^{\prime}, as desired. Finally, consider F\subset\widetilde{Z}_{t} and F^{\prime}\subset\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}. For each s<t, let F_{s} (resp. F^{\prime}_{s}) be the connected component of Y_{s} (resp. Y^{\prime}_{s}) that contains z_{0}. We have proved that F_{s}=F^{\prime}_{s}. So for each s<t we have
F\subset\widetilde{Z}_{t}\subset Y_{s}\quad\Rightarrow\quad F\subset F_{s}\quad\Rightarrow\quad F\subset F^{\prime}_{s}\quad\Rightarrow\quad F\subset Y^{\prime}_{s}.
Taking s\nearrow t, it follows that F\subset\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}, hence F\subset F^{\prime}.
∎
7 Simple clusters and heat equation
Suppose that a smooth family of convex curves \{\gamma_{t}\} evolve at the speed of 1/\kappa. Let h=h(t,\theta) be the collection of support functions of \gamma_{t}. Then we have
\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}=\langle\frac{\partial\gamma_{t}}{\partial t}\,,\,\nu_{\theta}\rangle=\kappa^{-1}=\frac{\partial^{2}h}{\partial\theta^{2}}+h.
(7.1)
See Subsection 2.3. In Section 4, we also built examples of simple clusters where (7.1) holds across the interfaces. The aim of this section is to show that \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h holds in a certain sense for weak IMCFs and simple IMCF clusters. Let w be either a weak IMCF or a simple cluster. We fix as usual
Y_{t}=\{w>t\},\qquad\widetilde{Z}_{t}=\{w\geqslant t\},\qquad Z_{t}=\Int(\widetilde{Z}_{t}).
If w is a weak IMCF, then Y_{t},Z_{t} are mean concave and \widetilde{Z}_{t}=\overline{Z_{t}} for all t. When w is a simple cluster, \widetilde{Z}_{t} may differ from \overline{Z_{t}} by line segments (see Lemma 3.7). Denote
\gamma_{t}=\partial Z_{t}\cap\Omega,\qquad\qquad\Gamma=\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\gamma_{t}.
(7.2)
If w is a simple cluster, then it is naturally associated with a continuous vector field \nu, with |\nu|=1 on \Gamma. If w is a weak IMCF, then the collection of inward unit normals of \partial Z_{t} (i.e. outer unit normals of \partial\{w<t\}), still denoted by \nu, is continuous (in fact, C^{0,1/2} by Lemma 2.10) on \Gamma as well. In either case, we obtain a map
\nu:\Gamma\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
which is continuous and orthogonal to each \gamma_{t}.
The main statement is as below. Recall the notation
\nu_{\theta}=(\sin\theta,-\cos\theta)=e^{i(\theta-\pi/2)},\qquad\tau_{\theta}=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)=e^{i\theta}.
Recall our convention that a “line segment” means a line segment or ray or line or point, and a “finite line segment” means a line segment with finite length, and a “nontrivial line segment” means a line segment that is not a point.
Theorem 7.1.
Suppose w is a weak IMCF or a simple IMCF cluster in a domain \Omega. Let \gamma_{t},\Gamma,\nu be as defined above. Assume that the map (t,\nu^{\perp}):\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1} lifts to a continuous map
\Theta:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}\times M,
with M=\mathbb{R} or M=\mathbb{S}^{1}(2k\pi), so that each preimage \Theta^{-1}(t,\theta) is a line segment. Denote \mathcal{J}=\Theta(\Gamma). Let h:\mathcal{J}\to\mathbb{R} be the (well-defined) support function such that
h(t,\theta)=\langle z\,,\,\nu\rangle=\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta}\rangle\qquad\text{for any }z\in\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta).
(7.3)
For each (t,\theta)\in\mathcal{J}, denote \sigma=\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta). Then:
(i)
If \sigma\Subset\Omega, then for any \varphi\in C^{2}(R) with R:=[t-\delta,t]\times[\theta-\delta,\theta+\delta] for some \delta>0, so that
\varphi(t,\theta)=h(t,\theta),\qquad\varphi\geqslant h\ \ (\text{resp. $\varphi\leqslant h$})\ \ \text{in}\ \ R\cap\mathcal{J},
it holds
\big(\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi\big)(t,\theta)\leqslant 0\ \ (\text{resp. $\geqslant 0$}).
(ii)
Suppose \sigma\not\Subset\Omega. Then \sigma is a nontrivial line segment. Let \chi_{\sigma}\in\{\pm 1\} be the (well-defined) sign so that -\chi_{\sigma}\nu is the outer unit normal of Z_{t} on \Int(\sigma). Then for any \varphi\in C^{2}(R) with R:=[t-\delta,t]\times[\theta-\delta,\theta+\delta] for some \delta>0, so that
\varphi(t,\theta)=h(t,\theta),\qquad\left\{\begin{aligned} &\varphi\geqslant h\ \ \text{in}\ \ R\cap\mathcal{J}\qquad(\text{if $\chi_{\sigma}=-1$}),\\
&\varphi\leqslant h\ \ \text{in}\ \ R\cap\mathcal{J}\qquad(\text{if $\chi_{\sigma}=1$}),\end{aligned}\right.
(7.4)
and
\varphi(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}\in\sigma,
(7.5)
it holds
\big(\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi\big)(t,\theta)\left\{\begin{aligned} &\leqslant 0\qquad(\text{if $\chi_{\sigma}=-1$}),\\
&\geqslant 0\qquad(\text{if $\chi_{\sigma}=1$}).\end{aligned}\right.
(7.6)
Whenever \mathcal{J} contains a box R=(t_{1},t_{2}]\times(\theta_{1},\theta_{2}) so that \Theta^{-1}(R)\Subset\Omega and h is continuous in R, item (i) and the standard viscosity theory [30, 45] imply that h is a smooth solution of \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h in R. However, h need not be continuous in general. Figure 25 shows an example where the solution first evolves as a translating soliton, then jumps over the shadowed region at t=0, then continues evolving with the outer obstacle (i.e. boundary tangency) condition on \{y=\pm 1\}. Then \mathcal{J}=\mathbb{R}\times(0,\pi), and h is not continuous across \{t=0\}.
Figure 25: A weak IMCF for which h(t,\theta) is not continuous.
Item (ii) is used in Theorem 7.3 below as well as in the \varepsilon-regularity Theorem 8.4. We refer to Section 8 for related discussions. Figure 26 below (in which \nu\approx-\partial_{y}) shows how the sign \chi_{\sigma} is determined.
Figure 26: The orientation \chi_{\sigma}.
Theorem 7.1 is proved using a geometric comparison: if the stated property fails, then the test function \varphi would generate a subsolution or supersolution \{\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\} of IMCF. Then \varphi\leqslant h or \varphi\geqslant h would allow us to compare the position of \gamma_{t} and \widetilde{\gamma}_{t}. The maximum principle for weak IMCF finally yields a contradiction. We do not know whether item (ii) can be extended to a full comparison, but an obstruction for us proving a full result is explained at around Figure 31.
Remark 7.2.
The theorem respects the following symmetries:
(i)
Rotation and time translation: this corresponds to translation of the objects given by \mathcal{J}\to\mathcal{J}+(t_{0},\theta_{0}) and h(t,\theta)\to h(t-t_{0},\theta-\theta_{0}), \varphi(t,\theta)\to\varphi(t-t_{0},\theta-\theta_{0}).
(ii)
Translation in \mathbb{R}^{2}: this corresponds to adding h,\varphi by a common factor of a\sin\theta-b\cos\theta, for some a,b\in\mathbb{R}, while leaving \mathcal{J} fixed.
(iii)
Orientation reversing: by Remark 3.2(v) this means \nu^{\prime}=-\nu and \chi_{i}^{\prime}=-\chi_{i} for all regions D_{i} (where a notation with a prime is associated to the cluster after orientation reversing). The effect on angle parametrization is
\Theta^{\prime}(z)=\Theta(z)+(0,\pi),\qquad(\Theta^{\prime})^{-1}(t,\theta)=\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta-\pi),\qquad\mathcal{J}^{\prime}=\mathcal{J}+(0,\pi).
By definition we have h^{\prime}(t,\theta)=\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta}\rangle for any z\in(\Theta^{\prime})^{-1}(t,\theta), thus
\displaystyle h^{\prime}(t,\theta)
\displaystyle=\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta}\rangle=-\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta-\pi}\rangle\qquad\forall\,z\in\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta-\pi)
\displaystyle=-h(t,\theta-\pi).
The test function \varphi should be transformed accordingly, so \varphi^{\prime}(t,\theta)=-\varphi(t,\theta-\pi). The order between h and \varphi is reversed, so is the sign of \partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi. In the context of item (ii), note that
\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi^{\prime}(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}
\displaystyle=\varphi(t,\theta-\pi)\nu_{\theta-\pi}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi(t,\theta-\pi)\tau_{\theta-\pi}
\displaystyle\in\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta-\pi)=(\Theta^{\prime})^{-1}(t,\theta),
so (7.5) is preserved. Finally, the orientation becomes \chi^{\prime}_{\sigma}=-\chi_{\sigma}, so the compatibility between (7.4) and (7.6) is preserved as well.
(iv)
If we reverse the orientation and then rotate by 180^{\circ}: this results in sending \chi_{i}\to-\chi_{i}, h\to-h, \varphi\to-\varphi while not changing \nu and \mathcal{J}.
(v)
Reflection around the y-axis: this fixes \nu=\partial_{y} hence \theta=0. Therefore, it corresponds to \theta\to-\theta and \mathcal{J} being reflected with respect to \{\theta=0\}, and h(t,\theta)\to h(t,-\theta), \varphi(t,\theta)\to\varphi(t,-\theta).
(vi)
Reflection around the x-axis: this corresponds to \theta\to\pi-\theta and \mathcal{J} being reflected with respect to \{\theta=\pi/2\}, and h(t,\theta)\to h(t,\pi-\theta), \varphi(t,\theta)\to\varphi(t,\pi-\theta).
(vii)
If we reflect around the x-axis and then reverse the orientation, then this results in \chi_{i}\to-\chi_{i}, and \mathcal{J} being reflected with respect to \theta=0, and h(t,\theta)\to-h(t,-\theta), \varphi(t,\theta)\to-\varphi(t,-\theta).
We prove the following fine regularity for planar weak IMCF, as a consequence of Theorem 7.1. In the theorem and its proof, we denote
Q_{\delta}(r)=(-r,r)\times(-\delta r,\delta r).
Theorem 7.3.
(i)
Let w be a weak IMCF in \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}, and \gamma be a connected component of \partial Z_{t}\cap\Omega. Then either \gamma is a nontrivial line segment, or we can decompose
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\cup\gamma_{2}\cup\gamma_{3},
where \gamma_{i} meets \gamma_{i+1} at endpoints, and \gamma_{1},\gamma_{3} are (possibly empty) line segments, and the interior of \gamma_{2} is smooth, nonempty, and strictly convex.
(ii)
Suppose \delta\leqslant 1, and w is a weak IMCF in Q_{\delta}(4) such that
|\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}\delta\ \ \text{in}\ \ \Gamma,\qquad|w|<e^{-20}\delta^{2}.
(7.7)
Then
\sup_{Q_{\delta}(2)}|\nabla w|\leqslant e^{-7}\delta.
(7.8)
We remark that the gradient estimate for the weak IMCF remains open in general (though weak IMCFs are a priori \Lip_{\loc} by definition), namely, it is unknown whether a weak IMCF w in a domain (\Omega,g) satisfies the uniform bound
\sup_{K}|\nabla w|\leqslant C(K,\Omega,g)\qquad\forall\,K\Subset\Omega.
(7.9)
Such an estimate is only known if w is the limit of \varepsilon-regularized solutions [44] or renormalized q-harmonic functions [61, 49, 59]. Item (ii) confirms a special case in \mathbb{R}^{2}.
Proof of Theorem 7.1.
If w is a simple cluster, then let \{D_{i}\},\{\chi_{i}\},\{\gamma_{j}\} be the associated regions and orientations and ridges. If w is a weak IMCF, then we set D_{1}=\Omega and \chi_{1}=1 and \{\gamma_{j}\}=\emptyset.
By rotation and time translation symmetry, it suffices to assume that the basepoint is (t,\theta)=(0,0). With a translation in \mathbb{R}^{2}, see Remark 7.2(ii), we may assume
h(0,0)=\varphi(0,0)=\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)=0
(7.10)
for any given test function \varphi. If the viscosity subsolution (or supersolution) property fails, then there is a test function \varphi defined in a backward neighborhood of (0,0), so that
h(0,0)=\varphi(0,0)=\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)=0,\quad\varphi\geqslant h\text{\ \ (or $\leqslant h$, depending on the context)},
(7.11)
but
\big(\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi\big)(0,0)>0\text{\ \ (or $<0$).}
(7.12)
In the context of item (ii), we also have
0=\varphi(0,0)\nu_{0}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)\tau_{0}\in\sigma.
(7.13)
Since (7.12) is an open condition, we may replace \varphi by \varphi\pm\varepsilon e^{t}(\theta^{2}-t) and restrict to a smaller neighborhood, thus assume that \varphi\in C^{2}\big([-\delta,0]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big) for some \delta>0, and
\varphi(0,0)=h(0,0),\qquad\varphi>h\text{\ \ (or $<h$)\ \ in\ \ $\big([-\delta,0]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big)\cap\mathcal{J}\setminus\{(0,0)\}$,}
but
\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi>0\text{\ \ (or $<0$)\ \ in\ \ $[-\delta,0]\times[-\delta,\delta]$.}
For convenience, we also extend \varphi to positive time: by suitably arranging the extension and further decreasing \delta, we may assume that \varphi\in C^{2}\big([-\delta,\delta]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big), and
\left\{\begin{aligned} &h(0,0)=\varphi(0,0)=\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)=0,\\
&\varphi>h\text{\ \ (or $<h$)\ \ in\ \ $\big([-\delta,0]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big)\cap\mathcal{J}\setminus\{(0,0)\}$,}\\
&\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi>0\text{\ \ (or $<0$)\ \ in\ \ $[-\delta,\delta]\times[-\delta,\delta]$.}\end{aligned}\right.
(7.14)
In the context of item (ii), adding \pm\varepsilon e^{t}(\theta^{2}-t) to \varphi does not affect the validity of (7.13). Therefore, to prove the theorem, it suffices to show that \nexists\varphi so that (7.14) holds if in item (i), or (7.14) (7.13) hold if in item (ii) with compatible touching conditions.
If w is a simple cluster, then Z_{t} has the local structure given by Lemma 3.7(i)(ia) (applied with \delta=1 there): at each z\in\cup\gamma_{j}, after a rigid motion that sends z\mapsto 0 and \nu(z)\mapsto-\partial_{y}, there is r>0 so that
Z_{t}\cap Q(r)=\Big\{x\in I,g_{1}(x)<y<g_{2}(x)\Big\},
with I=(-r,r) or I=(-r,a)\cup(b,r) with -r\leqslant a\leqslant b\leqslant r, and g_{1},g_{2} have the properties as in Lemma 3.7(i). The lifting condition (i.e. each \Theta^{-1}(t,\theta) is a segment) implies:
Lemma 7.4.
It cannot happen that -r<a=b<r. Hence, each Z_{t} is a disjoint union of concave polygons by the definition in Subsection 2.2.
Proof.
Suppose -r<a=b<r occurs for some z. We may restrict to a smaller closed square with a different center, thus assume that a=b=0. After the recentering, we have
Z_{t}\cap\overline{Q(r)}=\Big\{x\in[-r,r]\setminus\{0\},g_{1}(x)<y<g_{2}(x)\Big\},
where g_{1} (resp. g_{2}) is a e^{-9}-Lipschitz, concave (resp. convex) function on [-r,r], with g_{1}(0)=g_{2}(0) and g^{\prime}_{1}(0)=g^{\prime}_{2}(0), and g_{1}<g_{2} on [-r,r]\setminus\{0\}, by Lemma 3.7(i)(ic).
Denote \theta_{0}=g^{\prime}_{1}(0)=g^{\prime}_{2}(0). Consider
I_{1}=\big\{g^{\prime}_{1}(x):x\in[-r,r]\big\},\qquad I_{2}=\{g^{\prime}_{2}(x):x\in[-r,r]\}.
So I_{1},I_{2} are both closed intervals containing \theta_{0}. The lifting condition implies I_{1}\cap I_{2}=\{\theta_{0}\}: indeed, if \theta\in(I_{1}\cap I_{2})\setminus\{\theta_{0}\}, then \Theta^{-1}(t,\arctan\theta)\cap\overline{Q(r)} is not connected. Also, we have g^{\prime}_{1}(r)<g^{\prime}_{2}(r) and g^{\prime}_{1}(-r)>g^{\prime}_{2}(-r), since g_{1}(\pm r)<g_{2}(\pm r). This implies either
I_{1}=\{\theta_{0}\},\qquad I_{2}=[c,d]\ \ (c<\theta_{0}<d)
or
I_{1}=[c,d]\ \ (c<\theta_{0}<d),\qquad I_{2}=\{\theta_{0}\}.
For each s<t close enough to t, there is a C^{1} concave function g_{1,s} and convex function g_{2,s} with g_{1,s}<g_{1}\leqslant g_{2}<g_{2,s}, so that
Z_{s}\cap\overline{Q(r)}=\Big\{x\in[-r,r],\,g_{1,s}(x)<y<g_{2,s}(x)\Big\},
and
g_{1,s}\xrightarrow[s\nearrow t]{C^{1}([-r,r])}g_{1},\qquad g_{2,s}\xrightarrow[s\nearrow t]{C^{1}([-r,r])}g_{2}.
Consider the intervals I_{1,s}=\big\{g^{\prime}_{1,s}(x):x\in[-r,r]\big\}, I_{2,s}=\big\{g^{\prime}_{2,s}(x):x\in[-r,r]\big\}, so I_{1,s}\to I_{1} and I_{2,s}\to I_{2} as s\nearrow t. Hence I_{1,s}\cap I_{2,s}\neq\emptyset for all s close to t. Then \Theta^{-1}(s,\arctan\theta) is not connected whenever \theta\in I_{1,s}\cap I_{2,s}, contradicting our lifting hypothesis.
∎
Figure 27: Impossible case (blue segment represents \sigma, and \chi_{\sigma} is not defined).
Now since Z_{t} is a disjoint union of concave polygons, the sign \chi_{\sigma} in item (ii) is well-defined (we have ruled out cases such as Figure 27). The following two lemmas are useful:
Lemma 7.5.
\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta)\Subset\Omega implies \theta\in\Int(J_{t}), where J_{t}=\big((t,\theta)\mapsto\theta\big)(\Theta(\gamma_{t})).
Proof.
