The shape of Z_{t} in a neighborhood of \Theta^{-1}(t,\theta) falls into one of the cases in Figure 28 (\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta) is displayed as blue segments). Note that \theta\in\Int(J_{t}) in either case.
Figure 28: Local models in Lemma 7.5.
∎
Lemma 7.6.
The existence of test function \varphi implies 0\in\gamma_{0}, \nu(0)=-\partial_{y}, \Theta(0)=(0,0).
Proof.
Recall that we have reduced to the case h(0,0)=\varphi(0,0)=\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)=0. In the context of item (ii), our assumption (7.5) implies
0=\varphi(0,0)\nu_{0}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)\tau_{0}\in\sigma\subset\gamma_{0}.
Then \sigma=\Theta^{-1}(0,0) implies \Theta(0)\in\Theta(\sigma)=(0,0). For item (i), Lemma 7.5 implies that h(0,\cdot) is defined in a neighborhood of \theta=0. Then \varphi(0,0)=h(0,0) and \varphi\leqslant h or \varphi\geqslant h implies
0=\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)\in[\partial_{\theta}^{-}h(0,0),\partial_{\theta}^{+}h(0,0)],
where [a,b]=[b,a] if a>b. On the other hand, (2.11) implies
\displaystyle\Theta^{-1}(0,0)
\displaystyle=\Big\{h(0,0)\nu_{0}+c\tau_{0}:c\in[\partial_{\theta}^{-}h(0,0),\partial_{\theta}^{+}h(0,0)]\Big\}
\displaystyle=[\partial_{\theta}^{-}h(0,0),\partial_{\theta}^{+}h(0,0)]\times\{0\}.
Hence 0\in\Theta^{-1}(0,0)\subset\gamma_{0}. Finally, \Theta(0)=(0,0) always implies \nu(0)=-\partial_{y}.
∎
Depending on whether 0 lies in a region D_{i} or on a ridge, and whether \varphi touches h from above or below, the arguments are different. Hence, a case discussion is needed.
Case 1: w is a weak IMCF, or 0 lies in some region D_{i} in the cluster. So w is a weak IMCF in a small neighborhood of 0. (Warning: we cannot shrink \Omega and assume that w is a weak IMCF in \Omega, since the condition \Theta^{-1}(0,0)\Subset\Omega in the theorem is not local.) We may reverse the orientation as in Remark 7.2(iv) and assume \chi_{i}=1. Then observe that \nu is the inner unit normal of \partial Z_{t}, so in the context of item (ii), we have \chi_{\sigma}=1 and hence the suitable touching condition is \varphi\leqslant h.
Recall 0\in\gamma_{0} and \nu(0)=-\partial_{y}, thus the IMCF is downward evolving near 0. For r>0, consider the region
P=[-r,r]\times[-r,r].
Fix r\ll 1 so that P\Subset\Omega, and w is a weak IMCF in a neighborhood of P, and
|\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10},\qquad|\Theta|<e^{-10}\qquad\text{in}\ \ P\cap\Gamma.
(7.15)
Recall that \gamma_{t}\to\gamma_{0} graphically in C^{1} as t\nearrow 0. Thus, there is T<0 so that \gamma_{t}\cap P is the graph of a C^{1,1} convex function g_{t}:[-r,r]\to[-r/2,r/2] for each t\in[T,0]. Also, we have
g_{t}\xrightarrow[t\nearrow 0]{C^{1}([-r,r])}g_{0},\qquad g_{0}(0)=g^{\prime}_{0}(0)=0.
(7.16)
Denote
\underline{\theta}_{t}=\arctan g^{\prime}_{t}(-r),\qquad\overline{\theta}_{t}=\arctan g^{\prime}_{t}(r).
These are the angles of \gamma_{t} at their intersections with \partial P. The angle parameter \Theta(\gamma_{t}\cap P) may take range in [\underline{\theta}_{t}+2k_{t}\pi,\overline{\theta}_{t}+2k_{t}\pi] for some k_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}, but (7.15) forces k_{t}=0 for all t\in[T,0]. Hence
\Theta(\gamma_{t}\cap P)=[\underline{\theta}_{t},\overline{\theta}_{t}]\qquad\forall\,t\in[T,0],
(7.17)
with
\Theta\big((x,g_{t}(x))\big)=\arctan g^{\prime}_{t}(x),\qquad\forall\,t\in[T,0].
(7.18)
The following lemma shows the joint continuity of g_{t}.
Claim 1.
The map (t,x)\mapsto g_{t}(x) is upper semi-continuous in [T,0]\times[-r,r]. If T^{\prime}\in[T,0] and \rho\in[0,r] satisfy
\Int\big(\{w=t\}\big)\cap\{-\rho\leqslant x\leqslant\rho\}=\emptyset\qquad\forall\,t\in[T^{\prime},0],
then (t,x)\mapsto g_{t}(x) is continuous in [T^{\prime},0]\times[-\rho,\rho].
Proof.
Note that t\mapsto g_{t}(x) is decreasing for each x\in[-r,r], and
\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{s}=g_{t},\qquad\lim_{s\searrow t}g_{s}=g_{t}^{+}\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{1}([-r,r]),
(7.19)
where g_{t}^{+} is such that \graphh(g_{t}^{+})=\partial\{w\leqslant t\}\cap P. Since w is downward evolving, we have g_{t}^{+}\leqslant g_{t} in [-r,r]. This shows upper semi-continuity. If there are T^{\prime},\rho as stated, then we have g_{t}=g_{t}^{+} in [-\rho,\rho] for all t\in[T^{\prime},0], so the continuity follows from (7.19).
∎
With the above setups, we are ready to prove the viscosity properties.
Case 1(a). Supersolution. Suppose otherwise that there is \delta>0 and \varphi\in C^{2}\big([-\delta,\delta]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big), so that
\left\{\begin{aligned} &h(0,0)=\varphi(0,0)=\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)=0,\\
&\varphi<h\text{\ \ in\ \ $\big([-\delta,0]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big)\cap\mathcal{J}\setminus\{(0,0)\}$,}\\
&\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi<0\text{\ \ in\ \ $[-\delta,\delta]\times[-\delta,\delta]$.}\end{aligned}\right.
(7.20)
As this case appears in both items (i) and (ii), we have 0\in\gamma_{0} by Lemma 7.6, but we are not guaranteed \Theta^{-1}(0,0)\Subset\Omega. The touching condition implies:
Claim 2.
\partial_{t}\varphi(0,0)\geqslant 0.
The proof is postponed to the end of this case. Once Claim 2 is shown, we may add a small positive multiple of t to \varphi, thus assume that
\left\{\begin{aligned} &h(0,0)=\varphi(0,0)=\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)=0,\\
&\varphi<h\text{\ \ in\ \ }\big([-\delta,0]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big)\cap\mathcal{J}\setminus\{(0,0)\},\\
&0<\partial_{t}\varphi<\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi+\varphi\text{\ \ in\ \ }[-\delta,\delta]\times[-\delta,\delta].\end{aligned}\right.
(7.21)
Consider the comparison curves
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}=\Big\{\varphi(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}:\theta\in(-\delta,\delta)\Big\},\qquad\forall\,t\in(-\delta,\delta),
(7.22)
and the region that they sweep out
S=\bigcup_{t\in(-\delta,\delta)}\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}.
(7.23)
We may further decrease \delta, so that \delta<e^{-10} and S\Subset\Int(P). Now 0<\partial_{t}\varphi<\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi+\varphi implies that \{\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\} forms a downward evolving smooth supersolution of IMCF in S (note: in a supersolution of IMCF, the normal speed is smaller than 1/\kappa).
Choose a small \rho<r so that Q(\rho)\Subset S. Then find T^{\prime}\in[T,0) so that \widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\cap\overline{Q(\rho)}, for each t\in[T^{\prime},0], is the graph of a strictly convex function \widetilde{g}_{t}:[-\rho,\rho]\to[-\rho/10,\rho/10]. Note that \widetilde{g}_{0}(0)=\widetilde{g}^{\prime}_{0}(0)=0 since \varphi(0,0)=\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)=0. By strict convexity and continuity, we may further increase T^{\prime} and find a constant \mu>0, so that
\widetilde{g}^{\prime}_{t}(-\rho)<-\tan(2\mu),\quad\widetilde{g}^{\prime}_{t}(\rho)>\tan(2\mu),\qquad\forall\,t\in[T^{\prime},0].
(7.24)
This means that the angle parameters of \widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\cap\overline{Q(\rho)} contain [-2\mu,2\mu].
Finally, choose \rho^{\prime}<\rho so that |\Theta|<\mu in \overline{Q(\rho^{\prime})}. Consider \gamma^{\prime}_{t}=\gamma_{t}\cap Q(\rho^{\prime}). We need to compare the position of \gamma^{\prime}_{t} and \widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\cap\overline{Q(\rho)}=\graphh(\widetilde{g}_{t}) (the domains of these two graphs are different). A convenient statement is as follows (see Figure 29 for illustration):
Claim 3.
Let \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R} be the convex function obtained by extending \widetilde{g}_{t} in the C^{1} manner by gluing two linear functions on (-\infty,-\rho] and [\rho,\infty). Then
\gamma^{\prime}_{t}\subset\big\{y<\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)\big\}\qquad\forall\,t\in[T^{\prime},0),
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}\cap\big\{y\geqslant\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{0}(x)\big\}=\{0\}.
Proof.
Clearly, \gamma^{\prime}_{t} is a subset of \graphh(g_{t}) by restricting the domain to (-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}). Let us also consider the convex function \mathsf{g}_{t} so that \graphh(\mathsf{g}_{t}) is the linear extension of \gamma^{\prime}_{t} to \mathbb{R}. By construction of \rho^{\prime}, we have
\mathsf{g}^{\prime}_{t}\geqslant-\tan\mu\ \ \text{at}\ \ -\infty,\qquad\mathsf{g}^{\prime}_{t}\leqslant\tan\mu\ \ \text{at}\ \ +\infty.
In comparison, (7.24) implies
\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime}_{t}<-\tan(2\mu)\ \ \text{at}\ \ -\infty,\qquad\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime}_{t}>\tan(2\mu)\ \ \text{at}\ \ +\infty.
Hence, there is a unique c\in\mathbb{R} so that \graphh(\mathsf{g}_{t}+c) touches \graphh(\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}) from below at some point z=(x,y). Denote \theta=\arctan\mathsf{g}^{\prime}_{t}(x)=\arctan\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime}_{t}(x), so \theta\in[-\mu,\mu]. Notice that the support functions of \graphh(\mathsf{g}_{t}), \graphh(\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}) are restrictions of h(t,\cdot) and \varphi(t,\cdot). Hence
\varphi(t,\theta)=\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta}\rangle=h(t,\theta)-c\cos\theta>\varphi(t,\theta)-c\cos\theta\qquad\text{if}\ \ (t,\theta)\neq(0,0).
This implies, if t<0, that c>0 hence \mathsf{g}_{t}<\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}; and if t=0, that c\geqslant 0 and only touching point has \theta=0. The latter, along with \mathsf{g}_{0}(0)=\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{0}(0)=0, implies \{\mathsf{g}_{0}\geqslant\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{0}\}=\{0\}.
Figure 29: Comparing linear extensions of curves from their support functions.
∎
Summarizing heuristically, we have curves \{\gamma^{\prime}_{t}\} evolving by 1/\kappa and curves \{\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\} evolving more slowly than 1/\kappa, so that \widetilde{\gamma}_{t} lies above \gamma^{\prime}_{t} for each t<0, but \widetilde{\gamma}_{0} touches \gamma_{0} at 0. This should violate the maximum principle. The precise derivation is as follows.
Choose T^{\prime\prime}\in[T^{\prime},0) so that |\widetilde{g}_{t}|<\rho^{\prime}/2 in [-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}] for all t\in[T^{\prime\prime},0]. Claim 3 implies
g_{t}<\widetilde{g}_{t}\leqslant\rho^{\prime}/2\text{\ \ in\ \ $[-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}]$,}\qquad\forall\,t\in[T^{\prime\prime},0),
and
\{g_{0}\geqslant\widetilde{g}_{0}\}\cap[-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}]=\{0\}.
Notice that \widetilde{g}_{t}(x) is jointly continuous in t and x, and g_{t}(x) is jointly upper semi-continuous by Claim 1. Hence, there is a small positive constant y_{1}>0 so that
\displaystyle g_{t}(x)<\widetilde{g}_{t}(x)-y_{1}
(7.25)
\displaystyle\forall(t,x)\in\Big([T^{\prime\prime},0]\times[-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}]\Big)\setminus\Big([T^{\prime\prime}/2,0]\times[-\rho^{\prime}/2,\rho^{\prime}/2]\Big)
Consider the region
\Omega^{\prime}=\Big\{\!-\rho^{\prime}<x<\rho^{\prime},\ -\rho^{\prime}<y<\widetilde{g}_{T^{\prime\prime}}(x)\Big\}.
Then the function
w_{1}(x,y)=\min\big\{w(x,y-y_{1}),0\big\}
is a weak IMCF in \Omega^{\prime}, see Remark 2.13(iii). Define the function \widetilde{w} so that \widetilde{w}=t on \graphh(\widetilde{g}_{t}) for all t\leqslant 0, and \widetilde{w}=0 in \{y\leqslant\widetilde{g}_{0}(x)\}. Then \widetilde{w} is a weak supersolution of IMCF in \Omega^{\prime}, see Remark 2.13(i)(iii). Note that
\{w_{1}<t\}=\Omega^{\prime}\cap\{y>g_{t}(x)+y_{1}\},\qquad\{\widetilde{w}<t\}=\Omega^{\prime}\cap\{y>\widetilde{g}_{t}(x)\},\qquad\forall\,t\in[T^{\prime\prime},0).
So (7.25) implies that w_{1}\leqslant\widetilde{w} in a neighborhood of \partial\Omega^{\prime} in \overline{\Omega^{\prime}}, namely, \{w_{1}>\widetilde{w}\}\Subset\Omega^{\prime}. However,
w_{1}(0,y_{1})=\min\{w(0,0),0\}=0>\widetilde{w}(0,y_{1}).
These violate the maximum principle, see Remark 2.13(iv), hence proving Case 1(a).
Proof of Claim 2.
Suppose \partial_{t}\varphi(0,0)<0. If \partial_{\theta}^{2}\varphi(0,0)>0, then \varphi would be nonnegative in a backward neighborhood of (0,0). On the other hand, for each t\in[T,0), there is a point z_{t}\in\gamma_{t}\cap P with the smallest distance from 0, at which we have
h(t,\Theta(z_{t}))=\langle z_{t}\,,\,\nu(z_{t})\rangle=-|z_{t}|<0.
This contradicts \varphi\leqslant h. Hence \partial_{\theta}^{2}\varphi(0,0)\leqslant 0. Replacing \varphi by \varphi-\varepsilon\theta^{2} for some \varepsilon\ll 1, and further decreasing \delta, we may assume
\partial_{t}\varphi<\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi+\varphi<0\qquad\text{in}\ \ [-\delta,\delta]\times[-\delta,\delta].
Then consider the comparison curve \widetilde{\gamma}_{t} as in (7.22). We may decrease \delta and assume that \widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\Subset\Int(P) for all t\in[-\delta,0]. Then \widetilde{\gamma}_{t} is the graph of a strictly concave function \widetilde{g}_{t} over some interval in [-r,r]. Extend g_{t},\widetilde{g}_{t} linearly to \mathbb{R} in the manner of Claim 3, and denote the resulting functions by \mathsf{g}_{t},\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}. Note that
\displaystyle\mathsf{g}^{\prime}_{0}(-r)=g^{\prime}_{0}(-r)\leqslant 0,\qquad\mathsf{g}^{\prime}_{0}(r)=g^{\prime}_{0}(r)\geqslant 0;
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime}_{t}(-r)=\tan\delta,\qquad\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime}_{t}(r)=-\tan\delta,\qquad\forall\,t\in[-\delta,0].
Therefore, there exists t<0 so that \mathsf{g}^{\prime}_{t}(-r)<\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime}_{t}(-r) and \mathsf{g}^{\prime}_{t}(r)>\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime}_{t}(r). Then, note that
\mathsf{g}_{t}>\mathsf{g}_{0}\geqslant 0\geqslant\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{0}>\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}\qquad\text{in}\ \ [-r,r],
where the last inequality comes from \varphi(0,\cdot)<\varphi(t,\cdot) and the argument in Claim 3. These together imply \mathsf{g}_{t}>\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t} in \mathbb{R}. Hence, there is c>0 so that \graphh(\mathsf{g}_{t}) contacts \graphh(\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}+c) tangentially at a point z=(x,y). Denoting \theta=\arctan\mathsf{g}^{\prime}_{t}(x)=\arctan\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime}_{t}(x), we have
h(t,\theta)=\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta}\rangle={\big\langle{z-(0,c)}\,,\,{\nu_{\theta}}\big\rangle}-c\cos\theta=\varphi(t,\theta)-c\cos\theta,
contradicting \varphi\leqslant h.
∎
Case 1(b). Subsolution. Suppose otherwise that there is \delta>0 and \varphi\in C^{2}\big([-\delta,\delta]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big), so that
\left\{\begin{aligned} &h(0,0)=\varphi(0,0)=\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)=0,\\
&\varphi>h\text{\ \ in\ \ $\big([-\delta,0]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big)\cap\mathcal{J}\setminus\{(0,0)\}$,}\\
&\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi>0\text{\ \ in\ \ $[-\delta,\delta]\times[-\delta,\delta]$.}\end{aligned}\right.
(7.26)
Only item (i) is not vacuous for this case, thus \Theta^{-1}(0,0)\Subset\Omega by our assumption.
Claim 4.
We have g_{0}(\pm r)>0, g^{\prime}_{0}(-r)<0, g^{\prime}_{0}(r)>0, (\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi+\varphi)(0,0)>0.
Proof.
Denote \sigma=\Theta^{-1}(0,0)\Subset\Omega. Then Lemma 7.6 implies 0\in\sigma. Write \sigma=[x_{1},x_{2}]\times\{0\} with x_{1}\leqslant 0\leqslant x_{2}. Recall that we have reduced to the case where \nu(0)=-\partial_{y} and w is positively oriented in a neighborhood of 0. Hence, the shape of Z_{0} near \sigma must fall in one of the cases in Figure 30 (if \sigma is a point, then only case 1 can happen since w is an IMCF near 0). It follows that (see Table 1) in either case
\partial_{\theta}^{-}h(0,0)=x_{1},\qquad\partial_{\theta}^{+}h(0,0)=x_{2}.
Since \varphi touches h from above at (0,0), this forces x_{1}=x_{2}=0. Hence \sigma is a point, and g_{0}(\pm r)>0. Hence g^{\prime}_{0}(-r)<0 and g^{\prime}_{0}(r)>0 by convexity. Finally, (\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi+\varphi)(0,0)>0 follows from \partial_{\theta\theta}h+h>0 in the barrier sense.
Figure 30: Local models in Claim 4.
∎
We may further decrease \delta and assume that
\left\{\begin{aligned} &h(0,0)=\varphi(0,0)=\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)=0,\\
&\varphi>h\text{\ \ in\ \ $\big([-\delta,0]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big)\cap\mathcal{J}\setminus\{(0,0)\}$,}\\
&0<\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi+\varphi<\partial_{t}\varphi\text{\ \ in\ \ $[-\delta,\delta]\times[-\delta,\delta]$.}\end{aligned}\right.
(7.27)
By Claim 4 and g_{t}\to g_{0} in C^{1}([-r,r]), there is \varepsilon>0 and T^{\prime}\in[T,0) so that
g^{\prime}_{t}(-r)<-\tan(2\varepsilon),\qquad g^{\prime}_{t}(r)>\tan(2\varepsilon),\qquad\forall\,t\in[T^{\prime},0].
(7.28)
Consider the comparison curves \{\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\} and swept out region S as in (7.22) (7.23). We may decrease \delta so that \delta<\varepsilon and S\Subset\Int(P). Then 0<\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi+\varphi<\partial_{t}\varphi implies that \{\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\} forms a downward evolving smooth subsolution of IMCF (the normal speed is greater than 1/\kappa). Let \rho be small enough so that Q(\rho)\Subset S.
Using the same comparison argument as in Claim 3, noticing that \widetilde{\gamma}_{t} have angle \delta<\varepsilon while \graphh(g_{t}) have angle >2\varepsilon at endpoints, we obtain
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\subset\{y<g_{t}(x)\}\quad\forall\,t\in[T^{\prime},0),\qquad\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\cap\{y\geqslant g_{0}(x)\}=\{0\}.
(7.29)
Claim 5.
There is T^{\prime\prime}\in[T^{\prime},0) and \rho^{\prime}<\rho, so that
\left\{\begin{aligned} &|g_{t}|<\rho^{\prime}/2\\
&\Int\big(\{w=t\}\big)\cap\{-\rho^{\prime}\leqslant x\leqslant\rho^{\prime}\}=\emptyset\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\forall\,t\in[T^{\prime\prime},0].
Thus (t,x)\mapsto g_{t}(x) is continuous in [T^{\prime\prime},0]\times[-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}].
Proof.
Let g_{t}^{+} be such that \graphh(g_{t}^{+})=\partial\{w\leqslant t\}\cap P. Recall that in a weak IMCF, the set \partial\{w\leqslant t\}\setminus\partial\{w<t\} is a minimal surface. Then the set I_{t}:=\{x:g_{t}^{+}(x)<g_{t}(x)\} is either [-r,r] or is of the form [-r,a)\cup(b,r] with -r\leqslant a\leqslant b\leqslant r. Moreover, g_{t}^{+} is linear in each connected component of \{g_{t}^{+}<g_{t}\}.
Recall that g_{0}\in C^{1,1}([-r,r]) and g_{0}(0)=g^{\prime}_{0}(0)=0 and g_{0}(\pm r)>0. Thus there exists \rho^{\prime}<\rho so that
g_{0}(\pm 2\rho^{\prime})<\frac{r-2\rho^{\prime}}{r-\rho^{\prime}}g_{0}(\pm\rho^{\prime})+\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{r-\rho^{\prime}}g_{0}(\pm r).
Since g_{t}^{+}\to g_{0} in C^{1}([-r,r]) as t\nearrow 0, there is T^{\prime\prime}\in[T^{\prime},0) so that
g_{t}^{+}(\pm 2\rho^{\prime})<\frac{r-2\rho^{\prime}}{r-\rho^{\prime}}g_{t}^{+}(\pm\rho^{\prime})+\frac{\rho^{\prime}}{r-\rho^{\prime}}g_{t}^{+}(\pm r),\qquad\forall\,t\in[T^{\prime\prime},0].
(7.30)
It follows that I_{t}\cap[-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}]=\emptyset for all t\in[T^{\prime\prime},0]: otherwise, I_{t} would contain either [-r,-\rho^{\prime}] or [\rho^{\prime},r], and g_{t}^{+} would be linear in these intervals, contradicting (7.30). Then I_{t}\cap[-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}]=\emptyset implies the no-jump statement. Finally, the continuity statement follows from Claim 1, and the condition |g_{t}|<\rho^{\prime}/2 follows from further increasing T^{\prime\prime}.
∎
For each t\in[T^{\prime\prime},0], let \widetilde{g}_{t}:[-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}]\to[-\rho^{\prime}/2,\rho^{\prime}/2] be so that \widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\cap\overline{Q(\rho^{\prime})}=\graphh(\widetilde{g}_{t}). By (7.29) and Claim 5, there is a small y_{1}>0 so that
\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{t}(x)<g_{t}(x)-y_{1},
(7.31)
\displaystyle\forall\,(t,x)\in\Big([T^{\prime\prime},0]\times[-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}]\Big)\setminus\Big([T^{\prime\prime}/2,0]\times[-\rho^{\prime}/2,\rho^{\prime}/2]\Big).
To summarize, we have a downward evolving weak IMCF \{\gamma_{t}\} and a subsolution \{\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\}, so that each \widetilde{\gamma}_{t} lies strictly below \gamma_{t} with the only exception \widetilde{\gamma}_{0}\cap\gamma_{0}=\{0\}. Using the same maximum principle argument as in the end of Case 1(a), we may derive a contradiction.
To summarize, we have just proved Theorem 7.1 for the case where w is a weak IMCF in a neighborhood of 0.
We are not able to prove (i) when \Theta^{-1}(0,0)\not\Subset\Omega: the obstruction is that \varphi>h may no longer imply that \widetilde{\gamma}_{t} lies below \gamma_{t}. See for example Figure 31: here \widetilde{\gamma}_{0} lies above \gamma_{0} in the x>0 part, but we cannot rule this out from \varphi\geqslant h since the angle parameter of \gamma_{0} does not contain the \theta>0 portion.
Figure 31: A case where \varphi\geqslant h but \widetilde{\gamma}_{t} does not lie below \gamma_{t}.
Case 2: w is a simple cluster, and 0 lies on a ridge \gamma. We still denote
P=[-r,r]\times[-r,r].
Recall that 0\in\gamma_{0} and \nu(0)=-\partial_{y}. For r small enough, we may assume
|\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10},\quad|\Theta|<e^{-10}\qquad\text{in\ \ }P\cap\Gamma.
Let f_{\ridge}:[-r,r]\to[-e^{-9}r,e^{-9}r] be the e^{-9}-Lipschitz function so that
\gamma\cap P=\graphh(f_{\ridge}).
Denote
D_{1}=P\cap\{y<f_{\ridge}(x)\},\qquad D_{2}=P\cap\{y>f_{\ridge}(x)\}.
Thus, w consists of an upward evolving weak IMCF in D_{1} and downward evolving weak IMCF in D_{2}. Note that D_{1} has negative orientation (i.e. \chi_{1}=-1).
Recall the local model of Z_{0} given by Lemma 3.7(i)(ia), and that Lemma 7.4 excludes -r<a=b<r. Hence, up to a 180^{\circ} rotation and orientation reversing (Remark 7.2(iv)), we fall into one of the two cases below depending on whether 0 is a vertex or not:
1.
after further decreasing r, we have
Z_{0}\cap P=\Big\{x\in(0,r],\ g_{0,1}(x)<y<g_{0,2}(x)\Big\},
(7.32)
where g_{0,1} is concave, and g_{0,2} is convex, and g_{0,1}(0)=g_{0,2}(0)=g^{\prime}_{0,1}(0)=g^{\prime}_{0,2}(0)=0;
2.
after further decreasing r, we have
Z_{0}\cap P=\Big\{x\in[-r,r],\ y<g_{0,2}(x)\Big\},
(7.33)
where g_{0,2} is convex with g_{0,2}(0)=g^{\prime}_{0,2}(0)=0 and g_{0,2}\geqslant f_{\ridge}.
We first assume that possibility 2 holds. Then the (well-defined) new function
w^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &w\qquad\text{in}\ \ \big\{|x|<r,\ g_{0,2}(x)\leqslant y<r\big\}\\
&0\qquad\text{in}\ \ \big\{|x|<r,\ -r<y\leqslant g_{0,2}(x)\big\}\end{aligned}\right.
is a weak IMCF in \Int(P), by Remark 2.9(vi). Consider the restricted data in Q(r):
\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}_{t}=\partial\{w^{\prime}<t\}\cap Q(r)\subset\gamma_{t},\qquad\Gamma^{\prime}=\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\gamma^{\prime}_{t}\subset\Gamma,\qquad\nu^{\prime}=\nu|_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
\displaystyle\Theta^{\prime}=\Theta|_{\Gamma^{\prime}},\qquad\mathcal{J}^{\prime}=\Theta^{\prime}(\Gamma^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{J},\qquad h^{\prime}=h|_{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}.
Clearly, \Theta^{\prime} is a lift of (t,\nu^{\perp})|_{\Gamma^{\prime}} to \mathbb{R}\times M, with each preimage being a line segment. Since 0\in\gamma^{\prime}_{0} and \Theta^{\prime}(0)=(0,0), we have h^{\prime}(0,0)=0 as well. If \varphi touches h from below, then \varphi also touches h^{\prime} from below in \mathcal{J}^{\prime}. If (\Theta^{\prime})^{-1}(0,0)\Subset Q(r), then by the IMCF case of Theorem 7.1(i), which was already proved in Case 1, we have (\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi)(0,0)\geqslant 0. If (\Theta^{\prime})^{-1}(0,0)\not\Subset Q(r), then note that
\varphi(0,0)\nu_{0}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)\tau_{0}=0\in(\Theta^{\prime})^{-1}(0,0),
hence (7.5) is satisfied for w^{\prime}. Notice that \chi_{\sigma}=1 for our case here. Hence the IMCF case of Theorem 7.1(ii) implies (\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi)(0,0)\geqslant 0 as well. If \varphi touches h from above, then we have assumed \Theta^{-1}(0,0)\Subset\Omega. Similar to Claim 4, this implies that \Theta^{-1}(0,0) is a point, hence (\Theta^{\prime})^{-1}(0,0)=\{0\}\Subset Q(r). So the IMCF case of Theorem 7.1(i) gives (\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi)(0,0)\leqslant 0, as desired.
It remains to treat the case (7.32). Let us summarize the known conditions: we have
Z_{0}\cap P=\Big\{x\in(0,r],\ g_{0,1}(x)<y<g_{0,2}(x)\Big\},
(7.34)
where g_{0,1}:[0,r]\to[-e^{-9}r,0] is concave, g_{0,2}:[0,r]\to[0,e^{-9}r] is convex, the graphs of both functions are \nu-orthogonal, and g_{0,1}(0)=g_{0,2}(0)=g^{\prime}_{0,1}(0)=g^{\prime}_{0,2}(0)=0, and g_{0,1}\leqslant f_{\ridge}\leqslant g_{0,2} in [0,r], and g_{0,1}<g_{0,2} in (0,r], by Lemma 3.7(i)(ia)(i)(ic).
Next, using Lemma 3.7(i)(ib), we have
\widetilde{Z}_{0}\cap P=\Big\{x\in I,\ \widetilde{g}_{0,1}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant\widetilde{g}_{0,2}(x)\Big\},
where either I=[-r,r] or I=[-r,a]\cup[b,r], and \widetilde{g}_{0,1},\widetilde{g}_{0,2} are concave resp. convex in each interval in I. Since Z_{0}=\Int(\widetilde{Z}_{0}), it holds \widetilde{g}_{0,1}=g_{0,1} and \widetilde{g}_{0,2}=g_{0,2} in [0,r]. By (7.34), we must have \widetilde{g}_{0,1}=\widetilde{g}_{0,2} in I\cap[-r,0]. Therefore, depending on whether I=[0,r] or not, we fall into one of the following two possibilities by further decreasing r:
\widetilde{Z}_{0}\cap P=\Big\{x\in[0,r],\ g_{0,1}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant g_{0,2}(x)\Big\},
(7.35)
or
\widetilde{Z}_{0}\cap P=\Big\{x\in[-r,r],\ \widetilde{g}_{0,1}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant\widetilde{g}_{0,2}(x)\Big\},
(7.36)
where \widetilde{g}_{0,i}=g_{0,i} in [0,r] and \widetilde{g}_{0,i}\equiv 0 in [-r,0]. If the second possibility holds, then
w_{1}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &w\qquad\text{in}\ \ \big\{|x|<r,\ -r<y\leqslant\widetilde{g}_{0,1}(x)\big\}\\
&0\qquad\text{in}\ \ \big\{|x|<r,\ \widetilde{g}_{0,1}(x)\leqslant y<r\big\}\end{aligned}\right.
and
w_{2}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &w\qquad\text{in}\ \ \big\{|x|<r,\ \widetilde{g}_{0,2}(x)\leqslant y<r\big\}\\
&0\qquad\text{in}\ \ \big\{|x|<r,\ -r<y\leqslant\widetilde{g}_{0,2}(x)\big\}\end{aligned}\right.
are respectively an upward evolving and downward evolving weak IMCF in \Int(P), by Remark 2.9(vi). Note that w_{1} is negatively oriented. If \varphi touches h from below, then we may restrict the data to w_{2} as in the previous page, then use the IMCF case of Theorem 7.1 to obtain (\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi)(0,0)\geqslant 0. If \varphi touches h from above, then we may reflect around the x axis and change the orientation for w_{1} (see Remark 7.2(vii)). Then \varphi would touch h from below, and Theorem 7.1 implies the desired viscosity property as well.
Now, we may assume that (7.35) holds. We give further setups on Z_{t}\cap P for t<0. By Lemma 3.7(i)(ia) and Lemma 7.4 and Z_{t}\supset Z_{0}, we have
Z_{t}\cap P=\Big\{x\in I_{t},\ g_{t,1}(x)<y<g_{t,2}(x)\Big\},
where I_{t}=[-r,r] or I_{t}=[-r,a_{t})\cup(b_{t},r] with -r\leqslant a_{t}<b_{t}<0. By (7.35) and \widetilde{Z}_{0}=\bigcap_{t<0}Z_{t} and that Z_{t} is decreasing in t, there exists T<0 so that
Z_{t}\cap P=\Big\{x_{t}<x\leqslant r,\ g_{t,1}(x)<y<g_{t,2}(x)\},\qquad\forall\,t\in[T,0].
(7.37)
(Namely, I_{t} does not contain the interval [-r,a_{t}) for all t\in[T,0].) Also, note that \lim_{t\nearrow 0}x_{t}=0. We may further increase T and assume that x_{t}\in[-r/2,0] for all t\in[T,0].
Lemma 3.7(i)(ic) implies that g_{t,1}:[x_{t},r]\to[-r/2,r/2] is concave and e^{-9}-Lipschitz, and g_{t,2}:[x_{t},r]\to[-r/2,r/2] is convex and e^{-9}-Lipschitz, and the graphs of both functions are \nu-orthogonal. Regarding the relation with the ridge, we have
g_{t,1}(x)\leqslant f_{\ridge}(x)\leqslant g_{t,2}(x)\qquad\forall\,x\in[x_{t},r],
and
g_{t,1}(x)<g_{t,2}(x)\qquad\forall\,x\in(x_{t},r],
and
g_{t,1}(x_{t})=f_{\ridge}(x_{t})=g_{t,2}(x_{t}),\qquad g^{\prime}_{t,1}(x_{t})=f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(x_{t})=g^{\prime}_{t,2}(x_{t}).
The vertex x_{t} satisfies
x_{t}\in[-r/2,0]\quad\forall\,t\in[T,0],\qquad\text{and}\qquad x_{t}\nearrow 0\ \ \text{as}\ \ t\nearrow 0.
By the convergence and \nu-orthogonality, we also have
g_{t,1}\xrightarrow[\text{increasing}]{C^{1}([0,r])}g_{0,1},\qquad g_{t,2}\xrightarrow[\text{decreasing}]{C^{1}([0,r])}g_{0,2}\qquad\text{as}\ \ t\nearrow 0.
We are ready to prove the viscosity property. We first reduce all statements to one case. If \Theta^{-1}(0,0)\Subset\Omega, then concave polygon geometry (Figure 6, Table 1) yields
\exists\,\varphi\text{ touching $h$ from below}\quad\Rightarrow\quad g_{0,1}(x)<0\ \ \ \forall\,x>0,
(7.38)
and
\exists\,\varphi\text{ touching $h$ from above}\quad\Rightarrow\quad g_{0,2}(x)>0\ \ \ \forall\,x>0.
(7.39)
Through a reflection around the x-axis and an orientation reversal, see Remark 7.2(vii), proving the first case (7.38) implies the second case (7.39). Next, if \sigma:=\Theta^{-1}(0,0)\not\Subset\Omega, then either g_{0,1}\equiv 0 or g_{0,2}\equiv 0. If g_{0,2}\equiv 0, then g_{0,1}<0 in (0,r]. Then, the definition of \chi_{\sigma} in Theorem 7.1 implies \chi_{\sigma}=1, so \varphi should touch h from below in item (ii) of the theorem. If g_{0,1}\equiv 0, then g_{0,2}>0 in (0,r], hence \chi_{\sigma}=-1, hence \varphi should touch h from above. Through a reflection around x-axis and orientation reversal, the first case implies the second case as well. Therefore, we are reduced to assuming the condition
g_{0,1}<0\ \ \text{in}\ \ (0,r],\qquad\varphi\text{ touches $h$ from below,}
(7.40)
and trying to prove
(\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi)(0,0)\geqslant 0.
(7.41)
Recall that g_{0,1} is concave with g_{0,1}(0)=g^{\prime}_{0,1}(0)=0, so g^{\prime}_{0,1}(r)<0. Since \lim_{t\nearrow 0}g_{t,1}=g_{0,1} in C^{1}([0,r]), we may further increase T and find an \varepsilon>0, so that
g^{\prime}_{t,1}(r)<-\tan(2\varepsilon)\qquad\forall\,t\in[T,0].
(7.42)
It remains to prove the essentially unique case (7.40) (7.41).
Figure 32: Shape of Z_{t}.
Case 2(a). If the viscosity property fails, then by our reduction at the beginning of the entire proof, there are \delta<\min\{\varepsilon,e^{-10}\} and \varphi\in C^{2}\big([-\delta,\delta]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big) so that
\left\{\begin{aligned} &h(0,0)=\varphi(0,0)=\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)=0,\\
&\varphi<h\text{\ \ in\ \ $\big([-\delta,0]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big)\cap\mathcal{J}\setminus\{(0,0)\}$,}\\
&\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi<0\text{\ \ in\ \ $[-\delta,\delta]\times[-\delta,\delta]$.}\end{aligned}\right.
(7.43)
Claim 6.
(\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi+\varphi)(0,0)\leqslant 0.
Proof.
The local model (7.34) implies \partial_{\theta}^{-}h(0,0)=0. So for each \mu with 0<\mu\ll 1, there is a constant c_{\mu} so that \varphi(0,\theta)-\mu\theta+c_{\mu} touches h from below at a point \theta_{\mu}\in(-\delta,0). The concavity of g_{0,1} implies that (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(0,\theta)<0 in the barrier sense for all \theta\in(-\delta,0)\subset(\arctan g^{\prime}_{0,1}(r),0). By (7.43) we have c_{\mu}\to 0 and \theta_{\mu}\to 0 when \mu\to 0. Hence
\displaystyle(\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi+\varphi)(0,0)
\displaystyle=\lim_{\mu\to 0}\Big[\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi(0,\theta_{\mu})+\varphi(0,\theta_{\mu})-\mu\theta_{\mu}+c_{\mu}\Big]
\displaystyle=\lim_{\mu\to 0}\Big[(\partial_{\theta\theta}+\id)\big(\varphi-\mu\theta+c_{\mu}\big)\Big](0,\theta_{\mu})\leqslant 0.\qed
Thus, by subtracting a small multiple of \theta^{2} from \varphi, then further decreasing \delta, we may assume
\left\{\begin{aligned} &h(0,0)=\varphi(0,0)=\partial_{\theta}\varphi(0,0)=0,\qquad\delta<\varepsilon,\\
&\varphi<h\text{\ \ in\ \ $\big([-\delta,0]\times[-\delta,\delta]\big)\cap\mathcal{J}\setminus\{(0,0)\}$,}\\
&\partial_{t}\varphi<\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi+\varphi<0\text{\ \ in\ \ $[-\delta,\delta]\times[-\delta,\delta]$.}\end{aligned}\right.
(7.44)
Define the comparison curves \{\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\} and region S similarly as the previous case:
\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}=\Big\{\varphi(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}:\theta\in(-\delta,\delta)\Big\},\qquad S=\bigcup_{t\in(-\delta,\delta)}\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}.
Now \{\widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\} is an upward evolving subsolution of IMCF (\text{speed}>1/\kappa), with 0\in\widetilde{\gamma}_{0}. We may decrease \delta and assume that S\Subset\Int(P).
For convenience, we merge f_{\ridge} and g_{t,1} into a C^{1} function:
G_{t}(x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &f_{\ridge}(x)\qquad x\in[-r,x_{t}],\\
&g_{t,1}(x)\qquad x\in[x_{t},r].\end{aligned}\right.
The continuity of G_{t} and comparison between \gamma_{t},\widetilde{\gamma}_{t} are stated as follows:
Claim 7.
There are T^{\prime}\in[T,0) and \rho<r so that G_{t}(x) is jointly continuous for (t,x)\in[T^{\prime},0]\times[-\rho,\rho].
Claim 8.
There is T^{\prime\prime}\in[T^{\prime},0) and \rho^{\prime}<\rho, so that
g_{T^{\prime\prime},1}<f_{\ridge}\qquad\text{in}\ \ [-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}],
and \widetilde{\gamma}_{t}\cap\{|x|\leqslant\rho^{\prime}\}=\graphh(\widetilde{g}_{t}) with
\widetilde{g}_{t}(x)>G_{t}(x)\qquad\forall\,(t,x)\in[T^{\prime\prime},0]\times[-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}]\setminus\{(0,0)\}.
Combining Claims 7 and 8, there is y_{1}>0 so that
\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{t}(x)>G_{t}(x)+y_{1}
(7.45)
\displaystyle\forall(t,x)\in\Big([T^{\prime\prime},0]\times[-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}]\Big)\setminus\Big([T^{\prime\prime}/2,0]\times[-\rho^{\prime}/2,\rho^{\prime}/2]\Big).
Finally, consider the region
\displaystyle A=\Big\{\!-\rho^{\prime}<x<\rho^{\prime},\ g_{T^{\prime\prime},1}(x)<y\leqslant f_{\ridge}(x)\Big\}
together with the outer obstacle
O=\Big\{\!-\rho^{\prime}<x<\rho^{\prime},\ y=f_{\ridge}(x)\Big\}.
Let \widetilde{w} be the function in A so that \widetilde{w}=t on \widetilde{\gamma}_{t}-(0,y_{1}) for each t\leqslant 0, and \widetilde{w}\equiv 0 elsewhere. Then \widetilde{w} is a weak subsolution of IMCF in \Int(A), see Remark 2.13(iii). On the other hand, by the definition of simple clusters, w|_{A} is a continuously calibrated weak IMCF in \Int(A) with outer obstacle O. Finally, note that
\{w<t\}\cap A\subset\big\{y\leqslant G_{t}(x)\big\}\cap A,\quad\{\widetilde{w}<t\}\cap A=\big\{y<\widetilde{g}_{t}(x)-y_{1}\big\}\cap A,
thus (7.45) implies
\{\widetilde{w}>w\}\Subset A.
Moreover, we have
\widetilde{w}(0,-y_{1})=0>w(0,-y_{1}).
These together violate the maximum principle with obstacle, see Remark 2.13(v).
Proof of Claim 7.
Recall D_{1}=\{y<f_{\ridge}(x)\}, and \{w<t\}\cap D_{1}=\{y<G_{t}(x)\}. Since \graphh(G_{t}) is orthogonal to the continuous vector field \nu, it follows that \{G_{t}(x)\} is a uniformly C^{1} family of functions for t\in[T,0]. Also, we have
\lim_{s\nearrow t}G_{s}=G_{t}\leqslant G_{t}^{+}=\lim_{s\searrow t}G_{s}\qquad\text{in}\ \ [-r,r],
(7.46)
where G_{t}^{+}(x) is the function so that \{w\leqslant t\}\cap\overline{D_{1}}=\{y\leqslant G_{t}^{+}(x)\}.
We claim that \{G_{t}<G_{t}^{+}\} must be of the form (b,r] for some b\in[x_{t},r]. If this is not true, then there is a component (b,c) of \{G_{t}<G_{t}^{+}\} with x_{t}\leqslant b<c<r. Then w is constant in the region \big\{b\leqslant x\leqslant c,\ G_{t}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant G_{t}^{+}(x)\big\}, and the divergence theorem gives
\big|\graphh(G_{t}|_{[b,c]})\big|=\big|\graphh(G_{t}^{+}|_{[b,c]})\big|.
(7.47)
However, G_{t} is concave in (b,c) since G_{t}<G_{t}^{+}\leqslant f_{\ridge}. Then (7.47) is impossible to hold.
jump
Figure 33: Objects in Claim 7.
Recall G_{0}(0)=G^{\prime}_{0}(0)=0, and G_{0} is concave in [0,r] with G_{0}(r)<0. Similar to Claim 5, we may find T^{\prime}<0 and \rho<r so that G_{t}^{+} cannot be a linear function in [2\rho,r] for any t\in[T^{\prime},0]. This implies \{G_{t}^{+}>G_{t}\}\subset(\rho,r] for all t\in[T^{\prime},0]: indeed, if not, then G_{t}<G_{t}^{+}<G_{0}\leqslant f_{\ridge} in (\rho,r], hence \graphh(G_{t}^{+}|_{(\rho,r]}) is a line segment (since it does not touch the obstacle \graphh(f_{\ridge})), contradicting our nonlinearity conclusion above.
∎
Proof of Claim 8.
Let \widetilde{g}_{t} be the (concave) function so that \widetilde{\gamma}_{t}=\graphh(\widetilde{g}_{t}), so \widetilde{g}_{t} is defined in an interval that depends continuously on t. Recall that for all t\in[T,0], there are concave functions g_{t,1}:[x_{t},r]\to[-r/2,r/2] and convex functions g_{t,2}:[x_{t},r]\to[-r/2,r/2], so that \gamma_{t}\cap P=\graphh(g_{t,1})\cup\graphh(g_{t,2}). Set
\theta_{t}=\arctan g^{\prime}_{t,1}(x_{t})=\arctan g^{\prime}_{t,2}(x_{t})=\arctan f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(x_{t})
the angles of the vertices. We collect the following known facts:
•
x_{t}\in[-r/2,0] for all t\in[T,0], and x_{t}\nearrow 0 and \theta_{t}\to 0 as t\nearrow 0;
•
g_{s,1}(x)<g_{t,1}(x) and g_{s,2}(x)>g_{t,2}(x) for all T\leqslant s<t\leqslant 0 and x\in[x_{t},r];
•
g_{t,1}\nearrow g_{0,1} and g_{t,2}\searrow g_{0,2} in C^{1}([0,r]), as t\nearrow 0;
•
g^{\prime}_{t,1}(r)<-\tan(2\varepsilon) for all t\in[T,0], by (7.42), and \delta<\varepsilon by our setups;
•
\widetilde{g}_{t}<0 for all t<0;
•
(t,x)\mapsto G_{t}(x) is jointly continuous in [T^{\prime},0]\times[-\rho,\rho], where T^{\prime}\in[T,0] and \rho<r. Here we made use of Claim 7.
We may find T_{1}\in[T^{\prime}/2,0] so that
•
|\theta_{t}|<\delta/2 for all t\in[2T_{1},0];
•
0\in\dom(\widetilde{g}_{t}) for all t\in[2T_{1},0].
To summarize, we have the angle relations
-2\varepsilon<-\delta<-\delta/2<\theta_{t}<\delta/2<\delta,\qquad\forall\,t\in[T_{1},0].
Next, we select a \rho_{1}<\rho so that the following holds:
•
\dom(\widetilde{g}_{t})\Supset[-\rho_{1},\rho_{1}] for all t\in[T_{1},0];
•
Q(\rho_{1})\Subset Z_{T_{1}}; thus x_{T_{1}}<-\rho_{1} and g_{T_{1},1}(x)<-\rho_{1}, g_{T_{1},2}(x)>\rho_{1} for all x\in[-\rho_{1},\rho_{1}];
•
x_{T_{1}/2}<-\rho_{1} and g_{T_{1}/2,1}<f_{\ridge} in [-\rho_{1},\rho_{1}].
To summarize, g_{t,1},g_{t,2} both have domains [x_{t},r], while [-\rho_{1},\rho_{1}]\Subset\dom(\widetilde{g}_{t})\Subset(-r,r).
Let us extend g_{t,1}, g_{t,2}, \widetilde{g}_{t} in the C^{1} manner from their own domains to \mathbb{R}, by attaching two linear functions at their endpoints (same as in Claim 3). Let \mathsf{g}_{t,1}, \mathsf{g}_{t,2}, \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t} denote the resulting functions. Consider the convex sets
A_{t}^{1}=\big\{y\leqslant\mathsf{g}_{t,1}(x)\big\},\qquad A_{t}^{2}=\big\{y\geqslant\mathsf{g}_{t,2}(x)\big\},\qquad\widetilde{A}_{t}=\big\{y\leqslant\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)\big\},
See Figure 34 for a visualization. Note that A_{t}^{1}\cap A_{t}^{2}=\{x\leqslant x_{t},y=\mathsf{g}_{t,1}(x)=\mathsf{g}_{t,2}(x)\} is a ray. The support functions of \partial A_{t}^{1},\partial A_{t}^{2} are given by
h(t,\cdot)|_{[\arctan g^{\prime}_{t,1}(r),\theta_{t}]}\qquad\text{and}\qquad h(t,\cdot)|_{[\theta_{t},\arctan g^{\prime}_{t,2}(r)]}
respectively, while the support function of \partial\widetilde{A}_{t} coincides with \varphi(t,\cdot). Since t\mapsto\varphi(t,\theta) is decreasing for each \theta, the argument in Claim 3 implies that t\mapsto\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x) is strictly increasing in t for each x\in\mathbb{R}. In [-\rho_{1},\rho_{1}] we clearly have \mathsf{g}_{t,1}=g_{t,1} and \mathsf{g}_{t,2}=g_{t,2} and \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}=\widetilde{g}_{t}, for all t\in[T_{1},0], so we may freely switch between notations.
The comparison \varphi<h (with exception \varphi(0,0)=h(0,0)=0) implies: \nexists\,c\geqslant 0 so that \partial\widetilde{A}_{t}+(0,c) contacts tangentially with \partial A_{t}^{1}\cup\partial A_{t}^{2} for t<0 or \partial\widetilde{A}_{0}+(0,c) contacts tangentially with \partial A_{0}^{1}\cup\partial A_{0}^{2} at any nonzero angle.
Figure 34: Objects in Claim 8.
Claim 9.
A_{t}^{2}\cap\widetilde{A}_{t} must have a nonempty interior for all t\in[T_{1},0).
Proof.
Otherwise, we may translate \partial\widetilde{A}_{t} upward so that it meets \partial A_{t}^{2} tangentially.
∎
Claim 10.
A_{t}^{2}\cap\widetilde{A}_{t} is compact for all t\in[T_{1},0].
Proof.
This follows from the slope comparison \theta_{t}<\delta and -\delta<\theta_{t} at \pm\infty.
∎
By convexity, there are two intersections of \partial\widetilde{A}_{t} and \partial A_{t}^{2}. Denote their x-coordinates by \alpha_{t}<\beta_{t}, for all t\in[T_{1},0). By Claim 9 we have \mathsf{g}_{t,2}<\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t} in (\alpha_{t},\beta_{t}).
Claim 11.
x_{t}<\beta_{t}<0 for all t\in[T_{1},0).
Proof.
The fact \beta_{t}<0 comes from \mathsf{g}_{t,2}>\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t} in [0,\infty), due to
\mathsf{g}_{t,2}|_{[0,r]}\geqslant 0,\quad\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}|_{[0,r]}<0,\quad\mathsf{g}^{\prime}_{t,2}|_{[r,\infty)}\geqslant\tan\theta_{t}>-\tan\delta=\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime}_{t}|_{[r,\infty)}\qquad\forall\,t\in[T_{1},0).
If \beta_{t}\leqslant x_{t} for some t\in[T_{1},0), then \partial\widetilde{A}_{t} would intersect \partial A_{t}^{2}\cap\{x\leqslant x_{t}\} twice, hence intersect \partial A_{t}^{1} at least twice. On the other hand, for a large enough c>0, the translated curve \partial\widetilde{A}_{t}+(0,c) only has one intersection with \partial A_{t}^{1} in \{x<x_{t}\}, due to the slope comparison -2\varepsilon<-\delta at +\infty. So there exists c^{\prime}\geqslant 0 so that \partial\widetilde{A}_{t}+(0,c^{\prime}) contacts \partial A_{t}^{1} tangentially, contradicting the support function comparison.
∎
Claim 12.
\alpha_{t}<\max\{-\rho_{1},x_{t}\} for all t\in[T_{1},0).
Proof.
Suppose \alpha_{t}\geqslant\max\{-\rho_{1},x_{t}\} for some t\in[T_{1},0). Then notice that:
•
\mathsf{g}_{t,2}<\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t} in (\alpha_{t},\beta_{t}), see above Claim 11;
•
s\mapsto\mathsf{g}_{s,2}(x) is decreasing for all x\in[\alpha_{t},\beta_{t}] and s\leqslant t, since [\alpha_{t},\beta_{t}]\subset[x_{t},r];
•
\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{s}(x) is increasing in s for all x\in[\alpha_{t},\beta_{t}] and s\leqslant t;
•
\mathsf{g}_{T_{1},2}(x)>\rho_{1} and \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{T_{1}}(x)<0 for all x\in[-\rho_{1},\rho_{1}], by our choice of \rho_{1}.
By continuity (Claim 7), there is s\in(T_{1},t) so that \mathsf{g}_{s,2} contacts tangentially with \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{s} at some point x\in(\alpha_{t},\beta_{t}). This contradicts the support function comparison.
∎
Claim 13.
\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)>\mathsf{g}_{t,1}(x) for all t\in[T_{1},0) and x\geqslant\max\{-\rho_{1},x_{t}\}.
Proof.
If \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)\leqslant\mathsf{g}_{t,1}(x), then clearly \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)\leqslant\mathsf{g}_{t,2}(x). Hence either \alpha_{t}\geqslant x or \beta_{t}\leqslant x. The former case is ruled out by Claim 12. If the latter case occurs, then note that
\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}>\mathsf{g}_{t,2}\geqslant\mathsf{g}_{t,1}\ \ \text{in}\ \ (\alpha_{t},\beta_{t}),\qquad\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)\leqslant\mathsf{g}_{t,1}(x),\qquad\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x^{\prime})>\mathsf{g}_{t,1}(x^{\prime})\quad\forall\,x^{\prime}\gg 1.
Then \partial\widetilde{A}_{t}+(0,c) contacts \partial A_{t}^{1} tangentially for some c\geqslant 0, contradiction.
∎
Claim 14.
\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)\geqslant f_{\ridge}(x) for all t\in[T_{1},0] and x\in[-\rho_{1},x_{t}], whenever x_{t}\geqslant-\rho_{1}.
Proof.
Suppose \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)<f_{\ridge}(x) for some t\in[T_{1},0] and -\rho_{1}\leqslant x\leqslant x_{t}. Then x\leqslant x_{t} implies G_{t}(x)=f_{\ridge}(x). Recall that (t,x)\mapsto G_{t}(x) is continuous in [T_{1},0]\times[-\rho_{1},\rho_{1}]. Next, recall x_{T_{1}}<-\rho_{1} and g_{T_{1},1}<g_{T_{1}/2,1}<f_{\ridge} in [-\rho_{1},\rho_{1}] by our setup, hence G_{T_{1}}(x)<f_{\ridge}(x). By continuity, there is t_{1}\in(T_{1},t) so that \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)<G_{t_{1}}(x)<f_{\ridge}(x). The second inequality implies x>x_{t_{1}}, hence x\geqslant\max\{-\rho_{1},x_{t_{1}}\} and G_{t_{1}}(x)=g_{t_{1},1}(x)=\mathsf{g}_{t_{1},1}(x). By Claim 13 we have \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t_{1}}(x)>\mathsf{g}_{t_{1},1}(x). Finally, these lead to a contradiction since
G_{t_{1}}(x)>\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)>\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t_{1}}(x)>\mathsf{g}_{t_{1},1}(x)=G_{t_{1}}(x).\qed
Claim 15.
\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)>f_{\ridge}(x) for all t\in[T_{1}/2,0] and x\in[-\rho_{1}/2,x_{t}] with (t,x)\neq(0,0).
Proof.
Suppose \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)=f_{\ridge}(x) for some (t,x) as stated. Then x<x_{t} by Claim 13 (recall f_{\ridge}(x_{t})=\mathsf{g}_{t,1}(x_{t})). If x\leqslant x_{s} for some s<t, then Claim 14 implies
\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)>\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{s}(x)\geqslant f_{\ridge}(x),
as desired. The only remaining possibility is \lim_{s\nearrow t}x_{s}\leqslant x<x_{t}. In this case, note that
\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{1,s}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{2,s}=f_{\ridge}\qquad\text{on\ \ }[x,x_{t}],
hence f_{\ridge} is linear on [x,x_{t}]. But \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}=\widetilde{g}_{t} is strictly concave in [-\rho_{1},\rho_{1}], and \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)=f_{\ridge}(x), and \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}\geqslant f_{\ridge} on [-\rho_{1},x_{t}] by Claim 14, which is impossible.
∎
Claim 16.
\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{0}(x)>\mathsf{g}_{0,1}(x) for all x\in(0,\rho_{1}].
Proof.
Recall \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{0}(0)=\mathsf{g}_{0,1}(0)=\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime}_{0}(0)=\mathsf{g}^{\prime}_{0,1}(0)=0 and \widetilde{\mathsf{g}}^{\prime}_{0}(x)=-\tan\delta>-\tan(2\varepsilon)>\mathsf{g}^{\prime}_{0,1}(x) for x\gg 1. If \widetilde{g}_{0}(x)\leqslant g_{0,1}(x) for some x\in(0,\rho_{1}], then \partial\widetilde{A}_{0}+(0,c) would contact \partial A_{0}^{1} tangentially at a point with nonzero slope for some c\geqslant 0, contradiction.
∎
Recalling that
\left.\begin{aligned} G_{t}(x)&=f_{\ridge}(x)\qquad\forall\,x\in[-\rho_{1},x_{t}]\\
G_{t}(x)&=g_{t,1}(x)=\mathsf{g}_{t,1}(x)\qquad\forall\,x\in\big[\!\max\{x_{t},-\rho_{1}\},\rho_{1}\big]\ \ \\
\widetilde{g}_{t}(x)&=\widetilde{\mathsf{g}}_{t}(x)\qquad\forall\,x\in[-\rho_{1},\rho_{1}]\end{aligned}\right\}\ \forall\,t\in[T_{1},0],
Claim 8 now follows from Claims 13, 15, 16, by setting T^{\prime\prime}=T_{1}/2 and \rho^{\prime}=\rho_{1}/2. The fact g_{T^{\prime\prime},1}<f_{\ridge} in [-\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}] follows from our setup for \rho_{1}.
∎
∎
Proof of Theorem 7.3.
(i) Let \gamma be as stated. We may assume t=0, so \gamma\subset\partial Z_{0}\cap\Omega=\partial\{w<0\}\cap\Omega. Let \vartheta be an angle parametrization of \gamma, and J_{0}=\vartheta(\gamma). Fix any \theta\in\Int(J_{0}), denote \sigma=\vartheta^{-1}(\theta).
Claim 1.
\sigma\Subset\Omega.
Proof.
This follows from the connectedness of \gamma and the fact that \vartheta:\gamma\to\mathbb{R} is a monotone map (under a length parametrization) and \theta\in\Int(J_{0}).
∎
It suffices to show that \sigma is a point, and \gamma is a smooth strictly convex curve near \sigma. Once this is done, it follows that
\gamma=\vartheta^{-1}\big(\Int(J_{0})\big)\cup\vartheta^{-1}\big(\partial J_{0}\big),
where the first set is a C^{\infty} strictly smooth curve (and is empty iff \gamma is a line segment), and the second set is the union of two (possibly empty) line segments, as desired.
By symmetry, we may assume \theta=0 and \sigma=[-l,l]\times\{0\} for some l\geqslant 0, and \nu=-\partial_{y} on \sigma. Denote
P=[-l-r,l+r]\times[-r,r].
Fix r\ll 1 so that P\subset\Omega and |\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10} in a neighborhood of P. Then \gamma\cap P is the graph of a e^{-9}-Lipschitz convex function g_{0}:[-l-r,l+r]\to[-e^{-9}r,e^{-9}r] with g_{0}|_{[-l,l]}=0 and g_{0}>0 elsewhere. Fix
a=\frac{1}{2}\min\big\{g_{0}(-l-r),g_{0}(l+r)\big\},\qquad\delta=\frac{1}{2}\arctan\min\big\{\!-g^{\prime}_{0}(-l-r),g^{\prime}_{0}(l+r)\big\}.
Note that a,\delta>0. For each t<0, let g_{t},g_{t}^{+} be such that
\partial\{w<t\}\cap P=\graphh(g_{t}),\qquad\partial\{w\leqslant t\}\cap P=\graphh(g_{t}^{+}).
Since \lim_{t\nearrow 0}g_{t}=g_{0} in C^{1}([-l-r,l+r]), there is {\underline{T}}<0 so that
g_{2{\underline{T}}}\leqslant r/10\ \ \text{in}\ \ [-l-r,l+r],\qquad g_{2{\underline{T}}}(0)\leqslant a,
(7.48)
and
g^{\prime}_{t}(-l-r)<-\tan(1.5\delta),\quad g^{\prime}_{t}(l+r)>\tan(1.5\delta)\qquad\forall\,t\in[2{\underline{T}},0].
(7.49)
Recall that g_{t},g_{t}^{+} are convex for all t\in[{\underline{T}},0], and satisfy
\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{s}=g_{t}\geqslant g_{t}^{+},\qquad\lim_{s\searrow t}g_{s}=\lim_{s\searrow t}g_{s}^{+}=g_{t}^{+}\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{1}([-l-r,l+r]).
Denote
\Gamma_{0}=\Big(\bigcup_{t\in({\underline{T}},0]}\graphh(g_{t})\Big)\cup\Big(\bigcup_{t\in[{\underline{T}},0)}\graphh(g_{t}^{+})\Big).
Note that \Gamma_{0} is compact. Since |\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}, the map (t,\nu^{\perp}) on \Gamma_{0} has a unique lift
\Theta_{0}:\Gamma_{0}\to\mathbb{R}\times(-e^{-10},e^{-10}).
Claim 2.
Each preimage \Theta_{0}^{-1}(t,\theta) in \Gamma\cap P is a line segment.
Proof.
Each preimage of t is \graphh(g_{t})\cup\graphh(g_{t}^{+}). By the property of jumps, the set \{x:g_{t}^{+}(x)<g_{t}(x)\} is either [-l-r,l+r] or is of the form [-l-r,c)\cup(d,l+r] with -l-r\leqslant c\leqslant d\leqslant l+r. And g_{t}^{+} is linear in each interval where g_{t}^{+}<g_{t}. Since
g_{t}^{+}(0)<g_{2{\underline{T}}}(0)\leqslant a<g_{0}\big(\!\pm(l+r)\big)<g_{t}^{+}\big(\!\pm(l+r)\big),
we have \{g_{t}^{+}<g_{t}\}=[-l-r,c)\cup(d,l+r] with -l-r\leqslant c\leqslant d\leqslant l+r, namely, it cannot be [-l-r,l+r]. Then note that g_{t}=g_{t}^{+} and g^{\prime}_{t}=(g_{t}^{+})^{\prime} at c,d, thus
\Theta_{0}^{-1}(t,\theta)=\Big\{(x,g_{t}(x)):g^{\prime}_{t}(x)=\tan\theta\Big\}\cup\Big\{(x,g_{t}^{+}(x)):(g_{t}^{+})^{\prime}(x)=\tan\theta\Big\}
is a line segment.
∎
Denote \overline{\mathcal{J}}=\Theta_{0}(\Gamma_{0}). Then \overline{\mathcal{J}} is compact since \Gamma_{0} is compact and \Theta_{0} is continuous. The continuous function z\mapsto\langle z\,,\,\nu\rangle induces a support function h:\overline{\mathcal{J}}\to\mathbb{R} by Claim 2, and h is continuous since \Theta_{0} is a quotient map.
To use Theorem 7.1, we need to consider the restricted data
\Gamma=\bigcup_{t\in({\underline{T}},0]}\gamma_{t}\cap\Int(P),\qquad\Theta=\Theta_{0}|_{\Gamma},\qquad\mathcal{J}=\Theta(\Gamma).
The set \Gamma here aligns with the one in Theorem 7.1 with \Omega:=\Int(P)\cap\{y<g_{\underline{T}}(x)\}. We clearly have \mathcal{J}\subset\overline{\mathcal{J}}, and the support function on \mathcal{J} is given by h|_{\mathcal{J}}, which is also continuous in \mathcal{J}. Consider R=({\underline{T}},0]\times(-\delta,\delta). Then (7.49) implies R\subset\mathcal{J} and \Theta^{-1}(t,\theta)\Subset\Omega for all (t,\theta)\in R. Using Theorem 7.1(i) on the weak IMCF \min\{w,0\} in \Omega, and then using the classical viscosity theory, h is a smooth solution of \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h in R. Hence h(0,\cdot) is smooth in (-\delta,\delta) and satisfies \partial_{\theta\theta}h+h>0. Hence \sigma is a point and \gamma is a smooth strictly convex curve near \sigma.
(ii) Denote \gamma_{t}=\partial\{w<t\}\cap Q_{\delta}(4) and \gamma_{t}^{+}=\partial\{w\leqslant t\}\cap Q_{\delta}(4), so they are e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitz convex graphs over (-4,4) whenever they intersect \overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}. Denote P=[-3,3]\times[-3.5\delta,3.5\delta]. Let g_{t},g_{t}^{+}:[-3,3]\to(-3.5\delta,3.5\delta) be so that
\gamma_{t}\cap P=\graphh(g_{t}),\qquad\gamma_{t}^{+}\cap P=\graphh(g_{t}^{+}),
whenever these curves intersect \overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}. Thus g_{t},g_{t}^{+} are decreasing in t, and g_{t}\geqslant g_{t}^{+}, and
\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{s}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{s}^{+}=g_{t},\qquad\lim_{s\searrow t}g_{s}=\lim_{s\searrow t}g_{s}^{+}=g_{t}^{+}\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{1}([-3,3]).
(7.50)
Denote a_{t}=\arctan g^{\prime}_{t}(-3) and b_{t}=\arctan g^{\prime}_{t}(3).
Given any z_{0}\in Q_{\delta}(2)\cap\gamma_{t_{0}} so that \gamma_{t_{0}} is smooth and strictly convex near z_{0}. We need to show that
the curvature of \gamma_{t_{0}} at z_{0} is at most e^{-7}\delta.
(7.51)
Once this is done, recalling that |\nabla w| is the curvature of \gamma_{t} almost everywhere for almost every t, this implies the theorem since z_{0} is arbitrary.
Let \theta_{0}=\arctan g^{\prime}_{t_{0}}(x_{0}) where x_{0} is the first coordinate of z_{0}. Consider
\displaystyle{\underline{T}}
\displaystyle=\min\Big\{t:\gamma_{t}^{+}\cap\overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}\neq\emptyset,\ \text{$g_{s}^{+}$ is not a linear function in}
(7.52)
\displaystyle\text{$[-3,3]$ for all $s\in(t,t_{0})$}\Big\}.
By the fact that z_{0}\in Q_{\delta}(2) and g_{t_{0}} is not linear and (7.50), this minimum is attained with {\underline{T}}<t_{0}. The choice of {\underline{T}} is aimed to avoid large jumps in ({\underline{T}},t_{0}). Denote
\Gamma_{0}=\Big(\bigcup_{t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}]}\graphh(g_{t})\Big)\cup\Big(\bigcup_{t\in[{\underline{T}},t_{0})}\graphh(g_{t}^{+})\Big).
Then \Gamma_{0} is compact by (7.50). Let
\Theta_{0}:\Gamma_{0}\to(-e^{-20}\delta^{2},e^{-20}\delta^{2})\times(-e^{-10}\delta,e^{-10}\delta)
be the unique continuous lift of (t,\nu^{\perp}). The range of \Theta_{0} follows from (7.7). Then \overline{\mathcal{J}}:=\Theta_{0}(\Gamma_{0}) is compact. Arguing as in Claim 2 using the nonlinearity condition in (7.52), one may show that each preimage of \Theta_{0} is a line segment in \Gamma_{0}. Hence, the induced support function h is well-defined and is continuous in \overline{\mathcal{J}}. We prove a technical lemma:
Claim 3.
\partial\overline{\mathcal{J}} is a Jordan loop.
Proof.
For each t\in[{\underline{T}},t_{0}), denote a_{t}^{+}=\arctan(g_{t}^{+})^{\prime}(-3) and b_{t}^{+}=\arctan(g_{t}^{+})^{\prime}(3). Then
\lim_{s\searrow t}a_{s}=\lim_{s\searrow t}a_{s}^{+}=a_{t}^{+}<b_{t}^{+}=\lim_{s\searrow t}b_{s}^{+}=\lim_{s\searrow t}b_{s},\qquad\forall\,t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}),
(7.53)
where a_{t}^{+}<b_{t}^{+} follows from the nonlinearity in the choice of {\underline{T}}, and
\lim_{s\searrow{\underline{T}}}a_{s}=\lim_{s\searrow{\underline{T}}}a_{s}^{+}=a_{\underline{T}}^{+}\leqslant b_{\underline{T}}^{+}=\lim_{s\searrow{\underline{T}}}b_{s}^{+}=\lim_{s\searrow{\underline{T}}}b_{s},
(7.54)
and a_{t}\leqslant a_{t}^{+}, b_{t}\geqslant b_{t}^{+} for all t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}) due to the geometry of jumps, and
\lim_{s\nearrow t}a_{s}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}a_{s}^{+}=a_{t}<b_{t}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}b_{s}^{+}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}b_{s},\qquad\forall\,t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}].
(7.55)
Observe that \partial\overline{\mathcal{J}} is the union of the two connected compact sets
\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}
\displaystyle=\Big(\bigcup_{t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0})}\{t\}\times[a_{t},a_{t}^{+}]\Big)\cup\big\{({\underline{T}},a_{\underline{T}}^{+}),(t_{0},a_{t_{0}})\big\},
\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}
\displaystyle=\Big(\bigcup_{t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0})}\{t\}\times[b_{t}^{+},b_{t}]\Big)\cup\big\{({\underline{T}},b_{\underline{T}}^{+}),(t_{0},b_{t_{0}})\big\},
and the two segments \overline{J}_{t_{0}}, \overline{J}_{\underline{T}}, where these portions only intersect at endpoints (\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{\underline{T}} may be a point). Thus, it is sufficient to show that \Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2} are homeomorphic to [0,1].
We prove the case for \Gamma_{1}. Define an order on \Gamma_{1}, where (s,\omega)\prec(t,\theta) if s<t or s=t and \omega<\theta. Then \prec is a linear order, and ({\underline{T}},a_{\underline{T}}^{+}) is minimal, and (t_{0},a_{t_{0}}) is maximal. By the convergences (7.53) (7.54) (7.55), \Gamma_{1}\cap R is open in the order topology for any open box R in \mathbb{R}^{2}. Let us show that any open interval in the order topology is open in the subspace topology. Given (t,\theta)\in\Gamma_{1} and an open interval I\ni(t,\theta).
•
If t={\underline{T}}, then I contains \big((-\infty,t+\varepsilon)\times\mathbb{R}\big)\cap\Gamma_{1} for some \varepsilon>0.
•
If t=t_{0}, then I contains \big((t-\varepsilon,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\big)\cap\Gamma_{1} for some \varepsilon>0.
•
Suppose t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}). If a_{t}=a_{t}^{+}, then I\supset\big((t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon)\times\mathbb{R}\big)\cap\Gamma_{1} for some \varepsilon>0.
•
If t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}) and a_{t}\leqslant\theta<a_{t}^{+}, then I contains \big((t-\varepsilon,t)\times\mathbb{R}\big)\cap\Gamma_{1} and \big(\{t\}\times(-\infty,\theta+2\varepsilon)\big)\cap\Gamma_{1}, for some \varepsilon>0. But (7.53) implies \big([t,t+\varepsilon^{\prime})\times(-\infty,\theta+\varepsilon)\big)\cap\Gamma_{1}\subset\{t\}\times(-\infty,\theta+\varepsilon) for some \varepsilon^{\prime}>0. Hence I\supset\big((t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon^{\prime})\times(-\infty,\theta+\varepsilon)\big)\cap\Gamma_{1}.
•
If t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}) and a_{t}<\theta\leqslant a_{t}^{+}, then arguing similarly, we may show that I contains \big((t-\varepsilon,t+\varepsilon^{\prime})\times(\theta-\varepsilon,\infty)\big)\cap\Gamma_{1} for some small \varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}>0.
Hence, the order topology coincides with the subspace topology. Therefore, \Gamma_{1} with the order topology is connected, compact, metrizable, with endpoints. Hence \Gamma_{1}\cong[0,1], by [65, Theorem 6.16, 6.17].
∎
To use Theorem 7.1, we again need to consider the restricted data
\displaystyle\Gamma=\bigcup_{t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}]}\gamma_{t}\cap\Int(P),\qquad\Theta=\Theta_{0}|_{\Gamma},\qquad\Omega=\Int(P)\cap\{y<g_{\underline{T}}(x)\}
\displaystyle J_{t}=\Theta\big(\gamma_{t}\cap\Int(P)\big),\qquad\mathcal{J}=\Theta(\Gamma)=\bigcup_{t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}]}\{t\}\times J_{t}.
Then \mathcal{J}\subset\overline{\mathcal{J}}, and \Theta is a lift of (t,\nu^{\perp}) on \Gamma. The t-slice of \overline{\mathcal{J}} is equal to [a_{t},b_{t}], which also coincides with the closure of J_{t}. We thus denote it by \overline{J}_{t}. Note that \overline{J}_{t} is not a point for all t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}]: otherwise, \gamma_{t}\cap\Int(P) would be a line segment, hence \gamma_{t}^{+}\cap\Int(P) is also a line segment, contradicting (7.52).
Claim 4.
If t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}] and \theta\in\Int(J_{t}), then there is \varepsilon>0 so that
R\subset\mathcal{J},\qquad\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta)\Subset\Omega\ \ \forall\,(t,\theta)\in R,\qquad\text{where}\qquad R:=(t-\varepsilon,t]\times(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon).
Proof.
The conditions imply g^{\prime}_{t}(-3)<\tan\theta<g^{\prime}_{t}(3) and \graphh(g_{t})\cap Q_{\delta}(3)\neq\emptyset. Since \lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{s}=g_{t} in C^{1}([-3,3]), there is \varepsilon<t-{\underline{T}} so that g^{\prime}_{s}(-3+\varepsilon)<\tan(\theta-\varepsilon) and g^{\prime}_{s}(3-\varepsilon)>\tan(\theta+\varepsilon) and \graphh(g_{s})\cap Q_{\delta}(3)\neq\emptyset for all s\in[t-\varepsilon,t], as desired.
∎
Using Theorem 7.1(i) in \Omega with the data \Gamma,\Theta,\mathcal{J}, and using Claim 4, we have that h is a smooth solution of \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h in a backward box near each t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}] and \theta\in\Int(J_{t}). Hence, h(t,\cdot) is smooth and satisfies \partial_{\theta\theta}h+h>0 in each \Int(J_{t}). Recall that \partial_{\theta\theta}h+h is the inverse of curvature.
Suppose the curvature bound (7.51) fails. Recall (t_{0},\theta_{0})=\Theta(z_{0}), so (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t_{0},\theta_{0})<e^{7}\delta^{-1}. We need to obtain a contradiction. Consider
\phi(t,\theta)=h(t_{0},\theta_{0})\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})+\partial_{\theta}h(t_{0},\theta_{0})\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})+\frac{e^{7}e^{t-t_{0}}}{\delta}\Big((t-t_{0})+\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\theta_{0})^{2}\Big),
which solves \partial_{t}\phi=\partial_{\theta\theta}\phi+\phi. We first obtain some preliminary estimates. The conditions z_{0}\in Q_{\delta}(2) and |t|,|t_{0}|<e^{-20}\delta^{2} and |\theta|,|\theta_{0}|<e^{-10}\delta and \delta\leqslant 1 imply
\displaystyle|h(t_{0},\theta_{0})|
\displaystyle=|\langle z_{0}\,,\,\nu_{\theta_{0}}\rangle|\leqslant|x_{0}|\,|\sin\theta_{0}|+|y_{0}|\,|\cos\theta_{0}|\leqslant 2e^{-10}\delta+2\delta\leqslant 2.1\delta,
(7.56)
\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}h(t_{0},\theta_{0})|
\displaystyle=|\langle z_{0}\,,\,\tau_{\theta_{0}}\rangle|\leqslant|x_{0}|\,|\cos\theta_{0}|+|y_{0}|\,|\sin\theta_{0}|\leqslant 2+2e^{-10}\delta^{2}\leqslant 2.1,
(7.57)
and also
\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}\phi|
\displaystyle\leqslant|h(t_{0},\theta_{0})|\,|\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})|+|\partial_{\theta}h(t_{0},\theta_{0})|+e^{7}e^{t-t_{0}}\delta^{-1}|\theta-\theta_{0}|
(7.58)
\displaystyle\leqslant 2.1\delta\cdot 2e^{-10}\delta+2.1+e^{7}e^{2e^{-20}\delta^{2}}\delta^{-1}\cdot 2e^{-10}\delta
\displaystyle\leqslant 2.3,\qquad\qquad\forall\,(t,\theta)\in(-e^{-20}\delta^{2},e^{-20}\delta^{2})\times(-e^{-10}\delta,e^{-10}\delta).
Denoting \eta=\phi-h, we have \eta(t_{0},\theta_{0})=\partial_{\theta}\eta(t_{0},\theta_{0})=0 by construction, and
\partial_{\theta}^{2}\eta(t_{0},\theta_{0})=-\partial_{\theta}^{2}h(t_{0},\theta_{0})-h(t_{0},\theta_{0})+e^{7}\delta^{-1}>0.
So there exist t_{1}\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}) and \theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<\theta_{3}\in\Int(J_{t_{1}}) with
\eta(t_{1},\theta_{1})>0,\qquad\eta(t_{1},\theta_{2})<0,\qquad\eta(t_{1},\theta_{3})>0.
For any \varepsilon>0, consider the function
\psi=e^{-\varepsilon t-t}\eta.
Claim 5.
Suppose L is relatively open in \overline{\mathcal{J}}. Let \partial^{\prime}L be the relative boundary of L in \overline{\mathcal{J}}.
(i)
If \psi\leqslant 0 in \overline{L} and \min_{\overline{L}}\psi<\min_{\partial^{\prime}L}\psi, then \psi cannot be minimized in \overline{L} at a point (t,\theta) with t>{\underline{T}} and \theta\in\Int(\overline{J_{t}}).
(ii)
If \psi\geqslant 0 in \overline{L} and \max_{\overline{L}}\psi>\max_{\partial^{\prime}L}\psi, then \max_{\overline{L}}\psi cannot be attained in \{t>{\underline{T}}\}.
Proof.
Note that
\partial_{t}\psi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\psi=e^{-\varepsilon t-t}\big(\partial_{t}\eta-\partial_{\theta\theta}\eta-\varepsilon\eta-\eta\big)=-\varepsilon\psi
in each backward box near (t,\theta) with t\in({\underline{T}},t_{0}], \theta\in\Int(\overline{J}_{t}). Then (i) follows from the standard maximum principle. Suppose that \psi is maximized in \overline{L} at a point (t,\theta) with t>{\underline{T}}. Then again we must have \theta\in\partial\overline{J_{t}}. Recall that we defined a_{t}=\arctan g^{\prime}_{t}(-3) and b_{t}=\arctan g^{\prime}_{t}(3), so \overline{J_{t}}=[a_{t},b_{t}]. We may assume \theta=a_{t} (same argument for the case \theta=b_{t}). The maximizing condition implies
\partial_{\theta}\phi(t,a_{t})\leqslant\partial_{\theta}^{+}h(t,a_{t}).
The quantity \partial_{\theta}^{+}h(t,a_{t}) makes sense since \overline{J}_{t} is not a point (see above Claim 4). Combined with (7.58), we have \partial_{\theta}^{+}h(t,a_{t})\geqslant-2.3. Observe that h(t,\cdot) coincides with the support function of \gamma_{t}\cap P, thus (2.9) gives
\Theta_{0}^{-1}(t,a_{t})\cap\gamma_{t}\cap P=\Big\{h(t,a_{t})\nu_{a_{t}}+c\tau_{a_{t}}:c\leqslant\partial_{\theta}^{+}h(t,a_{t})\Big\}\cap P.
The facts \partial_{\theta}^{+}h(t,a_{t})\geqslant-2.3 and |\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}\delta imply that \Theta_{0}^{-1}(t,a_{t})\cap\gamma_{t}\cap P is a nontrivial line segment in \gamma_{t}. Hence a_{t}\in J_{t} as well. Denoting \sigma=\Theta^{-1}(t,a_{t})\cap\Int(P)\neq\emptyset, we have
\sigma=\Big\{h(t,a_{t})\nu_{a_{t}}+c\tau_{a_{t}}:c\leqslant\partial_{\theta}^{+}h(t,a_{t})\Big\}\cap\Int(P).
(7.59)
And clearly, we have \sigma\not\Subset\Omega.
We are ready to apply the boundary viscosity principle in Theorem 7.1(ii). Denote \lambda=\psi(t,a_{t})=\max_{\overline{L}}\psi>0, and set \varphi=\phi-e^{\varepsilon t+t}\lambda. We clearly have
\varphi(t,a_{t})=h(t,a_{t}).
Since L is relatively open in \overline{\mathcal{J}}, and (t,\theta)\notin\partial^{\prime}L by our assumption, there is \delta>0 so that
\varphi\leqslant h\qquad\text{in}\ \ \Big([t-\delta,t]\times[\theta-\delta,\theta+\delta]\Big)\cap\overline{\mathcal{J}}.
Denote z=\varphi(t,a_{t})\nu_{a_{t}}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi(t,a_{t})\tau_{a_{t}}. We need to show z\in\sigma. Since \varphi(t,a_{t})=h(t,a_{t}) and \partial_{\theta}\varphi(t,a_{t})=\partial_{\theta}\phi(t,a_{t})\leqslant\partial_{\theta}^{+}h(t,a_{t}), z indeed lies in the first part of (7.59). To show z\in\Int(P), we calculate
\displaystyle|\varphi(t,a_{t})|=|h(t,a_{t})|
\displaystyle=\max_{z\in\sigma}|\langle z\,,\,\nu_{a_{t}}\rangle|
\displaystyle\leqslant\max_{(x,y)\in\sigma}\Big(|x|\,|\sin a_{t}|+|y|\Big)\leqslant 3\cdot e^{-10}\delta+3.1\delta\leqslant 3.2\delta,
where |y|\leqslant 3.1\delta follows from \gamma_{t}\cap\overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}\neq\emptyset and e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitzness. We also have
|\partial_{\theta}\varphi(t,a_{t})|=|\partial_{\theta}\phi(t,a_{t})|\leqslant 2.3,
these apply to show z=\varphi(t,a_{t})\nu_{a_{t}}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi(t,a_{t})\tau_{a_{t}}\in\Int(P) in view that |\nu_{a_{t}}+\partial_{y}|\leqslant e^{-10}\delta. Also, recall \chi_{\sigma}=1 from our sign conditions. Finally, we may apply Theorem 7.1(ii) to the solution \min\{w,t_{0}\} in \Omega, and obtain (\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi)(t,a_{t})\geqslant 0. This contradicts
\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi=\underbrace{\partial_{t}\phi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\phi-\phi}_{=0}-\varepsilon e^{\varepsilon t+t}\lambda<0.\qed
Let V_{1},V_{3}\subset\overline{\mathcal{J}} be the connected components of \{\eta>0\} containing (t_{1},\theta_{1}) and (t_{1},\theta_{3}) respectively, and V_{2}\subset\overline{\mathcal{J}} be the connected component of \{\eta<0\} containing (t_{1},\theta_{2}).
Claim 6.
V_{i}\cap\overline{J}_{t}\neq\emptyset for all t\in[{\underline{T}},t_{1}) and i=1,2,3.
Proof.
If V_{1}\cap\overline{J}_{T}=\emptyset for some T\in[{\underline{T}},t_{1}), then by connectivity we have V_{1}\subset\{t>T\}. Since \eta>0 in V_{1} and \eta=0 on \partial^{\prime}V_{1} and on \overline{V_{1}}\cap\{t={\underline{T}}\} (the latter set is nonempty only if t={\underline{T}}), there is a small \varepsilon so that \max_{\overline{V_{1}}}\psi is attained in V_{1}\cap\{t>{\underline{T}}\}. Then Claim 5(ii) with L=V_{1} yields a contradiction. The case of V_{3} may be proved in the same manner.
If V_{2}\cap\overline{J}_{T}=\emptyset for some T\in[{\underline{T}},t_{1}], then for some small \varepsilon>0, the minimum of \psi in \overline{V_{2}} is attained at some (T^{\prime},\theta) with T^{\prime}>{\underline{T}}. By Claim 5(i) we have \theta\in\partial\overline{J}_{T^{\prime}}. But topologically this cannot happen, since V_{1},V_{3} are connected and they both intersect \{t={\underline{T}}\}.
∎
By (7.52), at least one of the possibilities below occurs at time {\underline{T}}:
•
\gamma_{t}^{+}\cap\big([-3,3]\times[-3.2\delta,3.2\delta]\big)=\emptyset for all t<{\underline{T}};
•
g_{t_{i}}^{+} is linear in [-3,3] for a sequence t_{i}\nearrow{\underline{T}};
•
\gamma_{{\underline{T}}}^{+}\cap\overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}\neq\emptyset but \gamma_{t}^{+}\cap\overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}=\emptyset for all t<{\underline{T}}.
If the first case happens, then g_{\underline{T}}^{+} is linear on [-3,3], thus \overline{J}_{\underline{T}} is a point, contradicting Claim 6. If the second case happens, then g_{\underline{T}}=\lim_{i\to\infty}g_{t_{i}}^{+} is linear on [-3,3], hence g_{\underline{T}}^{+} is linear on [-3,3], which is again impossible. So the first two possibilities cannot happen. Since g_{\underline{T}}^{+} is nonlinear, we have g_{\underline{T}}=g_{\underline{T}}^{+} somewhere in [-3,3], hence
\min_{[-3,3]}g_{\underline{T}}^{+}\geqslant\max_{[-3,3]}g_{\underline{T}}-0.1\delta
by e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitzness. Recall that
\lim_{t\nearrow{\underline{T}}}g_{t}^{+}=g_{\underline{T}},\qquad\lim_{t\searrow{\underline{T}}}g_{t}=g_{\underline{T}}^{+}.
This and \gamma_{t}^{+}\cap\overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}=\emptyset for all t<{\underline{T}} implies g_{\underline{T}}\geqslant 3\delta, hence g_{\underline{T}}^{+}\geqslant 2.9\delta, hence g_{t}>2.8\delta for all t>{\underline{T}} close enough to {\underline{T}}. For these t and all \theta\in\overline{J_{t}}, we have
\displaystyle h(t,\theta)
\displaystyle=\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta}\rangle\qquad\text{(for any $z=(x,y)\in\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta)$)}
\displaystyle=x\sin\theta-y\cos\theta\leqslant 3\cdot e^{-10}\delta-2.8\delta(1-e^{-10}\delta)<-2.7\delta.
On the other hand, by (7.56) (7.57) we have
\phi(t,\theta)\geqslant-2.1\delta-2.1\cdot 2e^{-10}\delta-e^{7}e^{2e^{-20}\delta^{2}}\delta^{-1}\big(2e^{-20}\delta^{2}+2e^{-20}\delta^{2}\big)>-2.2\delta
for all (t,\theta)\in(-e^{-20}\delta^{2},e^{-20}\delta^{2})\times(-e^{-10}\delta,e^{-10}\delta). Hence \phi(t,\cdot)>h(t,\cdot) in \overline{J_{t}}, contradicting V_{2}\cap\overline{J}_{t}\neq\emptyset. This concludes the proof.
∎
8 C^{1,1/3} estimates for almost linear solutions
For \delta,r>0, consider the anisotropic box
Q_{\delta}(r)=(-r,r)\times(-\delta r,\delta r).
Theorem 8.1.
Suppose \delta\leqslant 1, and u is \infty-harmonic in Q_{\delta}(4) where it holds
\Big|\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}-\partial_{x}\Big|<e^{-15}\delta,\qquad\big|\log|\nabla u|\big|<e^{-30}\delta^{2}.
(8.1)
Then
\Big\|\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}-\partial_{x}\Big\|_{C^{0,1/3}(Q_{\delta}(1))}\leqslant e^{10}\delta^{2/3},\qquad\big\|\log|\nabla u|\big\|_{C^{0,2/3}(Q_{\delta}(1))}\leqslant e^{10}\delta^{4/3},
(8.2)
and hence
\big\|\nabla u-\partial_{x}\big\|_{C^{0,1/3}(Q_{\delta}(1))}\leqslant e^{15}\delta^{2/3}.
(8.3)
It was already observed by Evans-Savin [37] that boxes of ratio 1\times\delta are suitable for an optimal \varepsilon-regularity theorem for the \infty-Laplacian in 2D. Theorem 8.1 and [37, Theorem 5.2] together yield a clean form:
Theorem 8.2.
There is a universal constant C so that: if \delta\leqslant 1, and u is \infty-harmonic in Q_{\delta}(4) in which
|u-x|<C^{-1}\delta^{2},
(8.4)
then
\Big\|\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}-\partial_{x}\Big\|_{C^{0,1/3}(Q_{\delta}(1))}\leqslant e^{15}\delta^{2/3},\qquad\big\|\log|\nabla u|\big\|_{C^{0,2/3}(Q_{\delta}(1))}\leqslant e^{15}\delta^{4/3},
and hence
\|u-x\|_{C^{1,1/3}(Q_{\delta}(1))}\leqslant e^{20}\delta^{2/3}.
The order on \delta is sharp in these results: considering u_{0}=|x|^{4/3}-|y|^{4/3} and its scaling
u_{\delta}(x,y)=\frac{3}{4}\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\Big[u_{0}(1+\delta^{2}x,\delta^{2}y)-1\Big],
it can be verified that
\|u_{\delta}-x\|_{C^{0}(Q_{\delta}(1))}=O(\delta^{2}),
and
\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{\nabla u_{\delta}}{|\nabla u_{\delta}|}-\partial_{x}\Big\|_{C^{0}(Q_{\delta}(1))}=O(\delta),\qquad\big\|\log|\nabla u_{\delta}|\big\|_{C^{0}(Q_{\delta}(1))}=O(\delta^{2}),
\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{\nabla u_{\delta}}{|\nabla u_{\delta}|}-\partial_{x}\Big\|_{C^{0,1/3}(Q_{\delta}(1))}=O(\delta^{2/3}),\qquad\big\|\log|\nabla u_{\delta}|\big\|_{C^{0,2/3}(Q_{\delta}(1))}=O(\delta^{4/3}).
Thus, all conditions and conclusions above have the consistent order of \delta as in u_{\delta}.
By Theorem 8.2 and the known C^{1} regularity, we have:
Theorem 8.3.
If \Delta_{\infty}u=0 in \Omega, then
u\in C^{1,1/3}_{\loc}\big(\Omega\setminus\Crit(u)\big),\qquad|\nabla u|\in C^{0,2/3}_{\loc}\big(\Omega\setminus\Crit(u)\big).
Theorem 8.1 is proved by combining p-harmonic approximation (Theorem 6.1) and the following regularity of simple clusters:
Theorem 8.4.
Let \delta\leqslant 1, and \big(w,\nu,\{D_{i}\},\{\chi_{i}\}\big) be a simple IMCF cluster in Q_{\delta}(4) with
|\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}\delta,\qquad|w|<e^{-20}\delta^{2}.
Then
\|w\|_{C^{0,2/3}(Q_{\delta}(1/4))}\leqslant\delta^{4/3},
(8.5)
and for any ridge \gamma intersecting Q_{\delta}(1/8), we have
\|\gamma\|_{C^{1,1/2}(Q_{\delta}(1/4))}\leqslant\delta.
(8.6)
The deduction from Theorem 8.4 to 8.1 is almost immediate. The following analytic lemma is a main ingredient:
Lemma 8.5 (= Lemma 2.14).
Suppose f,g are functions on (-1,1), with f^{\prime}=g^{\prime} whenever f=g, and
\sup_{(-1,1)}|f^{\prime}|\leqslant 1,\qquad\sup_{(-1,1)}|g^{\prime}|\leqslant 1,
and
\|f^{\prime}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(-1,1)}\leqslant\mu,\qquad\|g^{\prime}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(-1,1)}\leqslant\mu,
for some \alpha\in(0,1] and \mu>0. Let \nu be the (well-defined) upper normal vector field of \graphh(f)\cup\graphh(g). For s\leqslant 1, denote S=\big(\graphh(f)\cup\graphh(g)\big)\cap\{|x|\leqslant 1-s\}. Then
\|\nu\|_{C^{0,\alpha/(1+\alpha)}(S)}\leqslant 16\mu^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}s^{-\alpha}.
The Hölder exponent \frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha} is sharp, and it serves to turn the order \frac{1}{2} in (8.6) to the order \frac{1}{3}=\frac{1/2}{1+1/2} in the first expression of (8.2).
We now sketch the main ideas for proving Theorem 8.4. Recall that in a simple cluster, the “level sets” \gamma_{t}=\partial Z_{t} are unions of concave / convex curves with cusps at endpoints, see Lemma 3.7. The key to showing Theorem 8.4 is to bound the C^{1,1/2} norms of these curves up to the cusps. This is done in Claim 11 – 14 below. Once this is shown, we note that \gamma_{t} are tangential to the ridge at the cusps for each t, therefore, the ridge may be viewed as an envelope curve of the family \{\gamma_{t}\}. The C^{1,1/2} regularity of \gamma_{t} would imply the C^{1,1/2} regularity of the ridge. This is done as a part of Claim 14. The regularity of w in (8.5) follows in a rather routine way, thus we refer to Claim 15 for the proof.
To show the idea for proving C^{1,1/2} regularity of \gamma_{t}, let us first consider the model case where the simple cluster is a smooth IMCF by cuspidal curves (Figure 35). For each time t, the cuspidal curve \gamma_{t} has an angle parameter \vartheta whose range is denoted by (\underline{\theta}_{t},\overline{\theta}_{t}). Let \mathring{\theta}_{t} be the angle at the cusp in \gamma_{t}.
Figure 35: A smooth cuspidal IMCF.
Then the support function h(t,\theta) solves \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h in the generalized cylindrical domain \mathcal{J}=\bigcup\,\{t\}\times(\underline{\theta}_{t},\overline{\theta}_{t}). The quantity
\kappa^{-1}=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h
is the signed inverse curvature of \gamma_{t}, with \kappa^{-1}<0 in (\underline{\theta}_{t},\mathring{\theta}_{t}) and \kappa^{-1}>0 in (\mathring{\theta}_{t},\overline{\theta}_{t}) due to our sign conventions. Hence the root \mathring{\theta}_{t} of \partial_{\theta\theta}h+h is nondegenerate. The parabolic Hopf lemma then implies
|\kappa^{-1}|\geqslant c|\theta-\mathring{\theta}_{t}|
(8.7)
for some c>0. Let s be the length parameter of \gamma_{t}, with s=0 corresponding to the cusp. Then we obtain the ODE inequality
|d\theta/ds|=\frac{1}{|\kappa^{-1}|}\leqslant\frac{1}{c|\theta-\mathring{\theta}_{t}|},
which implies
\Big|\frac{d(\theta-\mathring{\theta}_{t})^{2}}{ds}\Big|\leqslant 2/c
and hence
|\theta(s_{1})-\theta(s_{2})|\leqslant\sqrt{2/c}\cdot|s_{1}-s_{2}|^{1/2}.
Hence the edges of \gamma_{t} are C^{1,1/2} up to the cusp.
There are two main issues in extending this argument to full generality: we need a uniform lower bound on the constant c, and to treat the analytic subtleties due to the low regularity of weak IMCF. To resolve the first issue, we observe that the Sturmian principle works as a quantitative Hopf lemma. Similar idea was also employed by Angenent [6]. The following is a toy case for explaining the idea:
Lemma 8.6.
Suppose f(t,x) solves \partial_{t}f=\partial_{xx}f smoothly in [0,1]\times[0,1], with f(t,0)\leqslant-1 and f(t,1)\geqslant 1 for all t\in[0,1], and \partial_{x}f(0,x)\geqslant 1 for all x\in[0,1]. Then
\partial_{x}f(t,x)\geqslant\frac{1}{2},\qquad\forall\,(t,x)\text{ with $|f(t,x)|\leqslant 1/2$}.
Proof.
If this is not true for some (t_{0},x_{0}), then there exists c<1/2 so that (t_{0},x_{0}) is a degenerate root of
g(t,x)=f(t,x)-f(t_{0},x_{0})-c(x-x_{0}).
Note that g has no root on \{x=0\}\cup\{x=1\}. By the Sturmian theory, g(0,\cdot) must have at least 2 roots in [0,1]. But this contradicts \partial_{x}g(0,x)>0.
∎
This argument is applied to show a statement of the form
\partial_{\theta}\kappa^{-1}\geqslant c for all (t,\theta) with |\kappa^{-1}|(t,\theta)\leqslant c^{\prime},
where c,c^{\prime} are universal constants. See Claim 11. This replaces the Hopf Lemma (8.7).
The second issue (low regularity of the weak IMCF) also causes significant difficulty. Consider Figure 36 (left), where a jump in the weak IMCF is present and partially overlaps with the ridge. Figure 36 (right) describes the image \mathcal{J} of the angle parameter. The orange curve represents the angle of the cusps – namely, the set of points (t,\theta) where \kappa^{-1}(t,\theta)=0. As an effect of the jump, the right boundary of \mathcal{J} changes discontinuously in t, and the quantity \kappa^{-1}=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h is not defined at (t,\theta_{0}). In general, \kappa^{-1} may lose continuity near \partial\mathcal{J} at each jump, and we do not have a strong enough boundary condition for \kappa^{-1} to avoid nodal lines ending on the two sides of \partial\mathcal{J}.
Figure 36: Discontinuity of the nodal line of \kappa^{-1}.
The resolution of this issue is not to compare \partial_{\theta\theta}h+h with a linear function, but to compare h with a degree 3 Taylor polynomial, in the use of Sturmian principle. One may show that h remains continuous up to \partial\mathcal{J}. The rough idea becomes the following: if \partial_{\theta}(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h) is too small at a point (t_{0},\theta_{0}), then for a suitable Taylor polynomial \phi, the function \phi-h would have 3 roots for all t<t_{0}. In the interior of \mathcal{J}, this is guaranteed by the Sturmian principle. On the two sides of \mathcal{J}, Theorem 7.1(ii) would guarantee that a nodal line cannot end; see Claim 12 for details. The sign conditions in Theorem 7.1(ii) are exactly in the favorable direction. Then, \phi-h would have at least 3 roots at the initial time (which is appropriately set up in the proof), contradicting other known facts. See Claim 11 for details.
The same technical difficulties have also arisen in Theorem 7.3(ii), and the proof there already implemented the above ideas. The main difference is that we compared h with its second order Taylor expansion in Theorem 7.3. Another technical trouble in Theorem 7.3(ii) arises from the fact that h may be discontinuous in \mathcal{J} due to large jumps (see around Figure 25). In the case here, h must be continuous due to the presence of cusps, see Claim 5 and 8.
Proof of Theorem 8.2 assuming Theorem 8.1.
[37, Theorem 5.2] implies the existence of universal constants \lambda_{0},C_{1} so that: if \lambda\leqslant\lambda_{0}, and |u-x|\leqslant\lambda^{2} in Q_{\lambda}(4), then
|\partial_{x}u-1|\leqslant C_{1}\lambda^{2},\qquad|\partial_{y}u|\leqslant C_{1}\lambda\qquad\text{in}\ \ Q_{\lambda}(3).
(8.8)
We remind that the notation “Q_{\lambda}” in [37] corresponds to (-1,1)\times(-\lambda^{1/2},\lambda^{1/2}). When \lambda is small enough, (8.8) implies
\Big|\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}-\partial_{x}\Big|\leqslant C_{2}\lambda,\qquad\big|\log|\nabla u|\big|\leqslant C_{2}^{2}\lambda^{2}\qquad\text{in}\ \ Q_{\lambda}(3),
(8.9)
where C_{2} is another universal constant. Now choose C>\max\{\lambda_{0}^{-2},e^{30}C_{2}^{2}\} and set \lambda=C^{-1/2}\delta<\lambda_{0}. For u satisfying (8.4) in Q_{\delta}(4), we have |u-x|\leqslant(C^{-1/2}\delta)^{2}=\lambda^{2}. Thus, using (8.9) in all vertical translations of Q_{\lambda}(4) we have
\Big|\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}-\partial_{x}\Big|\leqslant C_{2}C^{-1/2}\delta,\qquad\big|\log|\nabla u|\big|\leqslant C_{2}^{2}C^{-1}\delta^{2}\qquad\text{in}\ \ Q_{\delta}(3).
The result follows from the scaled versions of Theorem 8.1.
∎
Proof of Theorem 8.1 assuming Theorem 8.4.
Take any z_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1}), z_{2}=(x_{2},y_{2}) in Q_{\delta}(1/8). Denote
\overline{w}=-\log|\nabla u|,\qquad\overline{\nu}=-\frac{\nabla^{\perp}u}{|\nabla u|}.
Apply Theorem 6.1 with basepoints z_{1},z_{2} respectively: we obtain two simple IMCF clusters w_{1},w_{2} in Q_{\delta}(4), with the associated calibrations \nu_{i}=-e^{w_{i}}\nabla^{\perp}u. Notice that \nabla u=e^{-w_{i}}\nu_{i}^{\perp}, and
\nu_{i}(z)=\overline{\nu}(z)\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad w_{i}(z)=\overline{w}(z).
(8.10)
By Theorem 6.1(iii) and (6.6) and (8.1), we have
w_{i}\leqslant\overline{w}\leqslant e^{-30}\delta^{2},\qquad w_{i}\geqslant-\log\sup_{Q_{\delta}(4)}|\nabla u|\geqslant-e^{-30}\delta^{2}
in Q_{\delta}(4), for i=1,2. Also,
\displaystyle|\nu_{i}+\partial_{y}|
\displaystyle=\big|\!-e^{-w_{i}}\nabla^{\perp}u+\partial_{y}\big|=\Big|\!-e^{-w_{i}}\nabla^{\perp}u+\frac{\nabla^{\perp}u}{|\nabla u|}\Big|+\Big|\!-\frac{\nabla^{\perp}u}{|\nabla u|}+\partial_{y}\Big|
\displaystyle\leqslant\big|e^{-w_{i}}|\nabla u|-1\big|+e^{-15}\delta<e^{-10}\delta.
Hence, w_{1},w_{2} satisfy the condition for Theorem 8.4.
Also, we fall into the \varepsilon-regularity realm as appeared below Remark 6.2. From there it follows that w_{i} (i=1,2) contains only one ridge \gamma_{i}\ni z_{i}, and \gamma_{1},\gamma_{2} are e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitz graphs. By Theorem 6.1(vii), we have w_{i}=\overline{w} and \nu_{i}=\overline{\nu}=-(\gamma^{\prime}_{i})^{\perp} on \gamma_{i}, for each i=1,2. Here \gamma^{\prime}_{i} denotes the right-pointing unit tangent vector of \gamma_{i}.
