Consider the unique point z^{\prime}_{1}\in\gamma_{1} with the same x-coordinate as z_{2}. Denote z^{\prime}_{1}=(x_{2},y^{\prime}_{1}). Using Theorem 8.4 on w_{1}, we have
\displaystyle\big|\overline{w}(z_{1})-\overline{w}(z^{\prime}_{1})\big|
\displaystyle=\big|w_{1}(z_{1})-w_{1}(z^{\prime}_{1})\big|\leqslant\delta^{4/3}|z_{1}-z^{\prime}_{1}|^{2/3},
(8.11)
\displaystyle\big|\overline{\nu}(z_{1})-\overline{\nu}(z^{\prime}_{1})\big|
\displaystyle=\big|(\gamma^{\prime}_{1})^{\perp}(z_{1})-(\gamma^{\prime}_{1})^{\perp}(z^{\prime}_{1})\big|\leqslant\delta|z_{1}-z^{\prime}_{1}|^{1/2}.
It remains to connect z^{\prime}_{1} with z_{2}. Recall from Theorem 6.1(ix), see also (6.10), that
\displaystyle w_{1}(x_{2},y)
\displaystyle=-\log\max\Big\{|\nabla u(x_{2},y^{\prime})|:y^{\prime}\in[y,y^{\prime}_{1}]\Big\},
(8.12)
\displaystyle w_{2}(x_{2},y)
\displaystyle=-\log\max\Big\{|\nabla u(x_{2},y^{\prime})|:y^{\prime}\in[y,y_{2}]\Big\},
where we denote [a,b]=[b,a] if b<a. Take y_{3}\in[y^{\prime}_{1},y_{2}] so that
|\nabla u(x_{2},y_{3})|=\max\Big\{|\nabla u(x_{2},y)|:y\in[y^{\prime}_{1},y_{2}]\Big\}.
Then (8.12) yields
w_{1}(x_{2},y_{3})=-\log|\nabla u(x_{2},y_{3})|=w_{2}(x_{2},y_{3}).
Denoting z_{3}=(x_{2},y_{3}), we have by (8.5)
\displaystyle\big|\overline{w}(z^{\prime}_{1})-\overline{w}(z_{2})\big|
\displaystyle\leqslant\big|\overline{w}(z^{\prime}_{1})-\overline{w}(z_{3})\big|+\big|\overline{w}(z_{3})-\overline{w}(z_{2})\big|
(8.13)
\displaystyle=\big|w_{1}(z^{\prime}_{1})-w_{1}(z_{3})\big|+\big|w_{2}(z_{3})-w_{2}(z_{2})\big|\leqslant 2\delta^{4/3}|z^{\prime}_{1}-z_{2}|^{2/3}.
Then, \gamma_{1},\gamma_{2} have C^{1,1/2} norms bounded by \delta in Q_{\delta}(1/4), by (8.6). At each possible point z\in\gamma_{1}\cap\gamma_{2} we have
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(z)=\overline{\nu}^{\perp}(z)=\gamma^{\prime}_{2}(z),
hence the intersection of \gamma_{1} and \gamma_{2} is tangential. Finally, Lemma 8.5 with \alpha=1/2, \mu=\delta/2 and s=1/2 yields
\big|\overline{\nu}(z^{\prime}_{1})-\overline{\nu}(z_{2})\big|=\big|(\gamma^{\prime}_{1})^{\perp}(z^{\prime}_{1})-(\gamma^{\prime}_{2})^{\perp}(z_{2})\big|\leqslant 32\delta^{2/3}|z^{\prime}_{1}-z_{2}|^{1/3}.
(8.14)
Combining (8.11) (8.13) (8.14), we thus have shown
\|\overline{w}\|_{C^{0,2/3}(Q_{\delta}(1/8))}\leqslant 4\delta^{4/3},\qquad\|\overline{\nu}+\partial_{y}\|_{C^{0,1/3}(Q_{\delta}(1/8))}\leqslant 64\delta^{2/3}.
This implies (8.2) by a covering argument. It is elementary that (8.1) (8.2) imply (8.3).
∎
Proof of Theorem 8.4.
Let us prove the following: if in addition 0 lies on a ridge \gamma, then
\|w\|_{C^{0,2/3}(Q_{\delta}(1))}\leqslant e^{-1}\delta^{4/3},\qquad\|\gamma\|_{C^{1,1/2}(Q_{\delta}(1))}\leqslant e^{-1}\delta.
(8.15)
Indeed, this implies the theorem as follows: if Q_{\delta}(3/8) intersects no ridges, then (8.6) is vacuous. Next, Theorem 7.3(ii) in Q_{\delta}(3/8) and |\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}\delta, \langle\nu\,,\,\nabla w\rangle=|\nabla w| imply
|\nabla w|\leqslant 16e^{-7}\delta,\qquad\langle\nabla w\,,\,\partial_{x}\rangle\leqslant 16e^{-17}\delta^{2}\qquad\text{in}\ \ Q_{\delta}(1/4).
Hence, for two points z_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1})\in Q_{\delta}(1/4) and z_{2}=(x_{2},y_{2})\in Q_{\delta}(1/4), we have
\displaystyle|w(z_{1})-w(z_{2})|
\displaystyle\leqslant 16e^{-17}\delta^{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|+16e^{-7}\delta|y_{1}-y_{2}|
\displaystyle\leqslant 16e^{-17}\delta^{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2/3}+16e^{-7}\delta\cdot\delta^{1/3}|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{2/3}
\displaystyle\leqslant 32e^{-7}\delta^{4/3}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2/3},
hence showing (8.5). If Q_{\delta}(3/8) intersects a ridge, then it is the unique ridge appearing in the cluster (Lemma 3.5). Denote it by \gamma. Fixing a basepoint z_{0}\in\gamma\cap Q_{\delta}(3/8) and applying (8.15) in the ambient box Q_{\delta}(z_{0},3), we obtain (8.5) (8.6) as desired.
It remains to prove (8.15) assuming that 0 lies on a ridge \gamma. We encourage the reader to read the proof of Lemma 3.7 to gain more familiarity with the setups in this proof.
Let f_{\ridge}:(-4,4)\to(-4e^{-9}\delta,4e^{-9}\delta), with f_{\ridge}(0)=0 and |f^{\prime}_{\ridge}|\leqslant e^{-9}\delta, be such that \gamma=\graphh(f_{\ridge}), where \gamma denotes the ridge containing 0. So w consists of an upward evolving IMCF in \{y<f_{\ridge}(x)\} and a downward evolving IMCF in \{y>f_{\ridge}(x)\}, and
y\mapsto w(x,y)\text{\ \ is\ \ }\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{non-decreasing\ \ when\ \ $y\leqslant f_{\ridge}(x)$},\\
&\text{non-increasing\ \ when\ \ $y\geqslant f_{\ridge}(x)$},\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\forall\,x\in(-4,4).
(8.16)
As usual, denote
Y_{t}=\{w>t\},\qquad\widetilde{Z}_{t}=\{w\geqslant t\},\qquad Z_{t}=\Int(\widetilde{Z}_{t}).
By Lemma 3.7(i), each Z_{t} has the form
Z_{t}=\Big\{x\in I_{t},\ g_{1,t}(x)<y<g_{2,t}(x)\Big\},
where I_{t}=(-4,4) or I_{t}=(-4,a_{t})\cup(b_{t},4) with -4\leqslant a_{t}\leqslant b_{t}\leqslant 4, and g_{1,t},g_{2,t}:\overline{I}_{t}\cap(-4,4)\to[-4\delta,4\delta] satisfy g_{1,t}\leqslant f_{\ridge}\leqslant g_{2,t} and have the convexity properties stated in Lemma 3.7(i)(ic). We also have
Y_{t}=\Big\{x\in I_{t}^{+},\ g_{1,t}^{+}(x)<y<g_{2,t}^{+}(x)\Big\},
where I_{t}^{+}=(-4,4) or I_{t}^{+}=(-4,a_{t}^{+})\cup(b_{t}^{+},4) with -4\leqslant a_{t}^{+}\leqslant b_{t}^{+}\leqslant 4, and g_{1,t}^{+},g_{2,t}^{+}:\overline{I_{t}^{+}}\cap(-4,4)\to[-4\delta,4\delta] satisfy g_{1,t}^{+}<f_{\ridge}<g_{2,t}^{+} in I_{t}^{+}, and g_{1,t}^{+},g_{2,t}^{+} have the properties in Lemma 3.7(i)(ic). The fact Y_{t}\subset Z_{t} implies I_{t}^{+}\subset I_{t} and g_{1,t}^{+}\geqslant g_{1,t}, g_{2,t}^{+}\leqslant g_{2,t} in I_{t}^{+}.
Consider
{\underline{T}}=\operatorname{min}_{\gamma\cap\overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}}(w).
Then I_{t},I_{t}^{+}\supset[-3,3] for all t<{\underline{T}}, but I_{\underline{T}}^{+}\not\supset[-3,3].
Therefore, I_{\underline{T}}^{+}=(-4,a_{\underline{T}}^{+})\cup(b_{\underline{T}}^{+},4) with -4\leqslant a_{\underline{T}}^{+}\leqslant b_{\underline{T}}^{+}\leqslant 4 and [a_{\underline{T}}^{+},b_{\underline{T}}^{+}]\cap[-3,3]\neq\emptyset, and w(x,f_{\ridge}(x))\equiv{\underline{T}} in [a_{\underline{T}}^{+},b_{\underline{T}}^{+}]\cap[-3,3]. Note that g_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+},g_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+} are concave / convex in each interval in I_{\underline{T}}^{+}, by Lemma 3.7(i)(ic).
Consider the merged functions
\overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &f_{\ridge}(x)\qquad x\in[a_{\underline{T}}^{+},b_{\underline{T}}^{+}]\\
&g_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)\qquad\text{otherwise}\end{aligned}\right.,\qquad\overline{g}_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &f_{\ridge}(x)\qquad x\in[a_{\underline{T}}^{+},b_{\underline{T}}^{+}]\\
&g_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)\qquad\text{otherwise}\end{aligned}\right.,
Notice that \overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}\leqslant f_{\ridge} and \overline{g}_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+}\geqslant f_{\ridge}, and
w\big(x,\overline{g}_{i,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)\big)={\underline{T}},\qquad\forall\,x\in[-3,3],\ i=1,2.
(8.17)
Then, consider the regions
E_{1}=\Big\{x\in[-3,3],\ -3\delta\leqslant y\leqslant\overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)\Big\},\qquad E_{2}=\Big\{x\in[-3,3],\ \overline{g}_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant 3\delta\Big\}.
It is clear that E_{1}\cup E_{2}=\overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}\cap\{w\leqslant{\underline{T}}\}.
Claim 1.
The functions
w_{1}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &w\qquad\text{in}\ \ E_{1}\\
&{\underline{T}}\qquad\text{in}\ \ \overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}\setminus E_{1}\end{aligned}\right.,\qquad w_{2}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &w\qquad\text{in}\ \ E_{2}\\
&{\underline{T}}\qquad\text{in}\ \ \overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}\setminus E_{2}\end{aligned}\right.
are both weak IMCFs in Q_{\delta}(3).
Proof.
Notice by (8.17) that w_{1},w_{2} are Lipschitz in \overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}. Let us show that w_{2} is a weak IMCF in Q_{\delta}(3) (same argument for w_{1}). If w\equiv{\underline{T}} in (-3,3)\times[\delta,2\delta], then w\equiv{\underline{T}} in the region \big\{x\in(-3,3),\,\overline{g}_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)<y<2\delta\big\} by (8.16), hence w_{2}\equiv{\underline{T}} in (-3,3)\times(-3\delta,2\delta). Then Remark 2.9(vi) with r=3 and l=3\delta and f\equiv 1.5\delta implies the result. Otherwise, w(z_{1})<{\underline{T}} for some z_{1}\in(-3,3)\times[\delta,2\delta]. Then for all t\in(w(z_{1}),{\underline{T}}), the set \{w_{2}<t\} is a convex epigraph in Q_{\delta}(3)\cap\{y>f_{\ridge}(x)\}. Taking t\nearrow{\underline{T}}, it follows that \{w_{2}<{\underline{T}}\} is the epigraph of a convex function f\geqslant f_{\ridge}. Then Remark 2.9(vi) with r=3, l=3\delta implies the result.
∎
Using Theorem 7.3(ii) on w_{1},w_{2} respectively, we obtain
|\nabla w|\leqslant 2e^{-7}\delta\qquad\text{in}\ \ (E_{1}\cup E_{2})\cap Q_{\delta}(2).
(8.18)
Claim 2.
The curvature of \graphh(\overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}) and \graphh(\overline{g}_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+}) in Q_{\delta}(2) are bounded by 2e^{-7}\delta. Furthermore,
\|f_{\ridge}\|_{C^{1,1}([a_{\underline{T}}^{+},b_{\underline{T}}^{+}]\cap(-2,2))}\leqslant 2e^{-7}\delta.
(8.19)
Proof.
Since I_{t}\supset[-3,3] for all t<{\underline{T}}, we have
\widetilde{Z}_{\underline{T}}\cap\overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}=\Big\{x\in[-3,3],\ \widetilde{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant\widetilde{g}_{2,{\underline{T}}}(x)\Big\},
where \widetilde{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}=\lim_{s\nearrow{\underline{T}}}g_{1,s} and \widetilde{g}_{2,{\underline{T}}}=\lim_{s\nearrow{\underline{T}}}g_{2,s}. By (8.18), note that \graphh(\widetilde{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}})\cap Q_{\delta}(2) and \graphh(\widetilde{g}_{2,{\underline{T}}})\cap Q_{\delta}(2) have curvature bounded by 2e^{-7}\delta.
We claim that \overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+} is linear in each open interval in
[-3,3]\cap\big\{\widetilde{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}<\overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}<\widetilde{g}_{2,{\underline{T}}}\big\}.
Let (c,d) be such an interval. Then, for every subinterval [c^{\prime},d^{\prime}]\subset(c,d), there is \varepsilon>0 so that w\geqslant{\underline{T}} in the region
D=\Big\{x\in(c^{\prime},d^{\prime}),\ \overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)-\varepsilon<y<\overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)+\varepsilon\Big\}.
Notice that \graphh(\overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+})|_{(c^{\prime},d^{\prime})} is orthogonal to \nu, and w\equiv{\underline{T}}, |\nu|\equiv 1 there. Hence, the vector field \nu^{\prime}=e^{{\underline{T}}-w}\nu is a calibration of \graphh(\overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}) in D, as desired.
As a result, in [-3,3], the function \overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+} either is linear or coincides with \widetilde{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}} or \widetilde{g}_{2,{\underline{T}}}. The curvature bound of \widetilde{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}},\widetilde{g}_{2,{\underline{T}}} then implies that of \overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}, \overline{g}_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+}. Finally, noticing that f_{\ridge}=\overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}|_{[a_{\underline{T}}^{+},b_{\underline{T}}^{+}]}, the curvature bound of \overline{g}_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+} implies (8.19).
∎
Figure 37: An extreme case where Y_{\underline{T}}=\emptyset and w\equiv{\underline{T}} in the shaded region.
Consider the remaining regions
\displaystyle E_{3}
\displaystyle=\Big\{x\in[-3,a_{\underline{T}}^{+}],\,g_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant g_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)\Big\},
\displaystyle E_{4}
\displaystyle=\Big\{x\in[b_{\underline{T}}^{+},3],\,g_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant g_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(x)\Big\}.
Notice E_{3}=\emptyset if a_{\underline{T}}^{+}<-3, and E_{4}=\emptyset if b_{\underline{T}}^{+}>3. The main regularity claim is as follows:
Figure 38: In the second picture, a_{\underline{T}}^{+}=-4 and E_{3}=\emptyset.
Claim 3.
We have
\|w\|_{C^{0,2/3}(E_{4}\cap Q_{\delta}(1))}\leqslant e^{-2}\delta^{4/3}
(8.20)
and
\|f_{\ridge}\|_{C^{1,1/2}([b_{\underline{T}}^{+},3]\cap[-1,1])}\leqslant e^{-2}\delta.
(8.21)
A similar fact holds for E_{3}.
Having known this fact, noting that
E_{1}\cup E_{2}\cup E_{3}\cup E_{4}\supset\overline{Q_{\delta}(3)},
the main results (8.15) follow from Claim 2, 3 and (8.19), by triangle inequalities.
It remains to prove Claim 3. Since E_{3},E_{4} are symmetric via a reflection around the y-axis, it suffices to treat E_{4}. We may assume b_{\underline{T}}^{+}<1, otherwise the claim is vacuous. Consider
{\overline{T}}=\max_{E_{4}}(w)=\max_{E_{4}\cap\gamma}(w),
where the second equality follows from (8.16). Notice that b_{t},b_{t}^{+}<3 for all t\in[{\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}). Note: it may be possible that Z_{\overline{T}}=\emptyset or b_{\overline{T}}>3.
Recall from above that I_{\underline{T}}^{+}\not\supset[-3,3]. This implies:
•
b_{\underline{T}}^{+}\geqslant-3;
•
I_{t}=(-4,a_{t})\cup(b_{t},4) and I_{t}^{+}=(-4,a_{t}^{+})\cup(b_{t}^{+},4) with b_{t},b_{t}^{+}>-3, for all t>{\underline{T}}.
By Lemma 3.7(i)(ic) we have:
•
g_{1,t} (resp. g_{2,t}) is concave (resp. convex) in [b_{t},4), for all t>{\underline{T}};
•
g_{1,t}^{+} (resp. g_{2,t}^{+}) is concave (resp. convex) in [b_{t}^{+},4), for all t\geqslant{\underline{T}};
•
g_{1,t}\leqslant f_{\ridge}\leqslant g_{2,t} in [b_{t},4] for all t>{\underline{T}};
•
g_{1,t}^{+}<f_{\ridge}<g_{2,t}^{+} in (b_{t}^{+},4] for all t\geqslant{\underline{T}};
•
w(x,f_{\ridge}(x)) is non-decreasing for x\in[b_{\underline{T}}^{+},3];
•
the graphs of g_{1,t},g_{2,t},g_{1,t}^{+},g_{2,t}^{+},f_{\ridge} all have \nu as unit normal vector;
•
the following convergences hold:
\lim_{s\nearrow t}b_{s}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}b_{s}^{+}\leqslant b_{t}\leqslant b_{t}^{+}=\lim_{s\searrow t}b_{s}=\lim_{s\searrow t}b_{s}^{+},
and
\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{1,s}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{1,s}^{+}=g_{1,t}\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{1}([b_{t},3]),\qquad\forall\,t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}],
\displaystyle\lim_{s\searrow t}g_{1,s}=\lim_{s\searrow t}g_{1,s}^{+}=g_{1,t}^{+}\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{1}([b_{t}^{+},3]),\qquad\forall\,t\in[{\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}).
Similar facts hold for g_{2,t},g_{2,t}^{+}.
Let us denote
\displaystyle\gamma_{t}
\displaystyle=\graphh\big(g_{1,t}|_{[b_{t},3]}\big)\cup\graphh\big(g_{2,t}|_{[b_{t},3]}\big)=\partial Z_{t}\cap E_{4},\qquad\forall\,t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}],
\displaystyle\gamma_{t}^{+}
\displaystyle=\graphh\big(g_{1,t}^{+}|_{[b_{t}^{+},3]}\big)\cup\graphh\big(g_{2,t}^{+}|_{[b_{t}^{+},3]}\big)=\partial Y_{t}\cap E_{4},\qquad\forall\,t\in[{\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}),
(where \gamma_{\overline{T}} may be empty,) and
\Gamma_{0}=E_{4}\setminus\bigcup_{t>{\underline{T}}}\Int\big(\{w=t\}\big).
(8.22)
Clearly, \Gamma_{0} is compact and \gamma_{t},\gamma_{t}^{+}\subset\Gamma_{0}. It is an elementary topological fact
\Gamma_{0}=E_{4}\cap\bigcup_{t\geqslant{\underline{T}}}\big(\partial\widetilde{Z}_{t}\cap\partial Y_{t}\big),
(8.23)
hence |\nu|=1 on \Gamma_{0}. As |w|<e^{-20}\delta^{2} and |\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}\delta, the map (w,\nu^{\perp}):\Gamma_{0}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1} lifts to a continuous map
\Theta_{0}:\Gamma_{0}\to(-e^{-20}\delta^{2},e^{-20}\delta^{2})\times(-e^{-10}\delta,e^{-10}\delta).
Denote \overline{\mathcal{J}}=\Theta_{0}(\Gamma_{0}). We aim to show that each preimage of \Theta_{0} is a line segment.
For each t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}], consider the quantity
b_{t}^{-}=\lim_{s\nearrow t}b_{s}.
(8.24)
Hence b_{\overline{T}}^{-}\leqslant 3. We mention again that b_{t}<3 for all t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}), but possibly b_{\overline{T}}>3.
Claim 4.
f_{\ridge} is linear and w(x,f_{\ridge}(x))\equiv t in [b_{t}^{-},b_{t}]\cap[-3,3], for each t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}].
Proof.
Call I=[b_{t}^{-},b_{t}]\cap[-3,3]. For all s\in({\underline{T}},t), note that g_{1,s} is concave and g_{2,s} is convex in I. By the definition of b_{t}, the ascending limit \lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{1,s} and descending limit \lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{2,s} must agree (hence must agree with f_{\ridge}) on I. The linearity of f_{\ridge} follows from convexity. The constancy of w follows from w=s on \graphh(g_{1,s})\cup\graphh(g_{2,s}).
∎
We in fact have
\widetilde{Z}_{t}\cap E_{4}=\Big\{x\in[b_{t}^{-},3]:\widetilde{g}_{1,t}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant\widetilde{g}_{2,t}(x)\Big\},\qquad\forall\,t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}],
(8.25)
where \widetilde{g}_{1,t}:=\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{1,s} and \widetilde{g}_{2,t}:=\lim_{s\nearrow t}g_{2,s}. Notice that
\widetilde{g}_{i,t}=g_{i,t}\text{ on }[b_{t},3],\qquad\widetilde{g}_{1,t}=\widetilde{g}_{2,t}=f_{\ridge}\text{ is linear on }[b_{t}^{-},b_{t}].
(8.26)
This follows from \widetilde{Z}_{t}=\bigcap_{s<t}Y_{s} and the proof of Claim 4, see also Lemma 3.7(i)(ib).
Claim 5.
For all t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}), there exists i\in\{1,2\} so that
g_{i,t}(b_{t}^{+})=g_{1,t}^{+}(b_{t}^{+})=g_{2,t}^{+}(b_{t}^{+}),\qquad\quad g_{i,t}\equiv f_{\ridge},\ \ w(\cdot,f_{\ridge}(\cdot))\equiv t\ \ \text{in}\ \ [b_{t},b_{t}^{+}].
Proof.
This claim is nontrivial only when b_{t}<b_{t}^{+}. Suppose otherwise g_{1,t}(b_{t}^{+})<g_{1,t}^{+}(b_{t}^{+})=g_{2,t}^{+}(b_{t}^{+})<g_{2,t}(b_{t}^{+}). For \varepsilon>0, consider
z_{1}=\big(b_{t}^{+},f_{\ridge}(b_{t}^{+})\big),\quad z_{2}=\big(b_{t}^{+}+\varepsilon,g_{1,t}^{+}(b_{t}^{+}+\varepsilon)\big),\quad z_{3}=\big(b_{t}^{+}+\varepsilon,g_{2,t}^{+}(b_{t}^{+}+\varepsilon)\big).
When \varepsilon is small enough, the triangle with vertices z_{1},z_{2},z_{3} has a neighborhood contained in Z_{t}. Recall w\geqslant t in Z_{t}. Hence, the vector field e^{t-w}\nu calibrates \graphh\big(g_{1,t}^{+}|_{[b_{t}^{+},b_{t}^{+}+\varepsilon]}\big) and \graphh\big(g_{2,t}^{+}|_{[b_{t}^{+},b_{t}^{+}+\varepsilon]}\big), showing that they are both line segments, which is impossible.
Hence, either g_{1,t}(b_{t}^{+})=g_{1,t}^{+}(b_{t}^{+})=g_{2,t}^{+}(b_{t}^{+}) or g_{2,t}(b_{t}^{+})=g_{1,t}^{+}(b_{t}^{+})=g_{2,t}^{+}(b_{t}^{+}). We may assume that the first possibility occurs. This implies g_{1,t}(b_{t}^{+})=f_{\ridge}(b_{t}^{+}) as well. Now we have w(b_{t},f_{\ridge}(b_{t}))=w(b_{t}^{+},f_{\ridge}(b_{t}^{+}))=t since w=t on \partial Z_{t}\cup\partial Y_{t}. By the monotonicity of w|_{\gamma}, it follows that
w(\cdot,f_{\ridge}(\cdot))\equiv t\qquad\text{in}\ \ [b_{t},b_{t}^{+}].
The monotonicity in y-direction then implies w\equiv t in the set
\Big\{x\in[b_{t},b_{t}^{+}],\ g_{1,t}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant f_{\ridge}(x)\Big\}.
Using \div(\nu)=e^{t}\div(e^{-w}\nu)=0 and the divergence theorem in this region, we obtain
\big|\graphh\big(f_{\ridge}|_{[b_{t},b_{t}^{+}]}\big)\big|=\big|\graphh\big(g_{1,t}|_{[b_{t},b_{t}^{+}]}\big)\big|.
As g_{1,t} is concave, this forces f_{\ridge}\equiv g_{1,t} in [b_{t},b_{t}^{+}], as desired.
∎
Claim 6.
For all t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}), we have \{g_{1,t}^{+}>g_{1,t}\}\cap[b_{\underline{T}}^{+},3]=(c,3] for some c\in[b_{t}^{+},3], and g_{1,t}^{+} is linear in this interval. Analogous facts hold for g_{2,t}^{+}.
Proof.
Note that g_{1,t}^{+} is linear in any interval in \big\{g_{1,t}<g_{1,t}^{+}<f_{\ridge}\big\}: this follows from the geometry of weak IMCF. Recalling g_{1,t}^{+}<f_{\ridge} in (b_{t}^{+},3], we have that g_{1,t}^{+} is linear in each interval in \{g_{1,t}^{+}>g_{1,t}\}. Suppose \{g_{1,t}^{+}>g_{1,t}\} has a connected component of the form (c,d) with b_{t}^{+}\leqslant c<d\leqslant 3. Then
g_{1,t}<g_{1,t}^{+}<f_{\ridge}\text{\ \ in\ \ }(c,d),\qquad\qquad g_{1,t}(d)=g_{1,t}^{+}(d).
By concavity, this forces g_{1,t}(c)<g_{1,t}^{+}(c). By our choice of (c,d) as a connected component, this implies c=b_{t}^{+}. Then Claim 5 implies g_{1,t}^{+}(b_{t}^{+})=g_{2,t}(b_{t}^{+}). But this yields a contradiction since
g^{\prime}_{1,t}(d)=(g_{1,t}^{+})^{\prime}(d)=(g_{1,t}^{+})^{\prime}(b_{t}^{+})=g^{\prime}_{2,t}(b_{t}^{+})>g^{\prime}_{1,t}(b_{t}^{+})\geqslant g^{\prime}_{1,t}(d).\qed
Combining Claim 6 and 5, we may fully describe the shape of the set \partial Y_{t}\cap\widetilde{Z}_{t} for t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}). See Figures 39, 40 and Claim 8 below. We need one more auxiliary lemma:
Claim 7.
If b_{\overline{T}}<3, then there exists d\in[b_{\overline{T}},3] and i\in\{1,2\} so that
f_{\ridge}\equiv g_{i,{\overline{T}}}\ \ \text{in}\ \ [b_{\overline{T}},d],\qquad\qquad f_{\ridge}\text{ is linear in }[d,3].
Proof.
Note that w\equiv{\overline{T}} in the region
\Big\{x\in[b_{\overline{T}},3],\ g_{1,{\overline{T}}}(x)\leqslant y\leqslant g_{2,{\overline{T}}}(x)\Big\}.
The calibration argument implies that f_{\ridge} is linear in any interval where g_{1,{\overline{T}}}<f_{\ridge}<g_{2,{\overline{T}}}. Arguing similarly as in Claim 6, the set \{g_{1,{\overline{T}}}<f_{\ridge}<g_{2,{\overline{T}}}\} must take the form (d,3] with d\in[b_{\overline{T}},3]. The result follows as desired.
∎
Figure 39: The black and blue solid curves together represent \partial Y_{t}\cap\partial\widetilde{Z}_{t}.
Figure 40: Shapes of \partial Y_{t}\cap\partial\widetilde{Z}_{t}, \partial\widetilde{Z}_{t}, \partial Z_{t}=\gamma_{t} and \partial Y_{t}, respectively.
We now proceed to prove that
Claim 8.
\Theta_{0}^{-1}(t,\theta) is a line segment for each (t,\theta)\in\overline{\mathcal{J}}.
Proof.
The preimage of \{t={\underline{T}}\} is \gamma_{\underline{T}}^{+}, thus the claim holds for t={\underline{T}}. The preimage of \{t={\overline{T}}\} is
\partial\widetilde{Z}_{\overline{T}}\cap E_{4}=\Big\{x\in[b_{\overline{T}}^{-},3],\ y=\widetilde{g}_{1,{\overline{T}}}(x)\text{ or }y=\widetilde{g}_{2,{\overline{T}}}(x)\Big\},
namely, the union of a concave graph and a convex graph with tangential contact at endpoints. This also verifies the claim for t={\overline{T}}. Assume t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}). The preimage of t is
\displaystyle(\partial\widetilde{Z}_{t}\cup\partial Y_{t})\cap E_{4}
\displaystyle=\Big\{x\in[b_{t}^{-},3],\ y=\widetilde{g}_{1,t}(x)\text{ or }y=\widetilde{g}_{2,t}(x)\Big\}
\displaystyle\cup\Big\{x\in[b_{t}^{+},3],\ y=g_{1,t}^{+}(x)\text{ or }y=g_{2,t}^{+}(x)\Big\},
which by Claim 5, 6 and (8.26) is the union of \gamma_{t} and at most three line segments contacting tangentially (more precisely, with possibly one segment contacting at the cusp and two segments contacting on the edges). The result follows.
∎
As a consequence, the support function h defined by
h(t,\theta)=\langle z\,,\,\nu\rangle=\langle z\,,\,\nu_{\theta}\rangle\quad\text{for any $z\in\Theta_{0}^{-1}(t,\theta)$}
is well-defined on \overline{\mathcal{J}}, and is continuous since \Theta_{0} is a quotient map. Denote
\displaystyle\underline{\theta}_{\underline{T}}=\arctan(g_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+})^{\prime}(3),\qquad\overline{\theta}_{\underline{T}}=\arctan(g_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+})^{\prime}(3),
\displaystyle\underline{\theta}_{\overline{T}}=\arctan\widetilde{g}_{1,{\overline{T}}}^{\prime}(3),\qquad\overline{\theta}_{\overline{T}}=\arctan\widetilde{g}_{2,{\overline{T}}}^{\prime}(3),
and for each t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}), denote
\underline{\theta}_{t}=\arctan g^{\prime}_{1,t}(3),\qquad\overline{\theta}_{t}=\arctan g^{\prime}_{2,t}(3).
Notice that \overline{\theta}_{t}>\underline{\theta}_{t} for all t\in[{\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}), and from the proof of Claim 8, that
\overline{\mathcal{J}}=\bigcup_{t\in[{\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}]}\{t\}\times[\underline{\theta}_{t},\overline{\theta}_{t}].
Also, note the convergence
\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow t}\underline{\theta}_{s}=\underline{\theta}_{t}\leqslant\arctan(g_{1,t}^{+})^{\prime}(3)=\lim_{s\searrow t}\underline{\theta}_{s},
(8.27)
\displaystyle\lim_{s\nearrow t}\overline{\theta}_{s}=\overline{\theta}_{t}\geqslant\arctan(g_{2,t}^{+})^{\prime}(3)=\lim_{s\searrow t}\overline{\theta}_{s}.
To invoke Theorem 7.1, we need to consider the restricted data
\displaystyle\Gamma=\bigcup_{t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}]}\gamma_{t}\cap\Int(E_{4}),\qquad\Theta=\Theta_{0}|_{\Gamma},\qquad\mathcal{J}=\Theta(\Gamma)\subset\overline{\mathcal{J}}.
Let J_{t},\overline{J}_{t} denote the t-slice of \mathcal{J},\overline{\mathcal{J}}. Thus \overline{J}_{t}=[\underline{\theta}_{t},\overline{\theta}_{t}] is a nontrivial interval for all t\in[{\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}). Note: \overline{J}_{\overline{T}} is not a point if and only if Z_{\overline{T}}\cap E_{4}\neq\emptyset (in general, \widetilde{Z}_{\overline{T}}\cap E_{4} could be a line segment and thus Z_{\overline{T}}\cap E_{4} could be empty).
Claim 9.
|h|\leqslant e^{-6}\delta in \overline{\mathcal{J}}.
Proof.
Due to g_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(b_{\underline{T}}^{+})=g_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+}(b_{\underline{T}}^{+})=f_{\ridge}(b_{\underline{T}}^{+}) and f_{\ridge}(0)=0 and the e^{-9}\delta-Lipschitzness of all these functions, we have
E_{4}\subset[-3,3]\times[-10e^{-9}\delta,10e^{-9}\delta].
Hence, for all z=(x,y)\in E_{4}, we use |\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}\delta to bound
\big|\langle z\,,\,\nu\rangle\big|\leqslant|x|\cdot e^{-10}\delta+|y|\leqslant 3e^{-10}\delta+10e^{-9}\delta\leqslant e^{-6}\delta.\qed
Claim 10.
If t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}] and \theta\in\Int(J_{t}), then there is \varepsilon>0 so that
(t-\varepsilon,t]\times(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon)\subset\mathcal{J}
and
\Theta^{-1}(s,\omega)\Subset\Int(E_{4})\qquad\forall\,s\in(t-\varepsilon,t],\ \omega\in(\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon).
Proof.
The condition implies \underline{\theta}_{t}<\theta<\overline{\theta}_{t}. Since \lim_{s\nearrow t}\underline{\theta}_{s}=\underline{\theta}_{t} and \lim_{s\nearrow t}\overline{\theta}_{s}=\overline{\theta}_{t}, there exists \varepsilon<t-{\underline{T}} so that
g^{\prime}_{1,s}(3)<\tan(\theta-\varepsilon),\qquad g^{\prime}_{2,s}(3)>\tan(\theta+\varepsilon),\qquad\forall\,s\in[t-\varepsilon,t].
Hence [\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon]\subset J_{s} and \Theta^{-1}\big(\{s\}\times[\theta-\varepsilon,\theta+\varepsilon]\big)\Subset E_{4} for all s\in[t-\varepsilon,t].
∎
Combining Theorem 7.1(i) with \Omega=\Int(E_{4}) and the continuity of h in \overline{\mathcal{J}} (hence in \mathcal{J}) and the classical viscosity theory, it follows that h is smooth and solves \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h in every parabolic box in Claim 10. Hence h(t,\cdot) is smooth in \Int(J_{t}) for each t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}]. Denote
\varpi_{t}=\arctan g^{\prime}_{1,t}(b_{t})=\arctan g^{\prime}_{2,t}(b_{t})=\arctan f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{t})
the angle at the cusp of \gamma_{t}. For each t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}], we may decompose \gamma_{t} as
\displaystyle\gamma_{t}
\displaystyle=\Theta^{-1}\big(\{\underline{\theta}_{t}\}\big)\cup\Theta^{-1}\big((\underline{\theta}_{t},\varpi_{t}]\big)\cup\Theta^{-1}\big([\varpi_{t},\overline{\theta}_{t})\big)\cup\Theta^{-1}\big(\{\overline{\theta}_{t}\}\big)
\displaystyle=:\alpha_{1}\cup\alpha_{2}\cup\alpha_{3}\cup\alpha_{4},
(8.28)
where \alpha_{1},\alpha_{4} are (possibly empty or trivial) line segments, and the interior of \alpha_{2},\alpha_{3} are (possibly empty) smooth strictly concave resp. convex curves. Denote
\kappa^{-1}:=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h,\qquad\forall\,t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}],\ \theta\in\Int(J_{t}).
Then \kappa^{-1}(t,\varpi_{t})=0 whenever \varpi_{t}\in\Int(J_{t}), and
\kappa^{-1}(t,\cdot)<0\ \ \text{in}\ \ (\underline{\theta}_{t},\varpi_{t}),\qquad\kappa^{-1}(t,\cdot)>0\ \ \text{in}\ \ (\varpi_{t},\overline{\theta}_{t}).
(8.29)
By the Sturmian theory, the root \varpi_{t} is nondegenerate whenever \varpi_{t}\in\Int(J_{t}).
Claim 11.
For each t_{0}\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}] and \theta_{0}\in\Int(J_{t_{0}}) satisfying
|\partial_{\theta}h(t_{0},\theta_{0})|\leqslant 1.6,\qquad|\partial_{\theta\theta}h(t_{0},\theta_{0})|\leqslant e^{5}\delta^{-1},
(8.30)
we have
\partial_{\theta}^{3}h(t_{0},\theta_{0})\geqslant e^{15}\delta^{-2}.
Proof.
Suppose this is not true at some (t_{0},\theta_{0}). Consider
\displaystyle\phi(t,\theta)
\displaystyle=e^{t-t_{0}}\Big[h(t_{0},\theta_{0})+\partial_{\theta}h(t_{0},\theta_{0})(\theta-\theta_{0})+\partial_{\theta\theta}h(t_{0},\theta_{0})\Big((t-t_{0})+\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\theta_{0})^{2}\Big)
\displaystyle+\frac{e^{15}}{\delta^{2}}\Big((t-t_{0})(\theta-\theta_{0})+\frac{1}{6}(\theta-\theta_{0})^{3}\Big)\Big].
Hence \partial_{t}\phi=\partial_{\theta\theta}\phi+\phi. Recall \overline{\mathcal{J}}\subset(-e^{-20}\delta^{2},e^{-20}\delta^{2})\times(-e^{-10}\delta,e^{-10}\delta). We may estimate
\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}\phi|
\displaystyle=e^{t-t_{0}}\Big|\partial_{\theta}h(t_{0},\theta_{0})+\partial_{\theta}^{2}h(t_{0},\theta_{0})(\theta-\theta_{0})+\frac{e^{15}}{\delta^{2}}\Big((t-t_{0})+\frac{1}{2}(\theta-\theta_{0})^{2}\Big)\Big|
\displaystyle\leqslant e^{2e^{-20}\delta^{2}}\Big[1.6+\frac{e^{5}}{\delta}\cdot 2e^{-10}\delta+\frac{e^{15}}{\delta^{2}}\big(2e^{-20}\delta^{2}+2e^{-20}\delta^{2}\big)\Big]
\displaystyle\leqslant 1.7
(8.31)
and
|\partial_{\theta\theta}\phi|=e^{t-t_{0}}\Big|\partial_{\theta}^{2}h(t_{0},\theta_{0})+\frac{e^{15}}{\delta^{2}}(\theta-\theta_{0})\Big|\leqslant 3.1e^{5}\delta^{-1},
(8.32)
both inequalities hold in (-e^{-20}\delta^{2},e^{-20}\delta^{2})\times(-e^{-10}\delta,e^{-10}\delta). Also, by our assumption
\partial_{\theta}^{3}\phi(t_{0},\theta_{0})=e^{15}\delta^{-2}>\partial_{\theta}^{3}h(t_{0},\theta_{0}).
Denote \eta=\phi-h. Thus
\eta(t_{0},\theta_{0})=\partial_{\theta}\eta(t_{0},\theta_{0})=\partial_{\theta}^{2}\eta(t_{0},\theta_{0})=0,\qquad\partial_{\theta}^{3}\eta(t_{0},\theta_{0})>0.
By the Sturmian theory, for a slightly smaller t_{1}<t_{0}, there are \theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<\theta_{3}<\theta_{4} so that
\eta(t_{1},\theta_{1})<0,\qquad\eta(t_{1},\theta_{2})>0,\qquad\eta(t_{1},\theta_{3})<0,\qquad\eta(t_{1},\theta_{4})>0.
Similar to the proof of Theorem 7.3(ii), we need a boundary maximum principle. For an \varepsilon\ll 1, consider the function and compact set
\psi=e^{-\varepsilon t-t}\eta,\qquad\qquad\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{1}=\overline{\mathcal{J}}\cap\{t\leqslant t_{1}\}.
Arguing as in Claim 3, using (8.27), one may show that \partial\mathcal{J}_{1} is a Jordan loop.
Claim 12.
Let L be relatively open in \overline{\mathcal{J}}_{1}. Let \partial^{\prime}L be the relative boundary of L in \overline{\mathcal{J}}_{1}.
(i)
If \psi\geqslant 0 in \overline{L} and \max_{\overline{L}}\psi>\max_{\partial^{\prime}L}\psi, then \psi must be maximized in \overline{L} at a point (t,\theta) with t={\underline{T}} or \theta=\underline{\theta}_{t}.
(ii)
If \psi\leqslant 0 in \overline{L} and \min_{\overline{L}}\psi<\min_{\partial^{\prime}L}\psi, then \psi must be minimized in \overline{L} at a point (t,\theta) with t={\underline{T}} or \theta=\overline{\theta}_{t}.
Proof.
We prove (i); then (ii) follows from the same argument with the opposite signs. In each parabolic box R=(t_{2},t_{3}]\times(\theta_{5},\theta_{6})\subset\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{1}, we have shown that h is smooth and satisfies \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h. Hence
\partial_{t}\psi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\psi=e^{-\varepsilon t-t}\big(\partial_{t}\eta-\partial_{\theta\theta}\eta-\eta-\varepsilon\eta\big)=-\varepsilon\psi.
By the standard maximum principle and Claim 10, \psi cannot be maximized in a point (t,\theta) with t>{\underline{T}} and \theta\in\Int(J_{t}). It remains to rule out the case \theta=\overline{\theta}_{t}>\underline{\theta}_{t}.
Suppose otherwise that \psi is maximized in \overline{L} at (t,\overline{\theta}_{t}) with {\underline{T}}<t\leqslant t_{1}. Denote \lambda=\psi(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})=\max_{\overline{L}}\psi>0. The maximization implies
\partial_{\theta}^{-}\psi(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\geqslant 0\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\partial_{\theta}^{-}h(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\leqslant\partial_{\theta}\phi(t,\overline{\theta}_{t}).
(8.33)
The remaining argument is similar to Claim 5 in Section 7.
Denote \varphi=\phi-e^{\varepsilon t+t}\lambda. By the maximization of \psi and the relative openness of L, we have \varphi(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})=h(t,\overline{\theta}_{t}) and
\varphi\leqslant h\qquad\text{in}\ \ \overline{\mathcal{J}}\cap\big([t-\delta,t]\times[\overline{\theta}_{t}-\delta,\overline{\theta}_{t}+\delta]\big)
for some \delta>0.
Denote z=\varphi(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\nu_{\overline{\theta}_{t}}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\tau_{\overline{\theta}_{t}}. We need to verify \overline{\theta}_{t}\in J_{t} and z\in\Theta^{-1}(t,\overline{\theta}_{t}). Note that
\Theta_{0}^{-1}(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\cap\gamma_{t}=\Big\{h(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\nu_{\overline{\theta}_{t}}+c\tau_{\overline{\theta}_{t}}:c\geqslant\partial_{\theta}^{-}h(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\Big\}\cap\overline{Q_{\delta}(3)}.
By (8.33) and \varphi(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})=h(t,\overline{\theta}_{t}), \partial_{\theta}\varphi=\partial_{\theta}\phi, it is clear that z is contained in the first half of the expression. Using Claim 9 and (8.31), the coordinates of z are estimated as
\displaystyle|x|
\displaystyle=\big|h(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\sin\overline{\theta}_{t}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\cos\overline{\theta}_{t}\big|\leqslant e^{-6}\delta\cdot e^{-10}\delta+1.7<3,
\displaystyle|y|
\displaystyle=\big|\!-h(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\cos\overline{\theta}_{t}+\partial_{\theta}\varphi(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\sin\overline{\theta}_{t}\big|\leqslant e^{-6}\delta+1.7\cdot e^{-10}\delta<3\delta,
hence z\in\Theta_{0}^{-1}(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\cap\gamma_{t}\cap Q_{\delta}(3). It follows that z\in\gamma_{t}\cap\Int(E_{4}), and hence \overline{\theta}_{t}\in J_{t} and z\in\Theta^{-1}(t,\overline{\theta}_{t}) as desired. Then, observe that \chi_{\sigma}=1 as in Theorem 7.1(ii), since the outer unit normal of Z_{t} points upward on \sigma. Now all the conditions for Theorem 7.1(ii) (with \Omega=\Int(E_{4})) are met, and it yields (\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi)(t,\overline{\theta}_{t})\geqslant 0. But this contradicts
\partial_{t}\varphi-\partial_{\theta\theta}\varphi-\varphi=-\varepsilon e^{\varepsilon t+t}\lambda<0.\qed
Let V_{1},V_{3} be the connected components of \{\eta<0\}\cap\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{1} containing \theta_{1},\theta_{3}, and V_{2},V_{4} be the connected components of \{\eta>0\}\cap\overline{\mathcal{J}}_{1} containing \theta_{2},\theta_{4}, respectively.
Claim 13.
We have V_{1}\neq V_{3}, V_{2}\neq V_{4}, and V_{k}\cap\overline{J}_{t}\neq\emptyset for all t\in[{\underline{T}},t_{1}] and k=1,2,3,4.
Proof.
If V_{1}=V_{3}, then it separates V_{2} from the parabolic boundary \partial\overline{\mathcal{J}}\cap\{t<{\overline{T}}\}, and \psi would have a positive maximum (t,\theta) in V_{2} with t>{\underline{T}} and \theta\in\Int(\overline{J}_{t}) for some small \varepsilon, contradicting Claim 12. Similarly, V_{2}\neq V_{4}. Suppose V_{k}\cap\overline{J}_{t^{\prime}}=\emptyset for some t^{\prime}\in[{\underline{T}},t_{1}) and k\in\{1,3\}. Then the connectedness implies V_{k}\subset\{t>t^{\prime}\}. By Claim 12, the minimum of \eta in V_{k} is attained at (t,\overline{\theta}_{t}) for some t\in(t^{\prime},t_{1}]. However, by connectedness, this would imply
V_{4}\cap\{t={\underline{T}}\}=\emptyset,\qquad V_{4}\cap\{\theta=\underline{\theta}_{t}\}=\emptyset,
contradicting Claim 12(i) by looking at \max_{V_{4}}(\psi) for some small \varepsilon. The same argument works for V_{2},V_{4} as well, by looking at \min_{V_{1}}(\psi).
∎
Hence V_{k}\cap\overline{J}_{\underline{T}}\neq\emptyset for k=1,2,3,4. By connectedness, note that
\sup_{V_{k}\cap\overline{J}_{\underline{T}}}(\theta)\leqslant\inf_{V_{k+1}\cap\overline{J}_{\underline{T}}}(\theta),
hence, there are points \omega_{1}<\omega_{2}<\omega_{3}<\omega_{4}\in\overline{J}_{\underline{T}} so that
\eta({\underline{T}},\omega_{1})<0,\qquad\eta({\underline{T}},\omega_{2})>0,\qquad\eta({\underline{T}},\omega_{3})<0,\qquad\eta({\underline{T}},\omega_{4})>0.
(8.34)
Recall that \Theta_{0}^{-1}(\overline{J}_{\underline{T}})=\gamma_{\underline{T}}^{+}, which is the union of a concave graph g_{1,{\underline{T}}}^{+} and a convex graph g_{2,{\underline{T}}}^{+} over [b_{\underline{T}}^{+},3]. They have curvature bounded by 2e^{-7}\delta in Q_{\delta}(2), due to Claim 2. Note that h({\underline{T}},\cdot) is the support function of \gamma_{\underline{T}}^{+}. Denoting \varpi=\arctan f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{\underline{T}}^{+}), we have
(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)({\underline{T}},\cdot)<0\text{\ \ in\ \ }(\underline{\theta}_{\underline{T}},\varpi),\qquad(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)({\underline{T}},\cdot)>0\text{\ \ in\ \ }(\varpi,\overline{\theta}_{\underline{T}})
in the barrier sense, whenever these intervals are nonempty. If h is not differentiable at a point \theta in any of these intervals, then (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)({\underline{T}},\cdot) forms a negative resp. positive Dirac delta at ({\underline{T}},\theta), see the proof of (2.5). If h({\underline{T}},\cdot) is differentiable at \theta, then
|\partial_{\theta}h({\underline{T}},\theta)|\leqslant 1.9\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad h({\underline{T}},\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}h({\underline{T}},\theta)\tau_{\theta}\in Q_{\delta}(2),
by |h|\leqslant e^{-6}\delta (Claim 9) and |\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}\delta, hence the curvature bound gives \pm(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)({\underline{T}},\theta)\geqslant e^{7}/(2\delta), see (2.5). In summary, we have
\displaystyle(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)({\underline{T}},\theta)\leqslant-e^{7}/(2\delta)\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\theta\in(\underline{\theta}_{\underline{T}},\varpi)\ \ \text{and}
\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}h({\underline{T}},\theta)|\leqslant 1.9\text{\ \ or\ \ $\partial_{\theta}h({\underline{T}},\theta)$ does not exist}
and
\displaystyle(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)({\underline{T}},\theta)\geqslant e^{7}/(2\delta)\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\theta\in(\varpi,\overline{\theta}_{\underline{T}})\ \ \text{and}
\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}h({\underline{T}},\theta)|\leqslant 1.9\text{\ \ or\ \ $\partial_{\theta}h({\underline{T}},\theta)$ does not exist}.
Recalling |h|\leqslant e^{-6}\delta, and |\partial_{\theta}\phi|\leqslant 1.7 by (8.31), and |\partial_{\theta\theta}\phi|\leqslant 3.1e^{5}\delta^{-1} by (8.32), and the definition \eta=\phi-h, we obtain
\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{\theta\theta}\eta({\underline{T}},\theta)\geqslant 1\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\theta\in(\underline{\theta}_{\underline{T}},\varpi)\ \ \text{and}\\
&\hskip 168.0pt|\partial_{\theta}\eta({\underline{T}},\theta)|\leqslant 0.1\text{\ \ or\ \ $\partial_{\theta}\eta({\underline{T}},\theta)$ does not exist},\end{aligned}
(8.35)
\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{\theta\theta}\eta({\underline{T}},\theta)\leqslant-1\qquad\text{whenever}\qquad\theta\in(\varpi,\overline{\theta}_{\underline{T}})\ \ \text{and}\\
&\hskip 168.0pt|\partial_{\theta}\eta({\underline{T}},\theta)|\leqslant 0.1\text{\ \ or\ \ $\partial_{\theta}\eta({\underline{T}},\theta)$ does not exist},\end{aligned}
(8.36)
both in the barrier sense. On the other hand, recall from (8.34) that
\eta({\underline{T}},\omega_{1})<0,\qquad\eta({\underline{T}},\omega_{2})>0,\qquad\eta({\underline{T}},\omega_{3})<0,\qquad\eta({\underline{T}},\omega_{4})>0,
where \underline{\theta}_{\underline{T}}\leqslant\omega_{1}<\omega_{2}<\omega_{3}<\omega_{4}\leqslant\overline{\theta}_{\underline{T}}. Take any \omega_{5}\in(\omega_{2},\omega_{3}) with \eta({\underline{T}},\omega_{5})=0. There is a point \omega_{6}\in(\omega_{1},\omega_{5}) where \eta({\underline{T}},\cdot) is maximized. In view of (8.35), we obtain \omega_{6}\geqslant\varpi. There is another point \omega_{7}\in(\omega_{5},\omega_{4}) where \eta({\underline{T}},\cdot) is minimized. In view of (8.36), we obtain \omega_{7}\leqslant\varpi. Thus \varpi\geqslant\omega_{7}>\omega_{5}>\omega_{6}\geqslant\varpi, contradiction.
∎
Claim 14.
For each t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}] it holds
\|g_{1,t}\|_{C^{1,1/2}([b_{t},3]\cap(-1.5,1.5))}\leqslant e^{-6}\delta,\qquad\|g_{2,t}\|_{C^{1,1/2}([b_{t},3]\cap(-1.5,1.5))}\leqslant e^{-6}\delta.
(8.37)
For each t\in[{\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}) it holds
\|g_{1,t}^{+}\|_{C^{1,1/2}([b_{t}^{+},3]\cap(-1.5,1.5))}\leqslant e^{-6}\delta,\qquad\|g_{2,t}^{+}\|_{C^{1,1/2}([b_{t}^{+},3]\cap(-1.5,1.5))}\leqslant e^{-6}\delta.
(8.38)
We have
\|f_{\ridge}\|_{C^{1,1/2}([b_{\underline{T}}^{+},3]\cap[-1,1])}\leqslant e^{-2}\delta.
(8.39)
For almost every z=(x,y)\in E_{4}\cap\overline{Q_{\delta}(1)}\setminus\gamma, it holds
|\nabla w(z)|\leqslant e^{-4}\delta^{4/3}|y-f_{\ridge}(x)|^{-1/3}.
(8.40)
Proof.
For all t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}], \theta\in\Int(J_{t}) with |\partial_{\theta}h(t,\theta)|\leqslant 1.6 and |\partial_{\theta\theta}h|\leqslant e^{5}\delta^{-1}, Claim 11 gives
\partial_{\theta}(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta)\geqslant e^{15}\delta^{-2}-1.6\geqslant e^{14.9}\delta^{-2}.
Recalling (8.28), we may write \gamma_{t}=\alpha_{1}\cup\alpha_{2}\cup\alpha_{3}\cup\alpha_{4}, where \alpha_{1},\alpha_{4} are (possibly empty) line segments and \alpha_{2},\alpha_{3} are (possibly empty) smooth strictly convex curves. Denote \alpha=\alpha_{2}\cup\alpha_{3}. To show (8.37), it suffices to bound the C^{1,1/2} norms of \alpha\cap Q_{\delta}(1.5).
Let s be a length parameter of \alpha so that \alpha(0) is the vertex, and \alpha_{2} corresponds to s<0, and \alpha_{3} corresponds to s>0. The angle \theta is thus a function in s, with \theta(0)=\varpi_{t}. For each s with \alpha(s)=(x,y)\in Q_{\delta}(1.5), we have
|\partial_{\theta}h(t,\theta(s))|=\langle\alpha(s)\,,\,\tau_{\theta(s)}\rangle\leqslant|x|+|y|\,|\sin\theta(s)|\leqslant 1.5+1.5\delta\cdot e^{-10}\delta<1.6.
Thus, for these s either
|\partial_{\theta\theta}h(t,\theta(s))|>e^{5}\delta^{-1}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\partial_{\theta}(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s))\geqslant e^{14.9}\delta^{-2}.
In view that |h|\leqslant e^{-6}\delta, for these s either
\big|(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s))\big|>e^{4.9}\delta^{-1}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\partial_{\theta}(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s))\geqslant e^{14.9}\delta^{-2}.
(8.41)
The set \{s>0:\alpha(s)\in Q_{\delta}(1.5)\} is a (possibly empty) interval (s_{1},s_{2}) with 0\leqslant s_{1}. See Figure 41. For any s\in(s_{1},s_{2}) we argue as follows: if (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s))<e^{4.9}\delta^{-1}, then by (8.41), we have \partial_{\theta}(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s^{\prime}))\geqslant e^{14.9}\delta^{-2} for all s^{\prime}\in[s_{1},s]. Hence
(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s))\geqslant e^{14.9}\delta^{-2}\big(\theta(s)-\theta(s_{1})\big).
If (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s))\geqslant e^{4.9}\delta^{-1}, then trivially
(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s))\geqslant\frac{1}{2}e^{14.9}\delta^{-2}\big(\theta(s)-\theta(s_{1})\big)
since \theta(s),\theta(s_{1})\in(-e^{-10}\delta,e^{-10}\delta). Combining both cases, we always have
(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s))\geqslant\frac{1}{2}e^{14.9}\delta^{-2}\big(\theta(s)-\theta(s_{1})\big)\qquad\forall\,s\in(s_{1},s_{2}).
(8.42)
Therefore,
\frac{d\big(\theta(s)-\theta(s_{1})\big)}{ds}=\frac{1}{(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s))}\leqslant\frac{2e^{-14.9}\delta^{2}}{\theta(s)-\theta(s_{1})}.
Integrating this, we obtain
\theta(s^{\prime})-\theta(s)\leqslant e^{-6.5}\delta(s^{\prime}-s)^{1/2},\qquad\forall\,(s,s^{\prime})\subset(s_{1},s_{2}).
(8.43)
Similarly, the set \{s<0:\alpha(s)\in Q_{\delta}(1.5)\} is an interval (s_{3},s_{4}) with s_{4}\leqslant 0, where it holds
(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s))\leqslant-\frac{1}{2}e^{14.9}\delta^{-2}\big(\theta(s_{4})-\theta(s)\big)
(8.44)
and
\theta(s^{\prime})-\theta(s)\leqslant e^{-6.5}\delta(s^{\prime}-s)^{1/2}\qquad\forall\,(s,s^{\prime})\subset(s_{3},s_{4}).
(8.45)
Then, (8.43) (8.45) already imply (8.37). Also, recall the convergences g_{1,t}^{+}=\lim_{s\searrow t}g_{1,s}, g_{2,t}^{+}=\lim_{s\searrow t}g_{2,s}. Then (8.38) follows from (8.37) and this convergence.
Figure 41: The intervals (s_{1},s_{2}) and (s_{3},s_{4}).
Denote b=\max\{b_{t},-1.5\}. For our later convenience, let us show that
|\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h|(t,\theta(s))\geqslant e^{4}\delta^{-1}|x(s)-b|^{1/2},\qquad\forall\,s\in(s_{1},s_{2})\cup(s_{3},s_{4}),
(8.46)
where x(s) is the x-coordinate of \alpha(s). Suppose s\in(s_{1},s_{2}). If |\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h|(t,\theta(s))\geqslant e^{4.9}\delta^{-1}, then the result is obvious. If not, then (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\cdot) is increasing in (s_{1},s), hence
\displaystyle x(s)-b
\displaystyle=\int_{\theta(s_{1})}^{\theta(s)}(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\varpi)\cos\varpi\,d\varpi\leqslant(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s))\cdot\big(\theta(s)-\theta(s_{1})\big)
\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-14}\delta^{2}\cdot(\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\theta(s))^{2}\qquad\text{by \eqref{eq-ereg:C112_bound_1}},
as desired. The argument is similar if s\in(s_{3},s_{4}).
Next, we prove (8.39). Let us first show that
\displaystyle\big|f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{t}^{-})-f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{s}^{+})\big|\leqslant e^{-3}\delta(b_{t}^{-}-b_{s}^{+})^{1/2},
(8.47)
\displaystyle\text{whenever}\qquad{\underline{T}}\leqslant s<t\leqslant{\overline{T}},\ -1.2\leqslant b_{s}^{+}<b_{t}\leqslant 1.2.
The quantity b_{t}^{-} is defined in Claim 4. It is related to the structure of \widetilde{Z}_{t}, see (8.25). The properties of b_{t}^{-} that we need are the following: there is a concave function \widetilde{g}_{1,t}:[b_{t}^{-},3]\to(-3\delta,3\delta) and a convex function \widetilde{g}_{2,t}:[b_{t}^{-},3]\to(-3\delta,3\delta), so that
g_{1,s}^{+}<\widetilde{g}_{1,t}\leqslant\widetilde{g}_{2,t}<g_{2,s}^{+}\qquad\text{in}\ \ [b_{t}^{-},3],
(8.48)
and
\widetilde{g}_{1,t}(b_{t}^{-})=\widetilde{g}_{2,t}(b_{t}^{-})=f_{\ridge}(b_{t}^{-}),\qquad\widetilde{g}_{1,t}^{\prime}(b_{t}^{-})=\widetilde{g}_{2,t}^{\prime}(b_{t}^{-})=f_{\ridge}^{\prime}(b_{t}^{-}),
(8.49)
and
\|\widetilde{g}_{1,t}\|_{C^{1,1/2}([b_{t}^{-},3]\cap(-1.5,1.5))}\leqslant e^{-6}\delta,\qquad\|\widetilde{g}_{2,t}\|_{C^{1,1/2}([b_{t}^{-},3]\cap(-1.5,1.5))}\leqslant e^{-6}\delta.
(8.50)
The regularity (8.50) follows from (8.37) and the fact that \widetilde{g}_{i,t}\equiv g_{i,t} in [b_{t},3] and \widetilde{g}_{i,t} is linear in [b_{t}^{-},b_{t}], see (8.26).
Denote \Delta x=b_{t}^{-}-b_{s}^{+}. First, suppose otherwise that f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{t}^{-})>f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{s}^{+})+e^{-3}\delta\Delta x^{1/2}. Setting \rho=\Delta x/10, noting that b_{t}^{-}+\rho<1.5, we estimate
\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{2,t}(b_{t}^{-}+\rho)
\displaystyle\geqslant\widetilde{g}_{2,t}(b_{t}^{-})+\widetilde{g}^{\prime}_{2,t}(b_{t}^{-})\rho\qquad\text{(since $\widetilde{g}_{2,t}$ is convex)}
\displaystyle\geqslant g_{1,s}^{+}(b_{t}^{-})+\big(f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{s}^{+})+e^{-3}\delta\Delta x^{1/2}\big)\rho\qquad\text{(by our hypothesis)}
\displaystyle\geqslant g_{1,s}^{+}(b_{s}^{+})+f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{s}^{+})\Delta x-\frac{2}{3}e^{-6}\delta\Delta x^{3/2}+\big(f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{s}^{+})+e^{-3}\delta\Delta x^{1/2}\big)\rho
\displaystyle\text{(by \eqref{eq-ereg:reg_gtplus_E4})}
\displaystyle\geqslant g_{2,s}^{+}(b_{t}^{-}+\rho)-f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{s}^{+})(\Delta x+\rho)-\frac{2}{3}e^{-6}\delta(\Delta x+\rho)^{3/2}\qquad\text{(by \eqref{eq-ereg:reg_gtplus_E4})}
\displaystyle+f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{s}^{+})\Delta x-\frac{2}{3}e^{-6}\delta\Delta x^{3/2}+\big(f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{s}^{+})+e^{-3}\delta\Delta x^{1/2}\big)\rho.
Hence
\frac{2}{3}e^{-6}\delta(\Delta x+\rho)^{3/2}+\frac{2}{3}e^{-6}\delta\Delta x^{3/2}\geqslant e^{-3}\delta\Delta x^{1/2}\rho.
Since \rho=\Delta x/10, this gives
\frac{2}{3}e^{-6}\Big(\frac{11}{10}\Big)^{3/2}+\frac{2}{3}e^{-6}\geqslant\frac{1}{10}e^{-3},
which is not true. We thus obtain f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{t}^{-})\leqslant f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{s}^{+})+e^{-3}\delta\Delta x^{1/2}. The other inequality f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{t}^{-})\geqslant f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{s}^{+})-e^{-3}\delta\Delta x^{1/2} may be obtained by comparing \widetilde{g}_{1,t}(b_{t}^{-}+\rho) and g_{1,s}^{+}(b_{t}^{-}+\rho) in the same way. This proves (8.47).
In general, suppose x_{1},x_{2}\in[b_{\underline{T}}^{+},3]\cap[-1.2,1.2] and x_{1}<x_{2}. Call t_{1}=w(x_{1},f_{\ridge}(x_{1})) and t_{2}=w(x_{2},f_{\ridge}(x_{2})). Note that {\underline{T}}\leqslant t_{1}\leqslant t_{2}\leqslant{\overline{T}}, and {\underline{T}}<t_{1} if x_{1}>b_{\underline{T}}^{+}. Recall that:
•
f_{\ridge} is linear in [b_{t}^{-},b_{t}]\cap[-3,3] for each t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}] (Claim 4);
•
f_{\ridge} coincides with either g_{1,t} or g_{2,t} in [b_{t},b_{t}^{+}] for each t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}) (Claim 5);
•
f_{\ridge} either coincides with g_{1,{\overline{T}}},g_{2,{\overline{T}}} or is linear in [b_{\overline{T}},3] (Claim 7);
•
the C^{1,1/2} norms of g_{1,t},g_{2,t} are bounded by e^{-6}\delta in (-1.5,1.5), by (8.37) (8.38);
•
(8.47) holds, which connects b_{s}^{+} to b_{t}^{-}.
The triangle inequality implies
\displaystyle\big|f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(x_{2})-f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(x_{1})\big|
\displaystyle\leqslant\big|f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(x_{2})-f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{t_{2}}^{-})\big|+\big|f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{t_{2}}^{-})-f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{t_{1}}^{+})\big|
\displaystyle+\big|f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(b_{t_{1}}^{+})-f^{\prime}_{\ridge}(x_{1})\big|
\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{3}\cdot e^{-3}\delta|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1/2}\leqslant e^{-2}\delta|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1/2}.
Hence, we have shown
\|f_{\ridge}\|_{C^{1,1/2}([b_{\underline{T}}^{+},3]\cap[-1.2,1.2])}\leqslant e^{-2}\delta.
(8.51)
This in particular shows (8.39).
Finally, we prove (8.40). It suffices to show that for all t\in({\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}) and z=(x,y)\in\alpha\cap\overline{Q_{\delta}(1)}\setminus\text{(ridge)}, where \alpha is defined above, it holds:
the curvature of \alpha at z is bounded by e^{-4}\delta^{4/3}|y-f_{\ridge}(x)|^{-1/3}.
(8.52)
Let s be such that z=\alpha(s). Note that s\in(s_{1},s_{2})\cup(s_{3},s_{4}). We may assume s\in(s_{1},s_{2}) (the other case is analogous). In (8.46), if b_{t}<-1.2, then
|\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h|(t,\theta(s))\geqslant e^{4}\delta^{-1}(0.2)^{1/2}\geqslant e^{4}\delta^{-4/3}(6e^{-9}\delta)^{1/3}\geqslant e^{4}\delta^{-4/3}|y-f_{\ridge}(x)|^{1/3},
as desired. If b_{t}\geqslant-1.2, then (8.46) gives
|\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h|(t,\theta(s))\geqslant e^{4}\delta^{-1}\big(x(s)-b_{t}\big)^{1/2}.
(8.53)
The known C^{1,1/2} bounds in [-1.2,1.2] give
\displaystyle y(s)-f_{\ridge}(x(s))
\displaystyle=g_{2,t}(x(s))-f_{\ridge}(x(s))\leqslant\int_{b_{t}}^{x(s)}|g^{\prime}_{2,t}-f^{\prime}_{\ridge}|(x)\,dx
(8.54)
\displaystyle\leqslant\int_{b_{t}}^{x(s)}(e^{-6}+e^{-2})\delta(x-b_{t})^{1/2}\,dx\leqslant e^{-2}\delta\big(x(s)-b_{t}\big)^{3/2}
Combining (8.53) (8.54), we also obtain
|\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h|(t,\theta(s))\geqslant e^{4}\delta^{-1}\Big[e^{2}\delta^{-1}\big(y(s)-f_{\ridge}(x(s))\big)\Big]^{1/3}.\qed
Claim 15.
\|w\|_{C^{0,2/3}(E_{4}\cap Q_{\delta}(1))}\leqslant e^{-2}\delta^{4/3}.
Proof.
(8.40) implies for all (x,y_{1}),(x,y_{2})\in E_{4}\cap Q_{\delta}(1) that
\big|w(x,y_{1})-w(x,y_{2})\big|\leqslant e^{-4}\delta^{4/3}\int_{y_{1}}^{y_{2}}|y-f_{\ridge}(x)|^{-1/3}\,dy\leqslant e^{-3}\delta^{4/3}|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{2/3}.
(8.55)
Also, for all t\in[{\underline{T}},{\overline{T}}) and x so that -1<b_{t}^{+}<x<1, we have
\displaystyle w(x,f_{\ridge}(x))-w(b_{t}^{+},f_{\ridge}(b_{t}^{+}))
\displaystyle=w(x,f_{\ridge}(x))-w(x,g_{1,t}^{+}(x))
\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-3}\delta^{4/3}|f_{\ridge}(x)-g_{1,t}^{+}(x)|^{2/3}
\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-4}\delta^{2}|x-b_{t}^{+}|,
where the last line is obtained similarly as (8.54). In view that w(x,f_{\ridge}(x)) is non-decreasing for x\in[b_{\underline{T}}^{+},3] and is constant on [b_{t}^{-},b_{t}^{+}] for each t, it follows that
\big\|w\big(\cdot,f_{\ridge}(\cdot)\big)\big\|_{\Lip([b_{\underline{T}}^{+},3]\cap(-1,1))}\leqslant e^{-4}\delta^{2}.
(8.56)
Suppose z_{1}=(x_{1},y_{1})\in E_{4}\cap Q_{\delta}(1) and z_{2}=(x_{2},y_{2})\in E_{4}\cap Q_{\delta}(1). If |y_{1}-f_{\ridge}(x_{1})|\leqslant 8e^{-9}\delta|x_{1}-x_{2}|, then note that
\big|y_{1}-f_{\ridge}(x_{1})\big|^{2/3}+|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2/3}+|y_{2}-f_{\ridge}(x_{2})|^{2/3}\leqslant 2|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2/3}.
(8.57)
Hence
\displaystyle|w(z_{1})-w(z_{2})|
\displaystyle\leqslant\big|w(z_{1})-w(x_{1},f_{\ridge}(x_{1}))\big|+\big|w(x_{1},f_{\ridge}(x_{1}))-w(x_{2},f_{\ridge}(x_{2}))\big|
\displaystyle+\big|w(x_{2},f_{\ridge}(x_{2}))-w(z_{2})\big|
\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-3}\delta^{4/3}\Big(\big|y_{1}-f_{\ridge}(x_{1})\big|^{2/3}+|y_{2}-f_{\ridge}(x_{2})|^{2/3}\Big)+e^{-4}\delta^{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|
\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-2}\delta^{4/3}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2/3}.
The argument is the same if |y_{2}-f_{\ridge}(x_{2})|\leqslant 8e^{-9}\delta|x_{1}-x_{2}| or if y_{1}-f_{\ridge}(x_{1}) has opposite sign with y_{2}-f_{\ridge}(x_{2}), since (8.57) remains true in either case. It remains to treat the case
y_{1}>f_{\ridge}(x_{1})+8e^{-9}\delta|x_{1}-x_{2}|,\qquad y_{2}>f_{\ridge}(x_{2})+8e^{-9}\delta|x_{1}-x_{2}|.
The case with the opposite sign can be proved similarly. We may assume x_{1}<x_{2}. Find a segment \sigma\subset E_{4} with slope at most e^{-9}\delta, so that it connects z_{1} with another point z_{3}=(x_{2},y_{3}). Then note that
|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2/3}+|y_{3}-y_{2}|^{2/3}\leqslant 2|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2/3}.
(8.58)
Notice |y-f_{\ridge}(x)|\geqslant 6e^{-9}\delta|x_{1}-x_{2}| for all (x,y)\in\sigma. Then (8.40) and |\nu+\partial_{y}|<e^{-10}\delta yield
|\nabla w|\leqslant e^{-1}\delta^{4/3}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{-1/3},\qquad|\langle\nabla w\,,\,\partial_{x}\rangle|\leqslant e^{-10}\delta|\nabla w|.
Hence
\displaystyle\big|w(z_{1})-w(z_{3})\big|
\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-1}\delta|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{-1/3}\big(e^{-10}\delta|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|y_{1}-y_{3}|\big)
(8.59)
\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-9}\delta^{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{2/3}.
Also, by (8.55),
\big|w(z_{3})-w(z_{2})\big|\leqslant e^{-3}\delta^{4/3}|y_{3}-y_{2}|^{2/3}.
(8.60)
Combining (8.58) (8.59) (8.60) finally yields
\big|w(z_{1})-w(z_{2})\big|\leqslant e^{-2}\delta^{4/3}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2/3}.\qed
Combining Claim 15 and (8.39), we prove Claim 3.
∎
9 Sublevel sets of |\nabla u|
Suppose u is \infty-harmonic in a simply-connected domain \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}. Denote
Y_{t}=\big\{|\nabla u|<e^{-t}\big\},\qquad\widetilde{Z}_{t}=\big\{|\nabla u|\leqslant e^{-t}\big\},\qquad Z_{t}=\Int(\widetilde{Z}_{t}).
Theorem 9.1.
Suppose \Delta_{\infty}u=0 in a simply-connected domain \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}. Then:
(i)
Each Y_{t},Z_{t} is a disjoint union of simply-connected concave polygons. Each boundary edge of these polygons is a streamline of u.
(ii)
\widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}} is a disjoint union of nontrivial line segments that are streamlines of u.
(iii)
Let \sigma be a connected component of \widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}}. If \sigma\Subset\Omega, then \overline{\sigma}\cap\partial Z_{t}\neq\emptyset.
(iv)
Each Z_{t}\setminus Y_{t} is a disjoint union of nontrivial line segments whose endpoints either lie on \partial Z_{t} or is \infty. Each line segment is a streamline of u.
Item (i) a priori rules out accumulation of edges of \partial Y_{t} (Figure 42), since any compact set can only intersect finitely many edges of a concave polygon. We do not know whether such pathology occurs when \Omega is not simply-connected. Note that Y_{t} or Z_{t} may have infinitely many connected components.
Figure 42: Impossible picture of accumulating edges.
Proof of Theorem 9.1.
Let \{u_{p}\} be a sequence of p-harmonic functions with p\to\infty and u_{p}\to u in C^{0}_{\loc}(\Omega). For a basepoint z_{0}\in\Omega to be determined below, Theorem 6.1 yields a simple IMCF cluster \big(w,\nu,\{D_{i}\},\{\chi_{i}\}\big) in a region \Omega_{\reg}=\{w<+\infty\}. For each t\in\mathbb{R} we denote
Y^{\prime}_{t}=\{w>t\},\qquad\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}=\{w\geqslant t\},\qquad Z^{\prime}_{t}=\Int(\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}).
(9.1)
We make implicit their dependence on z_{0}.
(i) Let F be a connected component of Y_{t} or Z_{t}. If F is not simply-connected, then there is a domain D\Subset\Omega with \partial D\subset F but D\not\subset F. If F\subset Y_{t}, then |\nabla u|<e^{-t} on \partial D but |\nabla u|\geqslant e^{-t} somewhere in D. If F\subset Z_{t}, then |\nabla u|\leqslant e^{-t} on \partial D but |\nabla u|>e^{-t} somewhere in D. These violate the maximum principle for |\nabla u| (Lemma 2.5).
Take a basepoint z_{0}\in F. Let F^{\prime} denote the connected component of either Y^{\prime}_{t} or Z^{\prime}_{t} that contains z_{0}. By Theorem 6.1(xii), we have F=F^{\prime}. Recall that \Omega\setminus\Omega_{\reg}\subset\Crit(u) while |\nabla u|=e^{-t} on \partial F\cap\Omega, hence \partial F\cap\Omega\subset\Omega_{\reg}. If 0\in\partial F\cap\Omega and \nu(0)=-\partial_{y} (the general case is reduced to this via a rigid motion), then by Lemma 3.7(i) (with \delta=1, then restricting to the connected component F), for a small r we have
F\cap Q(r)=\Big\{x\in I,g_{1}(x)<y<g_{2}(x)\Big\},
where I=(-r,r) or I=(-r,a)\cup(b,r) with -r\leqslant a\leqslant b\leqslant r, and g_{1},g_{2}:\overline{I}\cap(-r,r)\to[-r,r] are functions with certain convexity conditions as stated in Lemma 3.7(i)(ic).
To show that F is a concave polygon, it suffices to rule out the case a=b=0. If this happens, then by the connectedness of F, we may find a simple loop \sigma\subset\overline{F} so that \sigma\setminus\{0\}\subset F and \sigma\cap Q(r)=\graphh((g_{1}+g_{2})/2). Let D be the region enclosed by \sigma, then D\not\subset F. If F\subset Z_{t}, then |\nabla u|\leqslant e^{-t} on \sigma but |\nabla u|>e^{-t} somewhere in D, contradicting Lemma 2.5. If F\subset Y_{t}, then |\nabla u|<e^{-t} on \sigma\setminus\{0\} but |\nabla u|\geqslant e^{-t} somewhere in D. But Lemma 2.4 implies that there are at least two points on \sigma on which |\nabla u|\geqslant e^{-t}, contradiction. Thus, we have shown that F is a concave polygon. Since \nu=-e^{w}\nabla^{\perp}u and \partial F^{\prime}\perp\nu, all edges of \partial F are streamlines of u.
(ii) For any z_{0}\in\widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}}, let \sigma be the connected component of \widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}} that contains z_{0}. Let F (resp. F^{\prime}) be the connected component of \widetilde{Z}_{t} (resp. \widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}) that contains z_{0}. By Theorem 6.1(xii) we have F=F^{\prime}\subset\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}. Also, we have Z_{t}\supset Z^{\prime}_{t} hence \overline{Z_{t}}\supset\overline{Z^{\prime}_{t}} due to w\leqslant-\log|\nabla u|. Hence
\sigma\subset F\setminus\overline{Z_{t}}\subset\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z^{\prime}_{t}}.
By Lemma 3.7(ii), the latter is a disjoint union of nontrivial line segments that are contained in the ridges (and recall that ridges are \nu-orthogonal). Let \sigma^{\prime} be the connected component of \widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z^{\prime}_{t}} containing z_{0} (i.e., it is a maximal line segment). So \sigma\subset\sigma^{\prime}.
Let us further prove that \overline{\sigma}=\overline{\sigma^{\prime}}. Observe that
\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z^{\prime}_{t}}\subset\{w=t\}\cap\text{(ridges)}\subset\big\{|\nabla u|=e^{-t}\big\}\subset\widetilde{Z}_{t}.
Then clearly \sigma^{\prime}\subset\widetilde{Z}_{t}, thus \sigma^{\prime}\subset F. Up to a rigid motion, we may assume that \sigma^{\prime} is contained in the x-axis and \nu=-\partial_{y} on \sigma^{\prime} (we do not know whether \sigma^{\prime} is bounded). Take any bounded interval I so that z_{0}\in I\times\{0\}\subset\sigma^{\prime}. Since \overline{\sigma^{\prime}} lies in a ridge, for sufficiently small r we have I\times(-r,r)\Subset\Omega_{\reg}, and w is an upward evolving weak IMCF in I\times(-r,0) and a downward evolving IMCF in I\times(0,r). Additionally, recall that w\equiv t on \sigma^{\prime} and that \sigma^{\prime}\cap\overline{Z^{\prime}_{t}}=\emptyset. Using the monotonicity of w in the y direction, we have
\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}\cap\big(I\times(-r,r)\big)=I\times\{0\}.
Since F=F^{\prime}, elementary topology then implies
F\cap\big(I\times(-r,r)\big)=I\times\{0\}.
This shows I\times\{0\}\subset\widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}}, hence I\times\{0\}\subset\sigma. By \sigma\subset\sigma^{\prime} and the arbitrariness of I, it follows that \overline{\sigma^{\prime}}=\overline{\sigma}.
(iii) Let \sigma^{\prime} be as in (ii) above, then \overline{\sigma}=\overline{\sigma^{\prime}}. Then Lemma 3.7(iii) gives \overline{\sigma^{\prime}}\cap\partial Z^{\prime}_{t}\neq\emptyset, hence \overline{\sigma}\cap\overline{Z_{t}}\neq\emptyset since \overline{Z_{t}}\supset\overline{Z^{\prime}_{t}}, and hence \overline{\sigma}\cap\partial Z_{t}\neq\emptyset by elementary topology.
(iv) This is a direct consequence of Lemma 2.4 with \text{``$\Omega$''}=Z_{t}.
∎
10 Precompact sublevel sets
Let \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2} be simply-connected, and \Delta_{\infty}u=0 in \Omega. We will implicitly use the fact that \nabla u is continuous. As usual, we denote
Y_{t}=\big\{|\nabla u|<e^{-t}\big\},\qquad\widetilde{Z}_{t}=\big\{|\nabla u|\leqslant e^{-t}\big\},\qquad Z_{t}=\Int(\widetilde{Z}_{t}).
We consider the case where a connected component of Y_{t} is precompact.
Theorem 10.1.
Let \Omega be simply-connected and u be \infty-harmonic in \Omega. Suppose t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}, and F is a connected component of Y_{t_{0}} with F\Subset\Omega. Then:
(i)
\Crit(u)\cap F is a nonempty finite set.
(ii)
Let \{z_{k}\} be the critical points of u in F. Then for each k there is d_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 2} so that
\lambda^{-\frac{d_{k}^{2}}{2d_{k}-1}}\Big[u\big(\lambda z+z_{k}\big)-u(z_{k})\Big]
(10.1)
converges in C^{1}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}) to a quasiradial solution of degree d_{k}, as \lambda\to 0.
(iii)
Let N be the number of edges of \partial F. Then
N-2=\sum_{k}\big(2d_{k}-2\big).
(iv)
For each k, we have u\in C^{1,1/3}(B(z_{k},r)) for some r>0.
The following is a direct consequence:
Theorem 10.2.
If K\Subset\Omega and \Crit(u)\cap\partial K=\emptyset, then \Crit(u)\cap K is a finite set.
Recall that each Y_{t},Z_{t} is a disjoint union of simply-connected concave polygons. Thus, each precompact component E of Y_{t},Z_{t} has a well-defined number of edges. We denote it by N(E) in this section. By Lemma 10.7, we have N(E)\geqslant 4 and N(E) is even. We show the following evolution pattern of sublevel sets of |\nabla u|:
•
When Z_{t} is connected and changes continuously in a time interval (t_{1},t_{2}), the support function of \partial Z_{t} solves \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h (Lemma 10.4).
•
When Z_{t} becomes disconnected or changes noncontinuously at a time T, it is cut by finitely many segments into smaller concave polygons (Lemma 10.5).
•
The quantity \sum_{E:\,\text{component of $Z_{t}$}}(N(E)-2) is constant in t.
•
There can only be finitely many splitting times. Eventually, the support function solves \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h with t\in[T,\infty) for some T in each polygon. The asymptotics at critical points follow by taking t\to\infty.
All lemmas below apply to any \infty-harmonic function u in a simply-connected domain \Omega. The following condition plays a central role:
Z_{t}\Subset\Omega,\qquad Z_{t}=Y_{t},\qquad Z_{t}\text{ is connected}.
(\textrm{P}_{t})
This condition is forward stable:
Lemma 10.3.
Let F be a connected component of Y_{t} so that F\Subset\Omega. Then there is T>t so that
Z_{s}\cap F is connected and is equal to Y_{s}\cap F, for all s\in(t,T].
In a time interval where (
P
t
) holds, the support function solves \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h:
Lemma 10.4.
Suppose -\infty\leqslant t_{1}<t_{2}\leqslant\infty, and (
P
t
) holds for all t\in(t_{1},t_{2}). Then:
(i)
t\mapsto N(Y_{t}) is constant in (t_{1},t_{2});
(ii)
\widetilde{Z}_{t}=\overline{Z_{t}} for all t\in(t_{1},t_{2}];
(iii)
u has no critical points in Y_{t_{1}}\setminus\widetilde{Z}_{t_{2}}. Denoting the constant in (i) by N, the map
\Big(\!-\log|\nabla u|,\,\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}\Big):Y_{t_{1}}\setminus\widetilde{Z}_{t_{2}}\to(t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
(10.2)
lifts to a degree -1 homeomorphism
\Theta:Y_{t_{1}}\setminus\widetilde{Z}_{t_{2}}\to(t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N-2)\pi\big).
The support function
h(t,\theta)={\big\langle{\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta)}\,,\,{\nu_{\theta}}\big\rangle}\qquad\forall\,(t,\theta)\in(t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N-2)\pi\big)
(10.3)
is a smooth solution of \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h in (t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N-2)\pi\big), and (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\cdot) has N nondegenerate roots for each t\in(t_{1},t_{2}).
In the lemma, if t_{1}=-\infty (resp. t_{2}=\infty), then our convention is Y_{t_{1}}=\Omega (resp. \widetilde{Z}_{t_{2}}=\Crit(u)). By concave polygon geometry, each preimage of \Theta will clearly be a line segment (once the quantity N is appropriately set up). The fact that \Theta is homeomorphism implies that each preimage is a point. Thus \partial Z_{t} does not contain nontrivial line segments.
At a time when (
P
t
) fails to hold, we show that \partial Z_{t} splits into simpler polygons whose number of edges are preserved as in (10.4):
Lemma 10.5.
Suppose (
P
t
) holds for all t\in[{\underline{T}},T), but does not hold for t=T. Then:
(i)
Z_{T}\setminus Y_{T} is a finite disjoint union of line segments with endpoints on \partial Z_{T}.
(ii)
Denote N=N(Z_{t}) for any t\in({\underline{T}},T), which is well-defined by Lemma 10.4(i). Let \{E_{k}\}_{1\leqslant k\leqslant l} be the connected components of Y_{T}. Then 2\leqslant l<\infty, and N(E_{k})\leqslant N-2 for all k, and
N-2=\sum_{k=1}^{l}\big(N(E_{k})-2\big).
(10.4)
We start proving the main results. We need two auxiliary lemmas:
Lemma 10.6.
For each K\Subset\Omega and T\in\mathbb{R}, there is a constant c=c(K,T) so that: if F is a connected component of Y_{t} or Z_{t}, with t\leqslant T and F\subset K, then F contains a disk with radius c.
Proof.
Let B be a largest open disk contained in F. Then \partial B\cap\partial F cannot be contained in an open semicircle in \partial B. Recalling that edges of \partial F are streamlines of u on which |\nabla u|=e^{-t}, we thus find points z_{1},z_{2}\in\partial B\cap\partial F with
e^{T}\big|\nabla u(z_{1})-\nabla u(z_{2})\big|\geqslant\Big|\frac{\nabla u(z_{1})}{|\nabla u(z_{1})|}-\frac{\nabla u(z_{2})}{|\nabla u(z_{2})|}\Big|\geqslant\sqrt{3}.
If the radius of B is too small, this would violate the uniform continuity of \nabla u in K.
∎
Lemma 10.7.
For any connected component F of Y_{t},Z_{t} with F\Subset\Omega, we have N(F)\geqslant 4 and N(F) is even.
Proof.
The vector field \nabla u/|\nabla u| is everywhere tangent to the edges of \partial F. If N(F) is odd, such a vector field does not exist, by Gauss-Bonnet (see Subsection 2.2). Since there exist no concave 2-gons, we have N\geqslant 4.
∎
Lemma 10.8.
If F is a connected component of Z_{t} with F\Subset\Omega, then F\setminus Y_{t} is a disjoint union of finitely many line segments with endpoints on \partial F. Letting \{E_{k}\}_{1\leqslant k\leqslant l} be the connected components of F\cap Y_{t}, we have 1\leqslant l<\infty and
N(F)-2=\sum_{k}\big(N(E_{k})-2\big).
(10.5)
Proof.
By Theorem 9.1(iv), F\setminus Y_{t} is a disjoint union of line segments whose endpoints lie on \partial F. Let \sigma be one of the line segments. Then \sigma contacts \partial F tangentially since they are both tangential to \nabla u. Then F\setminus\sigma is the disjoint union of two concave polygons F_{1},F_{2} with N(F)=N(F_{1})+N(F_{2})-2: this follows since a new vertex forms at each endpoint of \sigma. Applying this argument inductively, we obtain that: for any finite set of segments \{\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{l}\} in F\setminus Y_{t}, the set F\setminus\cup\sigma_{k} consists of l+1 components \{F_{1},\cdots,F_{l+1}\} so that
N(F)=\sum_{k}N(F_{k})-2l=\sum_{k}\big(N(F_{k})-2\big)+2.
(10.6)
By Lemma 10.7, F\setminus Y_{t} contains at most N(F)/2 segments; and (10.6) implies (10.5).
∎
Lemma 10.9.
If Z_{t}\Subset\Omega and Y_{t} is connected, then Z_{t}=Y_{t}.
Proof.
This is a direct consequence of Lemma 10.8.
∎
Proof of Lemma 10.3.
Let c=c(\overline{F},t+1) be the constant in Lemma 10.6. Since F is connected and open, we may find a connected set G\Subset F with N(G,c/3)\supset F. Since F=\bigcup_{s>t}(Y_{s}\cap F), there is T\in(t,t+1) so that Y_{s}\cap F\supset G for all s\in(t,T]. Then exactly one connected component of Y_{s} contains G. If Y_{s}\cap F has another connected component L, then L\cap G=\emptyset but L\subset\overline{F}. This violates Lemma 10.6 since N(G,c/2)\supset\overline{F}. Thus, Y_{s}\cap F is connected for all s\in(t,T). Next, Lemma 10.9 (with \text{``$\Omega$''}=F, note that F is simply-connected by Theorem 9.1) implies Z_{s}\cap F=Y_{s}\cap F, as desired.
∎
Proof of Lemma 10.4.
Denote
E=Y_{t_{1}}\setminus\widetilde{Z}_{t_{2}}.
Clearly, u has no critical points in E.
Note that \widetilde{Z}_{t_{2}}=\bigcap_{t\in(t_{1},t_{2})}\overline{Y_{t}}, and \overline{Y_{t}} are connected for all t\in(t_{1},t_{2}) by (
P
t
). Hence \widetilde{Z}_{t_{2}} is connected. Also, Y_{t_{1}}=\bigcup_{t\in(t_{1},t_{2})}Y_{t} is connected. Hence, E is homeomorphic to an open annulus. Fix a basepoint z_{0}\in\widetilde{Z}_{t_{2}}. Then Theorem 6.1 gives a simple IMCF cluster \big(w,\nu,\{D_{i}\},\{\chi_{i}\}\big) in an open set \Omega_{\reg}=\{w<\infty\}. Denote
Y^{\prime}_{t}=\{w>t\},\qquad\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}=\{w\geqslant t\},\qquad Z^{\prime}_{t}=\Int(\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}).
Observe that Z_{t}=Z^{\prime}_{t} for all t\in(t_{1},t_{2}): on one hand, w\leqslant-\log|\nabla u| implies Z_{t}\supset Z^{\prime}_{t}. On the other hand, letting F^{\prime}_{t} be the connected component of Z^{\prime}_{t} containing z_{0}, Theorem 6.1(xii) and the connectedness of Z_{t} imply Z_{t}=F^{\prime}_{t}\subset Z^{\prime}_{t}.
We claim that
\overline{\bigcup_{t\in(t_{1},t_{2})}\partial Z_{t}}\supset E.
(10.7)
Otherwise, there is z\in E and t\in(t_{1},t_{2}) and r>0, so that
B(z,r)\subset Z_{s}\text{\ \ for all\ \ $s<t$,}\qquad B(z,r)\subset E\setminus Z_{s}\text{\ \ for all\ \ $s>t$}.
(10.8)
Now \widetilde{Z}_{t}=\bigcap_{s<t}Y_{s}=\bigcap_{s<t}Z_{s}, hence B(z,r)\subset\widetilde{Z}_{t}, hence B(z,r)\subset\Int(\widetilde{Z}_{t})=Z_{t}. Also, we have Z_{t}=Y_{t}=\bigcup_{s>t}Y_{s}=\bigcup_{s>t}Z_{s}, hence B(z,r)\subset E\setminus Z_{t}, contradiction.
Combining (10.7), the fact Z_{t}=Z^{\prime}_{t}, and |\nabla u|=e^{-t} on each \partial Z^{\prime}_{t} by Theorem 6.1(viii), we obtain w=-\log|\nabla u| in E. Recalling \nu=-e^{w}\nabla^{\perp}u in Theorem 6.1(v), it follows that
\nu=-\frac{\nabla^{\perp}u}{|\nabla u|}\qquad\text{in}\ \ E.
(10.9)
In particular, |\nu|\equiv 1 in E, and the map (10.2) coincides with (-\log|\nabla u|,\nu^{\perp}).
Let -(N^{\prime}-2)/2 denote the degree of the map (10.2), i.e. the coefficient of the map \mathbb{Z}\cong H_{1}(E,\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}. Thus, (10.2) lifts to a degree -1 map
\Theta:E\to(t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N^{\prime}-2)\pi\big).
Forgetting the time coordinate, it projects to a degree -1 map
\vartheta:E\to\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N^{\prime}-2)\pi\big).
Since each \partial Z_{t} generates H_{1}(E,\mathbb{Z}), the restriction \vartheta|_{\partial Z_{t}} also has degree -1. But Gauss-Bonnet implies that \nu:\partial Z_{t}\to\mathbb{S}^{1} has degree -\frac{N(Z_{t})-2}{2}. Comparing the degrees, we have N^{\prime}=N(Z_{t}) for all t\in(t_{1},t_{2}). This shows item (i) since Y_{t}=Z_{t}.
It is now a trivial consequence that \Theta is also a lift of the function (w,\nu^{\perp}) from the set \bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\partial Z^{\prime}_{t}\cap E to (t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N^{\prime}-2)\pi\big). Thus, the lifting condition in Theorem 7.1 (with ambient domain E and M=\mathbb{S}^{1}((N^{\prime}-2)\pi)) is satisfied.
Set N=N^{\prime} (the notation is consistent in item (iii)). Let us show that \Theta^{-1}(t,\theta) is a line segment for each (t,\theta)\in(t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N-2)\pi\big). First, notice that
\Theta^{-1}\big(\{t\}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}((N-2)\pi)\big)=\big\{|\nabla u|=e^{-t}\big\}=\partial\widetilde{Z}_{t}
since Z_{t}=Y_{t} by our assumption (
P
t
). Thus
\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta)\subset\partial\widetilde{Z}_{t}=\partial Z_{t}\cup\big(\widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}}\big).
Since \widetilde{Z}_{t}\Subset\Omega, by Theorem 9.1(ii)(iii), \widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}} is a disjoint union of nontrivial line segments \sigma_{i} which contact \partial Z_{t}. However, \Theta^{-1}(t,\theta)\cap\partial Z_{t} is also a line segment (denoted by \alpha), and thus
\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta)=\alpha\cup\bigcup\Big\{\sigma_{i}:\overline{\sigma_{i}}\cap\alpha\neq\emptyset\Big\},
(10.10)
which is indeed a line segment (recall that \partial Z_{t} and \sigma_{i} are all tangent to \nabla u).
For each t_{3},t_{4} with t_{1}<t_{3}<t_{4}<t_{2}, the restricted map
\Theta:\widetilde{Z}_{t_{3}}\setminus Y_{t_{4}}\to[t_{3},t_{4}]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N-2)\pi\big)
is continuous, surjective, hence is a quotient map. It is shown above that each preimage is a line segment (whose direction is parallel to \nabla u). Hence, the continuous function
\overline{h}(z)={\Big\langle{z}\,,\,{-\frac{\nabla^{\perp}u}{|\nabla u|}(z)}\Big\rangle}=\langle z\,,\,\nu(z)\rangle
(10.11)
is constant on each preimage of \Theta, thus descends to a continuous function
h:[t_{3},t_{4}]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N-2)\pi\big)\to\mathbb{R}.
(10.12)
Then Theorem 7.1(i) implies that h is a viscosity solution of \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h on (t_{3},t_{4}]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N-2)\pi\big), hence a smooth solution as well. Letting t_{3}\searrow t_{1} and t_{4}\nearrow t_{2}, we obtain a smooth support function h that solves \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h in (t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N-2)\pi\big). So each time slice h(t,\cdot) is smooth, and by counting the number of vertices, (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\cdot) has N roots. The Sturmian theory then implies that all the roots are nondegenerate.
We next show that \Theta is a homeomorphism. By the invariance of domain, it suffices to show that \Theta is injective, namely, each preimage is a point. For all t_{3}\in(t_{1},t_{2}) note that
|\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h|\leqslant C(t_{3})\qquad\text{in}\ \ \big[t_{3},\min\{t_{3}+1,t_{2}\}\big)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N-2)\pi\big).
This implies that all edges of \partial Z_{t}=\partial Y_{t} have curvature at least 1/C(t_{3}). In particular, \partial Z_{t} does not contain nontrivial line segments and hence \Theta|_{\partial Z_{t}} is injective.
Suppose that \widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}} contains a nontrivial line segment \sigma for some t\in(t_{1},t_{2}]. Fix t_{3}>\max\{t_{1},t-1\} and z\in\Int(\sigma). We know that Z_{t}=Z^{\prime}_{t}, hence \widetilde{Z}_{t}=\bigcap_{s<t}Z_{s}=\bigcap_{s<t}\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{s}=\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}. Hence \widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}}=\widetilde{Z}^{\prime}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z^{\prime}_{t}}. Then apply Lemma 3.7(i): after a suitable rigid motion, there is a small enough r so that \widetilde{Z}_{t}\cap Q(r)=(-r,r)\times\{0\}, and for s<t close enough to t we have
Z_{s}\cap Q(r)=\Big\{x\in(-r,r),\,g_{1,s}(x)<y<g_{2,s}(x)\Big\}
for some concave g_{1,s} and convex g_{2,s} with g_{1,s}\nearrow 0, g_{2,s}\searrow 0 as s\nearrow t. But this violates the uniform curvature lower bounds of \graphh(g_{1,s}) and \graphh(g_{2,s}). Hence \widetilde{Z}_{t}=\overline{Z_{t}}.
In summary, we have shown that \widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus\overline{Z_{t}}=\emptyset for all t\in(t_{1},t_{2}], which implies (ii), and that \Theta is injective since \{|\nabla u|=e^{-t}\}=\widetilde{Z}_{t}\setminus Y_{t}=\partial Z_{t} and \Theta|_{\partial Z_{t}} is injective. Thus \Theta is a homeomorphism. Finally, it is clear that the support function h defined in (10.3) coincides with the function defined in (10.11) (10.12).
∎
Proof of Lemma 10.5.
Note that Z_{T} is either disconnected or is not Y_{T}, since (\text{P}_{T}) does not hold. Denote E=Y_{\underline{T}}\setminus\widetilde{Z}_{T}, so u has no critical points in \overline{E}. By Lemma 10.4, the map \phi=\nabla u/|\nabla u|:E\to\mathbb{S}^{1} has degree -(N-2)/2, where N=N(Y_{t})=N(Z_{t}) for all t\in({\underline{T}},T). Let \{F_{j}\} be the connected components of Z_{T}. Then \sum\partial F_{j} is homologous to \partial Y_{t} for any t\in({\underline{T}},T), and by Gauss-Bonnet, the map \phi has degree -(N(F_{j})-2)/2 on each \partial F_{j}. As a result,
N-2=\sum_{j}\big(N(F_{j})-2\big).
Using Lemma 10.8 on each F_{j}, we obtain (i) and (10.4). The fact N(E_{k})\leqslant N-2 follows from N(E_{k})\geqslant 4 for all k. The fact l\geqslant 2 follows from Lemma 10.9: if l=1, then Y_{T} is connected, hence Z_{T}=Y_{T}, hence (\text{P}_{T}) holds, contradiction.
∎
Proof of Theorem 10.1.
For each t\geqslant t_{0}, let l(t) be the number of connected components of Y_{t}\cap F, and denote
N(t)=2+\sum\Big\{N(G)-2:G\text{ is a connected component of $Y_{t}\cap F$}\Big\}.
Corollary 5.3 implies that each connected component of Y_{t} contains a critical point of u, hence a connected component of Y_{s} for all s>t. It follows that l(t) is nondecreasing in t.
Claim 1.
For each t\geqslant t_{0}, there is T>t so that l(t) is constant in [t,T].
Proof.
Fix t\geqslant t_{0}. Let \{F_{k}\} be the connected components of Y_{t}\cap F. By Lemma 10.3 with \text{``$F$''}=F_{k}, there is T>t so that Y_{s}\cap F_{k} is connected for all k and all s\in(t,T]. So Y_{s}\cap F has the same number of connected components as Y_{t}\cap F.
∎
Claim 2.
If l(t) is constant in [t_{1},t_{2}) and l(t_{2}^{-})<l(t_{2}), then N(t) is constant in [t_{1},t_{2}].
Proof.
Let \{F_{k}\} be the set of connected components of Y_{t_{1}}\cap F. Then Y_{t}\cap F_{k} is connected for each t\in[t_{1},t_{2}), by the constancy of l(t). Then Lemma 10.9 with \text{``$\Omega$''}=F_{k} implies Y_{t}\cap F_{k}=Z_{t}\cap F_{k}. Thus, the condition (
P
t
) is satisfied for all t\in(t_{1},t_{2}) when restricted to F_{k}. Then Lemma 10.4(i) applied inside each F_{k} implies that N(Y_{t}\cap F_{k}) is constant. Hence,
N(t)=2+\sum_{k}\big(N(Y_{t}\cap F_{k})-2\big)
stays constant in (t_{1},t_{2}) as well.
For each k: if Y_{t_{2}}\cap F_{k} is connected, then we use Lemma 10.3, 10.9 and 10.4(i) to conclude that Y_{t}\cap F_{k} is connected and N(Y_{t}\cap F_{k})=\text{const} for t\in(t_{1},t_{2}+\varepsilon), for some \varepsilon>0. If Y_{t_{2}}\cap F_{k} is not connected, then (
P
t
) does not hold at t_{2} when restricted to F_{k}, hence Lemma 10.5(ii) applies. Considering both cases, N(t) is constant in (t_{1},t_{2}].
Finally, notice that F_{k}=\bigcup_{t>t_{1}}(Y_{t}\cap F_{k}). Denoting
b_{k}=-\frac{N(Y_{t}\cap F_{k})-2}{2}\qquad\text{for any}\ \ t\in(t_{1},t_{2}),
by Lemma 10.4, the map \big(\!-\log|\nabla u|,\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}\big) is a degree b_{k} map from F_{k}\setminus\widetilde{Z}_{t_{2}} to (t_{1},t_{2})\times\mathbb{S}^{1}. This map clearly extends to \overline{F_{k}}\setminus\widetilde{Z}_{t_{2}} and has degree b_{k} as well. Hence, the tangent vector map \frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}:\partial F_{k}\to\mathbb{S}^{1} has degree b_{k}, which implies N(F_{k})=-2b_{k}+2=N(Y_{t}\cap F_{k}) for any t\in(t_{1},t_{2}). Summing this over k, it follows that
N(t_{1})=2+\sum_{k}\big(N(F_{k})-2\big)=2+\sum_{k}\big(N(Y_{t}\cap F_{k})-2\big)=N(t)
for any t\in(t_{1},t_{2}). Hence N(t) is constant in [t_{1},t_{2}].
∎
Therefore, there is a sequence of times t_{0}<t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots so that
l(t_{0})=l(t_{1}^{-})<l(t_{1})=l(t_{2}^{-})<l(t_{2})=\cdots.
Claim 2 implies that N(t) is constant for t\in[t_{0},\lim_{i\to\infty}t_{i}). However, since each connected component of Y_{t}\cap F has at least 4 edges, we have
N(t_{0})=N(t)\geqslant 2l(t)\qquad\forall\,t\in[t_{0},\lim_{i\to\infty}t_{i}).
Hence l(t^{-})<l(t) can only occur for at most N(t_{0})/2 times. As a result, there are finitely many splitting times, and there is T\in\mathbb{R} so that l(t) is constant in [T,\infty).
