Let \{F_{k}\} be the connected components of Y_{T}\cap F. Hence, the condition (
P
t
) holds for all t>T when restricted to each F_{k} (see the proof of Claim 2). Applying Lemma 10.4, the support function h(t,\theta) defined in (10.3) satisfies \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h in (T,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N_{k}-2)\pi\big), where N_{k}=N(Y_{t}\cap F_{k}) for all t>T. Denote d_{k}=N_{k}/2 (so d_{k}\geqslant 2). Then we may write
h(t,\theta)=\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\exp\Big(t-\frac{n^{2}}{(d_{k}-1)^{2}}t\Big)\Big(a_{n}\sin\frac{n\theta}{d_{k}-1}+b_{n}\cos\frac{n\theta}{d_{k}-1}\Big).
Lemma 10.4(iii) implies that each (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\cdot) has N_{k}=2d_{k} roots for all t>T. Hence, all the frequencies with n<d_{k}-1 must vanish, and the n=d_{k} frequency does not vanish. Hence for some a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{R} with (a,b)\neq(0,0), we have
\displaystyle\exp\Big(\frac{2d_{k}-1}{(d_{k}-1)^{2}}t\Big)\cdot\Big[h(t,\theta)-c\sin\theta+d\cos\theta\Big]
(10.13)
\displaystyle\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{\ C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1}((N_{k}-2)\pi))\ }a\sin\frac{d_{k}\theta}{d_{k}-1}+b\cos\frac{d_{k}\theta}{d_{k}-1}.
Recall that \partial Y_{t}\cap F_{k} can be described as
\partial Y_{t}\cap F_{k}=\Big\{h(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}h(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}:\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N_{k}-2)\pi\big)\Big\},
(10.14)
and in view that \nabla u=e^{-t}\tau_{\theta} on \partial Y_{t}\cap F_{k}, we have
\nabla u\Big(h(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}h(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}\Big)=e^{-t}\tau_{\theta},\qquad\forall\,(t,\theta)\in(T,\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N_{k}-2)\pi\big).
(10.15)
Denote z_{k}=(c,d). Then h(t,\theta)\to c\sin\theta-d\cos\theta in C^{1} implies \partial Y_{t}\cap F_{k}\to\{z_{k}\} in the Hausdorff topology, as t\to\infty. Therefore \Crit(u)\cap F_{k}=\{z_{k}\}. This proves item (i).
For (iii), the constancy of N(t) implies
N(F)-2=N(t_{0})-2=N(T+1)-2=\sum_{k}(N_{k}-2)=\sum_{k}(2d_{k}-2).
We next prove (ii). Fix a k. For a scaling factor \lambda>0, denote
u_{\lambda}(z)=\lambda^{-\frac{d_{k}^{2}}{2d_{k}-1}}\Big[u\big(\lambda z+z_{k}\big)-u(z_{k})\Big].
Then
Y_{t,\lambda}:=\big\{|\nabla u_{\lambda}|<e^{-t}\big\}=\lambda^{-1}\big(Y_{t^{\prime}}-z_{k}\big)\qquad\text{where}\ \ t^{\prime}=t-\frac{(d_{k}-1)^{2}}{2d_{k}-1}\log\lambda.
Therefore, \partial Y_{t,\lambda}\cap\lambda^{-1}(F_{k}-z_{k}) has support function
\displaystyle h_{\lambda}(t,\theta)
\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\Big[h(t^{\prime},\theta)-c\sin\theta+d\cos\theta\Big]
\displaystyle=\exp\Big(\!-\frac{2d_{k}-1}{(d_{k}-1)^{2}}t\Big)\exp\Big(\frac{2d_{k}-1}{(d_{k}-1)^{2}}t^{\prime}\Big)\Big[h(t^{\prime},\theta)-c\sin\theta+d\cos\theta\Big].
By (10.13), we have
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}h_{\lambda}(t,\theta)=\exp\Big(\!-\frac{2d_{k}-1}{(d_{k}-1)^{2}}t\Big)\Big[a\sin\frac{d_{k}\theta}{d_{k}-1}+b\cos\frac{d_{k}\theta}{d_{k}-1}\Big].
(10.16)
Call the right hand side \overline{h}. Let \overline{u} be the degree d_{k} quasiradial solution so that Y_{t,\infty}:=\{|\nabla\overline{u}|<e^{-t}\} has support function given by \overline{h} (see Example 4.3). Then (10.14) implies
\partial Y_{t,\lambda}\cap\lambda^{-1}(F_{k}-z_{k})\to\partial Y_{t,\infty}
(10.17)
in the Hausdorff topology as \lambda\to 0. By the maximum principle for the gradient, \{|\nabla u_{\lambda}|\} is equibounded in B(R) for all R>0. By Savin’s equicontinuity theorem and Arzelà-Ascoli and u_{\lambda}(0)=0, for each sequence \lambda_{n}\to 0 we may extract a further subsequential limit u_{\infty} of u_{\lambda_{n}} in C^{1}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}). Rescaling (10.15) and taking \lambda\to 0 gives
\nabla u_{\infty}\Big(\overline{h}(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}\overline{h}(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}\Big)=e^{-t}\tau_{\theta},\qquad\forall\,(t,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N_{k}-2)\pi\big).
But this shows \nabla u_{\infty}=\nabla\overline{u}, hence u_{\infty}=\overline{u} (see Example 4.3). Since the limit object is independent of the sequence \lambda_{n}, it follows that
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}u_{\lambda}=\overline{u}\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{1}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),
which shows (ii).
It remains to prove (iv). We may assume z_{k}=0 and u(0)=0. By item (i) and scaling, we may assume \Crit(u)\cap B(2)=\{0\}. It remains to show that u\in C^{1,1/3}(\overline{B}(1)). Suppose this does not hold. Then there is a sequence of points z_{i},\zeta_{i}\in\overline{B(1)}, so that
\frac{|\nabla u(z_{i})-\nabla u(\zeta_{i})|}{|z_{i}-\zeta_{i}|^{1/3}}\to\infty.
(10.18)
Then Theorem 8.3 implies z_{i},\zeta_{i}\to 0. By (ii), there is d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 2} and a quasiradial solution \overline{u} in \mathbb{R}^{2}, so that
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\lambda^{-\frac{d^{2}}{2d-1}}u(\lambda z)=\overline{u}(z)\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{1}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
(10.19)
It follows that
|\nabla u(z)|\leqslant C|z|^{1/3}\qquad\forall\,z\in\overline{B(1)}.
Hence, we must have
\frac{|z_{i}-\zeta_{i}|}{|z_{i}|+|\zeta_{i}|}\to 0.
(10.20)
Denote r_{i}=|z_{i}|. Passing to a subsequence, we may assume r_{i}^{-1}z_{i}\to q. Then (10.20) implies r_{i}^{-1}\zeta_{i}\to q as well, and r_{i}^{-1}|z_{i}-\zeta_{i}|\to 0. Let C be the constant in Theorem 8.2 (with \delta=1). Since \overline{u}\in C^{1}, we may find r\ll 1 and A\in\text{SO}(2) and b\in\mathbb{R}, c\neq 0, so that
\sup_{Q(4)}\Big|cr^{-1}\big(\overline{u}(Arz+q)-b\big)-x\Big|\leqslant\frac{1}{4C},
where x is the first coordinate of z. Then (10.19) implies
\sup_{Q(4)}\Big|cr^{-1}\Big(\lambda^{-\frac{d^{2}}{2d-1}}u(\lambda Arz+\lambda q)-b\Big)-x\Big|\leqslant\frac{1}{2C},\qquad\forall\,\lambda\ll 1.
Theorem 8.2 with \delta=1 implies
\Big\|c\lambda^{-\frac{(d-1)^{2}}{2d-1}}A^{T}\nabla u(\lambda Arz+\lambda q)-\partial_{x}\Big\|_{C^{0,1/3}(Q(1))}\leqslant C,\qquad\forall\,\lambda\ll 1.
Taking \lambda=r_{i} and setting \widetilde{z}_{i}=r^{-1}A^{-1}(r_{i}^{-1}z_{i}-q) and \widetilde{\zeta}_{i}=r^{-1}A^{-1}(r_{i}^{-1}\zeta_{i}-q), this gives
\displaystyle|c|r_{i}^{-\frac{(d-1)^{2}}{2d-1}}\big|\nabla u(z_{i})-\nabla u(\zeta_{i})\big|
\displaystyle\leqslant C\big|\widetilde{z}_{i}-\widetilde{\zeta}_{i}\big|^{1/3}
\displaystyle\leqslant Cr^{-1/3}r_{i}^{-1/3}|z_{i}-\zeta_{i}|^{1/3},\qquad\forall\,i\gg 1.
Since d\geqslant 2, we obtain
\big|\nabla u(z_{i})-\nabla u(\zeta_{i})\big|\leqslant C(r,c,C)|z_{i}-\zeta_{i}|^{1/3},
contradicting (10.18).
∎
Proof of Theorem 10.2.
Set t=-\log\min_{\partial K}|\nabla u|. Then Y_{t+1}\cap K\Subset K. Lemma 10.6 implies that Y_{t+1}\cap K has finitely many connected components, and then Theorem 10.1(i) (applied to each connected component) implies that \Crit(u)\cap K is finite.
∎
11 Discreteness of critical set
In this section we prove that:
Theorem 11.1.
Suppose u is nonconstant and \infty-harmonic in a domain \Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}. Then \Crit(u) is discrete in \Omega.
Theorem 11.1 is the final ingredient for showing C^{1,1/3} regularity:
Proof of Theorem 1.1.
Suppose \Delta_{\infty}u=0 in a domain \Omega and u is nonconstant. It is known by Theorem 11.1 that \Crit(u) is discrete. The result follows from Theorem 10.1(iv) and Theorem 8.3.
∎
Proof of Theorem 1.2.
We first use Theorem 11.1 to deduce that \Crit(u) is discrete. Then, we apply Theorem 10.1(ii) in a small disk centered at each critical point.
∎
A key idea in proving Theorem 11.1 is a Phragmén-Lindelöf type principle, which we use to bound the number of edges of the level sets of |\nabla u|. We also mention the work of Granlund-Marola [41], where a different Phragmén-Lindelöf principle is obtained.
Lemma 11.2.
Suppose 0<r<R, and D\subset B(R) is open with C^{1} boundary, and D\cap B(r)\neq\emptyset. Let w\in C^{0}(\overline{D}\cap B(R))\cap\Lip_{\loc}(D) be a continuously calibrated weak IMCF in D with outer obstacle \partial D\cap B(R), so that \inf_{D}(w)>-\infty. Then
\inf_{D\cap B(r)}(w)-\inf_{D}(w)\geqslant\log\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\frac{R-r}{|D\setminus B(r)|^{1/2}}\Big).
Proof.
Denote t_{0}=\inf_{D}(w). For any T>\inf_{D\cap B(r)}(w), we set E_{T}=\{w<T\}, so E_{T} is a C^{1} open set contained in D with E_{T}\cap B(r)\neq\emptyset. For all \rho\in(r,R), set
A(\rho)=\big|\partial E_{T}\cap B(\rho)\big|,\qquad S(\rho)=\big|\partial B(\rho)\cap E_{T}\big|,\qquad V(\rho)=\big|E_{T}\cap B(\rho)\big|.
Using (2.32) with F=B(\rho) for almost every \rho\in(r,R), we obtain
A(\rho)\leqslant e^{T-t_{0}}S(\rho).
The isoperimetric inequality and coarea formula gives
A(\rho)+S(\rho)\geqslant\sqrt{4\pi V(\rho)},\qquad\frac{dV}{d\rho}=S(\rho),\qquad\forall\,\text{a.e.}\,\rho\in(r,R).
Putting these together, it follows that
\frac{dV}{d\rho}\geqslant\frac{\sqrt{4\pi V}}{1+e^{T-t_{0}}}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad\frac{dV^{1/2}}{d\rho}\geqslant\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{1+e^{T-t_{0}}}\geqslant\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2e^{T-t_{0}}}.
Since V(r)>0, we may integrate this to obtain
\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2e^{T-t_{0}}}(R-r)\leqslant V(R)^{1/2}-V(r)^{1/2}\leqslant\big[V(R)-V(r)\big]^{1/2}\leqslant|D\setminus B(r)|^{1/2}.
The lemma follows by sending T\searrow\inf_{D\cap B(r)}(w).
∎
When |D| is small, we may upgrade the \log growth rate to a linear rate:
Lemma 11.3.
Suppose D\subset B(1) is open with C^{1} boundary, and D\cap B(1/2)\neq\emptyset, and w is a continuously calibrated weak IMCF in D with outer obstacle \partial D\cap B(1), so that \inf_{D}(w)>-\infty. If |D|\leqslant\frac{1}{32}, then
\inf_{D\cap B(1/2)}(w)-\inf_{D}(w)\geqslant\frac{1}{64|D|}.
Proof.
Set N=\lfloor\frac{1}{16|D|}\rfloor, so N\geqslant 2 and \frac{1}{32|D|}\leqslant N\leqslant\frac{1}{16|D|}. For k=0,1\cdots N, consider
\alpha_{k}=\inf_{D\cap B(1-k/2N)}(w),\qquad V_{k}=\big|D\cap B(1-k/2N)\setminus B(1-(k+1)/2N)\big|.
So \sum V_{k}\leqslant|D|. Lemma 11.2 gives
\alpha_{k+1}-\alpha_{k}\geqslant\log\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4NV_{k}^{1/2}}\Big),\qquad\forall\,k=0,1,\cdots,N-1.
Jensen’s inequality then implies
\alpha_{N}-\alpha_{0}\geqslant\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\log\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4NV_{k}^{1/2}}\Big)\geqslant N\log\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{4N^{1/2}|D|^{1/2}}\Big)\geqslant\frac{\log\sqrt{\pi}}{32|D|}\geqslant\frac{1}{64|D|}.\qed
Proof of Theorem 11.1.
Suppose otherwise that \Crit(u) is not discrete and \Crit(u)\neq\Omega. By choosing a non-isolated point z\in\partial\Crit(u)\cap\Omega and restricting to a small disk centered at z, it suffices to rule out the case that \Delta_{\infty}u=0 in B(1), and \sup_{B(1)}|\nabla u|<1, and
0\in\partial\Crit(u),\qquad\text{$0$ is a non-isolated critical point}.
(11.1)
Now assume that there exists u as in (11.1). Since 0\in\partial\Crit(u), there is a connected component V of B(1)\setminus\Crit(u) that intersects B(1/8). By the maximum principle for |\nabla u|, we have V\not\Subset B(1). So there is a path \Gamma\subset V joining some z_{1}\in V\cap B(1/8) and z_{2}\in V\cap\partial B(7/8). Then, we fix T\geqslant 1 so that
\min_{\Gamma}|\nabla u|>e^{-T}.
For the basepoint z_{0}=0, Theorem 6.1 gives a continuous function w:B(1)\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}, a regular domain \Omega_{\reg}=\{w<\infty\}, and a simple IMCF cluster \big(w,\nu,\{D_{i}\},\{\chi_{i}\}\big) in \Omega_{\reg}. Note that w(0)=-\log|\nabla u|(0)=+\infty. Denote
Y_{t}=\{w>t\}.
Let F_{t} resp. G_{t} denote the connected component of \{|\nabla u|<e^{-t}\} resp. Y_{t} that contains 0. Theorem 6.1(xii) and 9.1(i) imply that F_{t}=G_{t} is a simply-connected concave polygon. Notice that F_{t}\not\Subset B(1), otherwise 0 would be an isolated critical point of u by Theorem 10.1, contradicting (11.1).
For each t\geqslant T, let \mathcal{D}^{\prime} be the collection of connected components of D\cap\Int\big(\{w\leqslant t\}\big) with D ranging in \{D_{i}\} (note: some D_{i} may be separated into more than one region). We make implicit the dependence on t for notational simplicity. Each element in \mathcal{D}^{\prime} is a C^{1} domain in B(1) by the outer obstacle condition, and each edge of \partial F_{t} lies on the boundary of exactly one D\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}. Note that \overline{D}\cap B(1)\subset\Omega_{\reg}, so w\in C^{0}\big(\overline{D}\cap B(1)\big), for all D\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}.
Claim 1.
Let \{\gamma_{j}\} be the edges of \partial F_{t}, and D_{j}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime} be the region adjacent to \gamma_{j}. Then \{D_{j}\} are all distinct.
Proof.
If \gamma_{i},\gamma_{j} lie on different connected components of \partial F_{t}\cap B(1), then D_{i},D_{j} lie in different connected components of B(1)\setminus\overline{F_{t}}, as desired. If \gamma_{i} and \gamma_{j} are adjacent, i.e. they have a common endpoint z, then z\in\partial D_{i}\cap\partial D_{j}, hence D_{i},D_{j} have the opposite orientations (as regions in a simple cluster), hence D_{i}\neq D_{j}.
Assume that \gamma_{i},\gamma_{j} lie on the same connected component of \partial F_{t} but are not adjacent. Take another edge \gamma_{k} that lies between \gamma_{i} and \gamma_{j} on \partial F_{t}. We may assume D_{k}\neq D_{i},D_{j} (otherwise, we may replace \gamma_{i} or \gamma_{j} by \gamma_{k}). If D_{i}=D_{j}, then we may fix two points z_{i}\in\gamma_{i} and z_{j}\in\gamma_{j}, then connect them by a smooth path in D_{i}=D_{j} and another smooth path in F_{t}. These two paths form a loop that encloses \gamma_{k}, hence enclosing D_{k} as well, showing that D_{k}\Subset B(1). Then, \div(e^{-w}\nu)=0 in D_{k} and the outer obstacle condition yield a contradiction
0=\int_{D_{k}}\div(e^{-w}\chi_{k}\nu)=\int_{\partial D_{k}}e^{-w}\langle\chi_{k}\nu\,,\,\nu_{D_{k}}\rangle=\int_{\partial D_{k}}e^{-w}>0.\qed
For each t\geqslant T, there are at most 64\pi t elements in \mathcal{D}^{\prime} that intersect B(1/2): indeed, otherwise, some D among them would satisfy |D|<\frac{1}{64t}, and Lemma 11.3 implies
\inf_{B(1)}(w)\leqslant\inf_{D\cap B(1)}(w)\leqslant\inf_{D\cap B(1/2)}(w)-\frac{1}{64|D|}\leqslant t-\frac{1}{64|D|}<0.
Hence \partial\{w>0\}\cap B(1)\neq\emptyset. But Theorem 6.1(viii) implies |\nabla u|=e^{-w}=1 on \partial\{w>0\}\cap B(1), which contradicts our hypothesis \sup_{B(1)}|\nabla u|<1, as desired.
Recall 0\in F_{t} and \overline{F_{t}}\cap\Gamma\neq\emptyset and F_{t}\not\Subset B(1) and F_{t} is simply-connected, for all t\geqslant T. These imply that some connected component of \partial F_{t}\cap B(1/2), called \gamma_{t}, must have length at least 1/2. By Claim 1 and the above paragraph, \gamma_{t} has \leqslant 64\pi t edges, hence \leqslant 64\pi t vertices. Let E be the connected component of B(1/2)\setminus\gamma_{t} that contains 0, so E is a concave polygon with boundary \gamma_{t} in B(1/2). Applying Gauss-Bonnet on E, we have
\displaystyle 2\pi
\displaystyle=-\int_{\gamma_{t}}|\kappa|+\pi\cdot\text{(\# vertices in $\gamma_{t}$)}+2|\partial E\cap\partial B(1/2)|
\displaystyle+\sum\text{(exterior angles at $\overline{\gamma_{t}}\cap\partial B(1/2)$)},
which implies
\int_{\gamma_{t}}|\kappa|\leqslant 64\pi^{2}t+2\pi.
(11.2)
By Cauchy-Schwarz, we have
\int_{\gamma_{t}}\frac{1}{|\kappa|}\geqslant\frac{|\gamma_{t}|^{2}}{\int_{\gamma_{t}}|\kappa|}\geqslant\frac{1}{4(64\pi^{2}t+2\pi)},\qquad\forall\,t\geqslant T.
(11.3)
Notice that w\in\BV_{\loc}(\Omega_{\reg}): this follows from Lemma 3.5 (local finiteness of ridges) and [75, Lemma 2.24]. For almost every t, we have \mathcal{H}^{1}\big(\partial Y_{t}\cap(\cup\gamma_{j})\big)=0 and the curvature of \partial Y_{t} almost everywhere equals |\nabla w|. The coarea formula and (11.3) then gives
\frac{\pi}{4}\geqslant\big|\Omega_{\reg}\cap B(1/2)\big|\geqslant\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{\partial Y_{t}\cap B(1/2)}\frac{1}{|\nabla w|}\,dt\geqslant\int_{T}^{\infty}\int_{\gamma_{t}}\frac{1}{|\kappa|}\,dt=\infty,
(11.4)
which is a contradiction.
∎
12 Entire solutions with polynomial growth
The goal of this section is to prove the following:
Theorem 12.1.
Suppose that u is \infty-harmonic in \mathbb{R}^{2}, such that
|u|\leqslant C\big(1+|z|^{n}\big)
(12.1)
for some C,n>0. Suppose additionally that u is not a linear function. Then
(i)
There exists d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 2} so that the functions
u_{\lambda}(z)=\lambda^{-\frac{d^{2}}{2d-1}}u(\lambda z)
converge in C^{1}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}) to a degree d quasiradial solution as \lambda\to\infty.
(ii)
Let \{z_{1},\cdots,z_{l}\} be the critical points of u, and d_{k} be the degree of z_{k}. Then
\sum_{k=1}^{l}(d_{k}-1)=d-1.
Here, the degree of z_{k} is the number d_{k} appearing in Theorem 10.1(ii). As a result, for an entire solution with polynomial growth, the growth at \infty must fall into the discrete spectrum
|z|,\quad|z|^{4/3},\quad|z|^{9/5},\quad|z|^{16/7},\quad\cdots.
We further have a rigidity result:
Theorem 12.2.
Suppose that u is a nonlinear \infty-harmonic function in \mathbb{R}^{2}, such that
|u|\leqslant C\big(1+|z|^{9/5-\varepsilon}\big)
for some C,\varepsilon>0. Then u=|x|^{4/3}-|y|^{4/3} up to scaling and rigid motions.
Still, we denote
Y_{t}=\big\{|\nabla u|<e^{-t}\big\},\qquad\widetilde{Z}_{t}=\big\{|\nabla u|\leqslant e^{-t}\big\},\qquad Z_{t}=\Int(\widetilde{Z}_{t}).
The key step is to show that Y_{t} is connected, bounded, and has bounded number of edges for all t\leqslant T, for some T\in\mathbb{R}. Then Lemma 10.4 gives a support function h that is an ancient solution of \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h, and (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\cdot) has bounded number of roots. Then, Theorem 12.3 below implies that h has finitely many Fourier frequencies. This eventually leads to the identity
h(t,\theta)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\exp\Big(t-\frac{j^{2}t}{(d-1)^{2}}\Big)\Big(a_{j}\cos\frac{j\theta}{d-1}+b_{j}\sin\frac{j\theta}{d-1}\Big),
for \theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((2d-2)\pi\big) and t\ll-1, for some coefficients a_{j},b_{j}. By taking t\to-\infty of this identity, we obtain a degree d quasiradial solution as the blow-down limit.
Together with Section 10, we may fully describe the behavior of entire solutions with polynomial growth: the curves \partial Y_{t} are asymptotic to those of a quasiradial solution at t\to-\infty, then follow the IMCF and splitting pattern as in Section 10. It would be interesting to see whether all Fourier states at infinity are realizable when d\geqslant 4. For the d=3 case, this is answered in Subsection 4.1.
Proof of Theorem 12.1.
We have assumed that u is not affine or constant. By changing u(z)\mapsto a+bu(cz+d), we may assume
u(0)=0,\qquad|\nabla u(0)|=1.
(12.2)
For each t<0, let F_{t} be the connected component of Y_{t} that contains 0. Notice that F_{t}\neq\mathbb{R}^{2} for all t (otherwise, u is Lipschitz in \mathbb{R}^{2} hence linear, by [69, Theorem 4]).
Fix a sequence p\to\infty and p-harmonic functions u_{p}, so that u_{p}\to u in C^{0}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}). Apply Theorem 6.1 with basepoint z_{0}=0. We obtain a simple cluster \big(w,\nu,\mathcal{D}_{\reg},\{\chi_{D}\}\big) in an open set \Omega_{\reg}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}. Recall that \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\Omega_{\reg}\subset\Crit(u), and w is continuous from \mathbb{R}^{2} to \mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\} with \Omega_{\reg}=\{w<\infty\}. Note that w(0)=-\log|\nabla u(0)|=0. By (6.6) we have
\inf_{B(R)}(w)\geqslant-\log\sup_{B(R)}|\nabla u|>-\infty,\qquad\forall\,R>0.
(12.3)
Theorem 6.1(xii) implies that F_{t} coincides with the connected component of \{w>t\} containing 0, and Theorem 9.1 implies that F_{t} is a simply-connected concave polygon.
For each t<0, let \mathcal{D}^{\prime} be the collection of connected components of D\cap\Int\big(\{w\leqslant t\}\big) with D ranging in \mathcal{D}_{\reg}. Each element of \mathcal{D}^{\prime} is a C^{1} domain in \mathbb{R}^{2}, and each edge of \partial F_{t} lies on the boundary of exactly one D\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}. Following the proof of Claim 1 in Theorem 11.1, distinct edges of \partial F_{t} lie in the boundaries of distinct elements of \mathcal{D}^{\prime}.
Claim 1.
Set N(n)=16^{n+1}. Then \#\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\leqslant N(n) for all t<0.
It follows that
Claim 2.
\partial F_{t} has at most N(n) edges for all t<0.
Proof of Claim 1.
Suppose that \mathcal{D}^{\prime} contains M>N(n) regions \{D_{1},\cdots,D_{M}\}. Fix R\gg 1 so that B(R)\cap D_{i}\neq\emptyset for all i\in\{1,\cdots,M\}. It follows that
\inf_{D_{i}\cap B(R)}(w)\leqslant t<0\,\qquad\forall\,i.
(12.4)
For each i\in\{1,\cdots,M\} and j\geqslant 1, denote
\kappa_{ij}=\frac{|D_{i}\cap B(2^{j}R)|}{|B(2^{j}R)|},\qquad\osc_{ij}=\inf_{D_{i}\cap B(2^{j-1}R)}(w)-\inf_{D_{i}\cap B(2^{j}R)}(w).
Using Lemma 11.2 with D=D_{i}\cap B(2^{j}R) and \text{``$R$''}=2^{j}R and \text{``$r$''}=2^{j-1}R, we have
\osc_{ij}\geqslant\log\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}\frac{2^{j-1}R}{|D_{i}\cap B(2^{j}R)\setminus B(2^{j-1}R)|^{1/2}}\Big)\geqslant\log\Big(\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\kappa_{ij}}}\Big).
(12.5)
By Jensen’s inequality and \sum_{i}\kappa_{ij}\leqslant 1, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{M}\osc_{ij}\geqslant M\log\big(\sqrt{M}/4\big),\qquad\forall\,j\geqslant 1.
Summing this over j\in\{1,\cdots,J\} for J\geqslant 1 and using (12.4) yields
JM\log\big(\sqrt{M}/4\big)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{M}\Big(\inf_{D_{i}\cap B(R)}(w)-\inf_{D_{i}\cap B(2^{J}R)}(w)\Big)\leqslant-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\inf_{D_{i}\cap B(2^{J}R)}(w).
This implies
\inf_{B(2^{J}R)}(w)\leqslant-J\log\big(\sqrt{M}/4\big)<-Jn\log 4,\qquad\forall\,J\geqslant 1.
Since w=-\log|\nabla u| on each \partial\{w<t\}, this implies
\Big\{|\nabla u|=\exp\big(Jn\log 4\big)\Big\}\cap B(2^{J}R)\neq\emptyset,\qquad\forall\,J\geqslant 1.
But this violates the gradient estimate |\nabla u|\leqslant C\big(1+|z|^{n-1}\big) by (12.1).
∎
Using a blow-down argument, we show that:
Claim 3.
F_{t}\Subset\mathbb{R}^{2} for all t<0.
Proof.
Suppose F_{t} is unbounded for some t<0. Let \gamma_{t} be an unbounded component of \partial F_{t}. Fix z_{1}\in\gamma_{t}. For each R, let \gamma_{t,R} be the connected component of \gamma_{t}\cap B(R) containing z_{1}, and U be the connected component of B(R)\setminus\gamma_{t,R} containing 0. So U is a concave polygon with boundary \gamma_{t,R} in B(R). Using Gauss-Bonnet on U, we have
\displaystyle 2\pi
\displaystyle=-\int_{\gamma_{t,R}}|\kappa|+\pi\cdot\big(\text{\# vertices of $\gamma_{t,R}$}\big)
\displaystyle+R^{-1}|\partial U\cap\partial B(0,R)|+\sum\text{(exterior angles at $\overline{\gamma_{t,R}}\cap\partial B(R)$)}.
It follows that \int_{\gamma_{t,R}}|\kappa|\leqslant\pi N(n)+2\pi, and taking R\to\infty, that
\int_{\gamma_{t}}|\kappa|\leqslant\pi N(n)+2\pi.
Hence, \gamma_{t} must have an unbounded edge that is asymptotic to a ray. Denote this edge by \sigma_{t}, and up to a rotation, we may assume that \sigma_{t} is asymptotic to the positive x-axis.
Consider
M(R)=\max_{\overline{B}(R)}|u|,\qquad\forall\,R\geqslant 0.
The polynomial growth (12.1) implies
\lim_{R\to\infty}\Big[R^{-n-1}M(R)\Big]=0.
Therefore, we may find a sequence R_{i}\to\infty so that
(\lambda R_{i})^{-n-1}M(\lambda R_{i})\leqslant R_{i}^{-n-1}M(R_{i}),\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in[1,\infty),\ i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}.
(12.6)
Consider the rescaled functions
u_{i}(z)=\frac{u(R_{i}z)}{M(R_{i})}.
Clearly \sup_{B(1)}|u_{i}|=1, and (12.6) implies \sup_{B(\lambda)}|u_{i}|\leqslant\lambda^{n+1} for each \lambda\geqslant 1, i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}. By the interior gradient estimate and Arzelà-Ascoli, we may take a subsequential limit
u_{\infty}=\lim_{i\to\infty}u_{i}\qquad\text{in}\ \ C^{1}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).
Clearly u_{\infty}(0)=0 and \sup_{B(1)}|u_{\infty}|=1, and
|\nabla u_{i}|=\frac{R_{i}|\nabla u(R_{i}z)|}{M(R_{i})}=\frac{R_{i}e^{-t}}{M(R_{i})}\qquad\text{on}\ \ R_{i}^{-1}\sigma_{t}.
(12.7)
By comparison with cones, the function M(R)/R is nondecreasing in R. Then the superlinear growth implies \lim_{R\to\infty}\big[M(R)/R\big]=\infty. So (12.7) implies that |\nabla u_{\infty}|=0 on the nonnegative x-axis, contradicting Theorem 11.1.
∎
In summary: each F_{t} (t<0) is the connected component of Y_{t} that contains 0, it is bounded and has at most N(n) edges. Hence u has at most N(n) critical points in \mathbb{R}^{2}: otherwise, F_{t} would contain more than N(n) critical points for some t\ll-1, contradicting Theorem 10.1(iii). Fix T<0 so that F_{T} contains all the critical points of u.
Observe that Y_{t} is connected for all t<T: otherwise, some connected component V of Y_{t} does not contain critical points of u. Theorem 10.1(i) then implies V\not\Subset\mathbb{R}^{2}. This contradicts V\subset F_{s} for some s<t (indeed, take R>0 so that V\cap B(R)\neq\emptyset, then take s<t so that B(R)\subset F_{s}, then V\subset F_{s} by connectedness).
Next, note that Z_{t}\Subset\mathbb{R}^{2}, since Z_{t}\subset Y_{s} for all s<t. Thus, Lemma 10.9 implies Y_{t}=Z_{t} for all t<T. Now, the main condition (
P
t
) is satisfied for all t<T. By Lemma 10.4, the map
\Big(\!-\log|\nabla u|,\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}\Big):\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\widetilde{Z}_{T}\to(-\infty,T)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
lifts to a homeomorphism
\Theta:\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\widetilde{Z}_{T}\to(-\infty,T)\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big((N-2)\pi\big),
where N is the number of edges of \partial Y_{t} for all t<T. And, the support function
h(t,\theta)={\big\langle{\Theta^{-1}(t,\theta)}\,,\,{\nu_{\theta}}\big\rangle}
is a smooth solution of \partial_{t}h=\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h, with (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\cdot) having N nondegenerate roots.
Set d=N/2 (recall that N is even), so \theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\big(2(d-1)\pi\big). Using Theorem 12.3 below to a suitable parabolic rescaling of e^{-t}h, and comparing the number of roots of (\partial_{\theta\theta}h+h)(t,\cdot) with t\to-\infty, it follows that
h(t,\theta)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\exp\Big(t-\frac{j^{2}t}{(d-1)^{2}}\Big)\Big(a_{j}\sin\frac{j\theta}{d-1}+b_{j}\cos\frac{j\theta}{d-1}\Big)\qquad\forall\,t\in(-\infty,T),
for some coefficients a_{j},b_{j} with (a_{d},b_{d})\neq(0,0). Here, note that the constant term must vanish: indeed, the edges of \partial Z_{t} are all streamlines of u, thus the alternating length of edges of \partial Z_{t} must be zero, hence \int h(t,\cdot)=0 for each t, by (2.14).
As t\to-\infty, the highest frequency term dominates:
\exp\Big(\frac{2d-1}{(d-1)^{2}}t\Big)h(t,\theta)\xrightarrow[t\to-\infty]{C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1}(2(d-1)\pi))}a_{d}\sin\frac{d\theta}{d-1}+b_{d}\cos\frac{d\theta}{d-1}.
Arguing similarly as in Theorem 10.1(ii), one may show that the rescaled solution
u_{\lambda}(z)=\lambda^{-\frac{d^{2}}{2d-1}}u(\lambda z)
converges in C^{1}_{\loc}(\mathbb{R}^{2}) to a quasiradial solution as \lambda\to\infty. This proves item (i). Using Theorem 10.1(iii) to Y_{t} for any t<T, item (ii) follows.
∎
Proof of Theorem 12.2.
The growth condition implies that we must fall into the d=2 case of Theorem 12.1, hence from the proof, the set \partial Y_{t} has support function
h(t,\theta)=(a_{1}\sin\theta+b_{1}\cos\theta)+e^{-3t}(a_{2}\sin 2\theta+b_{2}\cos 2\theta)\qquad\forall\,t<T,\ \theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1},
for some T\in\mathbb{R}. Hence, after a rigid motion and scaling, we may assume that
h(t,\theta)=\frac{27}{128}e^{-3t}\sin 2\theta,\qquad\forall\,t<T,\ \theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.
Recall the recovery formula
\nabla u\Big(h(t,\theta)\nu_{\theta}+\partial_{\theta}h(t,\theta)\tau_{\theta}\Big)=e^{-t}\tau_{\theta},
see (10.15) in the proof of Theorem 10.1(ii). For the case here this gives
\nabla u\Big(\frac{27}{64}e^{-3t}\cos^{3}\theta,-\frac{27}{64}e^{-3t}\sin^{3}\theta\Big)=e^{-t}(\cos\theta,\sin\theta),\qquad\forall\,t<T,\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}.
This implies
\nabla u(x,y)=\frac{4}{3}\big(x^{1/3},-y^{1/3}\big)
for all (x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B(R), for some R\gg 1. Thus up to an additive constant, we have
u(x,y)=|x|^{4/3}-|y|^{4/3}\qquad\text{in}\ \ \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B(R).
By Jensen’s maximum principle, this implies u=|x|^{4/3}-|y|^{4/3} in the entire \mathbb{R}^{2}.
∎
Finally, we state the finite frequency lemma that we needed in Theorem 12.1:
Theorem 12.3.
Suppose f\in C^{\infty}\big((-\infty,0]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\big) solves the heat equation, such that f(t,\cdot) has at most 2N roots for all t\leqslant 0. Then there is k\leqslant N and constants a_{j},b_{j} (0\leqslant j\leqslant k), such that
f(t,\theta)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}e^{-j^{2}t}(a_{j}\cos j\theta+b_{j}\sin j\theta).
The theorem is a consequence of the following lemma:
Lemma 12.4.
Suppose f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1}) has 2k nondegenerate roots. Let a_{j},b_{j} denote the real Fourier coefficients of f (with the convention that b_{0}=0). Then
\|f\|_{W^{-2k-2,2}}\leqslant C(k)\sum_{j=0}^{k}\big(|a_{j}|+|b_{j}|\big).
(12.8)
Proof of Theorem 12.3 assuming 12.4.
By the Sturmian theory, there are k\leqslant N and t_{0}\leqslant 0 so that f(t,\cdot) has exactly 2k nondegenerate roots in \mathbb{S}^{1} for all t\leqslant t_{0}. Let a_{j},b_{j} denote the Fourier coefficients of f|_{t=0}. Then the Fourier coefficients of f|_{t=-\tau} are e^{j^{2}\tau}a_{j},e^{j^{2}\tau}b_{j}. Lemma 12.4 implies
\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{W^{-2k-2,2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\leqslant C(k)e^{k^{2}|t|}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\big(|a_{j}|+|b_{j}|\big),\qquad\forall\,t\leqslant t_{0}.
By the smoothing effect of the heat equation, it follows that
\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\leqslant C(k)\|f(t-1,\cdot)\|_{W^{-2k-2,2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\leqslant C\big(k,f|_{t=0}\big)e^{k^{2}|t|},\qquad\forall\,t\leqslant t_{0}.
This implies that f has vanishing Fourier frequencies beyond k, as desired.
∎
Lemma 12.4 may be viewed as a quantitative version of the Sturm-Hurwitz theorem. The latter states that if a nonzero function f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1}) has vanishing Fourier coefficients with frequencies \leqslant k, then f has at least 2k+2 roots. This corresponds to Lemma 12.4 with a_{0}=a_{1}=b_{1}=\cdots=b_{k}=0. An elegant proof of this fact (assuming that all roots are nondegenerate) appears in the book of Pólya-Szegö [68, II-141]: if otherwise f has 2l roots \theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{2l} (l\leqslant k), then
P(\theta)=\prod_{j=1}^{2l}\sin\frac{\theta-\theta_{j}}{2}
is a degree l trigonometric polynomial on \mathbb{S}^{1}, and satisfies \int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}fP>0 (since f,P have the same sign everywhere). These contradict the fact that f has vanishing low frequencies. Our proof is inspired by this argument.
Proof of Lemma 12.4.
If k=0, then f is either positive or negative in \mathbb{S}^{1}, hence
\Big|\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}f\varphi\Big|\leqslant\Big|\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}f\Big|\cdot\|\varphi\|_{C^{0}}\leqslant C|a_{0}|\cdot\|\varphi\|_{W^{2,2}}.
Now assume k\geqslant 1, and \theta_{1}<\theta_{2}<\cdots<\theta_{2k} are the roots of f. It suffices to show that
\Big|\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}f\varphi\Big|\leqslant C(k)\sum_{i=0}^{k}\big(|a_{i}|+|b_{i}|\big)\cdot\|\varphi\|_{C^{2k+1}},\qquad\forall\,\varphi\in C^{2k+1}(\mathbb{S}^{1}).
(12.9)
Indeed, (12.8) then follows from the Sobolev inequality.
It is always possible to find a point \omega\in\mathbb{S}^{1}, such that |\omega-\theta_{j}|>\pi/4k for each j. We fix any valid choice of \omega. Consider the (well-defined) trigonometric polynomials
P_{j}(\theta)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\sin\frac{\theta-\omega}{2}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant s\leqslant j}\sin\frac{\theta-\theta_{s}}{2}\qquad\text{(if $j$ is odd),}\\
&\prod_{1\leqslant s\leqslant j}\sin\frac{\theta-\theta_{s}}{2}\qquad\text{(if $j$ is even),}\end{aligned}\right.
and inductively,
Q_{1}=\varphi(\theta_{1}),\qquad Q_{j+1}=Q_{j}+\frac{\varphi(\theta_{j+1})-Q_{j}(\theta_{j+1})}{P_{j}(\theta_{j+1})}P_{j}.
So P_{j},Q_{j} are trigonometric polynomials with degree \leqslant\lceil j/2\rceil, with Q_{j}(\theta)=\varphi(\theta) for all \theta\in\{\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{j}\} and j\in\{1,\cdots,2k\}. In fact, Q_{j} are the Newton interpolations of \varphi.
We claim that the coefficients of Q_{j} are uniformly bounded by C(k)\|\varphi\|_{C^{2k+1}}, for all 1\leqslant j\leqslant 2k. This is clear for Q_{1}. Suppose that our claim holds for Q_{j}. Notice that \varphi-Q_{j} takes zero value in the set \{\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{j}\}. Notice the general fact: if f is a C^{r} section of a line bundle over \mathbb{S}^{1} and satisfies f(0)=0, then
\Big\|\frac{f(\theta)}{\sin(\theta/2)}\Big\|_{C^{r-1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}\leqslant C(r)\|f\|_{C^{r}(\mathbb{S}^{1})}.
(12.10)
Here, we view \sin(\theta/2) as a section of the non-orientable bundle over \mathbb{S}^{1}. To show (12.10), we view both sides as periodic functions on \mathbb{R}, and notice that
f(x)=\int_{0}^{x}f^{\prime}(y)\,dy=x\int_{0}^{1}f^{\prime}(xt)\,dt,
hence
\frac{f(x)}{\sin(x/2)}=\frac{x}{\sin(x/2)}\int_{0}^{1}f^{\prime}(xt)\,dt,\qquad\forall\,x\in[-\pi,\pi].
Then (12.10) follows since x/\sin(x/2) is real analytic in a neighborhood of [-\pi,\pi]. Now if j is even, then applying (12.10) successively with center \theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{j}, we obtain
\Big\|\frac{\varphi-Q_{j}}{P_{j}}\Big\|_{C^{2k+1-j}}\leqslant C(k)\|\varphi-Q_{j}\|_{C^{2k+1}}\leqslant C(k)\|\varphi\|_{C^{2k+1}},
(12.11)
where the last inequality uses the induction hypothesis. If j is odd, then applying (12.10) at \theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{j} gives
\Big\|\frac{\varphi-Q_{j}}{\prod_{s\leqslant j}\sin(\theta-\theta_{s})/2}\Big\|_{C^{2k+1-j}}\leqslant C(k)\|\varphi-Q_{j}\|_{C^{2k+1}}\leqslant C(k)\|\varphi\|_{C^{2k+1}}.
(12.12)
Next, notice that
\Big|\frac{\varphi(\theta_{j+1})-Q_{j}(\theta_{j+1})}{P_{j}(\theta_{j+1})}\Big|\leqslant C(k)\|\varphi\|_{C^{2k+1}},\qquad\forall\,j\in\{1,\cdots,2k\}.
(12.13)
Indeed, when j is even, this is a direct consequence of (12.11); if j is odd, this follows from (12.12) and |\omega-\theta_{j+1}|>\pi/4k. Our initial claim then follows from (12.13) and induction. As a byproduct, we find that (12.11) holds for j=2k.
Finally, we estimate
\displaystyle\Big|\int f\varphi\Big|
\displaystyle\leqslant\Big|\int fQ_{2k}\Big|+\int|f|\,|\varphi-Q_{2k}|
\displaystyle\leqslant\Big|\int fQ_{2k}\Big|+C(k)\|\varphi\|_{C^{2k+1}}\int|f|\,|P_{2k}|\qquad\text{(by \eqref{eq-sturmian:reg_Q/P})}
\displaystyle\leqslant\Big|\int fQ_{2k}\Big|+C(k)\|\varphi\|_{C^{2k+1}}\Big|\int fP_{2k}\Big|\qquad\text{($f,P_{2k}$ have the same sign)}.
Recall that the degrees of P_{2k},Q_{2k} are no greater than k, and the coefficients of P_{2k}, Q_{2k} are bounded respectively by C(k) and C(k)\|\varphi\|_{C^{2k+1}}. Then (12.9) follows.
∎
Appendix A Estimates on planar p-harmonic functions
The aim of this section is to prove Lemma 5.4. Let v solve (5.1) with p\in[3,\infty) and \varepsilon\in(0,1]. Denote J=(|\nabla v|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{1/2}. Hence v solves \div\big(J^{p-2}\nabla v\big)=0. It is easy to see that
\nabla J=J^{-1}\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v,\qquad\nabla J^{2}=2\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v.
(A.1)
The equation for v can now be written as
\Delta_{\infty}v+\frac{J^{2}}{p-2}\Delta v=0.
(A.2)
We prove (5.3) (5.4) closely following the arguments in [48] and [54]. Recall from [48, Lemma 2.1] that for any C^{2} function v it holds
\displaystyle-\det\nabla^{2}v
\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\div\big(\Delta v\nabla v-\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v\big),
(A.3)
\displaystyle|\nabla v|^{2}(-\det\nabla^{2}v)
\displaystyle=|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}-\Delta v\Delta_{\infty}v,
(A.4)
where all the expressions hold in the classical sense. Combining (A.2) (A.4) we have
|\nabla v|^{2}(-\det\nabla^{2}v)=|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}+\frac{J^{2}}{p-2}(\Delta v)^{2}\geqslant 0,
and hence
-\det\nabla^{2}v\geqslant\frac{|\nabla v|^{2}}{J^{2}}(-\det\nabla^{2}v)=|\nabla J|^{2}+\frac{(\Delta v)^{2}}{p-2}.
Combining this with (A.3), we obtain
\int\Big(\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\Delta v\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla\varphi\rangle}_{\operatorname{I}}\underbrace{-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}v(\nabla v,\nabla\varphi)}_{\operatorname{II}}\Big)\geqslant\int\Big(|\nabla J|^{2}+\frac{(\Delta v)^{2}}{p-2}\Big)\varphi
(A.5)
for any nonnegative test function \varphi\in C^{1}_{0}.
Proof of Lemma 5.4(ii).
Test (A.5) with \varphi=J^{2}\eta^{2} with \eta a cutoff function. We compute
\displaystyle\operatorname{I}
\displaystyle=\int\Delta v{\big\langle{\nabla v}\,,\,{\eta^{2}\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v+J^{2}\eta\nabla\eta}\big\rangle}=\int\eta^{2}\Delta v\Delta_{\infty}v+J^{2}\eta\Delta v\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla\eta\rangle
\displaystyle=\int-\frac{J^{2}\eta^{2}}{p-2}(\Delta v)^{2}+J^{2}\eta\Delta v\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla\eta\rangle
\displaystyle=\int-\frac{J^{2}\eta^{2}}{p-2}(\Delta v)^{2}
\displaystyle-\nabla v\cdot\Big(2\eta\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla\eta\rangle\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v+J^{2}\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla\eta\rangle\nabla\eta+J^{2}\eta\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla\eta+J^{2}\eta\nabla^{2}\eta\cdot\nabla v\Big)
\displaystyle=\int-\frac{J^{2}\eta^{2}}{p-2}(\Delta v)^{2}+\frac{2J^{2}\eta}{p-2}\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla\eta\rangle\Delta v-J^{2}\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla\eta\rangle^{2}
\displaystyle-J^{2}\eta\nabla^{2}v(\nabla v,\nabla\eta)-J^{2}\eta\nabla^{2}\eta(\nabla v,\nabla v),
and
\displaystyle\operatorname{II}
\displaystyle=\int-\eta^{2}|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}-J^{2}\eta\nabla^{2}v(\nabla v,\nabla\eta).
Hence, (A.5) implies
\displaystyle\int 2\eta^{2}|\nabla^{2}v
\displaystyle\cdot\nabla v|^{2}+\frac{2J^{2}\eta^{2}}{p-2}(\Delta v)^{2}
(A.6)
\displaystyle\leqslant\int\frac{2J^{2}\eta}{p-2}|\nabla v|\,|\nabla\eta|\,|\Delta v|+2J^{2}\eta|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\,|\nabla\eta|+J^{2}\eta|\nabla v|^{2}|\nabla^{2}\eta|.
Then using Young’s inequality, (A.6) implies
\int\eta^{2}\big|\nabla|\nabla v|^{2}\big|^{2}=4\int\eta^{2}|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}\leqslant C\int J^{4}\big(|\nabla\eta|^{2}+|\eta|\,|\nabla^{2}\eta|\big).
(A.7)
This implies (5.3) by a suitable choice of \eta.
∎
Proof of Lemma 5.4(iii).
Test (A.5) with \varphi=(v-P)^{2}\eta^{2} with \eta a cutoff function:
\displaystyle\operatorname{I}
\displaystyle=\int\Delta v(v-P)\eta^{2}\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla v-\nabla P\rangle+\Delta v(v-P)^{2}\eta\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla\eta\rangle
\displaystyle=\int-\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla v-\nabla P\rangle^{2}\eta^{2}-2(v-P)\eta\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla\eta\rangle\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla v-\nabla P\rangle
\displaystyle-(v-P)\eta^{2}\nabla^{2}v(\nabla v,2\nabla v-\nabla P)-2(v-P)\eta\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla v-\nabla P\rangle\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla\eta\rangle
\displaystyle-(v-P)^{2}\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla\eta\rangle^{2}-(v-P)^{2}\eta\nabla^{2}v(\nabla v,\nabla\eta)-(v-P)^{2}\eta\nabla^{2}\eta(\nabla v,\nabla v)
\displaystyle\leqslant\int-\big(|\nabla v|^{2}-\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla P\rangle\big)^{2}\eta^{2}+4|v-P|\cdot\eta\cdot|\nabla\eta|\cdot|\nabla v|^{2}\cdot\big(|\nabla v|+|\nabla P|\big)
\displaystyle+2|v-P|\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\cdot\big(|\nabla v|+|\nabla P|\big)
\displaystyle+|v-P|^{2}\cdot\eta\cdot|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\cdot|\nabla\eta|+|v-P|^{2}\cdot\eta\cdot|\nabla v|^{2}\cdot|\nabla^{2}\eta|.
By Cauchy-Schwarz, this gives
\displaystyle\operatorname{I}
\displaystyle\leqslant-\int\big(|\nabla v|^{2}-\langle\nabla v\,,\,\nabla P\rangle\big)^{2}\eta^{2}
\displaystyle+C\Big(\int|v-P|^{2}\big(|\nabla v|^{2}+|\nabla P|^{2}\big)\eta^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int|\nabla v|^{4}|\nabla\eta|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}
\displaystyle+C\Big(\int|v-P|^{2}\big(|\nabla v|^{2}+|\nabla P|^{2}\big)\eta^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}\eta^{2}\Big)^{1/2}
\displaystyle+C\Big(\int|v-P|^{4}|\nabla\eta|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}\eta^{2}\Big)^{1/2}
\displaystyle+C\Big(\int|v-P|^{4}\eta|\nabla^{2}\eta|\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int|\nabla v|^{4}\eta|\nabla^{2}\eta|\Big)^{1/2}.
Moreover,
\displaystyle\operatorname{II}
\displaystyle\leqslant\int|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\cdot\Big(|v-P|\cdot\eta^{2}\cdot\big(|\nabla v|+|\nabla P|\big)+|v-P|^{2}\cdot\eta\cdot|\nabla\eta|\Big)
\displaystyle\leqslant\Big(\int|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}\eta^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int|v-P|^{2}\big(|\nabla v|^{2}+|\nabla P|^{2}\big)\eta^{2}\Big)^{1/2}
\displaystyle+\Big(\int|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}\eta^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int|v-P|^{4}|\nabla\eta|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}.
Inserting these into (A.5), then using (A.7), then finally choosing an appropriate \eta, the inequality (5.4) follows.
∎
Next, we prove (5.2) (5.5) closely following the estimates in [39]. Let v be as stated above, so
\Delta_{\infty}v+\frac{J^{2}}{p-2}\Delta v=0.
(A.8)
Consider the associated linearized operator
\mathcal{L}\psi:=\nabla^{2}\psi(\nabla v,\nabla v)+2\nabla^{2}v(\nabla\psi,\nabla v)+\frac{J^{2}}{p-2}\Delta\psi+\frac{2\Delta v}{p-2}\langle\nabla\psi\,,\,\nabla v\rangle.
(A.9)
Notice the relation
\mathcal{L}(XY)=X\mathcal{L}Y+Y\mathcal{L}X+2\langle\nabla X\,,\,\nabla v\rangle\langle\nabla Y\,,\,\nabla v\rangle+\frac{2J^{2}}{p-2}\langle\nabla X\,,\,\nabla Y\rangle,
(A.10)
and \mathcal{L}\partial_{x}v=\mathcal{L}\partial_{y}v=0 by translation symmetry or explicit calculation. Then notice that
\mathcal{L}|\nabla v|^{2}=\mathcal{L}(v_{x}^{2})+\mathcal{L}(v_{y}^{2})=2|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}+\frac{2J^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla^{2}v|^{2}.
(A.11)
We may also calculate
\mathcal{L}v=3\Delta_{\infty}v+\frac{J^{2}+2|\nabla v|^{2}}{p-2}\Delta v=\Big(3-\frac{J^{2}+2|\nabla v|^{2}}{J^{2}}\Big)\Delta_{\infty}v=2\varepsilon^{2}J^{-2}\Delta_{\infty}v,
(A.12)
and
\mathcal{L}y=2\nabla^{2}v(\nabla v,\partial_{y})+\frac{2\Delta v}{p-2}\partial_{y}v=2\nabla^{2}v\big(\nabla v,\partial_{y}-J^{-2}(\partial_{y}v)\nabla v\big).
(A.13)
Proof of Lemma 5.4(i).
We first assume that v solves (A.8). Let \eta\in C^{2}_{0}(B(z,3.9r)) be a cutoff function with \eta|_{B(z,3.5r)}\equiv 1 and
|\nabla\eta|\leqslant Mr^{-1},\qquad|\nabla^{2}\eta|,|\Delta\eta|\leqslant Mr^{-2},
where M is a universal constant. We calculate and estimate term by term:
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\big(\eta^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}\big)
\displaystyle=\eta^{2}\Big(2|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}+\frac{2J^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla^{2}v|^{2}\Big)
\displaystyle+|\nabla v|^{2}\Big(\nabla^{2}\eta^{2}(\nabla v,\nabla v)+2\nabla^{2}v(\nabla\eta^{2},\nabla v)+\frac{J^{2}\Delta\eta^{2}}{p-2}+\frac{2\Delta v}{p-2}\langle\nabla\eta^{2}\,,\,\nabla v\rangle\Big)
\displaystyle+2\langle\nabla|\nabla v|^{2}\,,\,\nabla v\rangle\langle\nabla\eta^{2}\,,\,\nabla v\rangle+\frac{2J^{2}}{p-2}\langle\nabla|\nabla v|^{2}\,,\,\nabla\eta^{2}\rangle
\displaystyle\geqslant 2\eta^{2}|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}+\frac{2J^{2}\eta^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla^{2}v|^{2}-4M^{2}r^{-2}|\nabla v|^{4}
\displaystyle-4Mr^{-1}\eta|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\,|\nabla v|^{2}-\frac{4M^{2}r^{-2}J^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla v|^{2}-4Mr^{-1}\eta|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\,|\nabla v|^{2}
\displaystyle-8Mr^{-1}\eta|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\,|\nabla v|^{2}-\frac{8Mr^{-1}\eta J^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|.
Notice that
16Mr^{-1}\eta|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\,|\nabla v|^{2}\leqslant\eta^{2}|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}+64M^{2}r^{-2}|\nabla v|^{4}
and
\frac{8Mr^{-1}\eta J^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\leqslant\frac{J^{2}\eta^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla^{2}v|^{2}+\frac{16M^{2}r^{-2}J^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla v|^{2}.
Hence, we obtain
\mathcal{L}\big(\eta^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}\big)\geqslant\eta^{2}|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}-C_{2}r^{-2}|\nabla v|^{4}-\frac{C_{2}r^{-2}J^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla v|^{2},
(A.14)
for some universal constant C_{2}. Then, by (A.12) (A.10) we note that
\mathcal{L}v^{2}\geqslant-4|v|\,|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\,|\nabla v|+2|\nabla v|^{4}+\frac{2J^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla v|^{2}.
(A.15)
Denote F=C_{2}^{-1}r^{2}\eta^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}+v^{2}. Combining (A.14) (A.15) we have
\eta^{4}\mathcal{L}F\geqslant C_{2}^{-1}r^{2}\eta^{6}|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}+\eta^{4}|\nabla v|^{4}-4\eta^{4}|v|\,|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\,|\nabla v|.
Notice that
4\eta^{4}|v|\,|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\,|\nabla v|\leqslant C_{2}^{-1}r^{2}\eta^{6}|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}+4C_{2}r^{-2}v^{2}\eta^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}.
We finally obtain
\eta^{4}\mathcal{L}F\geqslant\eta^{4}|\nabla v|^{4}-4C_{2}r^{-2}v^{2}\eta^{2}|\nabla v|^{2}.
(A.16)
Suppose F is maximized at a point z_{0}\in\overline{B(z,3.9r)}. If \eta(z_{0})=0, then for all z^{\prime}\in B(z,3.5r) we have
C_{2}^{-1}r^{2}|\nabla v(z^{\prime})|^{2}\leqslant F(z^{\prime})\leqslant F(z_{0})=v(z_{0})^{2}\leqslant\sup_{B(z,4r)}|v|^{2}.
If \eta(z_{0})>0, then z_{0} is an interior critical point of F, and \mathcal{L}F(z_{0})\leqslant 0. By (A.16), this implies \eta(z_{0})^{2}|\nabla v(z_{0})|^{2}\leqslant 4C_{2}r^{-2}v(z_{0})^{2}. Hence for all z^{\prime}\in B(z,3.5r) we have
C_{2}^{-1}r^{2}|\nabla v(z^{\prime})|^{2}\leqslant F(z^{\prime})\leqslant F(z_{0})\leqslant C_{2}^{-1}r^{2}\cdot 4C_{2}r^{-2}v(z_{0})^{2}+v(z_{0})^{2}.
In either case, we obtain |\nabla v|^{2}\leqslant 8C_{2}r^{-2}\sup_{B(z,4r)}|v|^{2} in B(z,3.5r). This proves the desired estimate for solutions of (5.1). It is known that p-harmonic functions (3\leqslant p<\infty) can be locally approximated by solutions of (5.1), hence the estimate passes to p-harmonic functions as well. Finally, the \infty-harmonic case is well-known [29].
∎
Proof of Lemma 5.4(iv).
Recall that 0<\lambda\leqslant a, and v solves (5.1) and satisfies |v-ay|\leqslant\lambda r in B(4r). By the gradient estimate (5.2), there is a universal constant C_{3} so that
|\nabla v|\leqslant C_{3}a\qquad\text{in}\ \ B(3.5r).
Denote \Lambda=C_{3}a. Let \eta\in C^{2}_{0}(B(3r)) be a cutoff function, so that \eta|_{B(r)}\equiv 1 and
|\nabla\eta|\leqslant Mr^{-1},\qquad|\nabla^{2}\eta|,|\Delta\eta|\leqslant Mr^{-2}\qquad\text{in}\ \ B(3r),
for some universal constant M.
Combining (A.12) (A.13) we have in B(3.5r)
\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{L}(v-ay)\big|
\displaystyle=\Big|2(\varepsilon^{2}J^{-2})\Delta_{\infty}v-2a\nabla^{2}v\big(\nabla v,\partial_{y}-J^{-2}(\partial_{y}v)\nabla v\big)\Big|\leqslant 4(\Lambda+a)|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|.
Denote A=|\nabla v|^{2}-a\partial_{y}v. Thus in B(3.5r)
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(v-ay)^{2}
\displaystyle=2(v-ay)\mathcal{L}(v-ay)+2\langle\nabla v-a\partial_{y}\,,\,\nabla v\rangle^{2}+\frac{2J^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla v-a\partial_{y}|^{2}
(A.17)
\displaystyle\geqslant-8(\Lambda+a)\lambda r|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|+2A^{2}.
Using (A.11) (A.10) and \mathcal{L}\partial_{y}v=0, we further compute
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\eta^{2}A^{2})
\displaystyle=\eta^{2}\Big(2A\mathcal{L}A+2\langle\nabla A\,,\,\nabla v\rangle^{2}+\frac{2J^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla A|^{2}\Big)
\displaystyle+A^{2}\Big(\nabla^{2}\eta^{2}(\nabla v,\nabla v)+2\nabla^{2}v(\nabla\eta^{2},\nabla v)+\frac{J^{2}}{p-2}\Delta\eta^{2}+\frac{2\Delta v}{p-2}\langle\nabla\eta^{2}\,,\,\nabla v\rangle\Big)
\displaystyle+2\langle\nabla A^{2}\,,\,\nabla v\rangle\langle\nabla\eta^{2}\,,\,\nabla v\rangle+\frac{2J^{2}}{p-2}\langle\nabla A^{2}\,,\,\nabla\eta^{2}\rangle
\displaystyle\geqslant 4\eta^{2}A|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}+\frac{4J^{2}\eta^{2}A}{p-2}|\nabla^{2}v|^{2}+2\eta^{2}\langle\nabla A\,,\,\nabla v\rangle^{2}+\frac{2J^{2}\eta^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla A|^{2}
\displaystyle-A^{2}\Big(4M^{2}r^{-2}\Lambda^{2}+4Mr^{-1}\eta|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|+\frac{4M^{2}r^{-2}J^{2}}{p-2}+4Mr^{-1}\eta|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|\Big)
\displaystyle\left.\begin{aligned} &\qquad-\Big[2\eta^{2}\langle\nabla A\,,\,\nabla v\rangle^{2}+8A^{2}\langle\nabla\eta\,,\,\nabla v\rangle^{2}\Big]\quad\\
&\qquad-\Big[\frac{2J^{2}\eta^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla A|^{2}+\frac{8J^{2}A^{2}}{p-2}|\nabla\eta|^{2}\Big].\end{aligned}\right\}\text{(by Young's inequality)}
If A>0, then we use Young’s inequality to deduce
\displaystyle 8Mr^{-1}\eta A^{2}|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|
\displaystyle\leqslant 4\eta^{2}A|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}+4M^{2}r^{-2}A^{3}
\displaystyle\leqslant 4\eta^{2}A|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}+8M^{2}r^{-2}\Lambda^{2}A^{2},
and notice that
8A^{2}\langle\nabla\eta\,,\,\nabla v\rangle^{2}\leqslant 8M^{2}r^{-2}\Lambda^{2}A^{2},\qquad\frac{12M^{2}r^{-2}}{p-2}J^{2}A^{2}\leqslant 36M^{2}r^{-2}(\varepsilon^{2}p^{-1}+\Lambda^{2})A^{2}.
Inserting these into the main expression, we obtain
\mathcal{L}(\eta^{2}A^{2})\geqslant-CM^{2}r^{-2}(\varepsilon^{2}p^{-1}+\Lambda^{2})A^{2}\qquad\text{ in $B(3.5r)$, if }A>0,
(A.18)
for a universal constant C. Denote C_{4}=CM^{2}(\varepsilon^{2}p^{-1}+\Lambda^{2}). Consider the quantity
F=\eta^{2}\max\{A,0\}^{2}+C_{4}r^{-2}(v-ay)^{2}+C_{4}^{1/2}\lambda|\nabla v|^{2}.
By (A.11) (A.17) (A.18) and Young’s inequality, whenever A>0 we have
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}F
\displaystyle\geqslant C_{4}r^{-2}A^{2}-8(\Lambda+a)C_{4}r^{-1}\lambda|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|+2C_{4}^{1/2}\lambda|\nabla^{2}v\cdot\nabla v|^{2}
(A.19)
\displaystyle\geqslant C_{4}r^{-2}\big[A^{2}-8(\Lambda+a)^{2}C_{4}^{1/2}\lambda\big].
Suppose \max_{\overline{B(3.5r)}}(F) is attained at a point z_{1}\in\overline{B(3.5r)}. If A(z_{1})\leqslant 0 or \eta(z_{1})=0, then for all z\in B(r) with A(z)>0, we have
A^{2}(z)=\eta^{2}A^{2}(z)\leqslant F(z)\leqslant F(z_{1})\leqslant C_{4}\lambda^{2}+C_{4}^{1/2}\Lambda^{2}\lambda\leqslant\big(C_{4}a+C_{4}^{1/2}\Lambda^{2}\big)\lambda.
If A(z_{1})>0 and \eta(z_{1})>0 both hold, then z_{1} must be an interior critical point of F, and \mathcal{L}F(z_{1})\leqslant 0, and (A.19) is applicable to z_{1}. It follows that A(z_{1})^{2}\leqslant 8(\Lambda+a)^{2}C_{4}^{1/2}\lambda. Hence for all z\in B(r) with A(z)>0, we have
A^{2}(z)\leqslant F(z)\leqslant F(z_{1})\leqslant A(z_{1})^{2}+C_{4}\lambda^{2}+C_{4}^{1/2}\Lambda^{2}\lambda\leqslant\big(8(\Lambda+a)^{2}C_{4}^{1/2}+C_{4}a+C_{4}^{1/2}\Lambda^{2}\big)\lambda.
Inserting the definitions of C_{4},\Lambda, we obtain an estimate of the form
A\leqslant C(\varepsilon p^{-1/2}+a)^{3/2}\lambda^{1/2}\qquad\text{in}\ \ B(r),
for a universal constant C, as desired.
∎
